    - employment to population 64.6% (target 63.9);
    - participation 65.6% (67.1);
    - saving 5.8% (6.5);
    - renters' housing costs 17.3% (20.2);
    - group participation 51.8% (50.0);
    - affiliation −2.0 points a year (−1.64).
  - **Out:**
    - unemployment 1.5% (4.6): hiring is too quick (below);
    - unmet demand 11.8% (at most 5): the shortage spiral (below).
  - **Still shrinking:** about 100 people a year leave and 21 arrive, about half of the leavers for want of a home. That's the next thing to look at, with the spiral.
  - **Found on the way: renewals were evicting people.** The landlord works out a tenant's paying account from its leases in force. A renewal doesn't name it, so once the old lease ended the landlord saw nothing paid, counted two months' arrears, and gave notice. Now it looks at any of the tenant's leases (landlord 0.10.1).
  - **Found, not fixed: a shortage spiral in some seeds.** In 2 of 10 seeds households went without 28–39% of what they ordered, against 1–12% in the rest.
    - Shops run short, and the customer is refunded and orders the rest elsewhere.
    - The repeat orders count as demand, both for the shops and for the measure.
    - Shops order to that demand but are held back by cash (about 70% of restocks capped).
    - To do: count an order that's refunded and placed again once; and see why shops' cash can't keep up (their payouts to shareholders against their buffer).

- **Hiring that takes time: tried, and reverted** (2026-09-30). Unemployment sits near 1–2% (target 4.6%): employers choose among a posting's applicants at their next wake, so someone out of work is hired within days. A posting kept open 21 days before choosing (as employers advertise, then shortlist) sent unemployment to 38% (10 seeds, one year): firms post one job at a time, and a posting republished at a new wage starts its wait again, so they couldn't replace the people who left. Reverted.
  - **Next:** recruit as employers do: several postings at once for the headcount wanted, a closing date that doesn't restart when the wage moves, and all the applicants seen then. And the separation side: jobs ending run at about 2.8% of employment a month, against roughly 1–1.5% in labour force gross flows (to confirm).

- **A wake asked for between ticks takes the next tick, not a whole tick on** (2026-09-30; kernel `schedule.rs` `finished`). A unit woken by news between ticks, asking to wake again at a gathering's end ten minutes later, was put a whole tick (an hour) on: people stayed at the hall an hour past its end. Now the soonest is the next tick boundary after now; for a wake on a tick (the usual), that's the same as before.
  - **Revisit if:** a finer clock is wanted for news (then wakes on exact times, which the tick exists to batch).

- **Contract revision, 2026-10-01** (Naz: "yes to all"; docs/design/world-expansion.md §1; contracts/README.md "Changes from v0.7").
  - **Periodic tenancies:** `then: "periodic"` on an agreement's terms. Leases where the world's tenancy law says so (the profile's `tenancy.after_fixed_term`) run on past their term until notice. Landlords renew only leases that would end. A test runs 13 months and finds no tenancy ended without notice.
  - **Ratified as built:** `log.detail`, the run dataset format, `notices`, `framing.debrief_text`.
  - **Already in 0.7, now used:** beliefs attach to `assertion`s (so news, promises and rumours can be believed); decisions carry the mind's `state`; the `consult` purpose.
  - **Deferred still:** B13 (compact logs), B1 (generated types). Construction payments fold into materials from suppliers (world-expansion §6.1).
  - **Revisit if:** a place's law lets a landlord end a periodic tenancy only for reasons: then the reasons are the landlord's to give, and a tribunal's to judge (§5).

- **Nature: contract signed** (Naz, 2026-10-01: "nature's contract is signed off"; built on the `nature` branch, merged once the suite passes; docs/design/world-expansion.md §2; docs/behaviour/nature.md and environment.md).
  - **In Rust:** the physics, in `oikos-nature` (covers and their succession; fields that spread, decay and flow; populations; extinction; survey estimates). The world steps it daily, censuses it monthly, clears land on demolition, delivers surveys to the surveyor only, and shows conspicuous species to passers-by.
  - **In TypeScript:** the town's environment office (`city.environment`); planning applying a habitat law through the office's register; the generator's district around the town, from the profile's `nature`. The profile's species are the squirrel glider, the growling grass frog and the superb fairy-wren, with their densities and ranges sourced and their rates placeholders.
  - **Found by running it.** Each was a gap between the physics and how real populations behave, and is fixed in the physics, not tuned:
    - per-cell capacity can't hold a sparse animal, so crowding is felt over a home range, per 1,000 cells;
    - counting oneself as crowding halves a sparse species, so crowding counts the others;
    - dying on the first unsuitable step, and settling on the first poor cell, drained the fairy-wrens, so dispersers walk within a range and choose habitat by quality;
    - young born in place, and random walks that go nowhere, halved them again, so the young leave the home range on a correlated walk;
    - losses off the world's edge were an artefact, so the edge reflects;
    - the generator's opening densities aren't nature's own balance, so the world spins nature up before the start (10 years: the slowest species settle within it; a dense spin-up loads the small town's district in 1.5 seconds).
  - **The result:** five years of the small town's district after spin-up, three seeds: frogs 3,300–4,600, gliders 45–63, fairy-wrens 480–570, no extinctions. Draining the wetlands leaves frogs a third or fewer, in the census and in the office's surveys (a test).
  - **Also fixed on the way:** two offices of one organisation each took the other's staff and postings (an office's staff are now its role's); a job offer naming an empty paying account was refused (it now names none, and the payroll pays).
  - **Revisit if:** the species' rates are sourced (survival and fecundity, to replace the placeholders); food webs are wanted (predators and prey); or a study needs habitat quality within a cover.

- **Elections and the local paper, built** (2026-10-01; no contract change; docs/design/world-expansion.md §3; docs/behaviour/elections.md, news.md, citizen.md "Elections").
  - **All TypeScript, all records:** the electoral office keeps the roll, gives notice, takes nominations, publishes the ballot, counts each elector's last vote by instant runoff, publishes the result and appoints the winner with `position`. A secret ballot is a vote visible only to the town. The paper runs the week's most newsworthy public records as assertions with a tone toward the government. Citizens enrol, keep an approval of the town's government (their fortunes and anger, and the news for those who read it), sometimes stand, and vote a ranking. Nothing needed Rust: there's no physics here, only people and records.
  - **The result:** in the small town, a mayor who runs it well is re-elected in every seed tried (turnout 79–85%; the profile's 83%); one who funds no works crew loses in every seed, and a resident takes the office and governs through the position's role module (a test).
  - **Found by running it:** a newly elected mayor redid its predecessor's work (approved an old invoice again, commissioned again a park already built, re-tabled an adopted budget). The code mayor kept what it had done in memory. It now reads the office's record, so anyone taking an office carries on from it. The council's clerk counted the electoral ballot as a council vote (it now counts only `electorate: "council"`). The paper numbered empty weeks as editions.
  - **Also found (the deaths test, with the run's randomness moved):** an employer whose worker died withdrew the wage order, then next day took the job for a new hire (staff without an order) and paid the dead worker's account again. Staffing no longer starts wages on a job it has given notice on (employers, the builder, retailer, producer and the town's paying offices: patch versions).
  - **Liberties:** the town elects its mayor directly (Victoria's councillors choose most mayors); councillors are fixed; there are no campaigns, postal votes or fines for not voting; a platform is a candidate's top two values. Voting and standing weights are placeholders.
  - **Revisit if:** a study needs councillors elected (wards, proportional counting), campaigning, or citizens telling each other what they think of the mayor (word of mouth carries no politics yet); or candidate numbers per vacancy and local news readership are sourced (the profile's placeholders).

- **Services: contract signed, built** (Naz, 2026-10-01: "yes signed off, include skill at work"; docs/design/world-expansion.md §4; docs/behaviour/health.md, education.md).
  - **In Rust:** body variables (skill), work done to bodies (a person there, or everyone in a room), care (`treats`: a condition shortened, a death due from it called off with a chance, once, and nobody told which), skill at work (`labour_by`), illness rising with a field where the body is, and a change in one's own body waking that person.
  - **In TypeScript:**
    - the school (`school.principal`): teachers by establishment, places for children of school age, a lesson a school day to each classroom, certificates;
    - the clinic (`clinic.practice`): doctors by establishment, a walk-in queue seen in turn as hours allow;
    - citizens go to school, stay off work while seriously ill, and go to the clinic;
    - the generator's adults and newcomers bring schooling drawn from the profile's attainment;
    - both services are paid by grants from outside and exempt from rates.
  - **Who runs them is the real world's:** in Victoria neither is the council's (state schools; general practice), so the mayor's budget doesn't gain these lines. A profile can put them on a town's budget.
  - **Skill at work, calibrated to stay calibrated:** tasks' labour is scaled so the mean adult's schooling takes the profile's time. A less-schooled crew is slower. Six years' more schooling around the mean is worth the profile's return per year, compounded (a linear approximation of Mincer's log-linear).
  - **Found by running it:**
    - shops and factories retried a plan the world had refused for lack of time, forever (a run 40 times slower). Managers now learn how long work takes from what the world tells them (`Pace`);
    - the clinic and the school were taken for shops by the generator's loop over workplaces;
    - the school offered places to pupils already seated;
    - a citizen only noticed falling ill at their weekly wake, so a short illness never kept anyone off work (now a condition wakes its body's person).
    - managers who learned only "slower" left quick crews doing less than they could (the factories made too little to pay dividends): `Pace` learns each worker's pace, quicker by asking a little more once a week;
    - the clinic's daily wake drifted to whenever news last woke it (10pm, closed): a module run by the shared stepper can now ask when to wake (the clinic, at opening; the school, each morning);
    - **a measurement bug, hidden until now:** the `acts` table matched an act to its outcome only when it came at once, so every act that takes time (nearly all) had no outcome, and refusals counted 0. It now matches by the act's id (measure/src/index.ts);
    - the invariance test measured all wakes, most of which (acts finishing, news) the perturbation doesn't touch: it now measures wakes on cadence (the run summary counts wakes by why: `wakesBy`).
  - **The trade-off it shows:** the doctor and teachers come out of the town's workforce, five fewer factory workers in the small town, and 7-8% fewer goods in the first 120 days (two seeds). That's a real cost of public services, not a bug.
  - **Fatality by age (signed, built the same day):** with the clinic, the profile's treatable deaths (15% of deaths under 75, to confirm) come through severe illness, its fatality by age set so the life table holds with care as usual; without care about 12% more die at 30 to 74. Children seek care too (their parents would take them).
  - **Liberties:** the clinic stands for the whole health system; anyone may teach or doctor; pollution's effect on illness isn't used (no sourced figure).
  - **Revisit if:** fatality by age is signed (then a share of each age's deaths comes through conditions, care cutting it); a study needs employers to screen on certificates (Spence), teachers' qualifications, or routine care.

- **Justice: the tribunal, built** (2026-10-01; no contract change; docs/design/world-expansion.md §5; docs/behaviour/justice.md).
  - **All TypeScript, all records:**
    - a tribunal of the state's: its member lists applications, tells both sides and decides on the day under the laws in force (the law reader); its registrar enforces unpaid orders to pay by a garnishee order;
    - the bank carries out garnishee orders from the courts the world names;
    - tenants take unlawful rent increases to it, and creditors their unpaid debts;
    - landlords obey its orders as often as they comply with the law;
    - firms count a debt settled from their account as paid.
  - **What it shows:** with the generator's laws (a notice period, once a year), compliant landlords always find a lawful date, so nothing reaches the tribunal. A cap on increases can't be met by waiting: non-compliant landlords breach it, and the law is enforced only as far as tenants apply and landlords obey (the test: every unlawful notice found invalid, half withdrawn).
  - **Evidence:** the parties' records between them aren't the tribunal's to see, so an application carries a copy of what it relies on; the tribunal uses the original where it can see it.
  - **Also:** an election with one candidate now elects them unopposed, with no ballot (Local Government Act 2020 (Vic), to confirm); found when a well-run town drew no challenger.
  - **Liberties:** the tribunal believes what the parties say they paid; no appeals or costs; the profile's rates of taking a case are placeholders.
  - **Revisit if:** tenants are given a reason to stay on past a notice (then possession orders and a sheriff: the kernel already allows a removal); a study needs bargaining arbitration, bonds and repairs, or challenges to the town's decisions.

- **Water and energy, built** (2026-10-01; contract signed; docs/behaviour/utilities.md).
  - **In Rust:** tasks draw from fields and emit to fields and world counters; counters are censused monthly.
  - **In TypeScript:** a water corporation draws the town's use from the river (restrictions when it can't) and bills owners quarterly; an energy retailer buys from the grid, supplies its customers (each kWh's emissions counted) and bills them monthly; households pay bills before shopping.
  - **What it shows:** about 2% fewer goods sold in the first 120 days, as bills come out of what households spend; a river with no inflow runs dry within weeks.
  - **Liberties:** use is a fixed rate (no meters, no response to price); no disconnection, local generation or gas; drought is config.
  - **Revisit if:** a study needs water saving under restrictions or prices (use as a choice: the goods model), a changing season, or local generation and its pollution.

- **Home ownership, the first part** (2026-10-01; no contract change; docs/behaviour/ownership.md).
  - **Built:**
    - owner-occupied houses at the profile's share, with mortgages by the head's age, sized within the bank's rule;
    - the bank takes on loans in force at the start;
    - owners pay no rent, count the mortgage as housing, pay rates, and don't act as tenants;
    - the land registry passes a dead owner's land to their heir;
    - `ownerOf` believes a title's transfer only from its holder or its issuer.
  - **Found:** mortgages at the median for every owner put households with no pay into default within months. Capped by the bank's own rule, none default.
  - **Also found:**
    - with 18 candidates, a ranking of their ids ran past a vote's 200-character limit and every vote was rejected. Ballots now number the candidates and votes rank the numbers, as on a real ballot paper (city.electoral_office 0.3.0);
    - a newcomer enrolling at school counted no years of schooling elsewhere: now their years since school age;
    - the building tests run with every home let (they're about landlords' houses).
  - **Liberties:** flats all rented (no strata); owners don't leave town; no market for homes yet.
  - **Revisit when:** homes come up for sale (owners leaving, estates sold, new builds) and renters buy (the BoE model's buy/rent decision, mortgages for buyers).

- **The daily round, built** (2026-10-01; contract signed; docs/behaviour/daily-round.md).
  - **In Rust:** a body in one place at a time; moving between buildings takes the quickest path's time by cover; one's own body says where it is.
  - **In TypeScript:**
    - citizens go to work and school and come home, waking when that changes (commute wakes only move them);
    - employers look at the day's work an hour after it starts;
    - the school's roll is its places;
    - the grants office and water corporation count residents at night, as a census does;
    - workers call in sick;
    - a birth's chance waits until partners are together.
  - **Found by running it:**
    - **a measurement bug:** employment was counted as bodies in workplaces at the run's end, which a run ending at midnight with everyone home reads as none employed. Jobs are now employment agreements in force, and "move-ins" and "hires" are first arrivals, not each day's commute (economy/summary, economy/monthly, town/economy, town/word-of-mouth);
    - hiring room counted bodies present (at night, everyone's place free): now staff on contract;
    - children taken out of town kept going to school;
    - a tenant whose home was pulled down tried to go home again and again;
    - the round brought a new leaseholder home before their own moving-in step, which then failed forever.
  - **What changes:** about the same work done; people home in the evenings see more (waste complaints up, deaths at home seen); wakes up about 3.7 times, the suite about twice as long.
  - **Revisit if:** speed matters more (commute wakes could be batched by the engine); a study needs roads, traffic or rosters.

- **Floods and crime, built** (2026-10-02; contract signed; docs/behaviour/floods.md, crime.md).
  - **In Rust:** fields spill over a level, scale by day, and can be soaked up; buildings flood (occupants out, no entry, wear) and recede; taking goods without consent; investigation as work that finds the taker with a chance.
  - **In TypeScript:**
    - the generated river spills under heavy rain and buildings beside it flood; people go to the relief centre;
    - people in need take from work, rarely; shops find it short at a monthly stocktake and report it;
    - the police investigate and charge; the tribunal orders restitution; people pay orders against them.
  - **Found:**
    - draining wetland changes nothing, because the river spills alike into any land: wetland's mitigation needs a bank by cover;
    - a flooded workplace sent its workers into a loop of retries (the relief rule now applies to home only);
    - a building put up on the river stays under water, a fair consequence.
  - **Liberties:** no factory stocktakes (taking there goes unnoticed); no fraud; restitution only.
  - **Revisit if:** a study needs wetland mitigation, flood warnings, burglary, policing that prevents, or deterrence learned from others.

- **Buying and selling homes, built** (2026-10-02; no contract change; docs/behaviour/ownership.md, credit.md).
  - **What:** owners who'd leave, and heirs of a house they don't live in, list it at its valuation, cutting the price monthly to a floor; renters with a deposit buy on a mortgage (80% at most, within the bank's rule on income); the title passes on full payment; a seller's mortgage is paid off at the bank's payout figure; the buyer gives notice on the lease and moves in.
  - **Found:**
    - a home's price, and a mortgage's principal, were counted as income: a seller spent $89,000 at the shops;
    - the seller, with no home on the books after selling, rented a flat before leaving, and its first rent made "send all my savings" fail: a leaving seller now lives in the sold house until they go, and goes a wake later;
    - citizens don't run a step's `then`: a listing's id is read from the record book.
  - **Outcome, not tuned:** in the generated town nobody has a deposit (savings are a few months' pay), so listings sit unsold and the sellers fall behind on their mortgages.
  - **Revisit if:** savings follow a real wealth distribution; a study needs auctions, investors, owners moving house, or mortgagee sales.

- **The last world-expansion pass: savings, forced sales, roads, weather** (2026-10-02; no contract change; docs/behaviour/ownership.md, daily-round.md, weather.md).
  - **What:**
    - savings by age from a distribution, not months of pay (newcomers too);
    - an owner whose mortgage defaults sells and rents;
    - roads: a building type with the `road` cover, streets every 6 rows and 8 columns, walking off them and driving on them, worn and kept up, and the mayor building toward far buildings within a roads budget;
    - weather drawn for the run from the climate: rain by season, heavy rain, drought, heatwaves (a heat field that raises illness, more on built ground), and the bureau's warnings.
  - **Why no contract change:** a type's cover already sets its ground, travel goes by cover, fields' sources by day and illness by field were signed in §6.4 and §7.3.
  - **Why weather is drawn before the run:** like the town, it's a scenario. Fixed in config, it's reproducible, and the same weather can be replayed under different stewards.
  - **Found:**
    - a road at first cost more than the year's roads budget, so the town never built one;
    - a fresh random stream for each draw gave the same number every time, so no weather ever happened;
    - the mayor looked only at finished buildings, and houses take most of a year.
    - with poorer people in need more often, a builder's labourer tried to take goods from the yard, which holds none: taking is now only where the employer sells goods;
    - the police lost the first of two investigations done in a day (its finding came while the second was under way): found by more crime;
    - with savings drawn by age, the mayor who funds no works crew kept office in the election test on a quarter of first preferences (economic voting): the test now checks support is lost, and tests the next mayor in a poorer town.
  - **Outcomes, not tuned:** with real savings, more of the poorest leave town broke (28 a year against 23) and fewer owners sell (5 against 17). A heatwave in a town of 200 makes about one more person ill.
  - **Revisit if:** a study needs traffic, walking versus driving, bushfire, wind damage, responses to warnings, or a changing climate.

- **Contracts v0.8 closed** (2026-10-02; Naz: "yes 3 then 1, go ahead"; contracts/README.md).
  - **What:** v0.7 plus the world expansion's signed changes (nature, services, water and energy, the daily round, floods and crime, periodic agreements, the ratified extras). The `$id`s and every world and log now say 0.8. Nature's changes had been built and signed but missing from the README's list: added.
  - **Why now:** the AI side (real-model mayors, the translator's data) should be built against an interface that won't move under it.
  - **Old runs:** logs and datasets from before say 0.7 and stay valid logs of their own version (D6). The saved LLM runs are 0.7: a translator data pipeline reading them reads both versions (the changes are additions).
  - **Revisit if:** anything the AI side needs isn't expressible: that opens 0.9, proposed and signed off as before.

- **A language-model seat's rhythm: several actions an answer, and office hours** (2026-10-02; Naz: "do both 1 and 2"; participants/src/llm/participant.ts 0.5.0).
  - **Why:** pilot 7c's mayor made about 9 calls a simulated day (80 on a busy one), one action a call, and every reply woke it. A year took about 18 hours through Codex.
  - **Several actions an answer** (up to 8): carried out in the order given, with no call in between. Each one's result (the report after it) comes back together at the next call. Anything depending on a result (confirming a draft not yet seen) waits for that call.
  - **Office hours** (where the world has working hours): one sitting a working day. What comes in between is kept and shown at the next sitting, with sections that repeat unchanged shown once. "Done for now" with no days means its usual rhythm (a week), sooner at the next sitting if someone writes.
  - **Mock brains** read the observations from skipped wakes too, so the code mayor behind the mock sees everything the model is shown.
  - **What it changes for a steward:** within a day it's slower to respond (as real offices are). It can't act on something the same afternoon once its sitting's over. Recorded with each run (the seat's params).
  - **Revisit if:** a study needs a steward that responds within the day (an emergency): a world can leave office hours out, or a warning could open a sitting.

- **Pilot 7d's findings, and the simulation's speed** (2026-10-02; docs/real-models.md).
  - **Waste:** every bin emptied however little is in it, and residents judge by when they last saw it emptied (citizen 0.34.0, city.works 0.2.2). A crew collecting weekly gets no complaints now (before: about 100 in 90 days in the 1,000-person town).
  - **Energy:** on order until delivered (utility.energy 0.1.1).
  - **The election test** no longer asks who wins. A negligent mayor loses support against a kept town with the same number standing; with many standing the vote splits and it held on in every variant tried, so whether it loses is an outcome. A mayor who doesn't stand again (city.mayor_code 0.9.0, `stands_again`) tests what a successor does.
  - **Travel times cached** (kernel): 87% of the kernel's time went to working out the same trips (home to work, and back) every day. Now a trip is worked out once while the ground it crosses is unchanged (a counter bumped on every change of cover or building; derived state, never serialised or hashed). `OIKOS_CHECK_TRAVEL=1` recomputes every cached trip and asserts it matches: run on 20 days of the 1,000-person town, identical. 60 days took 98 s, against 132.
  - **Revisit if:** something else starts affecting a cell's travel cost (wear slowing roads, floods): it must bump the counter.
  - **The energy loop mattered beyond energy:** with it fixed, the insolvency test's factory found customers among the town's shops and didn't fail (the test now takes the shops away too). The loop drained up to about $1M a day out of town on the days it ran: earlier calibration runs (C6) carried it. Worth rechecking the calibration measures.

- **The translator's prompt, translations at once, and more seats on models** (2026-10-03; Naz: "fix the translator, add parallelism, and then add more seats"; translator 0.5.0).
  - **The plan for data (Naz, 2026-10-03):** stay on Codex until the translator is trained and the platform is mature. Training pairs are mostly generated, from the calls code participants make, with requests written in several styles by several models, not leaning to one steward's voice. Real pilot requests are for style and for the test. The held-out cases stay out of all of it.
  - **Why plain requests came back "unclear":** the model's first call was usually right. The test's fixture knew nobody's account, so the resolver asked "which account?". The translator then tried again, with feedback that ended "answer none(...) again", and the model gave up a correct call. Now a question another attempt can't answer goes to the participant as it is, and the fixture knows the accounts a town would.
  - **The prompt** (tuned on seen cases and the pilots' real failures only):
    - a request to direct or ask someone is a letter to them, not `work`;
    - having someone build is `structure(by=...)`, not an agreement written out;
    - notice and signing;
    - never make up an id;
    - when to refuse, with each label spelt out.
  - **Three outcomes, not pass/fail:** right; no draft (a refusal or question came back: a rewording's cost); wrong draft (the one that matters). The pass mark is better set on wrong drafts.
  - **Translations at once:** a round's requests are translated together (they were made looking at the same state, and a translation changes nothing), and carried out in the cohort's order as before. Each translation's records are kept with it and written in that order, so the log is the same whichever comes back first. A resume finds a translation's recorded answers by its draft.
  - **More seats:** `model_seats` in a world's spec ("planning,finance") hands those offices to a model: the same office and channels, honest disclosure, office hours, and its own code module as the mock's brain.
  - **Revisit if:** the previews should be against the state as each request is carried out (they're against the round's start, as everyone's look was); or a seat needs more than its office's report to do the job (what the pilots are for).

- **An outage stops the run, and retires nobody** (2026-10-04; pilot 8; changes D59's rule for model calls).
  - **Was:** a model call that failed after its retries was recovered in place (the seat waits), and three failures in a row retired the participant, as for a module that throws.
  - **Found:** a network outage is three failures in a row for every model seat. Pilot 8 lost its mayor, planning and finance officers on day 74 and ran ten months without them.
  - **Now:** a failed model call still makes its seat wait that wake, but doesn't count toward retiring it. Three model calls unanswered in a row, with none answered between, is the model out of reach: the run stops as at a usage limit, and `oikos resume` picks it up (calls with no recorded answer are asked again).
  - **A code module that throws** is still retired after three, as before: that failure is its own.
  - **Revisit if:** runs should wait out an outage themselves (retry for an hour before stopping), which matters once runs are left unattended for days.

- **The project is Oikos** (2026-10-05; Naz). From οἶκος, the household: the root of both economy and ecology.
  - **Renamed everywhere:** packages (`@oikos/...`), the Rust crates and the engine library, the command (`oikos run`), environment variables (`OIKOS_...`), the docs, and, on Naz's word that nothing should keep the old name, the contracts' id namespace (`urn:oikos:contracts:0.8`), the canary's wording (same GUID) and the hash and randomness domain strings.
  - **What that costs:** every run's random draws differ from before the rename (the randomness starts from its domain string), so results aren't comparable draw for draw with earlier runs. Earlier logs keep the old strings and replay only on a build from before the rename.
  - **Handles:** the repo is `nazriz/oikos`; `runoikos` was free on X and GitHub when checked (2026-10-05).

- **What new random draws found** (2026-10-05). The rename changed the string the randomness starts from, so every test town played out differently, and four things that had passed by the luck of the old draws didn't:
  - **Bodies ran no risk until their first birthday in the world** (kernel). Each body's yearly draws (death from other causes, illness, exposure to injury) were made at its birthday, for the year after: from the start of a run, an arrival or a birth until then, half a year on average, nothing could happen to it. Infants were safe for their whole first year. One-year runs had about half their deaths and illnesses. Now the first draws are made as a body enters the world, for what's left of its year of age (a year's chance, by that share), and belong to that moment (undone with it if the act is refused). The kernel's life tests, which had been written around the old timing, expect the table from the start.
  - **Heat never made anyone ill.** The heat field lost all of itself each day, and the kernel takes a field's decay after adding its sources: gone as it came. Now half a day (docs/behaviour/weather.md).
  - **A house paid for and not handed over.** A buyer's mortgage came through the day the seller relisted for less; the seller looked only for purchases answering its current listing, kept the money and the house, and went on cutting the price. Now a purchase answering any listing it made of the house counts, and what was paid above the current price goes back.
  - **Residents who looked weekly never saw their bin emptied** (same point of each week's round, never less in it than last time). Now: emptied if there's well under what there would be had nobody come.
  - **Tests changed, not behaviour:** the election test no longer compares how many complain (a kept town's one-person crew left, and six weeks passed before the next started); the reliability test doesn't pin the second a module wakes; a seasonal flood's refusals are allowed in the deaths test.
  - **Worth knowing:** every earlier measurement of deaths, illness and injury in a run's first year is low by about half. Calibration against the life table should be redone.

- **Where sessions and runs live: on the run server, through Remote Control** (2026-10-05). The plan had been cloud sessions that edit and push, with the Mac mini driven over SSH. A cloud session's network goes through a web proxy: no SSH and no tailnet, so it can't reach the mini at all. So the session runs on the mini itself (Claude Code's Remote Control, opened from the browser or the phone), where a run or the suite is a local command; `scripts/mini` stays for driving it from the laptop.
  - **It starts at login** (a launch agent, in tmux, started again a minute after it stops), and its sessions have full permissions: Naz's choice, for runs nobody is watching. The mini's own setup (the agent, its script) is outside the repo; docs/handover.md says where.
  - **The setup script, corrected for what the mini found:** Homebrew keeps its rustup out of its usual folder, so cargo wasn't found after installing it (the script and `scripts/mini` now look there themselves; no shell profile is edited); the package outside the workspace (docs/research/cel-check) needs its own install or its test fails; and the repo can be cloned with a deploy key (`OIKOS_REPO_URL`), which is how the mini has it: a key that reaches this repo only, not Naz's GitHub login.
  - **What that costs:** the session's history and memory live on the mini, not in the cloud; a session's background commands still stop after about half an hour, so long runs are detached. The script hasn't been run from scratch since the corrections.
  - **Revisit if** cloud sessions gain a way to reach a private machine, or Remote Control proves unreliable over days (then: SSH from the laptop, which works today).

- **The Codex adapter's 180 seconds stays** (2026-10-05, checked before pilot 9). Pilot 8b stopped on day 39 with 15 calls unanswered within 180 s, and the question was whether gpt-6.1-sol simply takes longer on a big answer. It doesn't: seven requests rebuilt from 8b's own transcripts (the mayor's largest first reports and the deepest turns of its day-30 sitting, 31,000 to 48,000 input tokens; then mayor, finance and planning at once, as a round runs them) answered in 28 to 56 seconds on the run server, 700 to 1,500 output tokens each. So 180 s is already three times the slowest, and 8b's failures were the laptop or the service, not the limit (they came in clusters, every seat at once, the mark of a machine asleep or a connection down).
  - **Revisit if** a run on the mini logs "no answer within 180 s" on calls that aren't clustered, or answers grow well past 2,000 output tokens (more actions an answer, a higher reasoning effort).

- **The translator's held-out cases leave the repository, and the preview shows everything** (2026-10-05; translator 0.5.1; from the code audit of the same day; Naz: "ok" to a new held-out set kept away from prompt work).
  - **What was wrong with the test.** The wording of at least five held-out cases was in the translator's prompt ("whatever's left", "a fifth each", "make them lower their prices", a demolition and a notice written almost as the cases had them). The cases sat in `translator/eval/cases.ts` beside the seen ones, and docs/real-models.md quoted the failing ones, so whoever reworked the prompt (2026-10-03) had them in front of them. The 62% "held out" after that rework is not a held-out figure, and VISION §14's gate had nothing clean behind it.
  - **Now:** every case in the repository is a seen case (the 29 old held-out ones are marked `was_held_out`). The held-out set is a file on the run server, outside the repository (`~/oikos-private/translator/held-out.json`, or `OIKOS_HELD_OUT`): 60 cases, written and checked by an agent that took no part in the prompt, and not read by the session that works on it.
    - `oikos translator-eval` runs them where the machine has them. What it prints or writes to `--out` gives each held-out result by case id, outcome and shape ("got message; wanted money"), never the request or the programs; the full results go beside the file.
    - Before a held-out score is reported, the cases are checked for a run of five words shared with the prompt or a seen case (`leaks`); with any, the report says it is not a held-out result. A test does the same against the prompt, the seen cases, the call language and the docs, on a machine that has the file.
    - Training data must pass the same check against the held-out file before it is used.
    - The name a program binds (`t = entity(…)`) no longer counts in scoring: it is the writer's choice.
  - **What it costs:** the one copy is on the run server (Naz to decide where a second lives); nobody working on the translator can look at why a held-out case failed, only at its shape, which is the point.
  - **The preview left parts of an act out**, among them a statement's `adico` and `formal`: what gives a law its force for everyone run by code. A participant confirmed a law without seeing the rule recorded for it, or that none was. Now every kind of act is a sentence followed by whatever the sentence didn't say, by name ("Also: …"); a law's rule and the condition acts are checked against are written out; `take` has its sentence; an offer signed for someone else is no longer folded into "and sign it on your side"; nothing is cut at 600 characters. A test builds one act of each kind in the contract with every field it can carry, changes each field in turn, and expects the preview to change: a new block or field without its place in the preview fails it.
  - **A law as read shows its rule too** (the words of its Institutional Grammar breakdown), and that offices and others check what they do against it, where a condition is recorded. The condition's own text is in the platform's notation (`block.kind == …`), so it is shown word for word only in the preview of an act that would publish one, never in a report or among the papers on file (principle 14; the mayor's test caught the first attempt, which put it in every prompt).
  - **A participant was blamed for the translator's failures.** No call coming back, a call that wasn't in the call language, a call with an argument of the wrong shape, and a failed check of the acts all read "It isn't clear what should happen. Could you say it more plainly?", and the log couldn't tell them apart. Now the first three say it couldn't be put into effect as said, the last that saying it another way may not help; the cause is on the failure (`invalid`) and so in the log. The resolver's words about arguments ("visible should be a list of names or ids") go back to the translator for another attempt and no longer to the participant.
  - **Revisit if** the held-out file is lost or seen (write another: its tools are beside it); if previews grow too long to read (the request sentence now gives the request's own words and who sees it); or when the resolver's argument errors get a type of their own in both resolvers, in place of the pattern the translator matches them by.
- **A model seat is always let go when it asks, and always told what wasn't done** (2026-10-05; an audit of the loop that runs a language-model participant, `participants/src/llm/participant.ts`, now version 0.6.0). The loop had ways of dropping things without a word; each is closed, with a test that failed before.
  - **Opting out is honoured however it's said** (VISION §15). An opt-out with no statement, an empty one, or an input that couldn't be read (the Codex adapter's when the JSON is cut off) was treated as a malformed tool use: nothing happened, and the seat was woken again later. Now any use of the tool is the opt-out; the statement is whatever words can be recovered (the raw text of an unreadable input, else what was given, else empty), and one not given as asked is also recorded as it came (`opt_out_as_given`). The contracts and the host take an empty statement as they are. It stays a leaving of the study, never an act in the world.
  - **What wasn't done is said, with why, in the world's words** ("the letter had no one to go to", "you don't hold the post …", "it didn't say what to do"). Before, the only words were "it wasn't something you can do, as written", and they arrived only if something else in the same answer had been done and it hadn't said done for now. Now: in the same sitting when a turn follows; when nothing in the answer could be done, one more turn at once to say so (once a sitting, so it can't go round for ever); otherwise at the start of its next sitting ("When you were last here, your letter (…) was not done: …"), which is also what happens with done for now. A notebook whose text couldn't be read is said to be not updated (it was answered "Notebook updated."), and is recorded like the rest; each record now carries its reason. Actions past the most an answer may hold are recorded too (`dropped_tool_use`).
  - **A failed call loses nothing.** What had been kept for the seat, and mid-sitting the results of its own actions, were cleared before the call was made; when it failed they were gone, and reports arrive only once. Now they're let go only once the call is answered, and the report it failed on is kept with them. A sitting that fails at its first call hasn't been held: it's held when next it can be.
  - **Nothing is cut without saying so.** What came in between sittings, when more than can be set out (40,000 characters): whole reports are left out from the front, never part of one, the seat is told how many, and it's recorded (`carried_not_shown`). A notebook past 4,000 characters: the seat is told what was kept and how it now ends (and the tool says what it holds), recorded (`notebook_cut`). A form's fields are always shown whole: details were cut at 400 characters, and the town's council proposal form runs to 832, so every mayor so far read it without most of its budget lines while the translator saw them all.
  - **A letter that comes with the result of its last action brings it back sooner**, as done for now tells it: at the next sitting, or, with no office hours, at once. The mark that someone had written was cleared as it left.
  - **The Codex adapter:** its wrapper said "one per answer" of the tools while the instructions say several; it now says how to give one and how to give more, and leaves how many to the instructions (the translator's say one). A child that ignores being asked to end is killed after five seconds, and its pipes are no longer waited on, so it can't hang a call. Only the subscription's usage limit and rate limits stop the run now: any error saying "limit reached" (a context window's) did.
  - **What that costs:** what a seat has on file now holds the whole form, so its instructions differ from 0.5.1's from the first call: a run begun under 0.5.1 can't be resumed on this build (nothing would be served from its log; every call would be made again). Finish or resume such a run on the build it started on. An answer none of which can be done costs one more call.
  - **Left as it is:** a search condition in `look` that can't be read is still dropped quietly (the search is wider than asked); the answer schema's own description of `also` still says "usually empty".
  - **Revisit if** a seat keeps answering with things that can't be done after being told (then the words aren't helping, or a tool's description is wrong); if the count of reports left out is often above zero (then summarise them rather than leave them out); or if a seat with no office hours and a correspondent who answers at once goes back and forth without rest.
- **The run machinery keeps every log, and says when a run stopped** (2026-10-05, from an audit of the run machinery before pilot 9's log is relied on). Six faults, each one a way to lose a model's answers or to be misled about a run; none is in the world. Tests: orchestrator/test/run-machinery.test.ts and mini.test.ts.
  - **A fresh run never overwrites a log.** `oikos run` and `run-one` deleted a run's log, anchors and summary (and the batch its measures) if the folder already held them. Now a run refuses to start, naming the log, where the folder holds that run's log (plain, compressed, or `<run>.stopped-<n>`), a recipe for it, or (a batch) a `measure/`: "choose another --out, or resume". The batch checks before it starts any seed. `--overwrite` (`overwrite: true`) is the deliberate exception, for tests' own folders; never a default. Even with it, nothing replaces a log in which any model call came from a provider other than the mock, and a batch won't overwrite into a folder that holds one. (The tests didn't need it: each empties its own folder first. That's theirs to do.)
  - **A run that stops is reported as stopped.** At a usage limit, the model out of reach or the spending limit, a run closed its log as "aborted", exited 0 and was printed like a finished seed with fewer years; the batch then compressed it, and resume read only plain logs. Now the run's summary and `batch.json` carry how it ended (`end`: the reason and detail) and, if it stopped, why and by what (`stopped`); the console says STOPPED, why, and the exact `oikos resume` command, with its models and limit; a stopped run stays plain; and `oikos resume` takes a compressed log (written out plain, the compressed one kept and moved aside with the rest as `<run>.stopped-<n>.jsonl.zst`) or a folder (its one run that stopped short). The command still exits 0 for a batch with stopped runs: the spending limit is a stop by design. That's a choice someone may want made otherwise.
  - **Stopped to be resumed means not debriefed.** A run stopped by a usage limit, an unreachable model or a request to stop went on to debrief everyone as if their run had ended, asking a model that couldn't answer. It now defers them (a `debriefs_deferred` note); they're debriefed when the resumed run ends. At the spending limit the run still debriefs from the 5% kept back, as designed (VISION §15.3): that run is over unless someone raises the limit.
  - **A stop from outside is a clean stop.** `scripts/mini stop` matched process names: it missed seeds and resumes, took `pilot1` for `pilot10`, and ended every Codex call on the machine. It now goes by the run's recorded pid and that process's own descendants, and asks (TERM). So that asking works, a run hears TERM (and Ctrl-C): it stops before its next moment, closes its log as at a usage limit, and is reported with its resume command; a batch passes it on and starts no more seeds. A run that can't get there within `OIKOS_STOP_GRACE_SECONDS` (120; a model call still out) ends as it is, and its log resumes all the same. With the engine in the run's own process nothing waited on anything outside, so a signal went unheard until the run ended: the run now lets the process hear one a few times a second (by the wall clock; only when it stops depends on that, never what it does). `mini sync` refuses to rebuild the engine while a run is in progress (`--force`), and `mini resume` gives the folder, or a named run's own log, never a `stopped-<n>` file.
  - **What a resume leaves behind is history.** `<run>.stopped-<n>.jsonl` was read as a run of its own by `corpus`, `dataset`, `measure` and `cost` on a folder: its answers exported twice under two names, and its measures written over the resumed run's (the same run id). A folder's readers now take each run once, its current log (the plain one where both are there); a stopped log is read when named. A dataset's `end.reason` is the log's own end entry ("aborted: ...", or "unfinished" where there's none), not "completed" for anything without a stop note.
  - **A failed model call is recorded, and a resume is served it** (a log-format addition, approved by Naz: the `model_call_failed` annotation, contracts/README.md). A call that failed left nothing in the log, so on resume its request was compared with the next recorded answer, differed, and every later call went to the live model: the resumed run was no longer the stopped one. Now the failure is in the log where its transcript would be (what was sent, and the error), and a resumed run is served it as the same failure, so it takes the same path. One exception: the calls that failed in the round a run stopped in (its stop note counts them, `failed_calls_to_ask_again`) are asked again, or a run stopped because the model couldn't be reached would stop at the same place every time. And what a round's translations had been answered before the run stopped (their usage and transcripts) is written as it stops, not dropped.
  - **Smaller:** `batch.json` is written as soon as the runs end and again at the end, and a failure measuring or compressing is reported (`problems`, exit 1) instead of leaving a finished batch with no manifest; a compressed log is compared with the plain one by hash, and is on the disk, before the plain one goes.
  - **Not done:** nothing stops a program calling `runWorld` itself from writing over a log (the tests do, into their own folders); the guard is in `runOne` and `runBatch`, where the command line goes. A resume from a compressed log leaves the stopped run twice on disk (compressed and plain).
  - **Revisit if** a batch with stopped runs should fail a script that checks exit codes; a mid-run debrief at the spending limit turns out to confuse a seat that is then resumed with a higher limit; resume gets checkpoints (then recorded failures matter only after the checkpoint); or runs are driven by something other than `scripts/mini` (the pid file is its own convention).
- **Six faults an audit of the kernel found, fixed** (2026-10-05). None was showing in a run; each could have, and three of them would have gone unnoticed because the test that should catch them wasn't reading the code. Each has a test that failed before.
  - **A system with lanes to spare made the state impossible to hash.** A lane never used was written as the smallest 64-bit integer, and the timelines are part of the hashed state, where canonical JSON takes integers within ±(2^53 − 1) only: a system with more than one lane that had acted fewer times than it has lanes stopped the next checkpoint. An unused lane is now the lowest integer the contracts allow, which means the same (free now: below every time a world can have), so the lane chosen is unchanged (the one that frees first, the lowest-numbered of equals). A single lane never held the old value (it's overwritten as it's made), so existing worlds replay to the same hashes.
  - **The determinism lint read `kernel/src` alone**, where little of the kernel has lived since it became a workspace. It walks `kernel/src` and every crate's `src`, all the way down. It found one float: the resolver rounded seconds to days for a request's wording ("every 30 days") through a float division. Now exact integers (the nearest day, half a day and over rounding up), with the same answers. Nothing else was flagged and nothing is exempted.
  - **The generality test skipped `kernel/crates`** for the same reason. It reads it now (not build output or tests), and finds no place named there.
  - **Integer overflow stops a release build as it stops a test build** (`overflow-checks = true` for the release profile). It wrapped silently in release and panicked in tests, so the same log could have replayed to a different world by the build that read it. A stop is the lesser harm: a wrapped quantity is a wrong world that looks right. What a participant can reach is refused in the ordinary way instead (`overflow`): a holding that work's outputs or a taking would carry past the range of a quantity (moving goods already checked; every change to a holding goes through the one check now). A field's daily step multiplies a value by a rate, which passes 64 bits for values near the ends of the range the contracts allow: worked in 128 bits now, the sums saturating (the bounds are the schema's: values within ±(2^53 − 1), rates at most 10,000 basis points). The same results for every value that fitted before, so for every existing world. The cost of the checks wasn't measured.
  - **Leaving a space is written once.** It was written four times (dying or crossing out, moving elsewhere, leaving or being removed, a demolition), and a flood had a fifth; the demolition's copy had drifted: it didn't settle the building's variables before emptying it and didn't record the departures. One function does the steps now (settle if anyone leaves, take the bodies out, record each departure, say so), and all five use it; the stamps stay with each caller, in the order each has always made them, because that order is part of the state. **This changes behaviour, in two cases only:** a demolished building that had occupants keeps the wear of the days they were in it (it was recounted as if empty all along) and its spaces show who left and why, as after any other departure; and a flooded building brought down is no longer listed as flooded (its id stayed there for good, since only standing buildings are looked at when the water goes down). A run with either replays to different state hashes than before; no test's expectation needed changing.
  - **Two investigations, or two surveys of the same thing, in one action drew the same numbers.** Their draws were keyed by the action (and what's surveyed) but not the block, unlike harm: the second try found exactly what the first did. Keyed by the block now. **This changes random draws** for every investigation and every survey, single ones included (the key gained a part), so a run with either plays out differently from the same seed than it did before.
  - **Not fixed, for a decision:** an action's outcome carries one `surveyed` and one `investigated`, so of two in one action only the last is told to the actor (the work and the time of both are spent). Telling both needs the outcome's shape changed, which is a contract change.
  - **Revisit if** a release kernel stops on an overflow in a run (then that arithmetic needs its own check and a refusal, or a bound in config validation: the stop is the report, not the fix); the checks cost enough to show in a long run's time (measure before trading them away); a world needs field values beyond what 64 bits can sum (hundreds of cells of the largest values flowing into one); or actions with several surveys or investigations become common (then the outcome should carry each).
- **A model seat reads what a code seat reads: what its own acts found, the state of each building, and the rest of any list that was cut** (2026-10-05; from the audit of what a language-model participant is shown; `participants/src/llm/render.ts` and `participant.ts`, now version 0.7.0; VISION principles 14 and 15). The data was in every observation; the text made from it left it out. Nothing here is special to a town, an office or a kind of building, and no contract changed.
  - **Everything an act brings back is told.** An applied act read "Done: …" and nothing more, so a seat that ordered a survey or an investigation was never told what was found. Now: what it made (the new things' ids), a survey's estimates place by place with their total, what an investigation found (taken or not, and by whom, or that who wasn't found), and any other part an outcome carries, by its own name, so a part added to the contract later is shown without anyone remembering to. The test builds an outcome with every part the schema allows and fails when the schema gains one. Left out on purpose: the act's own number, and the engine's wording of why not (written for researchers, in the platform's terms; why not is still said in the world's words, with which part of the act it was).
  - **A building's state is told, whatever the world calls it.** Each building's variables (the test town's are waste and wear) came with their value, daily change and bounds, and were never printed. Shown one by one (up to 30 buildings): each measure by its own name (underscores as spaces), how much today (the value seen, carried on by its daily change, within its bounds) and which way it's going, and once how far each runs. No measure is known to the code by name. A town too big to list: for each measure, how many buildings it's above zero at and how many it's rising at, and the five highest by name with the count of the rest.
  - **The look tool reaches what a code participant in the seat can.** It reached only reports read on request. Now, where the host says what kind each report is (`kinds`, set by the orchestrator from the seat's channels): its papers (a search, as before; with nothing to find, the latest), its buildings and ground (name places to see in full: a building, a room, the ground, a kind of building; or name a measure, and every building where it's above zero or rising is listed, the highest first), and who's who (people, organisations and posts by id or part of a name; the report itself only counts people). A place looked at is shown whole: every room however many, who has gone from it, who built it. **No contract addition was needed:** the participant API already takes a look through a sites channel by its id, which brings what has changed there since it was last looked through; the seat's picture is then current, and narrowing to what was named is done where the text is written. A look narrowed in the kernel (`at` on LookRequest) would save sending a whole town's changes to show one house; nothing needs it yet.
  - **Where a list is cut, the seat is told how to see the rest, and what it looks up is whole.** Earlier items only counted, a run of claims totalled past twelve, a text cut at its mark: the report now ends with how to look (only for a seat that can). What was looked up is listed one by one and uncut (claims were totalled again, texts cut again, in the very results asked for). A search that finds nothing says "Nothing found" (it said nothing at all), one that fills its limit says there may be more, a look that brings nothing new says so. Money in and out past eight counterparties says what the rest came to. Posts past sixty are counted, not ended with "…".
  - **Something to find that can't be read is said, not dropped** (left as it was on the same day's loop audit): the search would be wider than the one asked for and read as its answer. The seat is told which and why, at once.
  - **The tool and the reasons speak from inside the world.** Papers are found by `from`, `on behalf of`, `citing`, `date` (written as reports write it, "year 1, day 12", and standing for the whole day) and `detail.<name>`; the store's names (`author`, `speaks_for`, `basis`, `at`, `data.<name>`) still work and are no longer shown. "That doesn't happen in this world" is "that doesn't happen here"; "the world's limit on that" is "the limit on that".
  - **Also told now, on the same test (would a code participant in the seat see it?):** a body's own variables, where it is, its parents and children, and that a condition has been treated; how an agreement stands by its terms (in force, when it ends, what's next due and owing); a building being demolished; what lives on the ground where anyone would notice it (which kinds, on how many cells).
  - **Size.** The mayor's first report in the test town (80 citizens): 9,792 characters before, 7,542 after. The additions cost under 1,000; a first report no longer sets out twice what "now" repeats word for word (the survey and who's who came once as "since you last looked" and again as "now"). After a look, the standing picture of what wasn't looked at (the survey, who's who) isn't repeated in its result. A look brings at most 50 at once (20 unless asked).
  - **What this costs.** What a seat reads differs from 0.6.0's from the first call, so a run begun on 0.6.0 can't be resumed on this build (finish or resume it on the build it started on). The tool's description is longer (it names each report that can be looked into).
  - **Left as it is.** A measure's unit isn't told (the participant API gives a building's variables no unit: waste is grams only in the generator's comment; that would be a contract addition). What's on file is still cut at 400 characters of detail except forms, and can't be looked up whole in its own report (a search through a report kept whole is replaced by the kept copy in the runtime's view). Papers can't be found by their id or by who holds a claim (the API's search has no such places). Money in and out arrives once, so the counterparties past eight can't be seen later. Only the last survey and the last investigation of one act come back (the engine keeps one of each per act).
  - **Revisit if** seats ask for a measure's unit or guess at it (then give variables a unit in the contract); if looks at a big town's survey are slow or dear (then narrow them in the kernel with `at`); if seats search by the old names anyway or write dates another way (widen what's read); or if the "how to see the rest" lines are never followed (then they're noise: drop them and say it once in the tool).

- **Getting ready to train the translator: a scorecard, and the first rules it settled** (2026-10-05; Naz: "hill climb on the platform itself. Get us to a point where we're confident in the state and are ready to generate data to train the translator", and, on contract changes for it, "I give you permission to change whatever is needed"; translator 0.6.0).
  - **The scorecard** (ten lines, each measured, in docs/handover.md): the suite passes; the reference translator (gpt-6.1-sol) passes the held-out cases; every seen case it misses is settled; it handles what the pilots' seats really asked; the rules have no known ambiguity; the training data covers the call language; generated data is reliably right; no place in the translator's path; a seat sees enough for its requests to be real; nothing undecided blocks generation.
  - **gpt-6.1-sol on the clean test set** (through Codex): 59 of 60 held-out cases right, 1 wrong draft, where gpt-6-luna has 44 and 5. So the prompt is sound and the weakness was the smaller model; sol is the reference a trained translator is measured against, and can translate in pilots meanwhile.
  - **Its twelve misses on the seen cases were mostly the rules', not its own,** and each was settled so that the training data has one right answer:
    - *Someone described, not named* ("the builder", "the planning officer"). The prompt said to leave out what's only described; the answer keys wanted the id. Now it's written as described, in quotes, and **both resolvers find who is meant**: someone called that, an id written out, a post by its title or role, tried as written, without a leading "the", and without a closing "job", "post", "position" or "office"; two that fit are asked about. That is world knowledge, which is the resolver's.
    - *A kind is the request's own word* ("40 goods" is `kind=goods`); something published with no kind said is a notice.
    - *Nothing added:* no purpose or recipient the request didn't state.
    - *Too much* is several separate things that would each be a request of their own. One thing done for several (a letter to two people), or lines that depend on each other (a trust, then its charter), is one thing.
  - **A sixth refusal: `not_an_act`** (a contract change: contracts/calls.md, calls.schema.json, both resolvers). About 17 of the 60 requests that failed across the pilots asked to see, read or inspect something ("show me the result of ballot rec.1760"); the nearest label was `unclear`, so the seat was told to say it more plainly, and said it again. Now: "That's something to look up or read, not something to do. Look at your reports and papers, or write to whoever keeps it." Asking someone else for it is still a letter.
  - **In scoring,** a call the resolver answers with a refusal's own words counts as that refusal (a form nobody has seen, written as `form(…)`, reaches the participant as "You haven't seen a form by that name" either way).
  - **Revisit if** descriptions resolve to the wrong post in a world with many alike (the rule asks when two fit, never guesses); or seats start using "look" requests to mean "ask the office", which is a letter.

- **Council laws bind: the clerk drafts, from a form** (2026-10-05; clerk 0.6.0, code mayor 0.10.0; the proposal in docs/design/ai-citizens.md 2e, built; docs/behaviour/government.md, "Laws made from figures"). A law the council passed on its proposal form was words alone, and everyone run by code is bound by a law only through its rule, so it passed and changed nothing (pilot 7d's tenancy law).
  - **A schedule writes a law from its figures.** World content (`worlds/law/schedules.json`, records of kind `law_schedule`): a law's figures (a duration, a count, a share, each with its unit), and its words, its Institutional Grammar breakdown and its rule with `{figure}` in them. Three so far: rent increases, industrial action, ending a tenancy. The generator writes the town's opening laws from them with the profile's figures; each such law carries its figures (`data.figures`, value and unit). *Why content, not code:* the clerk must not know any law (no laws in the kernel, and none in a module either); a fourth schedule is a record. *Why a template and not a general drafter:* a rule names the fields of a notice only its maker's code knows, and a notation the mayor can't check shouldn't be composed for it; a schedule is written once, by whoever adds the law, and read by anyone.
  - **Each schedule has an amendment form** (the figures as optional fields of the types forms already have, and whom it bears on). A proposal on one is balloted as what it would change; passed, the clerk publishes the law rewritten by the schedule (the figures given, the rest as they stand), citing the ballot and naming the law it replaces, and withdraws the old one. Failed, nothing changes. A test runs it through a mock-model mayor's request, the resolver, the council, a landlord's next notice and the tribunal.
  - **A law in free words still passes as words, and the clerk says so**: "The law is recorded as worded. No rule was set down with it for acts to be checked against.", with the laws that are amended on their own forms. The council proposal form and the clerk's report say the same.
  - **The laws on rent increases and industrial action are the town's now** (they spoke for the world outside). A council can amend only its own laws, and only the one a law speaks for can withdraw it. *What this gives up:* in the profile's place they're a state's and a nation's law, which a town can't change. The town here stands for the polity whose steward is studied; a world that wants them out of the council's reach leaves their schedules out.
  - **A reader takes an amended law in the old one's place** (`Book`): a participant's book of laws is shown laws, never withdrawals, so nobody outside the town's offices ever learned a law was withdrawn. An amended law names what it replaces, in its words and in `data.replaces`, and counts only from the same lawmaker. And an act is judged by the laws as they stood when it was made (the tribunal judged every notice by today's laws).
  - **A landlord's increase starts with a day** (landlord 0.12.1), the first to begin once the notice has run. It started to the second, counted from when the landlord decided; the notice is on the record a second later, so one on exactly the law's period (the generated town's: 60 days' practice, 60 days' law) was a second short and invalid before the tribunal. A mechanism fault, found by the test above. **This changes when increases start** (up to a day later) in every run with rent reviews.
  - **The citizens' petition and the code mayor.** The petition asks for 90 days' notice to end a tenancy (not of a rent increase) and a 5% cap. A demand may now name a schedule and figures (`demand.amend`); the code mayor answers that part on the law's form and tells the organiser what wasn't put. The notice goes on the form of the law on ending a tenancy, which has no rule (nobody's acts are checked against it), so it still changes no one's behaviour, and the result says so. The cap isn't put: no schedule covers a cap.
  - **Offices that apply a rule by its id honour withdrawal**: the planning officer (the habitat law), the rates officer and the tax clerk (their law), the finance officer (the approval rule, with the payments procedure it's given). Each follows a replacement that names the record.
  - **Existing worlds:** the generated town gains three schedules and three forms, the three laws gain their figures, two change whom they speak for and all three their wording slightly ("on written notice of at least 60 days"), so a generated world's hashes change. No test pinned one.
  - **Not done:** a law of a new kind that binds (a rent cap), as 2e says. A law repealed without a replacement still isn't known to be by landlords, tenants, the union or the tribunal (their books show laws only): it needs their channels to carry withdrawals of laws, which is a world-config choice with a cost in what every citizen is shown, or a published register of the laws in force. The union's notice of action has the same one-second shortfall the landlord's had (its law's notice is in days, and no one judges it to the second yet).
  - **Revisit if** a law needs a figure that isn't a duration or a count (an amount of money: the form has the type; the schedule's wording of it would need the currency); schedules multiply until writing rules by hand is the bottleneck (then the translator's design decides whether a model may compose one, with a check the mayor can read); a council should be able to add a schedule itself (today only a world's content can); the town's laws need to be out of a council's reach in some worlds (a higher lawmaker with its own clerk); or model mayors pass free-worded laws anyway and expect them to bind (then the result's wording isn't doing its job).
- **No currency in the code: amounts are read and written as the world's config says** (2026-10-05; from the audit of place in the code, on Naz's standing permission to change what generality needs, contracts included). Amounts in words were dollars and cents everywhere: both resolvers read `$`, "dollars", "bucks" and "cents" at 100 to the unit, and everything shown or written said `$x.xx`. A world whose currency has no decimals, or three, or another sign, read "500" as 50,000 units and saw "$500.00".
  - **A contract addition** (contracts/README.md): each of `world.currencies` may say how it's written: `symbol`, `unit_words`, `minor_words` (all optional; singular first and plural last). Presentation only, like `minor_units`; the kernel reads none of them. Without them a currency is written with its code ("XYZ 500.00"). The profile gives the town's (`$`; dollar, dollars, bucks; cent, cents), so the town's config carries them and nothing it reads or writes has changed.
  - **One small value, passed in:** `Currency { code, minor_units, symbol, unit_words, minor_words }`, the config's first currency. `parse_amount` and `format_money` (the kernel's resolver) and `parseAmount` and `formatMoney` (TypeScript) take it; neither has a default. The kernel's session reads it at start and gives it to each seat's knowledge; the TypeScript `Knowledge` is made with it. The two parsers are the same steps in the same order (the TypeScript one was a pattern; it's now the kernel's procedure), and say the same refusals.
  - **What's read:** the sign or the code first ("$500", "XYZ 500"), digits grouped or not, decimals up to the currency's own (up to three with a multiplier, as before), k/m/b/t and thousand to trillion, then a unit's word, the code, or a word for the smallest unit. Bare digits are whole units. Exact, never finer than the smallest unit, 38 digits at most in both languages (the kernel's stopped two digits short through an intermediate product; it doesn't now). **New for the town:** its code is read too ("AUD 500", "500 aud"), where before it was refused.
  - **The behaviour modules' wording reads the currency of the world being stepped** (`inCurrency`, set by the run before each step and by a model-run participant before it words what it shows; `moneyNow`, `smallestNow`, `calls.amountText`), not a value handed to each of about 160 places that word an amount. A step is synchronous, so two worlds in one process can't cross, and wording an amount with no world's money set is an error, not a default. It is still state outside the arguments, chosen for the size of the change and because other work was in those files: `ctx.world.currency` carries the value, and the modules can take it from there one at a time.
  - **Texts that said "cents"** (rents, wages, dues, invoices, the bank's failed payments, tax notices: "1500 cents a month") are written with the currency's own word for its smallest unit, and as money where it has none. The role in an employment agreement's text is found by the text's shape, not by the word "cents". Household members' letters about rent shares and pay are written and read back in the currency's canonical form.
  - **Amounts that were literals in the generator are the profile's**, as placeholders to source: opening balances (the bank, the town, the builder, a landlord, a shop, a factory), the least an adult has saved, the yearly parks line, the lending office's reserve, and the payment the town's procedure needs the mayor's approval over (the procedure's text is built from the figure and the currency, and the finance officer is given the same figure; it read "$50,000" as a literal while the module had its own 5,000,000).
  - **The guardrail** (orchestrator/test/generality.test.ts): platform code under the kernel, participants, translator, orchestrator and measure writes no currency's sign and none of its words. Left alone, each listed there with its reason: the block language's own `$name` reference sign; a pattern's `"$1"`; what a model run costs its operator (billed in US dollars: not the world's money); the translator's prompt examples; Rust's unit tests; and the name `cents` for an amount in the smallest unit (read only in texts the code writes or matches).
  - **Not done:** only the world's first currency is read and written (an account in a second currency is still worded in the first; the resolver has the account in hand and could use its own); the translator's prompt examples and the training data's other forms of an amount are still in dollars (the prompt was being changed and a model trained on it); the finance officer module's own default for `approval_over` stays; the names `usd` and `cents` stay; measures (measure/) report amounts as integers and needed nothing.
  - **Revisit if** a world has accounts in more than one currency (then amounts must be worded in the account's); a currency is written with its sign after the amount, or groups its digits otherwise than in threes with commas, or uses a decimal comma (the format has one shape); a multiplier's letter is also a currency's word (multipliers win, today); a second profile's words collide with a duration's or a count's; or anything steps a participant without the run (then it must say whose money, or the wording stops).
- **The translator's training data covers the whole call language, any world's forms and any world's money** (2026-10-06; translator/train/synthetic.ts and data.ts 0.2.0, orchestrator/src/training.ts; design §16.4 and §16.10). Gold programs sampled from code participants' logs were lopsided: of about 6,200, money 1,510 and work 1,199, and a handful or none for take, position, entity, claim, system and harm. Form examples carried no form, so nothing could be learned about filling one in. Amounts' other forms and the prompt's examples were in dollars.
  - **Generated gold, from the contract** (`synthetic`): for every function of calls.schema.json, 300 different programs by default across its sensible combinations of arguments (calls.md's meanings), with values from seeded pools in the styles logs show (acc-…, rec.N, c0042, ent.N, lot-NN-NN, str.N, apts-N/dwelling.N, job-…), invented organisations and people (by id, by name alone, or only described), and kinds, tasks, templates and types as ordinary words. Every one parses, passes the contract's argument table and is askable. Programs of several lines are dependent ones only (an organisation then its charter, account or claim; an account then a payment into it; a post then its appointment; a notice then a letter about it; a newcomer then where they live; a system then its post): unrelated lines are `too_much`. Each function's programs come from a generator of their own (the seed and the function's name), so asking for one function or for more changes nothing about the rest.
  - **Invented forms** (`formSets`, `syntheticForms`): a name of one to three words, an office, two to seven fields of every type the resolver reads (amount, duration, count, text, choice, space, structure_type, and the id types lot, cell, account, record, entity, person, structure, position), some required, choices of two to five. Each is filled in several ways (all required fields, some others, the office named or not), and the example carries the form's line exactly as `translatorContext()` writes it (`formLine`; a test holds the two together, and the resolver takes every generated fill). Forms come in small sets, what one person knows of: a form is shown alone or beside one or two of its set, and now and then two offices have a form by the same name (then the call says whose). A log's or a seen case's form request carries its form too, from the log's header or the test's fixture. The built `unknown_form` refusal leaves the right form out and keeps the others.
  - **A claim's content is slots, not JSON to write.** `about={"action":"build","object":"lot-12-03"}` stays as the call language writes it in the program; what a request must carry (`needed`, `spansIn`) is `about.action` (a word of the request) and `about.object` (an id, exactly), so a model that copies and chooses fills two slots and the decoder writes the object.
