Only one to four named words or ids count as slots. *What this gives up:* a claim with nested or numeric content can't be asked for through the first model.
  - **Every other structured argument is out of the first model's scope,** and a program carrying one is no gold program, counted by function and argument when a log is read (`whyNot`, `JSON_OUT_OF_SCOPE`): statement `data`, `adico`, `offer`, `practice`, `assertion`, `registry`; presence `variables`; position and entity `channels` and `role_module`. A list of names or ids is copied name by name and stays in. A statement's `formal` (a CEL condition, as text) and a keeper's `per_request` are never generated.
  - **Amounts in any world's money.** `amountWritings(n, currency)` writes an amount every way the parser reads it in that currency (sign or code first, grouped or not, decimals, the unit's words, the code after, bare digits, k and m, thousand and million, and counted in its smallest unit where that has a name), each checked to read back as the same number; `amountForms` and `surfaceVariant` take the currency (a log's own, from its header). Generated programs draw from eleven invented currencies (a sign before, a code only, no decimals, three). **The translator's prompt teaches none:** its rule is "An amount is copied exactly as written, with whatever sign or word of money the request used", the contract's line on amounts (which shows three currencies) is replaced in what the translator is told, and the examples' amounts are digits alone (an amount in any world). calls.md itself is unchanged. A test asserts no currency's sign or word in `translatorSystem()`. The translator's version wasn't bumped here (other work is on the same file): it should be with the next change to the prompt.
  - **A floor under every function** (`balancedGold`, the default for `oikos translator-data`; `floor: 0` is the old behaviour): the logs' programs (each also on another surface) and the seen cases', then generated ones up to the floor (300), and at least 100 generated per function whatever the logs hold; an optional ceiling samples a function's log programs down. Every gold says where it came from (`origin`: log, case, synthetic), and the manifest counts per function, per source and per shape, with what was left out of the logs and why. A floor under each refusal label (`refusalFloor`) is kept when requests are written; `not_an_act` now has briefs of its own (it had none). A trial run's `--limit` takes a program of each function in turn.
  - **Styles the pilots' office-holders wrote in** (pilot 8b's requests; pilot-style.md wasn't in `dev` yet): the capacity first ("As Mayor of …"), a name with its id in square brackets after it, a record cited in passing, a closing "do not …", and one long instruction of stacked clauses. A rider adds nothing to the program and the example records it (`riders`: its words, where they are, `adds: "nothing"`); the program's values are looked for outside it. A citation is a rider only where the call has nowhere to put one: where the program has a `basis`, that record is what's worded as the citation, and where it could have one and doesn't, the request is written formally with none (a citation there would be a different program).
  - **Training and validation are split by gold program** (`split`, written as train.jsonl and validation.jsonl): all of a program's requests, its other surface and any refusal made from it on one side; a share of every function's programs and of every refusal label's; form examples by form, so a validation form is never shown in training, as the one to fill in or beside another (whole sets go together). Refusals built from several requests are built again on each side from its own. A function with fewer than four programs has none in validation.
  - **Not done:** flags for the floor, the ceiling, the refusal floor and the validation share on `oikos translator-data` (cli.ts was being changed by other work; the options are on `trainingData`); the generality test's exemption for the prompt's example lines is no longer needed and can go; the form a field belongs to isn't checked in a request mechanically (the writer is told to say which field each value is for; the round trip checks it).
  - **Revisit if** the trained model's misses cluster on generated wording that no seat uses (then the pools want real worlds' words, from profiles); claims need content beyond an action and an object; a world's forms use a field type not listed here (the resolver reads any other type as an id, and the generator would have to learn its name); a currency is written in a way `amountWritings` doesn't write (a sign after the amount, a decimal comma); or the floor makes generated programs outnumber real ones so far that validation stops predicting how seats' requests fare (then validate on logs' and pilots' requests alone).

- **The translator is tested on what the pilots' seats really asked for** (2026-10-06). The test set's requests were written by us, in our idea of a mayor's voice. The logs hold 195 requests made by model seats (mayor, finance officer, planning officer; codex pilots 8 to 10, pilots 7d, 7e, 8, 8b and 9), and more than half of them never became a draft. They are now seen test cases, word for word, each with the answer that is right under the rules as written today (`translator/eval/pilot-cases.ts`: 180 distinct), and a description of how seats phrase things for whoever generates training data (`translator/eval/pilot-style.md`).
  - **The answers were decided by hand:** a draft the seat confirmed and the kernel applied is the answer unless today's rules say otherwise (a park commissioned as a hand-written agreement is `structure(...)` now); a declined or failed one gets what it should become. 63 are requests to read or inspect something (`none(not_an_act)`), 46 a form, 23 a letter, 15 a withdrawal, 7 several things at once. Several answers are accepted where the rules don't settle it, and the case's note says so.
  - **Scored by shape, not by acts** (`pilot-run.ts`, `passesReal`): these requests name their own towns' ids, so an answer can't be resolved against the test town. The same functions and argument names, every id, amount and duration equal by what it comes to, free text not compared ("…"), a refusal by its label. `passes` for the fixture cases is unchanged. *What this gives up:* nothing checks that the ids exist or that the acts would be allowed; a letter's wording is never read.
  - **Run as a run runs it,** one attempt, shown the forms the seat knew (as the logs' transcripts have them) and nothing else of its town: `oikos translator-eval --model … --real` (a third group), or `--only-real`. Without the forms every form request would rightly be refused as unknown, so "an empty Knowledge" has that one exception.
  - **36 requests ask for something the call language can't say,** and the cases name what (`GAPS`): a standing arrangement held and released (11); what an office does by itself, stopped (7); going to see in person (9); a record known only by description (3); an act that depends on what a look finds (3); two records cited as the basis (3). None of the six refusals says "no call can do this", so for these any refusal counts as right. Nothing is changed in the contract here: they are findings for Naz.
  - **First result, codex/gpt-6.1-sol:** 144 of 180 right, 29 no draft, 7 wrong drafts. 27 of the 36 misses trace to the prompt, not the model: a form cited by its record's id is refused as unknown (10: the forms are listed by name only); a proposal with "ask the clerk to put it to the ballot" is refused as too much (13 of this kind); cancelling a standing payment isn't known to be a withdrawal (3); "prepare it for my review" is read as looking (1). Wording for each is proposed to the prompt's owner, not made here.
  - **Pilot 9 had stopped, not finished:** its last request's draft was written `for=<a proceeding's record>`, a statement in a record's name, which the log's contract refused. That request is a case too.
  - **Revisit if** the rules change (the answers are tied to today's wording: re-decide the cases the change touches, and say so in their notes); a gap is closed (its cases get a real answer); the set is used to pick between translators (then the cases accepting several answers want settling first, and a second run to see the spread); or seats' style shifts with a new model (add the new pilots' requests; the early and late voices already differ by a factor of two in length).

- **The training data's pipeline, after an independent audit of a trial** (2026-10-06). A trial of 190 gold programs was audited by hand, request by request. Of 172 examples kept, 97.1% were right in substance, 2.9% wrong, and 19% right but unnatural (the call read out, not a person asking). Of 47 rejected, 21 were good requests lost, 16 deserved it, and 10 were the gold program's fault. Each fault is answered generally, in `translator/train/` (`data.ts`, `synthetic.ts`, new `meaning.ts`) and `orchestrator/src/training.ts`; data version 0.3.0.
  - **Programs are compared by value, not by text** (`meaning.ts`, `sameMeaning`; `sameProgram` uses it): amounts by `parseAmount` in the gold's own money, durations by `parseDuration` ("every 28 days" is "28 days"), counts by `parseCount`, a flag as yes or true (gold programs write `leave=yes`, and `goldText` makes them), one id as a list of it and a list as a set, a label in words as the resolver makes it, a form by name, words or record id with its office filled in where the forms known give one, and a letter to several as the same letter to each. Every equivalence is one the resolver makes. *Deliberately not equal:* work by a list of people against one call each (`units` is the whole job, so four calls of 12 units are 48), and `presence(leave=c0045)` against `leave=yes, who=c0045` (the resolver asks a question of the first). Of the trial's 21 lost good requests this accepts 5; the other 16 are the checker's own mistakes (a required template dropped from a birth, 7; a purpose or a holder dropped; `kind=` written for `survey=`; too_much for the same act twice), which a comparison must not forgive: they are what a second checker is for. The file depends on the call language and the resolver's label rule only, so the eval can use it.
  - **A gold program is one a sentence could sensibly ask for** (`implausible`, `plausible`; each one dropped is counted by its reason, a log's under `excluded` as "implausible: …", the generator's in the gold report). It drops all 10 of the trial's gold-at-fault programs: a form with no office (the rules write `to=` from the list; a log's form now has it written in, `withOffice`), lines that don't depend on each other, a line that repeats another, a letter "about" something just published whose words are about something else, the other side of an agreement committing to its own moving in or to something built for us, an exchange in which nobody pays, `for=` where nothing reads it, a claim to moor at a record, a second parent who is an office, an age that doesn't fit what someone comes as. The generator no longer makes any of these: commitments come from exchanges people make (let and rent, supply and pay, lend and repay), with a template that fits; what is published reads as its kind; ids always carry a digit, a dot, a hyphen or a slash, so the copy check holds a writer to them (`timber`, `mayor` and `inspector_aid` were being written as words). *What this gives up:* generated ids no longer include a plain word like a real town's `works` or `builder`; those come from logs only.
  - **The writer is told the act, not the call** (`writerSystem`, `bringsAbout`): each call goes with what it brings about in the words of the translator's own gloss, made particular where an argument settles it (someone "comes back into the world from outside", not "is present"; a system "is put to work"; "notice is given to end an agreement"). Four mechanical checks run before any model is asked, each counted in `dropped`: an open quote, a bound name (v1) in the request, an argument's name with "=" or as "the … field" outside a form, and "0 days" said of something that starts now (a wait of no time is no longer looked for in the request). A citation is a rider only where no argument could absorb it: a letter has `re=`, so "in accordance with rec.3495" there is no rider.
  - **Spans point at the right occurrence** (`spansIn`, `occurrences`): a value said more than once is placed by the other values of its own call (a line, or a nested commitment), a bracketed id is preferred to the same word in a title, and where nothing settles it every occurrence is recorded and the span marked `ambiguous` (counted in the manifest). A span in a program of several lines says which line. A second "Do not …" is a rider: the brief gives two about a third of the time, and one the writer adds unasked is recorded too.
  - **too_much as people make it:** two or three related, separate acts in one message, both built from kept examples that share an id (never a letter or a second act of the same kind, which the rules call one request) and written by a writer from generated groups (`piled`, `piledBriefs`). A third of the built ones stay the long pasted kind. The refusal floor is generic over `calls.LABELS`: a label short of it is asked for again, twice at most, and one nothing makes yet is reported short (`no_such_act` today, until its brief lands).
  - **The checker is never the writer** (`checkerFor`; `backs` on `generate` and `trainingData`, `--back-model` given more than once): each request goes to the first checker that is another model, the example records which, and the manifest counts each pairing. With only the writer's own model to check, the example is kept and marked `verified_by_writer`.
  - **Not done, and not measured:** no model was called, so the 19% unnatural share and the 21 lost requests are answered by construction and by tests with stand-ins, not by a second trial. Run over the trial's kept requests, the four mechanical checks reject 12, every one of which the audit had flagged (5 with an open quote among those it called right in substance, 7 it called unnatural), but wording such as "in a quantity of 33" is left to the instructions: a rule for it would reject "12 units of make", which is how the rules themselves say it.
  - **Revisit if** a second trial still loses good requests to the check (then look at whether the checker's rules, not the comparison, need the change: a birth's template is the first place to look); the mechanical checks reject natural requests (each reason's count is in the manifest; `rejects.jsonl` has the requests); ambiguous spans are more than a few in a hundred (then the writer should be told not to repeat a value); or the call language gains a function where one call to several equals one each (add it to `ONE_EACH`), or a flag (add it to `FLAGS`).
- **The real requests' answers are keyed to the seventh refusal, and the prompt says three things it left open** (2026-10-06). With `no_such_act` in the language, "any refusal" (`none(*)`) is nobody's answer any more, and the last run's 140 of 180 (2 no draft, 38 wrong drafts) on codex/gpt-6.1-sol was mostly the keys' doing. After three rounds: **173 of 180, 4 no draft, 3 wrong drafts** (round by round 166 / 171 / 173; translator 0.7.2).
  - **The keys' (43 cases re-keyed).** The 33 gap cases whose answer was `none(*)` lost it (the other three cite two records and always had a program): 27 are `none(no_such_act)` alone and six keep a second answer, where the request can fairly be read another way ("through finance", convening three offices, a review that is mostly a look, numbered separate actions, letters to be forwarded); none is `not_an_act` (going to see for oneself is `no_such_act` by the rule's own example). Two letters to the bank that the pilot had applied for "place a hold" are no longer accepted: nobody was asked to be told, and a letter isn't a hold. One key named the clerk by an id the request never gave. Three form requests with the clerk named accept the form with or without a word to the clerk. Six more cases gained `no_such_act` beside their answer, with a gap named (a payment to stop that is named only by describing it; a reconciliation to be reported that depends on what letters bring back; an entry in the books that moves no money, a new gap, `ledger_entry`).
  - **The scoring's.** A form's `to` isn't compared (`passesReal`): absent, the office the form goes to, or whoever there deals with it are the same act by the rules, and which names come to that office is the town's knowledge, which these cases come without. *What this gives up:* `to=council-clerk` on a form that goes to `city` passes here, and in a run the resolver answers "it goes to city" and the call is tried again. Nothing else is looser; `none(*)` is gone from the scoring too.
  - **The rules' (five sentences added to `translatorSystem()`, all general).** (1) A request that wants something no function does and also something one does is still `no_such_act`: the part must not be written for the whole. In pilot 9 a withdrawal was applied for "withdraw the posting and suspend the process that recreates it", nothing told the seat the suspension was unsaid, and it asked four more times. (2) What becomes of a form once it's handed in (put to a vote, decided) adds no line unless someone the request names is to be told: "Put it to the councillors' ballot" had become a letter to "the council" or "the councillors". (3) A form takes its own fields and no others: a petition cited was written `basis=`, which the resolver refuses on a form. (4) A building commissioned has no separate `money(...)` for its price (the pilots' translator made the same double payment). (5) What a request says must stay as it is, or wants shown with the draft, adds nothing and is no reason to refuse; and a request that says withdraw or cancel is a withdrawal whatever reason it gives ("pending verification … a temporary pause on hiring" was being refused as a pause).
  - **The model's, and what is two-sided (7 left).** Its own: a form with two withdrawals refused as `no_such_act`; answers to a tribunal refused as an unknown form though no form is named; an amount copied with its restatement in brackets ("5000000 cents ($50,000)"). Two-sided: an inspection that also says "preserve the records"; "obtain from the clerk and inspect" (a letter, or a look); three letters forwarded to five offices (letters, or `too_much`: the rule calls the same kind of thing done several times one thing, and the model still refuses the longest of them). The language's: one act citing two records, written as a list in `basis`.
  - **The seen and outside cases after the changes:** seen 60 of 62 (1 no draft, 1 wrong draft), outside 64 of 66 (0 no draft, 2 wrong drafts). On the 0.7.1 prompt, run the same hour: seen 61 of 62, outside 64 of 66. The one seen case lost is "Buy one finished park … from GreenCo", now refused as `no_such_act` where the key is `structure(...)` (one run each, so it may be chance; but buying something finished isn't plainly having it built, and the firmer wording on `no_such_act` may be what tipped it).
  - **Training data:** `refusalBriefs` writes `no_such_act` to a brief, three kinds in turn (a payment made every so often, from a gold program, to be held and resumed, not ended; a place a gold program names to be gone to and checked in person; a routine of the speaker's own office to be changed, which needs nothing of the gold), and `refusalFloor` counts the label like the rest. A record named only by description has no brief: the rule that a name without an id is written in quotes pulls the other way, and a writer's request would land on either.
  - **Revisit if** seats take the whole-request refusal badly (a seat that loses the sayable half and doesn't ask again: then write the half and say what was left out, which needs a way for a draft to say so); `to` on forms starts failing in runs (then score it against a town's knowledge, or have the resolver take a description of whoever deals with the form); a call can cite two records, hold a standing arrangement or write a ledger entry (those cases get a real answer); or the cases are used to pick between translators (36 still accept more than one answer, most of them a form with or without a word to the clerk).
- **The training data after a second audit: two rules settled, the uniform made various, blind checking a step** (2026-10-06). A second independent audit of a trial (274 examples) found **240 right, 31 right but unnatural, 3 wrong** (98.9% right in substance), and a blind reader of another model family, given only the requests and the forms, agreed with the gold on **262**. On 8 of 26 cases the reader was "wrong" only because of two things the call language left unclear; the three wrong programs and most of the unnatural requests were systematic. Each is answered generally (`translator/train/data.ts`, `synthetic.ts`, new `blind.ts`; `orchestrator/src/training.ts`, `cli.ts`); translator 0.7.3, data version 0.4.0. No model was called: all of it is held by tests with stand-ins (`translator/test/train-audit2.test.ts`, `train.test.ts`, `orchestrator/test/training.test.ts`).
  - **Two rules the language left unclear, and two it left unsaid, are in `translatorSystem()`** (one sentence each, general; two examples added). Things moved: `to=` is who receives them, `at=` the place they are delivered to, `from_at=` the place they come from; a place named as where they go is `at=`, never `to=`, and with no one named they are the asker's own. Work by several people is one `work(...)` with `by` as a list, never a line each (`units` is the whole job: `meaning.ts` already refused to call the two equal). `template=` is the request's own word for the kind of newcomer, child or system, and left out if it uses none. `every=` is the request's own words for how often, copied whole. Two seen cases added (`move-goods-place`, `work-several`); the generator's goods never have a place as `to`, and its work is one call however many do it.
  - **A call nested in an agreement is the call as it is by itself.** A commitment's goods drew their own arguments, with no place of origin to say, and a writer made one of the destination: "we deliver 19 goods from [works-41] to [c2883]" for `at=works-41` (two of the three wrong programs). Goods are now drawn one way wherever they appear (`synthetic.ts`, `delivery`), with `from_at` now and then in a commitment too; the writer is told what each value is (`ROLES`: "works-41 is the place they are delivered to (never where they come from)"); and a request that says "from" of the place the program delivers to is dropped mechanically (`place_reversed`). A test holds every nested call's arguments to a set the same function has standing alone. An offer that names nobody is no gold program (`implausible`).
  - **Gold is kept in one spelling whatever wrote it** (`goldText`): a count, an amount and a length of time quoted, a list of one unwrapped, a flag as `yes`. The third wrong program was a log's `units=1, by=["c0007"]` beside generated `units="1", by=c0007`. A time counted in seconds ("5914794 seconds", as a town's code writes it) is nothing anyone says, and no gold program.
  - **`every` is the request's own span.** The same wording, "every 28 days", stood for `every="every 28 days"` in one example and `every="28 days"` in the next. Each request is now asked for with how often said one of the ways people say it (`howOften`: "every 28 days", "every 4 weeks", "monthly", "a month", "per month", "every fortnight"; never the bare length, which is how long), chosen by seed; the writer is told the words, the example's program has them, and a request that says it another way has lost the span and is dropped. *What this leaves:* the parser reads "every" and "per" before a length and not "each" ("each fortnight" is refused by `parseDuration`), so no gold says it that way; a seat that does will have it copied and then refused. That is the contract's to change, not this pipeline's.
  - **Several ways of saying each act** (`SAYINGS`, `sayingFor`, `writerNotes`). Told only what a function brings about, a writer says it back: "physically harm" in 13 of 13 requests to harm, "without their agreement" in 12 of 12 to take, "have someone move into" in 7 of 7 moves with nobody named, "for good" in 6 of 6 retirements, "recorded as an infant" in 12 of 13 births. Every function now has a small table of ways to say it, by which arguments the call has (three to fourteen to a pattern: hit, attack, lay into; grab, help myself to, make off with; I'm moving into, put me in; we're having a baby), of which one is chosen by seed and given as "said something like"; the description is marked as not for reuse and no longer holds the phrases; where nobody is named the writer is told it is the speaker ("I", never "someone"). The echoes the audit found are dropped mechanically if they come back (`description_echoed`), as is a form's label read out with its underscore.
  - **Refusals are worded many ways.** `not_an_act`: thirty ways of asking to look (see, read, check, review, inspect, look up, tell me, what is, who holds; all eight in the trial began "Show me"), and the writer is told not to add that nothing is to be changed. `not_yours`: thirty kinds of someone else's decision (five of eight were "lower their prices"), ten ways of demanding it, and someone to demand it of where the gold names nobody. `unclear`: ten kinds of vagueness (five of eight were "something about X"). Built `too_much`: nothing between the requests, one sentence with "and", a numbered list, a list after a line saying several things follow, or one of twenty ways of running on (`runTogether`; every built one had been joined by "Also:" or "And then:"); only the first part may open with a capacity; each says which examples it was built from (`built_from`).
  - **A span says how its value comes from the request** (`Span.how`). `copy`: the span's characters are the value. `choice`: the span is the request's words and the value what was chosen for them (a form's name, a kind, a task, a template, a role, an option of a form's list, a claim's action), so no copy head is trained to make `footpath_declaration` of "footpath declaration". `implied`: a yes-or-no the wording implies (`leave`, `sex`, a claim's `position`, `visible=public`, a wait of no time), recorded with no span. The manifest counts each.
  - **A refusal built from one positive stays with it in the split.** An `unknown_form` refusal is its positive's request word for word with the form left out; these were built anew on each side of the split, so the ones in the data were not the ones split. They now go where their positive goes, by the gold program both carry; only refusals built from several examples are built again on each side.
  - **Blind checking is a step** (`blind.ts`; `oikos translator-data-check <DIR> --blind`, and `--answers FILE --reader NAME`). The data folder gets `blind.jsonl`, the requests and forms alone in a seeded shuffled order (the first trial handed them over in the data's order, refusals last, so position gave the label away). A reader's `{id, program}` answers are compared by meaning in each example's own money and with its forms, and the folder gains `checked.jsonl` (agreed, marked `verified_by` the reader, with who translated it back kept), `disagreed.jsonl` (both programs) and the counts per function in the manifest. The examples file is left as it was: what to train on is for whoever reads the disagreements.
  - **Measured on the audited trial:** run over its 213 kept positives, the new mechanical checks reject 49: both goods programs the audit called wrong (`place_reversed`), 16 it called unnatural (13 echoes, 3 form labels read out), and 31 it called right, every one of them "physically harm", "without their agreement" or "have someone move into" (the uniformity above, which the audit counted apart from its 31 unnatural). None is a request in other words.
  - **Not done, and not measured:** no trial was run on the changed pipeline, so whether the unnatural share falls from 31 in 274 is not known; the translator's prompt gained four sentences and two examples and was not run against the seen, outside or real cases on a model (the tests hold only that it parses and teaches no currency).
  - **Revisit if** a trial's manifest shows `description_echoed` or `place_reversed` rejecting natural requests (the reasons are counted and the requests kept in `rejects.jsonl`); writers copy the suggested way word for word (then the requests for a function are a dozen sentences, not one: give two ways, or none in some styles); the four new sentences cost the translator on the real cases (run `translator-eval --real` before a pilot); the parser comes to read "each fortnight" (add it to `howOften`); a blind reader's disagreements cluster in one function (the next rule the language leaves open); or a function is added to the call language (it needs its entry in `SAYINGS`: a test fails until it has one).
- **Five reliability faults from a code audit, each a mechanism bug fixed where it was** (2026-10-06). None changes a rule of behaviour; each has a test that failed before.
  - **The kernel's stdio client takes its requests one at a time** (`orchestrator/src/kernel.ts`, `exchange`). A reply is matched to its request only by its place in the stream, and `request` wrote its line and then read "the next line" with nothing holding the next caller back: two callers at once (several model seats translating in one round) each waited on the same reader, and one took a chunk holding both replies while the other waited for ever, or took the other's. Every exchange (one request, or a batch) now waits for the one before it, in the order asked. The in-process transport was already safe (synchronous) and is unchanged. Test: 300 requests of four kinds made at once against a kernel subprocess, each reply checked against its own request (`orchestrator/test/reliability.test.ts`).
  - **A log the kernel can't write ends the run** (`kernel/src/protocol.rs`, `run.rs`; `KernelLogFailed`). The kernel reported it as any refused request (`{"ok":false,"error":"log write failed: .."}`), and `tryRequestMany` and `tryRequest` turn a refusal into `null`: a command skipped, a unit's wake lost, the run going on with a hole in its record. The reply now carries `"fatal": true` (the protocol is not a contract), and the client throws `KernelLogFailed` on it on both transports, from every form of request; a batch's diagnosis says "log not written". Tested with a log that is a named pipe whose reader has gone (every write fails, as on a full disk): the client alone, and a whole run. *What it doesn't do:* the kernel itself goes on answering after the failure; it is the host that stops.
  - **A landlord judges arrears lease by lease** (`firm.landlord` 0.12.2, `participants/src/behaviour/firms.ts`). The audit was right: what a tenant had paid was one running total per paying account, never reset, set against the dues of one lease counted from its signing. On a renewed lease (or a second one from the same landlord) everything paid under the first counted as credit: a tenant who had paid twelve months could stop for twelve more, and two besides, before any notice. Payments are now kept with their times, and a lease is credited only with those made since its rent began: its signing, or for a lease that renews one of ours (its `basis`) the end of that one's term, which is when the tenant's rent under it starts (on the same days as before: `citizen.ts`); its dues are counted from then too. Test (`participants/test/landlord.test.ts`): two tenants renew; the one who pays nothing under the new lease gets notice at the first wake two months into it, the one who pays never does. *What it gives up:* old arrears aren't carried into the new lease (the landlord offered it with no notice given, so they were under two months), and a late payment of the old lease's rent made after it ended is credited to the new one. **This changes worlds that already run:** wherever a tenant on a renewed lease falls behind, a notice now comes that didn't, so the state hashes of such worlds differ from 0.12.1's.
  - **A statement taken down is news wherever statements like it are shown** (`kernel/crates/perception/src/observe.rs`, `matches`, `taken_down`). Why a plain repeal reached nobody: it was perception, not the book. A channel that picks statements by kind (`statement_kinds`, a book of laws) or by what they carry (`has`) asked each record "are you a statement of this kind?", and a withdrawal is not a statement, so it was never delivered; `Book` applies a withdrawal as soon as it is shown one. Now a withdrawal made by whoever the statement speaks for (or its author's entity: the same test that ends its standing in a search) passes those two conditions if the statement it takes down does, so every look that would have shown the statement shows its withdrawal, once (the channel's cursor), and the full picture has it too. Someone else's "withdrawal" of a law takes nothing down and is not delivered this way. A filter that lists the record types it shows (`records`) is as it was: it names withdrawals or it doesn't. Searches are as they were (they find records as they are; `standing` already left the withdrawn out). No contract's text changes: the world-config contract says a channel delivers "what changed within its scope", and already says so of a withdrawal for `involving`. Tests: the kernel's (`kernel/tests/attention.rs`: by kind and by field, once, not a stranger's, not through a `records` list, in the full picture); a town where a law forbidding rent increases is withdrawn by the town on day 120: no increase before, the landlords' reviews go ahead after, and each landlord, the tribunal's member and the union's organiser is shown the repeal through its laws once (`orchestrator/test/justice.test.ts`; on the kernel before, no increase ever came); the tribunal finds a notice given after a repeal valid and one given before it invalid (`participants/test/tribunal.test.ts`). **What this changes in worlds that already run:** every channel of this sort now carries withdrawals (price lists replaced, listings let, forms withdrawn), so a code participant's `book.open(kind)` stops counting what was taken down. Measured on the default town, 200 days, no law withdrawn: the same state hash and the same running time as before (302 withdrawals in the run). A world where a law is withdrawn will differ, which is the point; others may, where a module counted a withdrawn statement as open. One did, and the suite found it: a household buying a home gave up when "its listing was no longer open", which it had never been able to see; a seller relists 5% cheaper each month, so with withdrawals shown the buyer dropped the purchase at every relisting and never bought (`orchestrator/test/home-market.test.ts`). The buyer now goes on while the listing it answered, or the seller's later one for the same lot, stands (`citizen.default` 0.35.3): a home relisted cheaper is the same home, and the seller already takes a purchase answering any of its listings. That keeps what buyers did before; one whose home is really taken off the market now stops, as the rule always meant. Some 130 other places read `book.open` or `book.withdrawn`; the suite passes, and none was read through one by one. *Not done:* a model seat's "On file" (`renderStanding`) lists statements only, so it still shows a repealed law as on file, and says nothing of the withdrawal it now receives; changing what seats read is a change to their prompts, left for Naz's word.
  - **A union's stoppage starts with a day** (`union.organiser` 0.4.1, `participants/src/behaviour/union.ts`). It was the landlord's fault of 0.12.1 exactly: the stoppage started `notice + lead` seconds from when the union decided, the notice is on the record a moment later, and the union finds its lead by adding a day at a time until no law is breached, so it always lands on exactly the law's period: on the record, 431,999 seconds of a five-day law's 432,000. Fixed the same way: the stoppage starts with the first day to begin once the notice has run (and the law is read against that date). Test: the strike test (`orchestrator/test/unions.test.ts`) now holds every notice as recorded to the law's five days in full, starting on a day. **This changes when stoppages start** (up to a day later) in every run with industrial action, so those worlds' state hashes differ.
  - **A law that can't be read against an act says so** (`participants/src/behaviour/common.ts`: `holdsOf`, `Book.unread`, `noted`, `noting`). The three places that weigh an act against the laws (`breaches`; the planning officer's and the inspector's own in `institutions.ts`) took a condition's error for "no breach" with `"value" in v && v.value`: a law naming a field the act lacks (written for another kind of act, or written wrongly by a council or a model mayor) never bound anyone, and nothing anywhere showed it. Who is bound is unchanged: an error still isn't a breach (a law about permits must not bind a notice of rent increase because it can't be read against one). But the reader's book now counts it per law (`Book.unread`), and the reader says it once per law in the reasons of its next act ("(A law that can't be read against an act doesn't bind it: law-x couldn't be read against publish rent_increase (the error), so it wasn't applied.)"), which is the path a code participant's reasons already take to the log (`because`, the act's `reasoning`). The six modules that weigh laws (landlord, union, planning officer, inspector, tribunal member, citizen) have their actions `noted`. No module's version changes: no rule did. Test (`participants/test/tribunal.test.ts`): beside a law that reads cleanly and binds as before, a cap written against a missing field is counted, said once, and appears in the tribunal's reasons with its orders unchanged. The default town's laws all read cleanly: 200 days of it has no such note, and the same state hash with all five changes as with none. *What it doesn't do:* lean logs drop code participants' reasons, and the note with them; a reader that never acts again never says it; nobody is told but the log's reader (whether the law's maker should be is a question of institutions, not of this).
  - **Revisit if** a machine does run on the stdio transport for real runs and its throughput matters (the queue is strictly serial; batches already go as one exchange); the kernel should refuse everything after a failed log write itself (today the host stops); tenants are found to be credited or charged across a renewal in a way the dates don't settle (then attribute each payment to its lease by the standing order it was made under, `per_instruction`, which needs the landlord to see the order's `basis`); withdrawals on kind channels cost too much to show in a large town (measure first: 200 days of the default town ran no slower), or a channel needs statements without their withdrawals (then a filter flag, a contract addition); model seats should stop seeing repealed laws on file (the `renderStanding` change above, with Naz's word); a law's notice period is ever counted in something finer than days (the day boundary then over-gives by up to a day); or notes of unreadable laws flood a log (a law written without a guard on the act's kind errs against every other kind of act its readers weigh: one note per reader per law today).
- **Runs are about a quarter faster, with every result the same** (2026-10-06). A pass over where a run's time goes, fixing the largest costs one at a time; nothing about what a world does changed, and no contract.
  - **How "the same" was held.** Two reference runs, before anything and after each change: the 200-person town for two years on two seeds, and the 1,000-person town for 90 days (code participants only). Each change left the final state hash of all three runs as it was (`b4c35a0c…`, `07de0c7b…`, `dfe25cff…`) and the whole log the same byte for byte, apart from one thing: the log's header carries the kernel's build stamp (the commit it was built from), so every entry's `hash` and `prev`, and the final chain hash with them, differ between any two builds of the kernel, changed or not. The comparison was therefore of the whole log with the stamp and each entry's `hash`/`prev` taken out (the header's config and state hash, every command, event with its observation hash, checkpoint and annotation, in order). On an unchanged kernel the chain hashes repeat exactly.
  - **Where the time went before** (one seed of the town, 93.5 s; the 1,000-person run, 78.5 s; an always-on machine shared with two other jobs). Observing: 41.4 s, 44% (1,000: 40.4 s, 51%), of which 36 s in the engine and 5 s reading the reply. Time moving on (`next`: acts taking effect, nature's day, checkpoints and their state hash at about 1%): 12.1 s, 13% (6.8 s, 9%). Acting: 5.5 s, 6% (4.3 s). Starting: 3.1 s (4.5 s, 6%), nearly all nature's ten-year spin-up. Looking, resolving calls, annotations, wakes: 5 s together. Participants' own stepping: 18.6 s, 20% (14.3 s), the citizens 7.6 s, the payment system 3.3 s, shops 2.4 s. The orchestrator's bookkeeping: 7.8 s, 8%. Writing the log is inside those (about 6% of the engine's thread). After the runs, compressing two logs: 7.7 s, 8% of the batch. The engine runs in the host's process (the native module), one line of JSON each way per request; an observation averages 10 KB in the larger town, 70% of it records.
  - **What was changed** (each its own commit, in `kernel/` unless said):
    - An observation was turned to JSON three times (for its hash, for the reply, and writing the reply out): once now, the hashed bytes being the reply's (`protocol.rs`, `kernel.rs`). About 14% of the town's run, 17% of the larger.
    - Canonical JSON is written faster to the same bytes (`canon.rs`): strings copied in stretches, digits written straight out, keys already in order not sorted again, the place of a bad number worked out only for one. A sixth of observing.
    - A record, and a cell of ground, delivered to many is encoded once and its bytes written into each observation that shows it (`kernel.rs`, `Delivered`; the resolver's knowledge takes what each adds the same way, `RecordFacts`, `CellFacts`). A record is reused only while it's the state's own; a cell only where it's equal, field for field, to how it was last shown, so nothing rests on a change stamp. A fifth of observing in the town.
    - Nature's spin-up works out each field's sources per cell once; a share in basis points is worked in 64 bits where it fits (`nature`). Starting: 3.1 to 1.7 s, 4.3 to 2.4 s. Nature's day walks the state's cells beside the ground's and no longer looks each up.
    - A sites channel keeps the cells it covers (until its entity moves; a place's and a listed set for the run), and a channel over all or much of the ground walks the cells (`observe.rs`). About 1%.
    - A run checks a command once against one copy of its JSON, where it was checked twice or three times (`run.rs`, `apply_prechecked`). A fifth of acting, 5% of observing.
    - A batch's logs are compressed side by side and read a few megabytes at a time (`orchestrator/src/logs.ts`, `batch.ts`): the same compressed bytes; a gigabyte log in 2.5 s where it took 3.8, two in 3 s where they took 7.7.
    - A run's summary now says where its time went by request (`profile.requests`: how many, and the time writing, in the engine, and reading).
  - **After.** The town's batch: 74 to 78 s where it took 96 to 101 (158 s of processor time where it took 203). The 1,000-person run's: 55 to 60 s where it took 78 to 81 (65 s where it took 88). Observing in the engine: 17.3 s where it took 36.4. The full suite: 520 s at 5 jobs, 449 passing; the 66 files timed before took 2,663 s between them and take 2,214 (the machine was busier for the second figure).
  - **Tests.** The perception property test now holds, for every observation it makes, that the bytes delivered are the observation's canonical JSON and that a seat's knowledge takes in the same from them; both reference runs were also run once with every observation checked against the plain encoding (1.6 million of them). The canonical writer has two more unit tests (key order by UTF-16 where it differs from bytes; every string against the rule a character at a time).
  - **Checked and left.** A state's copy for an act shares structure (dropping one is under 1%). `state.takings` is a plain list copied with every such copy and encoded whole at every checkpoint, but these worlds take nothing (0 and 1 takings), so it costs nothing here. The tests' spans are already cut to what each asserts (a poll day and five more, 100 or 150 days of bargaining), and no two runs in a file are the same world, so none was shortened or shared. Running the kernel's tests beside the test files was tried and dropped: on the busy machine the slowest files slowed by more than the minute saved.
  - **Not done, each needing more than a mechanism fix** (what is left is each under about 5%, bar these):
    - *Who is in which space, kept as a lookup.* Finding the building a body is in walks every building's occupants, at every person's observation and in several acts (1.3% of the town's run, 3.5% of the larger: it grows with the town). A person-to-space lookup beside `occupancy` would end it; it's a second copy of state to keep true, so it wants a decision.
    - *Observations without the JSON tree.* What isn't a record or a cell still goes through a JSON value for the resolver's knowledge and the hash (about 5%). Writing canonical bytes straight from the types, and the knowledge from them, would end that, with a second encoder to keep equal to the first.
    - *The boundary.* Reading replies is 6 to 8% and can only fall if less crosses: a seat sent a record it has seen by id only, which changes what an observation is (a contract).
    - *Behaviour modules* (20 to 27% now, spread thin: no one place over 2%). The payment system and the grants office are the dearest per step (160 and 270 microseconds).
    - *`takings` as a persistent list*, before a world with much theft is run long.
  - **Revisit if** a world's observations stop being mostly records and cells (the kept bytes then help less: measure with `profile.requests`); the cells kept per run (up to four ways each) or the records' bytes weigh on memory in a very large world (they're as large as what was delivered once); towns of several thousand are run (the walk over buildings above comes first, then a channel over all the ground, which reads every cell at each look); the kernel's build stamp is wanted out of the hash chain so that chain hashes can be compared between builds (a contract change: the header); or replies are no longer JSON (the reply's bytes being the hashed bytes is then no saving).

- **Tests for the mechanisms that had none, and what writing them found** (2026-10-06; from the code audit: whole mechanisms of the code-run townspeople and offices had no test, and one bug, landlords' arrears, had sat unseen until read). Each mechanism is now stepped on hand-built observations and its acts read back (`participants/test/`: `council`, `elections`, `news`, `bank`, `citizen-voice`, `citizen-rent`, `citizen-household`, `citizen-civic`; a small helper, `stepping.ts`, reads a module's calls back as values and can stand a townsperson up alone). A test pins what the mechanism does as its documentation says, never an outcome; where a share is tested (44% sign petitions, 15% noise) it's the mechanism's own draw over a few hundred seeds, within wide bounds.
  - **Found and fixed, each in its own commit with a test that failed before:**
    - *Any statement of kind `news` moved readers' approval*, whoever published it, by whatever tone it claimed: one item from anyone could set every reader's approval of the town's government to either end, and a tone that wasn't a number made it not a number for good. Readers now count only what's published in the paper's name (a new citizen parameter, `paper`, which the generator gives) and hold the tone to the paper's range, -1 to 1 (citizen 0.35.6). In the generated town nothing that happens changes; state hashes differ for the new parameter in the config.
    - *Votes made after a ballot closed were counted* if they reached the counter before it counted: the council's clerk (0.6.1), which wakes daily, and the electoral office (0.3.1), which counts on the morning of polling day and also counted votes made before the ballot opened. Code voters never vote outside those times, so no code-only run changes.
    - *The bank's failure note and its reader shared no words*: the tenant took any letter about its rent order with "failed" in it as rent missed. The note's words and how a reader knows them are in one place now (`PAYMENT_FAILED`, common.ts; citizen 0.35.4). The note is unchanged.
  - **Found and pinned, not changed** (each marked `DISAGREES WITH docs` in its test, or said in the test's name; for Naz):
    - *A household's word can wait a week* (the phase-A note in docs/gaps.md: "members now follow a leaseholder a round later"). A member told to come while an act of theirs is under way (both home from work at the same moment) gets the word as the act is done, in a wake that only takes them where they belong, and it waits for their next weekly plan. They do join in the end. **A fix was made, committed (citizen 0.35.5) and taken back out (0.35.7)**: a word unheeded made the next look a full plan. It worked on the member alone, and the town tests for households, tenancy, invariance and truth passed; but it moves when people plan, and the full suite's deaths test then failed (deaths at home registered within a fortnight: 11 of 18 where it had 14, exactly its threshold). The same deaths happen in both runs; who sees them differs, because a death at home is seen only in a full plan made at home while it is still among the home's last few departures, and commuting overwrites those within a day or two. So that test has been passing on timing, and the two want fixing together (a household's word, and the sight of a death, taken as news at any wake). Left for a decision rather than chased: one fix had already uncovered the next. (The commit that made the fix, f58a6f7, and the two after it fail that one test; the commit that takes it out passes the whole suite.)
    - *A proposal marked for neither side gets no from every councillor.* It sits at 0 with the status quo, so it's never "closer"; only the 15% noise can pass it (three flips of five: under 3%). So an amendment of a law that isn't about tenants or development, or a free-worded law, almost never passes a code council. That is the documented rule read literally, and probably not what was meant; what a councillor should do with such a proposal needs a basis (government.md now says what happens).
    - *Plurality still counts round by round*: elections.md says "for one round". The winner is the same (nothing is passed on from a ballot cut to its first preference), but the result's `rounds` show several, and a tie for first goes to the candidate later in order.
    - *A member's move the world refuses is tried again only by the daily round.* In a world without `round` the member counts itself as living in the new home and never tries again.
    - *Only the bank's "failed: insufficient funds" note counts as rent missed*; a payment the world refused ("couldn't be made") doesn't, though the rent wasn't paid either.
  - **Looked for and not found:** the two copies of the rent-order re-timing (after a rent increase; after a new lease) follow one rule and, run against the bank's payment system, pay rent once every 30 days throughout with no month missed or doubled; they differ only in where the old order's stop goes when it's stopped at once (the same act as the new order, or an act of its own).
  - **Two small fixes with them.** A model seat's papers "on file" listed laws that had been withdrawn or replaced: they show only what stands now, read as a code participant's book reads them (LLM participant 0.7.1). And where a body is, is looked up: the state keeps, beside who is in each building, which buildings each body is in (`State::whereabouts`, derived, never serialised, kept by the only two ways in and out of a space), and `structures_of` reads it. This is the lookup the speed pass above left for a decision; it was asked for with the condition that no result change, and none does (the reference run's final state and log are the same). It is a second copy of state: a debug build checks it against the walk over buildings every time it's used, and the feasibility property test checks it for every body after every step.
  - **Not covered:** the leaseholder's side of a household's move on records alone (signing, moving in with the children, telling the others: the town test `orchestrator/test/households.test.ts` covers it in a run).
  - **Revisit if** a reader should know the paper by more than the name an item is published in (anyone can publish in the paper's name: closing that needs citizens to see who holds the paper's posts, or the engine to check names, which it deliberately doesn't); a world has more than one paper (`paper` is one entity); councillors are given a rule for proposals off their axis; the lookup of where bodies are is ever found out of step (then `enter` and `left_space` are not the only ways in and out any more); or a mechanism's documentation changes (its test's name says what the documentation said).
- **The translator's training requests can be written by file, with no model behind an adapter** (2026-10-06). The Codex subscription's weekly limit was reached on 2026-10-06 at 08:38 with 12 of the production set's 24 parts written (`runs/translator-data-2026-10-06/`), and it stays closed for four days. The other 12 parts are written by other models through files: a writer (a Claude subagent, or anything that reads and writes files) is handed a file of briefs and hands back a file of requests. Reading back already worked this way (the blind check); writing now mirrors it.
  - **Briefs out** (`oikos translator-data … --briefs`; `trainingData`'s `briefsOnly`): the same sources, part, seed and floors as a run with models, and no model. The folder gets `plan.json` (every item: its id, its gold program with its world's money and forms or its refusal's label and what must be copied, its style, riders and the way of saying it chosen; the seed, sources, part, options and the pipeline's version), `briefs.jsonl` (`{id, kind, brief}`, the brief being exactly the message a model is sent for that item) and `writer-instructions.txt` (the two system prompts, word for word, and the format to hand back). `--writers a,b,c` deals the items in turn (item k to writer k mod n: with eleven styles, every writer gets every style and every function) into `briefs-a.jsonl`…, and the plan says who was asked for which.
  - **Requests in** (`oikos translator-data-write DIR --requests FILE --writer NAME`, repeated per writer; `writtenData`): `{id, request}` lines against the plan. Each gets every mechanical check a model's request gets (what must be copied and its spans, the riders, `badlyWritten`, said before, wording shared with a held-out case where the machine has them), and the folder gets what a run with models writes (`examples.jsonl` with `written_by` the writer's name, `rejects.jsonl`, `blind.jsonl`, `manifest.json`), so `oikos translator-data-check` works on it unchanged. The manifest also says how many planned items had no request, how many ids were written twice (the first is kept) and how many were for nothing planned.
  - **One plan, one path.** Who writes what in which style was settled inside `generate`; it is its own function now (`planOf`), and a model run and a file run both carry a plan out through the same code (`fromPlan`): `generate` fetches each item's request from a model, `fromWritten` looks it up in what was handed in. A test holds the two to the same examples for the same texts.
  - **What changed for a run with models:** the refusals' later rounds (asked only for a label that came out short of its floor) are planned up front with the rest, so their styles and writers follow from their place in the plan and not from how many were skipped before them. The first round and every request for a program are as they were. By file, those later rounds' briefs are written out too (marked `spare`): a writer can't be asked again mid-run, so they are written ahead and used only where a label is short. That is more writing: in part 13 of the production set, 120 of 650 briefs (466 for programs, 64 first-round refusals, 120 spare). `--no-spares` leaves them out of the briefs files; they stay in the plan, and a label that then comes out short is said to be, in the manifest.
  - **What a file run doesn't do:** translate back. No model is called, so examples carry no `verified_by` until the blind check, which is the check of meaning for these parts (a reader of another family than the writer).
  - **Not changed:** the pipeline's version (`DATA_VERSION` 0.4.0): the examples a request becomes are the same. The plan records the version it was made with, and the manifest of a file run keeps it as `plan_version`.
  - **Revisit if** writers by file turn out to need asking again for what failed a check (then: a second briefs file of the rejected and missing items, which the plan's ids already allow); the spare rounds' extra writing isn't worth it and `--no-spares` should be the default; or a writer's voice is found to follow from how the items are dealt (with a number of writers that divides the number of styles, each writer would get only some styles).

- **The translator’s first training set: what is in it and how it was split** (2026-10-06, `runs/translator-data-2026-10-06/` on the run server; data, not in git).
  - **Written by:** codex/gpt-6.1-sol for parts 1 to 12; Claude Sonnet, Haiku and Opus subagents for parts 13 to 24 (by file, 176 briefs a writer a part). Each part is its own shard of the gold programs with its own seed, so no gold program is in two parts.
  - **Checked twice:** mechanically as written (exact copies, quotes, the held-out cases’ wording), then blind: a reader given only the translator’s prompt (0.7.3) translates each request, and an example is kept when the reader’s program means the same as the gold. Agreed: 6,897 of 7,144 sol-written (96.5%), 5,843 of 6,156 Claude-written (94.9%); 12,740 of 13,300 in all. The rest are set aside in each part’s `disagreed.jsonl`, not deleted.
  - **By writer (parts 13 to 15, where it was counted):** set aside by the blind check 3.6% of Opus’s, 4.7% of Sonnet’s, 9.2% of Haiku’s; Haiku also lost the most to the mechanical checks. Haiku was kept as a writer for the variety of voice: what it gets wrong is filtered, at the cost of fewer examples from it (1,596 kept, against 1,948 and 2,055).
  - **The split:** once, over all parts (`split.ts` there, the pipeline’s own `split`, 15% of each function’s gold programs, seed in the manifest): 10,856 training and 1,880 validation examples; no gold program and no request on both sides; all seven refusal labels on both. Refusals built by running several requests together are built again on each side from that side’s own examples, as the pipeline does, so those are not the blind-checked ones.
  - **Weakness, recorded:** the blind reader was Claude Sonnet throughout. For the sol half that is a reader of another family; for the Claude half it is not, and Sonnet read Sonnet’s own. Agreement there may flatter the data.
  - **A quirk seen, then fixed** (the same day, for sets made from now on; `DATA_VERSION` 0.4.1): the plan could give a refusal with no record in it the style that cites a record’s id, and a writer then invented one (two seen, both still plainly refusals). Such a brief is now written formally (`refusalStyle`), the rule a request that could take a citation and has none already followed. The first set is left as written: its plans record 0.4.0.
  - **Revisit if** a model trained on this does worse on sol-read validation than on Sonnet-read (then re-read parts 13 to 24 with gpt-6.1-sol and drop what it disagrees with); Haiku’s share turns out to carry errors the checks miss; or the held-out test shows a function or label the set is thin on.

- **The translator’s own model: begun, from nothing first** (2026-10-06; Naz: train it from scratch, since it is so small, with a tiny pretrained model as the second arm of the same pipeline if it greatly helps; the test decides).
  - **Agreed before training** (docs/design/translator-and-resolver.md §16.5 asked for this): it serves frontier models first and weaker models and AI citizens too; humans are out of scope for now. **Pass mark:** on the held-out and the real requests, confidently wrong on at most 1%, right on at least 90%, the rest asked back as unclear. Asking back often at first is acceptable; it must not become a way to learn what a seat doesn’t know, and a seat must not get stuck. Naz writes no more test cases himself.
  - **Two rules made plain in the reference prompt (0.7.4),** both already the design’s: a message’s text is copied from the request, its own words for what is asked, nothing added (the prompt’s examples had added “Please”; §16.10: a text is a span, never generated); and having or getting someone to do what is theirs to decide is asking them, a message, while making, ensuring or seeing to it that they do is `not_yours` (VISION §6: whoever has to act is asked, and they decide). They are the platform’s rules, versioned with the contracts, not a world’s config. gpt-6.1-sol on 0.7.4: seen 62/64, held out 64/66 (as before); the real requests are still to run (the Codex limit cut them off).
  - **Deciding and copying is enough for the call language.** `translator/model/encode.ts` turns a program into steps, each a choice from a closed set or a span of what the model is shown (the request, then the forms known), and back. All 12,736 checked examples come back exactly. The closed set is 111 choices; a program is 9 steps on average, 37 at most. A form’s fields are pointed at in the form as shown, never a list (first written as a list, which hid validation’s fields in the vocabulary: caught by a test).
  - **The model** (`translator/model/py`, PyTorch, pinned with uv): reads pieces (runs of letters and digits, and each other mark), each known by hashes of itself and of its letters, so there is no word list and an unseen id can still be pointed at; a small reader and writer (about 10M parameters, most of it the hash tables); each step a choice or a span’s first and last piece. On the mini’s CPU an answer takes 2 to 9 ms. Behind the usual adapter as `decide/<name>` (`translator/model/adapter.ts`), so `oikos translator-eval` scores it as it scores any model.
  - **What the first runs found** (`runs/translator-model/` on the run server): 82% of validation right by meaning, then 88% once ids and amounts were given other digits each time an example is seen (the steps point at places, so the example stays true and nothing can be learned by heart). But on the tests: seen 26/64, held out 12/66, real 12/180. **The training set does not look like what seats ask:** forms were shown only beside requests to fill one in (so the model filled one in whenever any was shown: now unrelated forms are shown beside every kind of request in training); long requests were mostly several run together (so a long real request was `too_much`); and no message was asked for indirectly (“Direct X to …”: a sixth of real requests).
  - **Next:** requests written in the seats’ own voice (translator/eval/pilot-style.md was written for this and the generator’s styles don’t follow it), indirect messages among them; real requests stay for style and the test, never training (38 of the 180 real test cases are pilot 9’s). Then grammar-constrained steps, calibration and the threshold below which it asks back.
  - **Revisit if** the from-nothing model can’t reach the pass mark with data in the seats’ voice (then the pretrained arm: Flan-T5-small, Apache-2.0, or DeBERTa-v3-xsmall, MIT; licences read 2026-10-06); or deciding and copying turns out not to cover a later version of the call language (the round trip is the test).

- **The translator's training requests in the seats' voice** (2026-10-06; `DATA_VERSION` 0.5.0; `translator/train/data.ts`, `orchestrator/src/training.ts`). The first set taught the model that a long request is several requests (real: 12 of 180), had no letter asked for indirectly, and had refusals of a sentence or two. The style source is `translator/eval/pilot-style.md`; nothing was read from the real test cases or the held-out ones, and no real request is in the generator.
  - **Two new styles, `early` and `late`,** the two voices of the pilots' seats, each a whole way of writing where the older styles are one habit each. `early`: formal, about 300 characters, a description with its id in square brackets, opening with an imperative or (half the time, given word for word as a rider) with a capacity in one of the three ways the first mayor wrote his ("As mayor", "As Mayor of …", "As mayor [mayor]"). `late`: an imperative, bare ids, the act first and whole, then two to five sentences that qualify it. Which kinds of sentence follow is drawn by seed and told to the writer (what must stay as it is; a "Do not …"; what it does not amount to; what to report if it can't be done; what to show first); the writer words them to suit the act.
  - **Why the qualifying sentences are not given word for word** (as the `do_not` style's closings are): eight fixed sentences would be the mark of a long request, and a model would learn the sentences. The cost: nothing mechanical holds a `late` request to its length or its kinds; the brief does, and the blind check reads the result. A "Do not …" among them is still recorded as a rider when the example is made (`doNots`), as before.
  - **Half of what is dealt is in these voices** (`DEAL`, the order `planOf` deals styles in when the caller names none): every other request, `late` twice as often as `early` (the later pilots' seats are the ones runs have now), the eleven older styles each in turn between them. None is removed. A program asked for twice is asked for in a seat's voice and in another. Naming styles (`styles`) still overrides it.
  - **A long single act is never a part of `too_much`.** The refusals built by running requests together (`builtRefusals`, and again on each side of a split) leave `late` requests out, of the related kind and of the pasted kind: such a request closes by ruling out anything further, so a message that went on to ask for more would contradict itself, and it is long because it is one act. Several acts in the seats' voice are written, not built: the piled briefs are dealt the styles like every brief. A smaller set therefore has fewer positives to build from (a test needed four requests a program where two did).
  - **A new mechanical check, for these two styles only** (`strayIds`, reason `stray_id`): a request that names an id its program doesn't have is dropped: whatever stands in square brackets by itself, and anything written as a record's or a law's id (`rec.412`, `law-rates`). Riders given to the request, texts the program copies whole and a form's own record id are looked past. Real seats do write "Keep ballot rec.2679 unchanged"; here it would be an id the translator must ignore with nothing to say so, and by file nothing translates a request back. It is narrow on purpose (ids of other shapes, `acc-…` written bare, are the blind check's to catch) and it is not applied to the older styles, so what they keep is as it was.
  - **Letters asked for indirectly** ("Direct / Ask / Tell / Instruct / Have / Get them to …"): of the programs that are one plain letter (`message(to=…, text=…)`, to one or several; not one that says what it is about, `re=`), a share is asked for this way (`planOf`'s `indirect`; `trainingData` asks for a half, a caller of `planOf` that names no share gets none). The brief shows the call with its text left open and tells the writer to invent what they are asked to do and to mark exactly those words with ⟦ and ⟧. The marks come off (`marked`); the marked words are the example's `text` in place of the gold program's, and its span is where they were marked (`how: copy`). The example says `indirect`, and its `gold` is still the program it was planned from, so a split keeps it with that program's other requests. Rejected with reason `marks` (and which: missing, unbalanced, empty, several, quoted; unasked for marks in any other request): the request is recorded as handed in. A space inside the marks and a full stop before the closing one are not part of the words, as the prompt's rule has it (0.7.4: the text is their own words for what is asked, nothing added). One path for a model run and a file run (`fromPlan`), held to the same examples by tests; a model translating back is shown the request without marks and held to the program with the marked words.
  - **In the late voice an indirect letter's further sentences speak of the direction itself** ("This is a direction only.", "Do not send it to any other office."), one or two of them: "Preserve …" after "Direct the works manager to …" could be read as more words for the works manager, and the text is one span. What they are asked to do is itself long there (two or three clauses), which is where real directions carry their length.
  - **Refusals are dealt the same styles** (they always took the next style in turn; the two voices are worded for a situation in `REFUSAL_STYLES`: "what is wanted" where a program's say "the act"), so a request to read or inspect, to pause, to go and see, or a vague one also comes long and qualified.
  - **`not_yours` is never worded as having or getting them to.** Two of the ten suggested wordings were "get them to …" and "have them …", which are messages since 0.7.4. They are gone, and every such brief says so: making, ensuring, seeing to it or making sure that they do; never "have them", "get them to", asking, telling, directing or instructing.
  - **Writers are told** that a style may ask for more than three sentences, that what it adds asks for nothing, and that the marks are used only where the brief says the request is asked indirectly (`writerSystem`, `writer-instructions.txt`).
  - **Not changed:** the checks that were there (none is loosened); what an older plan gives when its requests are taken in (a 0.4.x `plan.json` has neither the styles nor indirect items; the manifest says `plan_version`); the first set, left as written. The manifest gains `indirect` and two drop counts (`marks`, `stray_id`).
  - **Seen on one part** (part 1 of 48, seed 500, two requests a program, floor 12, no spares; briefs only, nothing written or trained): 330 briefs, 266 for programs (84 late, 49 early, 133 in the older styles) and 64 refusals (21 late, 11 early); 7 of its 8 plain letters fell to indirect (a half by seed; on eight draws that is luck, and a whole set evens out).
  - **Not verified:** that writers follow these briefs (no model wrote to them), that the blind reader agrees with long late requests at the rate it did with short ones, and that a model trained on the result does better on the real requests. That is the next run.
  - **Revisit if** the blind check sets aside late requests much more often than the rest (then the kinds of sentence are at fault: look at which, and whether "show … for confirmation" or "report that limitation" reads as a second act); writers ignore the count of sentences and the set's late requests come out short (then a mechanical floor on length, which there deliberately isn't yet); the reader puts an indirect letter's further sentences into its text, or readers and writers disagree on where the words asked of them begin and end (then the marks' rule is wrong, not the reader); `stray_id` drops many requests for ids that were the call's in other letters; a trained model now takes several acts in the late voice for one (then build `too_much` from late requests too, with their closings cut); or real requests show a third voice.

- **The seats' voice, after its first written part: parts weighted toward what seats ask, and requests at a seat's length** (2026-10-06; `DATA_VERSION` 0.6.0). A trial part was written to the 0.5.0 briefs (566 briefs; Sonnet, Haiku and Opus by file; read blind by Sonnet on prompt 0.7.4): 543 taken in, 508 agreed; late voice 144 of 160; indirect letters 13 of 13; all 54 mechanical rejects Haiku's (34 `stray_id`, 19 `not_copied`, 1 marks missing). The path works. Three things did not.
  - **The mix was far from what seats ask** (pilot-style.md: reading a third, a form a quarter, a direction to another office a sixth, a withdrawal a tenth, a pause or a stopped practice a tenth, going to see one in twenty). A gold set has a floor under every function, so a letter is one program in twenty: 14 indirect letters in 566 briefs. Three options now weight a part, by file and with models alike, each recorded in `plan.json` and the manifest (`only`, `indirect_share`, `refusal_labels`): `--only FN,…` writes requests for those functions' programs alone (`none`: for none, so only refusal briefs are planned); `--indirect SHARE` is the share of plain letters asked for indirectly (a half if absent); `--refusal-labels L,…` plans, builds and holds to the floor those labels alone. The mix itself is not built in: which parts are written at which weights is the caller's, since the real mix is a measurement that will move.
  - **What `--only` does not narrow:** the refusal briefs are still made from the whole gold set (or part): a payment with its amount taken out needs the payments, a place to visit needs the places. With `--only`, `--generated` and `--limit` count the named functions' programs only, so a part of letters can have many more generated letters without twenty times the gold.
  - **How many plain letters there are:** a default gold set (floor 300, the calibration town's log) has 300 message programs, of which 223 are one plain letter (220 distinct recipients; seed 500; 227 at seed 501), 8 of them from the log. That is across all parts: one part of 48 has about five. With `--only message --generated 400` (seed 600): 426 message programs, 332 plain letters, 321 distinct recipients (86 to two or three, 87 to a person or organisation by id, 78 to an office by id, 45 to an organisation by its own id (those three told apart by the shape of the id, so roughly), 18 by name in words, 18 described in words: "the works manager"). The generator already drew every kind of recipient; what was missing was a way to ask for more of one function, so nothing was added to it.
  - **Dealing some labels only.** Briefs come label by label in turn, and so do styles (every other one a seat's voice) and writers: with two labels, one label got every seat's voice and the other none (seen in the first trial of the flag). With `refusalLabels` the briefs of a round are dealt in a seeded order. With every label asked for the order is as it was, so earlier plans are unchanged.
  - **Late-voice requests came out short:** a median of 139 characters, a ninth decile of 299 (real: 400 overall, 630 late). The brief said how many sentences and showed each kind with a five-word example, and the writers wrote five-word sentences. Now the style says the lengths (the act with every particular the call has, each in a clause of its own; each further sentence twelve to thirty words; 300 to 700 characters in all, "from saying each thing in full, never from adding anything"), each kind is shown by a sentence of the length meant (written here, not a seat's: nothing of a real request is in the generator), and the writer is told those show the kind and the length, not the words. An indirect letter's own words in the late voice: two or three clauses, 20 to 45 words, all between the marks.
  - **Still no mechanical floor on length.** The manifest now counts request lengths by style (`request_length`: how many, median, ninth decile), which says at once whether a part came out long. A floor would drop requests that are right and short, mostly the weakest writer's; it is the next step if the numbers stay low.
  - **Requests to read, to pause and to visit were short too.** In the seats' voices (and only there: the other styles' refusals stay as short as people's are) a brief now says what such a request carries (`RefusalBrief.seats`): to read or inspect, the stretch of time in the world's own days and what each entry is to show, three or four things by name; to pause, what is kept so that it can be resumed and that a permanent cancellation is not wanted; to go and see, what is looked for and noted; a routine changed, what stays in place. All in general words: no place's names or figures. The routines an office carries on by itself are 24 where they were 8, since a part can now be nothing but these.
  - **Seen** (briefs only, nothing written to them): `--only message --indirect 0.9 --generated 400`, two requests a program: 852 briefs, all for letters, 664 for plain letters, 587 of those indirect (180 late, 109 early). `--only none --refusal-labels not_an_act,no_such_act --refusal-floor 60 --no-spares`: 120 briefs, 60 of each label (the three kinds of `no_such_act` 20 each), 60 in the seats' voices (35 and 25), each of those with its particulars.
  - **Not verified:** that writers now write at these lengths, or that longer requests keep the blind reader's agreement (longer sentences have more room to ask for something); that a part of nothing but letters or looks reads as varied (the same recipient kinds and the same thirty ways of looking, many times over).
  - **Revisit if** late requests still come out under 300 characters (then the mechanical floor); the longer further sentences bring more disagreements than the 16 in 160 seen (then which kind: "show … for confirmation" and "report that limitation" are the likely ones); writers copy the sentences shown, so the same eight turn up across a part (then show two of each and vary which); particulars asked of a request to read get read as a second request; or weighting by hand proves error-prone and the mix should be a named profile of parts.
- **The translator's own model, second arm: a small pretrained encoder as the reader** (2026-10-06; agreed in advance as the second arm of the same pipeline, the test deciding; `translator/model/py`).
  - **What it is.** `train.py --encoder NAME` puts a pretrained encoder (first `microsoft/deberta-v3-xsmall`, MIT; any encoder-only model with a fast tokenizer can be named) where the hash reader was. The writer, the choice head, the span heads and the loss are the ones the from-nothing arm has. 78.0M parameters: the encoder 70.7M (22M of layers, 48M its word table), fine-tuned at 5e-5; the new layers 7.4M (a writer of 3 layers, width 384, 8 heads) at 6e-4; one cycle, AdamW. Which line a token is on (the request, then each form) is an embedding added to what the encoder is given, begun at zero so training starts from the encoder as published. The from-nothing arm runs by the same flags as before; `config.json` says which reader a model has (`encoder`, `max_tokens`), and `predict.py` and the adapter serve either.
