  - **Spans stay exact.** The encoder has its own tokens, so `tokens.py` reads the input with the tokenizer's offsets and turns each span of pieces into the first and last token covering exactly its characters, and a model's span of tokens back into pieces: outside the model everything is still pieces, and `decode` rebuilds the program as before. `uv run python tokens.py <DATA>` checks a whole exported set: on data-4 all 48,532 spans and on data-5 all 57,739 are exactly a run of tokens and come back as the same pieces (none inexact). A span that isn't (a token running across its edge) would leave its example out of training, counted in the log and `report.json`; after an augmentation it leaves the example as it was. Both augmentations are kept (other digits; unrelated forms appended), each read again as tokens.
  - **Length.** data-5 in tokens: median 30, ninth decile 136, 99th percentile 273, longest 698 (training), 584 (validation); 13 and 4 over 512. In the tests the longest input was 502 tokens (the real requests: median 207, ninth decile 336). DeBERTa-v3 knows a token's place only by its distance from another, so it runs past the 512 it was trained at: the limit is 1024 for such an encoder and 512 (or its own limit) for one that counts places from the start. Beyond the limit nothing is cut: training leaves the example out (none was) and `predict.py` answers `none(unclear)`, as it does for too many pieces. **Not verified:** that it reads well between 512 and 1024 (few training examples are that long; none in the tests).
  - **A batch is taken in parts.** The first run (`pretrained-1`) put 48 inputs padded to the longest in one step; attention's cost is the square of the length, the machine's memory filled, and it stalled the other arm's run beside it (stopped by hand, no model). A batch is now one optimiser step taken in parts of at most `--token-budget` padded tokens (12,288), each part's loss weighted by its share of the batch's steps, which is the batch's loss exactly.
  - **What it scored** (`runs/translator-model/pretrained-3`, data-5, 8 epochs, 2 h 17 min of training on the mini's GPU, MPS without trouble). Validation: 2,740 of 3,024 exactly (90.6%), 2,826 by meaning (93.5%; refusals 579/581); asking back below 0.8 of sureness: 86.7% right, 12.6% asked back, 0.7% confidently wrong. The tests: seen 50/64, held out 36/66 (13 wrong drafts), **real 64/180 (34 wrong drafts)**. The from-nothing arm's latest (`scratch-5-e15`, trained on earlier data, so not like for like): 34/64, 24/66, 30/180. After two epochs the pretrained arm already had 44/64, 33/66, 60/180: six more epochs moved validation from 82.6% to 90.6% and the real requests by four. On the CPU an answer takes 32 ms at the median, 63 ms at the ninth decile (the from-nothing arm 9 and 17).
  - **With a threshold** (`OIKOS_DECIDE_THRESHOLD`): at 0.7 the real requests are 52 right, 125 asked back, 3 wrong; at 0.9, 44, 135 and 1. So the wrong drafts can be had near the 1% of the pass mark today, at the price of asking back on seven requests in ten: the sureness is worth something on real requests, and the reading is what is short.
  - **What it gets wrong on real requests** (by function, from `eval.json`; 116 of 180): a form filled in wrongly or not the form meant (16), a form taken for `too_much` or an unknown form (9, and 13 more of a form with a message), one refusal label for another (`not_an_act` answered `no_such_act` 10, `no_such_act` answered `too_much` 6), a statement refused as `no_such_act` (4, and 4 of several statements), a refusal answered with a message or an agreement (9). A better reader did not fix these: validation is at 93% and the real requests at 36%, so **the gap is still the training set against what seats ask**, as the first runs found, not the reader.
  - **Not verified:** the two arms on the same data (the from-nothing arm has no run on data-5 yet: until it has, how much of 30 to 64 is the encoder and how much the third wave is unknown); another encoder (MiniLM, Flan-T5's encoder: the name is a parameter, only DeBERTa-v3-xsmall was run; an encoder-decoder's encoder would need its own loading); other seeds (one run); other learning rates; whether the answers log's lengths for "the real requests" are exactly those 180 (a request is sometimes asked twice).
  - **Revisit if** the from-nothing arm on data-5 comes within a few requests of this one on the tests (then the encoder isn't earning its 78M parameters and 32 ms); the pretrained arm stays far ahead (then it is the arm to carry forward, and grammar-constrained steps and calibration are built on it); inputs past 512 tokens turn up in runs (then test reading at that length, or lower the limit and ask back); or a span turns up that is not a run of tokens (then count them per export, and consider reading by pieces with the tokenizer told the pieces).

- **One act that names many things: a rule reversed** (2026-10-06; `DATA_VERSION` 0.7.0). Two waves were written to the 0.6.0 briefs (3,833 examples, Sonnet and Opus; 3,685 agreed blind, 96.1%; late-voice median 462 characters; 631 indirect letters, 97% agreed). A model trained on them still answers `none(too_much)` to long real requests of one act: 20 of 55 reads, 15 of 31 pauses and visits, 21 of 30 forms, 12 of 19 letters.
  - **The likely cause is a rule this log recorded two entries up:** the sentences after an act "name no id, amount, record or person the call doesn't have", held to by `strayIds`. Real seats do the opposite (pilot-style.md: a median of four ids a request, a tenth with ten or more; "Keep replacement proposal [rec.2678] and its ballot [rec.2679] unchanged."). In our data every further id meant a further act, so the model learned that many ids are several acts. The rule was Naz's to give and is his to reverse; it kept the first sets clean and taught the wrong thing.
  - **Now, in the seats' voices, a request for an act is given other things to name by id** (`styleBrief`, `mentions`): one to four in the late voice, one or two in the early, as things the act leaves alone, does not touch or is not about, in the sentences after the act. The ids are invented in the shapes worlds' ids have (a record, an account, a person, a place, an organisation, an office, a standing instruction; usually all of one kind, as seats list them), never the call's own or part of one, and none is any place's. The writer is given them and must use each (left out: `not_copied`, like any rider).
  - **Recorded as riders that add nothing** (`kind: "mention"`, `text` the id, `at` where it stands, an entry for every place it stands), beside the capacity, the citation and the "Do not …": so whoever reads the data, or trains on spans, can tell the ids that are the act's (in `spans`) from the ids only mentioned (in `riders`). The program's values are never looked for inside one. A "Do not …" that names one is still recorded as a "Do not …" (its sentence no longer ends at the full stop inside `rec.412`: that was a fault of the sentence finder, hidden until ids stood in such sentences).
  - **`strayIds` allows exactly the ids given and still drops any other,** and it now knows more shapes than a record's and a law's (`ID_SHAPED`: accounts, people, places, organisations, offices, standing instructions, in the shapes the generator and the logs write). It was narrow while requests were told to name nothing; now that they are told to name some, a writer adding its own is likelier and would be an id nobody recorded. Still for the two voices only. It will drop a request in which a writer writes part of a longer id by itself in one of these shapes; that is a request the copy check mostly drops already.
  - **Requests to read, pause and visit name three to ten ids in the seats' voices** (`RefusalBrief.mention`; to read three to ten, to pause three to six, to visit three to five; a changed routine none): the accounts whose entries are wanted, the records to read, an instruction's payees. In those voices the request must carry them (they join what it must copy); in the other styles the brief doesn't give them.
  - **A letter asked for indirectly carries ids inside its marked words in the seats' voices** (two or three late, one or two early; places, records, people, accounts; a sum of money four times in ten where the program's world says how money is written): real directions do ("…at house-199, apts-13 and apts-15"). They are part of the letter's text, which is copied whole, so they are not riders; left outside the marks, the request is dropped (`not_copied`). Its sentences after the act still name nothing.
  - **Length:** the late voice may run to 1,000 characters where it has ids to name; 300 stays the least.
  - **What is too much stays several acts.** The built kind runs whole positives together, each its own act with its own values, as before; a part may now mention other things (an early-voice part with "[rec.2678] stays as it is" is taken into the related kind too, where any rider used to bar it), so a pile is not the only thing with many ids and one act is not the only thing that mentions. Late-voice requests stay out of built piles (their closings rule out anything further). Several acts in the seats' voices are written: a piled brief in those voices now says each act is a sentence of its own, in sequence or numbered as seats write them, and gives one to three other ids for the sentences about the whole; and `--refusal-labels too_much` plans as many piles as the floor (a third otherwise), since a part of refusals alone has no positives to build from.
  - **Seen** (briefs only): part 1 of 48, seed 500: 330 briefs; of 133 requests in a seat's voice 130 are given ids to mention (late: 18 with one, 31 two, 24 three, 9 four; early: 30 one, 18 two) and the other 3 are letters asked indirectly with ids inside; no other style has any. `--only message --indirect 0.9 --generated 400`: 587 indirect, 289 of them in a seat's voice and all of those with ids inside (60 one, 132 two, 97 three; 136 with a sum). `--only none --refusal-labels not_an_act,no_such_act,too_much --refusal-floor 60`: 180 briefs, 60 a label; in a seat's voice 31 to read (three to nine ids each), 30 to pause, visit or change a routine (21 with three to six, the 9 routines none), 29 piles (one to three).
  - **Not verified:** that writers name the given ids as things left alone and not as things to act on (a sentence like "pay acc-c0351 nothing" is fine; "also notify c1501" is a second act the brief forbids and nothing mechanical catches); that the blind reader still reads one act (it has never seen requests with ids that aren't the act's); that invented ids of one kind read as plausible lists; and the point of it all, that a model trained on this stops taking many ids for several acts.
  - **Revisit if** the blind check sets aside many more of these than the 4% before (then look first at whether the reader took a mentioned id for an argument: `basis`, `re`, a second recipient); a trained model now takes a mentioned id for the act's (then fewer, or only of kinds the call has none of); real piles are still read as one act or real single acts as piles (then the contrast wants late-voice piles built as well as written); `stray_id` drops much of a good writer's work (then its shapes are too wide); or seats' requests turn out to name amounts and people in passing too, which these still don't.

- **Translator training requests written in situ, and labelled by two readers** (2026-10-06; `orchestrator/src/in-situ.ts`, `translator/train/readers.ts`; commands `oikos in-situ`, `in-situ-requests`, `in-situ-blind`, `in-situ-label`). Both arms of the translator's own model agree with the generated validation data (about 93%) and get 64 of 180 real pilot requests: a request written from a brief does not read like what a seat in its situation asks. Naz has allowed the pilots' real requests and the seats' real situations to be used for training; the 180 real test cases (`translator/eval/pilot-cases.ts`) and the held-out file stay out of it.
  - **A situation is one time a model seat was asked what to do,** read back from a run's log: the seat's system prompt as sent, and what it was shown, as text: the sitting so far (what it was first shown; then, turn by turn, "What you did then" and "What came back", as the transcripts carry them). Beside it: the forms the seat knew of then (every form among the papers it had been shown, less what was withdrawn, each as the translator is shown one; where a log kept no observations of a seat, the forms the translator was shown with its nearest request), how the world writes money, and the requests the seat really made in that turn through `do`, each with its draft. The log is read once, a line at a time (`corpus.ts` gives its reader for that: `corpusReader`).
  - **A writer is put in the seat and told the truth:** no world is running, the situations are from runs that are over, nothing it answers takes effect, and it may answer nothing (VISION §15: no cover story is needed, so none is used). It is given the seat's ways of acting in the words seats are given (`tools()`), and hands back only what it would ask through `do`, in its own voice: none, one or several requests a situation. No requests is a good answer.
  - **The test cases are kept out three ways.** A situation is marked `test` if a test case was asked in it (matched by run, seat and draft, and by day or by its words: pilot 9 was stopped and resumed, and one case's draft is on another day in the log than the case says), if it follows one in its sitting (it is shown it), if what it shows quotes the beginning of one (a seat is told what wasn't done by its first 80 characters), or if its seat asked something that begins as a test case of any run does (the pilots start from the same town, and their seats ask alike: a check of the first chunks found a pilot-7c situation showing the first 60 characters of another run's test case). No turn of a seat's day with a marked turn in it goes to a writer. And a request, real or written, in a test case's exact words or beginning as one does (60 characters) is never taken in.
  - **A writer is given one seat's day in three, one turn of it** (`--every`; neighbouring days of a run are nearly the same situation), spread evenly through each run, a different third for each writer; of a day's turns the one where the seat itself asked for something, where there is one, else any (seeded). Chunks hold at most 120,000 characters of prompt and situation (`--max-chars`), the turns where the seat asked first, so whoever hands chunks out can stop early.
  - **A request with no program is labelled by two readers** who see the request and the forms and nothing else (the blind file generated data uses). It is an example only where (1) both answers parse and pass the contract; (2) they mean the same (`sameProgram`), with two leniencies the checks by hand had been granting: a list with bare items is that list, and copied text naming an id in square brackets is the same text with or without them; (3) every value of the program is in the request (`needed`, `spansIn`), so it has spans as generated examples do, and its steps rebuild it (`encode`, `decode`, and the pieces of `export.ts`). The program kept is the first reader's if it passes (3), else the second's, in gold's one spelling (`goldText`); a form's office that the request doesn't name is left out where the forms known send it to one office (the resolver fills it; `meaning.ts` reads the two as the same). Dropped requests keep both answers and a reason: `unanswered`, `not_a_program`, `contract`, `disagreed`, `reworded_text` (a letter's or a form's text that isn't the request's words: prompt 0.7.4 says copy), `not_copied`, `not_encodable`.
  - **Seen** (the eight pilots 7a to 9, read only; files in `runs/translator-data-in-situ/` on the run server): 2,940 situations, 373 marked test, 2,567 usable, none without its forms or its system prompt, no sitting broken; all 120 test cases of these runs found among the seats' requests. System prompts 4,560 to 47,016 characters (median 17,827), what was shown 2,708 to 289,989 (median 26,586). Of 267 requests the seats made, 120 are test cases, 1 more is in a test case's exact words, 7 begin as one does: 139 real requests for readers (104 of pilot 9's). Writers `sonnet` and `opus`: 249 situations in 111 chunks and 252 in 119; 204 situations are larger than a chunk and go to no one. The label step run with no model, on pilot 9's 82 drafted non-test requests with the pilot translator's own call given as both readers: 62 pass (3), 15 fail as reworded text, 4 as not copied (`formal=true`, `retire=true`, a `to=city` nobody said), 1 not encodable.
  - **Not verified:** that writers ask through `do` at all in most situations (the seats did in 267 of 2,940 turns, and a chunk is about 30,000 tokens to read: the first chunks, where the seat asked, are the ones to try first); that what they ask reads like the seats' requests (the point of it: only a model trained on it and scored on the 180 can say); that two readers agree often on requests this long; and the leniencies on anything but hand-made cases. The seats' `do` is described to writers as it is today ("not for reading or asking"), which the pilots' seats were not yet told: writers will ask to read less often than the test set does.
  - **Revisit if** writers hand back almost nothing (then give them only turns where a seat asked, or more than a third of the days); (3) drops much of what two readers agree on (then look at what `needed` asks to be in the request: a flag such as `formal=true` is implied by the wording and is not in `IMPLIED`); a trained model scores on the 180 as before (then the gap is not voice); or a second world's logs are used (the marking by a test case's beginning assumes runs that begin alike).
  - A fault met on the way, not fixed here: a file written with `Bun.file(path).writer()` over a longer one keeps the longer one's end (in-situ.ts empties the file first). `dataset.ts` writes that way too; its folders have so far been new each time.

- **What data from real situations did for the translator’s model** (2026-10-06; `runs/translator-data-in-situ/`, `runs/translator-model/` on the run server).
  - **The rule changed** (Naz: “we can absolutely use real pilot tests in training”): real pilot requests and seats’ real situations may train the model. The 180 real test cases and every seat-day they come from stay out, as does the held-out file. CLAUDE.md says so now.
  - **Why it was needed.** On the same written set (three waves in the seats’ voice), the from-nothing arm got 30 of 180 real requests and the pretrained arm 64: the encoder doubled it, and neither was near the mark. Both fitted the written data (93% of validation by meaning for the pretrained arm): requests written from briefs do not look like what a seat in its situation asks.
  - **What was made.** 139 real requests that are not test cases, of which 92 were kept; and requests written in situ by Claude Sonnet and Opus seats (the seat’s own prompt and what it was shown, no world running), 1,402 written from 198 situations, of which 1,119 were kept. Kept means two readers of different models (Sonnet, Opus), each given only the translator’s prompt, wrote programs that mean the same, and every value is found in the request (the example carries spans as generated ones do). 1,211 examples in all: reads 478, messages 476, forms 96, pauses and visits 78, statements 51, little else.
  - **A departure from the seat’s own frequency, on purpose:** a seat acts in about one turn in eleven and a situation is 45,000 characters to read, so writers were asked for up to eight different grounded requests a situation (`writer-addendum.txt` there), reads among them. The requests are a real seat’s in voice and content; their mix is not.
  - **What it did** (pretrained arm, the written set plus the first 680, each four times; `pretrained-4`): real requests 64 → 132 of 180, held out 36 → 41 of 66, seen 50 → 52 of 64. Wrong drafts on real requests 26 of 180 (14%): the mark allows 1%.
  - **Known limits.** The situations are from the same pilots as the real test: the test’s own seat-days are excluded, but neighbouring days share the town and its topics, so 132 may flatter (a pilot the model has seen no part of is the honest check). Requests asking for several things are nearly absent from what was kept (two readers seldom write the same several-line program), and forms are thin. Labels are two readers’ agreement, not correct by construction.
  - **Revisit if** a fresh pilot scores well below the real test (then the gain was familiarity); more in-situ data stops moving the tests (then: forms and several-act requests need their own source, or a third reader to settle disagreements instead of dropping them); or gpt-6.1-sol in the seat becomes affordable (its voice is the test’s own).

- **Written briefs for the two things the trained model still gets wrong: forms of several amounts, and a second act that is a direction** (2026-10-06; `DATA_VERSION` 0.8.0). With data from seats' real situations the pretrained model has 129 to 132 of 180 real requests. What is left is mostly two kinds, and both are ones a written brief gets right by construction where two readers of a real request rarely agree.
  - **Forms of several amounts** (13 of 30 real form requests come back with the right form and its amounts under the wrong fields, or left in the proposal's words). `synthetic.ts amountForms` invents forms with three to seven fields of type amount (named for ordinary things money is spent on, with or without an ending such as `_line`: no place's budget lines), one or two choices and one long text, in sets of two, and fills each in four ways: every amount a different one, written the one way its world writes money; in about three of ten only some of the amounts (always two or more); a text of two to five sentences that, half the time, says some of the form's amounts again in prose, sometimes what they come to, sometimes a figure that is no field's ("Last time the whole came to …"). They carry `tag: "amount_form"` and are not held to `askable` (a text of up to some 400 characters, a dozen values: that is what a seat's form request is), only to the contract and `implausible`.
  - **How they are asked for** (`amountNotes`, for any form filled in with two or more amounts, a real world's too): the writer is given one of four ways seats say them, by seed, shown with the call's own values (label then amount; label, amount and a few words of the writer's own for what it is for, which fill nothing; one list with the form's choices in it; a sentence); told to give the long text whole after its label; told, where the text says amounts again, to say each against its label all the same, and that an amount standing only in the text fills nothing; and told to say nothing of the amounts left empty.
  - **Where the field's amount is.** An amount said against its label and again inside the copied text stood in the request twice, and `spansIn` marked both as possible (one call alone: nothing settled it). Now an occurrence inside another, longer value that has been placed is not this value's where the value also stands outside it: the field's span is the one against the label, the text's span is the whole text, nothing is ambiguous. This holds for every program, not only forms (a payee named again inside a payment's purpose is placed outside it too). And the other side: a form's amount that stands ONLY inside another value is under no label, so nothing in the request says which field it is; such a request is dropped (`onlyInsideAnother`, counted as `not_copied`), for forms only. Until now it was kept with its span pointing into the text.
  - **Two acts that are one request** (`pairOf`; 13 real requests want a form and a letter, others a withdrawal and a letter or two withdrawals, and the model writes the first act and stops). Made from a gold program of one act: a form handed in and a direction to someone about it; something withdrawn and a direction about it; two things withdrawn. The program has two lines. The direction is a letter asked for indirectly, as single ones are: the writer invents what is asked and marks it, the marks come off, and the second line's `text` is the marked words with their span (`withText` now changes the letter's line only: it used to give every line the text, which a single letter never showed). Who it goes to is invented: an office by id (with what it is called, for the writer), a person or an organisation by id, or an office in words. The letter says what it is about (`re=`) only for a withdrawal and only three times in ten, and then the brief has the request name the id again where they are directed.
  - **These are not too much, by the prompt's own line**, which I read before generating against it: `too_much` excepts "doing something together with writing to someone about that same thing (hand in a proposal and ask the clerk to put it to a ballot; file an answer and send it to the other side)" and "the same kind of thing done several times"; and of forms, what becomes of one once handed in "adds no line, unless the request asks for someone it names to be told". So the direction must be about the same thing and must name who is directed: the brief says both. In the late voice the further sentences are about the act and come before the direction, which is the request's last sentence; that an act with qualifiers and then a direction is still one request is my reading of the prompt (it says qualifiers "add no line" and are "no reason to refuse"), not something it states of the two together.
  - **Nothing builds or labels a pair as too much:** a pair is no part of a built pile of either kind (the related kind never took programs of two lines; the pasted kind now leaves them out), on either side of a split. `implausible` still calls two lines that don't depend on each other implausible for programs read from logs or drawn by the generator; pairs are made after it and are not put through it. That rule is narrower than the prompt's and was left alone: changing it changes which log programs are gold.
  - **Asking for them:** `--amount-forms N` adds N such forms to the gold set (four programs each); `--only amount_form` writes requests for those alone (`only` now takes a program's tag as well as its function). `--pairs SHARE` asks for that share of the forms filled in and of the withdrawals as pairs; `--pairs-only` writes requests for the pairs alone. All in `plan.json` and the manifest (`amount_forms`, `pairs_share`, `pairs_only`; the manifest also counts `pairs` by kind, and each example says its `pair`). None is on unless asked: a part written without them is as before.
  - **Seen** (briefs only): `--only amount_form --amount-forms 40`, two requests a program: 320 briefs for 160 programs of 40 forms; the text says amounts again in 142, with what they come to in 68 and a figure that is no field's in 32; some amounts left empty in 92; half in the seats' voices. `--only form,statement --pairs 1 --pairs-only --generated 300`: 732 briefs: 630 a form and a direction, 57 a withdrawal and a direction (20 saying what it is about), 45 two withdrawals; the direction to an office by id in 322, a person or organisation by id in 204, an office in words in 161.
  - **Not verified:** that writers say each amount against its label when the text already says it (the check drops a request that doesn't, so the cost is yield, not wrong data); that a reader agrees on where a long unquoted text ends when further sentences follow it (the mechanical span is right by construction, since the text is the gold's own; a blind reader may still stop early or run on); that readers take the further sentences of a pair for the act's and not for words to whoever is directed; and that a model trained on these stops giving one field's amount to another or stopping after the first act.
  - **Revisit if** the blind check sets aside many amount forms for the text's end (then quote the text, which seats mostly don't); the model learns that the amount in the text is never the field's and real seats turn out to say an amount only there (the in-situ data would show it); pairs make the model add a letter where a request only says what will become of a form ("it then goes to ballot", nobody named: no line by the prompt; such requests are not generated yet and may need to be, as the counter-case); or a pair with a late voice's qualifiers is ruled too much after all (then the prompt wants a sentence on it, and these a re-read).

- **Forms and pairs, after their first written parts: equal amounts, the amount's end, and the form that names nobody** (2026-10-07; `DATA_VERSION` 0.9.0). With the 0.8.0 parts the pretrained model went from 129 to 139 of 180 real requests (a form and a letter 6 to 9 of 13; forms 17 to 19 of 30; blind readers agreed with 99% of both parts). What it still gets wrong on real forms (11 of 30 with fields wrong) is three things the generated forms never showed it.
  - **Two fields with the same amount.** Real requests often have two lines alike ("services 100,000; parks 100,000"); I had made every amount in a form different, so the model had never seen it. Now in a third of the forms filled in two or three fields have the same amount, and in about one in eight a line is nothing at all (`synthetic.ts amountForms`).
  - **Whose a shared amount is: the label nearest before it** (`labelBefore`, in `spansIn`): of all the form's labels, the one that stands last before the amount. A field's span is the place where its value stands straight after its own label, if one place alone does; this is tried before the older rule (nearest its own call's other values), which says nothing within one form. Every way of saying amounts that is offered for such a form puts the label first (the label then the amount; the same with a purpose after it; one list). The fourth way, the amount and then its label ("… 100,000 under services and 100,000 under parks"), is undecidable by that rule for two equal amounts, so it is not offered for a form that has them; it stays for the rest.
  - **And a request that doesn't say it so is dropped** (`sharedAmountsPlaced`, counted as `not_copied`; amounts only, forms only): a shared amount said once for both ("100,000 each for services and parks"), or with its label after it, leaves whose it is to the reader. The brief says so in as many words. Seats may well write "each"; these examples don't teach it, on purpose: a model that copies and points has one span to point at, and which fields it stands for is then a guess.
  - **Where an amount ends.** Real: "staff 470,000 for the works crew. Total 670,000 from the town account", and the model copied through the purpose and into the total. So the way that puts a purpose straight after the amount, with no mark between, is now offered twice as often as each other way (two in five where the label-after way is also offered, a half where it isn't), and a third of the requests for a form of several amounts are given a sentence to put after the last amount, outside the long text: what the amounts come to ("Total …", "In all …", four makes), computed here, given word for word, recorded as a rider that adds nothing (`total`). No field's span runs into either: a span is the amount's own characters, as it always was; what is new is that the request has something straight after them.
  - **The form that closes with what becomes of it, nobody named** (`what_next`): "It then goes to the councillors' ballot.", "Put it to a vote at the next meeting." By the prompt this "adds no line, unless the request asks for someone it names to be told", and the pairs alone would teach that any closing sentence about what happens next is a letter. Of the forms that would have been asked for with a direction, a fifth are asked for alone with such a sentence instead (a quarter as many as those with a direction), in the same voices; the sentence is one of eighty makes (eight openings, ten bodies it goes to: a council, a board, a committee, members; no place's), given word for word as the request's last, and recorded as a rider. It follows `--pairs`; there is no flag of its own, and `--pairs-only` keeps both. Only forms: the prompt's sentence is about forms.
  - **Seen** (briefs only): `--only amount_form --amount-forms 40`: 320 briefs; 102 with two or three fields alike (none of them offered the label-after way), 38 with a line of nothing, 120 with a total to say after the amounts; the purpose-after-the-amount way in 121, the label then the amount in 76, one list in 76, the amount then its label in 47. `--only form,statement --pairs 1 --pairs-only --generated 300`: 732 briefs: 494 a form and a direction, 136 the form alone with what becomes of it (61 different sentences; 42 late, 26 early, 68 other styles), 57 a withdrawal and a direction, 45 two withdrawals.
  - **Not verified:** that writers put each shared amount after its own label (those that don't are dropped: yield, not wrong data); that a label word turning up in a purpose ("… for cleaning the hall" where cleaning is another field) doesn't misplace a span often enough to matter (it would have to stand between a label and its own amount to do harm, and then the request is dropped or the span is still at an occurrence of the right value); that a blind reader writes one line for the closing sentence (the prompt says so; readers have agreed with the prompt on pairs); and that the model's three faults go.
  - **Revisit if** real requests are found to say a shared amount once for several fields often (then that needs a way to be written down, which the call language's copying doesn't have: a question for the contract, not the generator); the model starts reading every sentence after the amounts as no field's and drops a real last amount; the blind check sets aside the closing sentences as letters to "whoever takes it in" (then the prompt's rule is unclear, not the data); or eighty closing sentences turn out few enough to be learned by heart.

- **A training run that is cut short goes on, and is not scored as if it had ended** (2026-10-07; `translator/model/py/train.py`). The first run on the second-round parts (data-9: data-8 plus parts a2 and p2, whose blind readers agreed with 780 of 784 and 851 of 863; the 16 others are set aside) ran out of the graphics device's memory in its fourth epoch of four, 80 minutes in. The script then scored what was on disk, the model kept after epoch 2, and reported 123 of 180 real requests as the run's result. It is not one; that folder is kept as `pretrained-7-e2` with a note.
  - **The memory:** batches of many shapes leave the graphics device holding memory it no longer needs (the error counted 66 GiB of it outside the pool, on a 64 GiB machine with nothing else large running). The trainer now gives it back every 25 batches (`torch.mps.empty_cache()`; on that device only).
  - **Going on:** after each whole epoch the trainer keeps the weights, the optimiser, the schedule and the draws (`checkpoint.pt`, written beside and moved into place; removed when the run ends). `--resume` goes on from it if it is there and starts as usual if not. `--stop-after N` ends after epoch N with the checkpoint kept (for trying this). A run of three epochs stopped after the first and resumed gave losses 4.371 and 4.038 against 4.367 and 4.044 uninterrupted: close, not the same, because the graphics device's own draws (dropout) are not among those kept.
  - **The script** (`next3.sh`, in the runs folder): train with `--resume`, again up to four times if cut short, and score only if the run's `report.json` exists.
  - **Not verified:** that giving memory back is enough for four epochs of this set (the rerun will show; if it is cut short it now goes on); what the pause costs in speed (not measured; it is one call in 25 batches).
  - **Revisit if** runs are still cut short (then the cause is elsewhere: batch parts too large for a long input, and `--token-budget` is the knob); or exact repeatability across a resume comes to matter (then draw the dropout from a generator that can be kept, or train on the processor).

- **The second round of generated forms and pairs moved nothing; requests written by the seats' own model in real situations** (2026-10-07; data-9, data-10; no code changed).
  - **The result.** `pretrained-7` (data-9: data-8 plus parts a2 and p2, four epochs, uninterrupted): validation 2,850 of 3,255; seen 52 of 64, held out 38 of 66, real 136 of 180 (21 no draft, 23 wrong). `pretrained-6` on data-8 had 51, 43 and 139. Case by case on the real requests: 127 right in both, 12 only in the older, 9 only in the newer. So two runs differ by about ten real cases either way from training alone, a difference of three says nothing, and 1,600 more generated examples aimed at the model's faults did not move it. (No seed was varied to measure this spread properly: it is read off two runs on slightly different data.)
  - **What this changes.** More generated data of the kinds we have is no longer where the score is. Tried next, one thing at a time on the same data or the same reader: a larger reader (`pretrained-8`: `microsoft/deberta-v3-small`, MIT, on data-9), and requests in the voice the translator will actually read.
  - **Requests by `gpt-6.1-sol`, in situ** (Naz, 2026-10-07: use Codex to generate data, `gpt-6.1-sol`, in one session if that is cheapest). The pilots' seats are that model, and every written request so far was Claude's. It was given the unwritten chunks of situations with the writers' instructions as they stand (`runs/translator-data-in-situ/sol/`: sixteen chunks a session, one chunk at a time, each answer file written before the next; `from.tsv` says which chunk each was). One session cannot hold them all (the 142 unwritten chunks are 13.6 MB), and a chunk is new text whatever the session, so one long session saves little. Four sessions and part of a fifth before the usage window closed: 78 chunks, 169 situations, 976 requests, 0.23 to 0.45 million tokens a session. 64 chunks are still unwritten.
  - **Labelled as the others were** (`batch-6`): two blind readers (Sonnet, Opus; ten parts each; the translator's prompt 0.7.4), an example only where both read the same program. 670 of 975 (69%; the Claude-written batch before it 82%): 283 disagreed, 14 not copied, 7 not encodable, 1 reworded. The disagreements are mostly where a letter's words end, and one letter against two against too much. By function: 358 messages, 238 not an act (asking to be shown something), 57 no such act, 10 needs calculation, 3 forms, 3 statements. No example is from a seat's day with a marked test situation (checked against `situations.jsonl`); some are from the day after one.
  - **data-10** is data-9 plus these, the real-situation examples four times as before: 26,883 training examples. `pretrained-9` trains on it with the reader `pretrained-7` had.
  - **Not verified:** that sol's voice is what the real test wants (the 670 are nearly all letters and requests to be shown: they add nothing on forms and statements, where most of the remaining real failures are); that two readers agreeing less often on sol's requests is the requests' doing and not the readers' (the rejects are kept, with both answers); the spread between runs.
  - **Revisit if** `pretrained-9` is no better on real requests (then the voice is not the gap, and what is left is the kinds of request: forms and statements in situ, which a writer must be asked for pointedly); the larger reader is clearly better (then the reader's size is the limit and the next size is the question, with its cost in time for each answer); or the 30% the readers disagree on turn out to be the hard cases a seat really writes (then they want a third reader or a ruling, not dropping).

- **The reader's size is what moved the score; a call's arguments are now written in the order the request says them** (2026-10-07; `translator/model/encode.ts`, data-11).
  - **Results.** `pretrained-8` (data-9, reader `microsoft/deberta-v3-small`, MIT; the same data and settings as `pretrained-7`): validation 2,933 of 3,255; seen 50 of 64, held out 49 of 66, real 144 of 180 (17 no draft, 19 wrong). Against 52, 38 and 136 with the smaller reader: held out is up by more than two runs have differed by. An answer on the processor: median 57 ms, nine in ten within 101 ms (the smaller reader beside it 26 and 42; both timed while another run trained). `pretrained-9` (data-10: sol's first 670, the smaller reader): seen 50, held out 38, real 141 (14 no draft, 25 wrong): sol's first round did nothing that can be told from the spread.
  - **What the larger reader still gets wrong on real requests** (36): 17 want a form, and in 13 of those it writes a form with fields wrong. Field by field: in eight the amounts missing are `services`, `staff` or `roads`, never `environment` or `parks`; the form's long text is `proposal`. The steps were written with arguments by name, so every missing field is one that came after the long text. After copying several hundred characters the writer closes the call. (Also there: a form's name run on into the next words, three times; an amount's span run on into the rest of the request, in one; a direction to the clerk put in as the proposal's text, four times.)
  - **The change:** `encode` writes a call's arguments in the order the request says them: first what the request doesn't spell (implied values, names bound earlier), by name; then each argument by where its value first stands. The writer goes through the request once, and closing the call follows the last thing said. `decode` never depended on the order (the program's text sorts), so models trained either way answer through the same adapter; `OIKOS_ENCODE_ORDER=name` exports the old order. Tested: the round trip over the checked examples still holds, and the order itself (`model-encode.test.ts`).
  - **Sol's second round** (`batch-7`; the task asked, where a situation gives ground, for two or three forms or formal statements): 159 situations, 999 requests, 604 examples (331 disagreed, 44 not copied, 20 not encodable): 204 statements, 197 not an act, 172 messages, 23 no such act, and 3 forms. Asked pointedly, sol makes statements; it almost never fills in a form in these situations, so forms stay the generator's.
  - **data-11** is data-10 plus those, in the new order: 29,299 training examples. `pretrained-11` trains on it with the larger reader; `pretrained-10` (data-10, larger reader, old order) after it.
  - **Not verified:** that the order is the cause and not only where the faults show (`pretrained-11` differs from `pretrained-8` by the order and by 1,274 examples: if the missing fields go it is the order, since those examples have six forms among them; the cleaner comparison, data-10 exported both ways, is not run); that request order is harder nowhere (a value said twice is placed at its span, which the pipeline chose; a nested call is placed by its first value).
  - **Revisit if** fields are still dropped after the long text (then it is the length of the copy and not the order: the writer may need the text's end marked, or the long text last by rule); or request order costs elsewhere (seen or held out fall with it).

- **Seats on a Claude plan, through Claude Code** (2026-10-07; Naz: "we might as well experiment with it now … get this setup so that it can work, cache etc."; he is on Max 5x, $100 of Agent SDK credit a month by Anthropic's page). A provider `claude-code/<model>` (`participants/src/llm/claude-code.ts`), built as the Codex one is: one `claude -p` a call. docs/real-models.md has how it runs and the caveats.
  - **Why it is worth having:** Codex's window is what holds pilots up, not their size (a simulated year with three seats is 250 calls, 4.8 million tokens in: $12.59 at Sonnet's uncached price, from `runs/pilot-8/cost.txt`). A second way in removes that, gives a second seat model, and Claude Code replaces its whole system prompt with ours, which Codex can't.
  - **It is priced, not free:** the credit is dollars at API rates, so a run needs `--budget`. The recorded cost of a call is what Claude Code reports where that is more than the tokens come to at the price on file (`Usage.reported_cost_microusd`, an adapter's to give; the log's `cost_microusd` is unchanged in shape). The smoke run's five calls: $0.24 reported, $0.09 by tokens.
  - **Caching** is Claude Code's own (an hour). The tools and the answer's shape are put with the system prompt so that all of what repeats is one cached prefix: in the smoke run the mayor's fourth call read 17,221 tokens from the cache and wrote none.
  - **The answer's shape is asked for, not forced** (`--json-schema` costs two to four model turns an answer).
  - **Seen:** `runs/pilot-10a-claude-code-smoke`: the 1,000-person town, mayor `claude-code/claude-sonnet-5-5`, three simulated days, 70 seconds, four seat calls and the debrief, no errors, no request made through `do` (so the translator was not called).
  - **Not verified:** what the account is really charged (the usage page is the only truth: about $0.56 by Claude Code's count for everything run today); why the reported cost is 2.7 times the tokens'; what is said when the credit runs out (the limit's wording is guessed from Claude Code's other messages, `CLAUDE_CODE_LIMITED`: until one is seen, a spent credit may show as an ordinary failure, retried three times and then recorded as a failed call); a seat that asks through `do`; several seats at once; a long run.
  - **Not changed:** CLAUDE.md's line that the driver seat is `gpt-6.1-sol`. That is Naz's to change: this adds a way to run a seat, it does not choose the seat.
  - **Revisit if** the usage page shows more drawn than Claude Code reports (then the limit must be set from the page, by hand); a low effort is what makes it affordable (then the effort is a variable of the study and every comparison must name it); or Anthropic's terms for `claude -p` on a plan change again (they changed three times in 2026 by the reporting).

- **Request order did what it was for and cost more than it gave; a third order is being tried** (2026-10-07; `encode.ts` `ORDER`, data-11 and data-12).
  - **Result.** `pretrained-11` (data-11, request order, the larger reader): validation 3,146 of 3,255 (the same reader on data-9 by name: 2,933); seen 49 of 64, held out 46 of 66, real 134 of 180 (11 no draft, 35 wrong). `pretrained-8` by name: 50, 49, 144 (19 wrong). On the real requests 124 are right in both, 20 only by name, 10 only in request order.
  - **What it fixed:** the amounts after the long text. Of the eight forms that dropped `services`, `staff` or `roads`, five now have every amount.
  - **What it broke.** (1) The same forms now leave `proposal` out altogether: in those requests no words are marked as the proposal's text, and by name the writer always came to `proposal` in its turn and copied something, which the test accepts (`proposal="…"`). In request order nothing says it is owed. (2) Two acts run into one call: `form(…, to=council-clerk, text="put it to the ballot")` where a form and a letter were wanted, nine times: the letter's arguments follow the form's in the request, so they are written on without closing the call. (3) Arguments the request doesn't spell near the others (`affects`, `from`) left out. So by name, the writer knows what comes next; in request order it has to tell from the words, which it does in validation (generated requests say everything in a pattern) and not in what seats write.
  - **A third order, `text_last`:** by name, with copied texts of 80 characters or more after everything else. What comes next is still known by its name, and no argument waits behind a long copy. Exported as data-12 (the examples of data-11); `pretrained-12` trains on it with the larger reader after `pretrained-10` (data-10, by name).
  - **The default is still request order in the code and should not stay so on this evidence:** it is set when `pretrained-10` and `pretrained-12` are in, to whichever of the three is best on held out and real together. Each data folder now has a NOTE saying its order.
  - **Not verified:** how much of the fall is the order and how much the 604 statements added with it (`pretrained-10` against `pretrained-11` differs by both; `pretrained-12` against `pretrained-11` by the order alone); the spread between runs.
  - **Revisit if** `text_last` also loses `proposal` where no text is marked (then what a form requires has to come from the form as shown, and the writer has to be held to it when answering: a check on the answer, not an order); or validation keeps rising while the tests fall (then validation, all generated, has stopped telling us anything and a part of the real-situation examples should be held out of training to stand in for it).

- **A second validation set, of requests written in real situations** (2026-10-07; `translator/model/export.ts`, `py/train.py`). Validation was all generated requests, and rose from 2,933 to 3,146 while the real test fell from 144 to 134: it no longer says which model is better. Where a data folder has `validation-real.jsonl` the export carries it (adding nothing to the closed set of choices: an example that makes a choice training never did is left out and counted) and the trainer scores it beside the first (`real_right`, `real_of` in each validation line; its answers in the run's folder). Which epoch is kept is still chosen on the first.
  - **The set** (`runs/translator-data-in-situ/joined-7`): of the 2,485 examples from real situations, those of one seat-day in ten (by a hash of run, seat and day, so the same every time; whole days, since a day's requests are about the same things): 228 examples of 59 seat-days kept out, 2,257 in training (four times, as before).
  - **The export now writes `export.json`** beside the steps: the order the arguments were written in, where the examples came from, and the counts.
  - **Not verified:** that it tracks the tests (the first run with it will show: it is the reason for it); that 228 is enough to tell two models apart (a difference under about 15 is within what two runs differ by on the 180 real cases).
  - **Revisit if** it rises and falls with the generated set and not with the tests (then situations of the same pilots are too like training, and whole pilots must be kept out instead of days).

- **Pilot 10, on Claude seats; and a form that can't be one of the forms shown is not offered** (2026-10-07; `translator/model/adapter.ts` `formsMisfit`).
  - **Pilot 10** (Naz: "if you want to run a pilot using claude, by all means go for it"; `runs/pilot-10`, its `start.sh` and commit beside it): the 1,000-person town generated from seed 2 (every earlier pilot's is seed 1, so its people, lots and records are new), the mayor and the planning and finance offices on `claude-code/claude-sonnet-5-5`, the translator on the same, a spending limit of 10. It stopped at the limit on day 56, to be resumed: 126 calls (mayor 52, finance 44, planning 22, translator 5, three debriefs), none failed, three seats at once without trouble. By Claude Code's count 9.53, by tokens at the published price 3.56: about 0.075 a call, some 60 a simulated year at that pace, not the 6 to 12 I told Naz from the smoke run (the first calls of a run are its cheapest: the notebook and what is shown grow). Claude Code also caches what a seat is shown each turn, which is never read again, at the cache's higher price, and that can't be turned off from the command line.
  - **Five requests through `do` in 56 days.** These seats write letters and look things up; a pilot is a dear way to get translator requests (about 2 each here), and a good way to get situations.
  - **Our best model on those five** (`pretrained-8`, replayed from the log's transcripts): a payment right; a law proposal with its text right; a law proposal forwarding a petition addressed to the petitioner (wrong); an amendment form given a form's name that doesn't exist (wrong); a budget form of five amounts asked back. Too few to score; the same faults as the tests show.
  - **The check.** The forms a person knows of are shown with their fields, which are required and which take one of a few values. A program whose form is none of them by name is answered `none(unknown_form)` (the call language's own refusal for that); one that fills in a field not the form's own, leaves a required field out, or gives a choice field something it can't be is answered `none(unclear)`. Only what the forms' lines say is used, so a draft that fails could never have been handed in. `OIKOS_DECIDE_UNCHECKED=1` turns it off.
  - **Measured** (the same two models scored with and without): real wrong drafts 19 to 16 (`pretrained-8`) and 17 to 14 (`pretrained-10`), right answers unchanged at 144; seen and held out unchanged. (A first version answered `unclear` for an unknown form and lost a seen case that wants `unknown_form`.)
  - **Not verified:** that asking back on a required field left out is better for a seat than a draft it could decline (a seat sees the draft before it takes effect either way; asked back, it has to say it again); that every world's forms are shown in this shape (the check passes what it can't read).
  - **Revisit if** a form's line changes shape (then `formsShown` reads nothing and the check silently stops: a test on the translator's own rendering would catch it, and there isn't one yet); or seats are seen stuck asking the same form again after `unclear` (then the answer should say what is missing, which the translator's feedback is for).

- **Why a run grew faster than its town, and what was done about it** (2026-10-07; `kernel/src/kernel.rs`, `kernel/crates/perception/src/observe.rs`, `kernel/crates/resolver/src/knowledge.rs`, `kernel/crates/world/src/travel.rs`, `orchestrator/src/kernel.ts`, `participants/src/behaviour/common.ts`, `worlds/generator/generate.ts`). Naz wants towns far larger than 1,000. A 30-day run of the 1,000-person town, and of the same town three times over, code seats only: wakes and commands grew 3 times, kernel time 6.2, module time 6.4, memory 4.6 (5.9 GB to 27 GB).
  - **What it was.** Every citizen has two channels that show it the whole town's register: every lot and every title (1,285 of each at 1,000 people, 3,750 at 3,000). So a citizen's first look was 0.77 MB of JSON at 1,000 people and 1.9 MB at 3,000, and all of it was done once per citizen: copied out of the state, written, hashed, parsed by the host, and kept, a copy each, in the citizen's book and in what the kernel's resolver knows of the seat. That is the square: at 3,000 people 30 of the 47 seconds of looking were those 3,079 first looks, the host held 134 million strings (11.8 GB), and the kernel, by an estimate from the counts, some 8 GB of lots' cells, one copy a seat. Three smaller ones beside it: a trip's path was worked out cell by cell from the state (two lookups by name and a formatted id for every cell tried), over a rectangle that grows with the town; the mayor looked through every lot for every cell; and a citizen at every wake read every claim in the register (to find what is owed by it) and, shopping, every price list once per shop.
  - **What changed. Nothing a run does: the log of the 1,000-person month and of the 3,000-person month is the same entry for entry (466,506 and 1,456,576), apart from the kernel's build and the modules' source hashes in the first two.**
    - *A record is handed to the host once.* An observation shows the state's own records (shared, not copied). A host that says it keeps what it is handed (`record_refs` in the `start` request; the orchestrator's kernel client does) gets each record whole the first time it is shown to anyone and as its id from then on, and gives every reader the same record, frozen. What is hashed and logged is the whole observation, as before. The protocol between kernel and host is not a contract; without `record_refs` nothing differs.
    - *What a seat knows of a lot or a form is shared between seats* (the kernel's resolver), not copied per seat.
    - *What each cell costs a traveller is worked out once for the whole ground,* and again when the ground changes; the search is the same.
    - *A book answers without rereading:* the lot over a cell, the claims someone holds, owes or that are about a thing, a seller's latest price list. Each is the plain answer exactly (`participants/test/book.test.ts` checks them against the plain way over seeded streams of records). A kind's standing statements are kept only for the kinds a module asks for.
    - *A run starts from its config where it is* (read without a copy, written to the header from the one there is).
    - *The generator* puts the water works on the nearest row to the river with a lot free (the row beside it is a street where the streets' spacing puts one there, as at 127 by 95, where row 93 is one: that was "no lot beside the river"), and finds a lot's title without looking through every record (a 10,000-person town took 30 seconds to generate; it takes 2). The existing worlds generate the same, byte for byte.
  - **Measured** (30 days, one seed, the run server, `/usr/bin/time -l`; before is main at d8b16c5):

    | | Wall | Kernel | Modules | Most memory held |
    |---|---|---|---|---|
    | 1,000 people, before | 23 s | 9.8 s | 4.2 s | 5.9 GB |
    | 1,000 people, after | 16 s | 6.0 s | 3.4 s | 2.2 GB |
    | 3,000 people, before | 102 s | 53.8 s | 24.2 s | 28.2 GB |
    | 3,000 people, after | 56 s | 24.1 s | 14.0 s | 7.8 GB |

    Three times the people now cost 4.0 times the kernel time, 4.2 the module time and 3.6 the memory. Final states 7596cffa9d339a58 and d1f018ee3c2cf458, as before; the 1,000-person one the same over the stdio transport. The whole suite passes (546 tests and the kernel's).
  - **10,000 people: generated and started, not run.** The town generates and its run starts (15 seconds, 14.6 GB held by the kernel after starting). The run was stopped on its first day at 36 GB (it reached 46 GB while stopping), on a machine with about 40 GB free. Two things in how the town is made grow with the square of its size, and neither can change without changing what every run does, so they are Naz's:
    - *The registers in every citizen's channels* (`lots`, `titles`): each citizen still keeps a line in its own book for every lot and title (by an estimate from the 3,000-person run, about 1.6 MB a citizen at 10,000, 16 GB together), and the kernel still hashes each citizen's first look whole (about 6 MB each, 60 GB hashed). A person does not read the whole land register before breakfast: the channels could be `on_request` (searched when buying, selling or building), and a citizen's own titles come through `mine`.
    - *Every household holds shares in every firm:* the 600 firms' shareholder records are 152 MB of the 244 MB config, and a dividend is 600 times some 4,000 payments. A fund that holds the firms, with households holding the fund, would be the usual institution.
  - **Not verified:** a 10,000-person run to its end; anything with a model seat (the client is the same, and a seat's recorded observations are written from the whole records, but no model run was made); runs longer than 30 days at 3,000 (the bank's payment system reads every standing instruction at each step: 1.3 ms a step at 3,000, the largest module cost left after the citizens'); the spread between runs of the timings (one run each; other work was using two or three of the machine's cores).
  - **Revisit if** a module is found to change a record it was shown (frozen, the change now fails and is recorded as that participant's error, where before it changed only its own copy: the fix is to copy first); the protocol gains a second host on one session (what has been handed over is kept per session); a cell is given an id that isn't its place (travel reads a cell's place from its id, as the path search always did); or observation hashes are wanted cheaper at scale (hashing each record once and the observation over its records' hashes would end the last per-citizen pass over the registers, and changes the log contract).

- **The first batch on a rented GPU: the spread between runs, the order settled, the reader's size no longer the limit; what a cut-off buys; and what a new town showed** (2026-10-08; `runs/translator-model/runpod/`, `pod-*`).
  - **The pod** (Naz: the RunPod credit is "yours to use"; then "should we just start using runpod now?"). One RTX 5080 on the community cloud at 0.39 an hour (the secure cloud about twice that; the 4090s that could be reached were gone). An epoch of the larger reader takes about 230 seconds there against 2,300 to 2,700 on the mini: a run about 18 minutes. What went to it: the trainer's four files and the exported steps (`data-13`, `data-14`, `data-15`); the tests never left the mini. `pull.sh` brings each finished run home and scores it here; `watchdog.sh` ends the pod when all are home or at a deadline; `stop.sh` ends it by hand. Two false starts: a pod with no reachable SSH port (ended after six minutes), and steps too large for 16 GB (`--token-budget 4096` there; the loss is the same, taken in more parts).
  - **The spread** (three seeds, `microsoft/deberta-v3-small`, data-13, by name): real 147, 148, 148 of 180; held out 44, 47, 44 of 66; seen 52, 52, 50; wrong drafts on real 13, 20, 16; the real-situation validation 177, 172, 174 of 228. So right answers on real differ by one between trainings and held out by three; wrong drafts by more. A difference of five on real is a difference.
  - **The order: by name** (now the default; `said` and `text_last` stay selectable, each export says its own). `text_last` twice: real 143 and 149, held out 47 and 46: not different from by name. Request order, once on the mini: 134.
  - **The reader's size:** `microsoft/deberta-v3-base`, once: real 146, held out 44, seen 51, real-situation validation 180. No better than the small one, at more than twice the time an answer. From xsmall to small it paid (held out 38 to the mid 40s); from small to base it does not.
  - **The real-situation validation set** runs 169 to 180 of 228 over six models whose real scores run 143 to 149: it does not separate them either, but it agrees that they are the same, which the generated one (2,904 to 2,985) could not have said.
  - **A cut-off on the model's sureness** (`pod-small-s1`, scored on the tests at each): none: real 147 right, 13 wrong, 20 asked back. 0.5: 131, 3, 46. 0.7: 119, 1, 60. 0.9: 103, 1, 76. Held out at 0.5: 40 right, 7 wrong; at 0.8: 38 and 3. So wrong drafts can be had under 1 in 100 on real requests today, at the price of asking back on two in five. The model is often right and not sure. The values were read off the tests: none is set as the default (`OIKOS_DECIDE_THRESHOLD` is still unset), and the one to ship is to be fixed on the real-situation validation set.
  - **A new town, another seat model** (`runs/translator-data-in-situ/pilot-10-requests`: the 39 requests pilot 10's Claude seats made through `do` in half a year; never training data). Two blind readers gave the same program for 10. Nearly all the rest differ in `basis` alone: asked to pay "citing dockets rec.1, rec.2, within budget rec.3", one reader gives the budget, the other all three. Leaving `basis` out they agree on 36. On those 36 `pod-small-s1` is right on 8, asks back on 1, and refuses or is wrong on 27: 21 of the 36 are the planning office granting a building permit, which is a liberty put on record (`claim`), and it read none of them so (11 an unknown form, 7 too much, 2 a building order). Training had 586 claims, every permission said as "may" or "is permitted to".
  - **Done about it:** the brief for a liberty now offers the granting of it (data 0.9.1); part `c1` (602 briefs of claims, two writers; 618 examples, 611 agreed by blind readers mixed with 206 decoys; 119 of them say grant, approve or permit); `data-15` is data-13 with them, and two runs on it are queued on the pod. Pilot 10's requests have now shaped training data: they are no longer a town the model has never been near, and the next pilot is.
  - **For Naz:** what `basis` holds when a request cites several records. I would say every record the request cites in support, in the order said (the fullest trail), and say so in the translator's prompt; until it is settled, examples the readers split on are dropped, which is most of what a finance seat writes.
  - **Not verified:** that `c1` closes the permit gap (the two runs will say); the larger reader more than once; what the pod's runs cost in all (about 0.12 each by the hour's price; the account's balance is the figure).
  - **Revisit if** a seat is found that never says a thing the way any writer did (it will be: each new seat model so far has; then the check is not more phrasing by hand but requests written in situ by that seat's model before it is put in a seat); or sureness stays low on right answers after more real-voice data (then the writer's head, which multiplies a probability a step, is the thing to look at: one unsure step in thirty sinks a right program).

- **The permission-granting examples did not close the permit gap, and the batch's other ends** (2026-10-08; `pod-small-c1-s1`, `-s2`, `pod-base-s2`).
  - **Result.** On data-15 (data-13 with part `c1`), two seeds: real 144 and 143 of 180, held out 44 and 45, seen 51 and 51. On pilot 10's 36 agreed requests the first reads none of the 21 permits as a `claim` (7 an unknown form, 6 an agreement, 2 a building order, the rest asked back) and is right on 7 of 36, where the model without `c1` was right on 8. So 611 more claims, 119 of them said as granting, changed nothing there, and the real score is four lower than without them (the edge of what the spread calls a difference).
  - **Why, as far as it can be seen:** the seat writes "Grant the house building permit on application 49348 (landlord-10, lot-24-12)", and the person's forms include `permit_application`. The model takes "permit … application" for that form (hence an unknown or unfitting form). The written examples say "grant c0042 the permit to build on lot-00": the holder and the lot in a sentence, no application, no form of that name beside it. A phrasing added by hand did not reach what the seat writes.
  - **So `data-13` stays the set to train on**, and `c1` is in the written set's folder but should be left out of a build until it is shown to help (`split.ts` takes every checked part: a build that wants data-13's examples must leave `part-c1` out, or use `joined-7`).
  - **What would close it** is what closed the last gap of this kind: requests written in situ by the seat's own model, here Claude in pilot 10's planning situations, labelled by two readers. That is held on one thing: the readers split on `basis` for every one of these requests, so nearly all would be dropped until the prompt says what `basis` holds. Naz's to settle (the entry above).
  - **The larger reader's second seed:** real 151 (9 wrong drafts), held out 41, seen 50. With the first (146, 44, 51) it is level with the small reader on real and no better on held out: the small reader stays.
  - **The pod** ended itself when the last run was home (01:36). Nine runs and the setting up cost 1.72 of the credit (10.97 to 9.25); nothing is left running there.
  - **A correction to what I told Naz and wrote in the handover:** a seat's system prompt does not change most calls. In pilot 10 it had 10 versions in 157 to 201 calls (when a price list on file changed). The cache written on every call is Claude Code caching what the seat is shown that turn. There is nothing to split; the handover now says so.
  - **Revisit if** a build with `c1` is wanted for another reason (then score it on the real test first: it cost four there); or the `permit_application` confusion shows in other worlds as a form's name colliding with an act said in the same words (then the examples need forms shown beside them whose names are near what is asked: a generator change, and a general one).

- **The whole program's sureness is the right thing to cut on; its parts are reported, not used** (2026-10-08; `py/model.py translate`, `py/predict.py`, the adapter's `answers`). The model is often right and not sure, and a long copied text is many unsure decisions, so I tried cutting on less than the whole: what it chose alone (`choices`), that and where ids stand (`places`), and its least sure decision. On the real test with `pod-small-s1`, right answers kept at the same number of wrong drafts: at 3 wrong, the whole keeps 131, choices and places 125, the least sure decision 125, choices alone 103; at 1 wrong, the whole 119, the least 106. The whole is best at every level: where a text ends is evidence about the rest. Nothing changes in what is cut on. `predict.py` now gives the parts with each answer (and `OIKOS_DECIDE_LOG` records them), for looking at; each answer of that run is in its folder as `real-parts.json`.
  - **Not verified:** on held out (not scored this way: that file stays unread, and the test's own scoring would have to be run at each cut-off for each measure); a measure learned from the parts (a small fit on the real-situation validation set) instead of one of them alone.
  - **Revisit if** more real-voice data does not raise sureness on right answers (then a learned measure, or training that asks for it, is next).
- **Starting a run, the bank's standing orders, and where a 10,000-person town's memory is** (2026-10-08; `kernel/crates/core/src/canon_ser.rs`, `canon.rs`, `kernel/crates/contracts/src/config.rs`, `kernel/crates/world/src/kernel.rs`, `state.rs`, `kernel/crates/nature/src/lib.rs`, `kernel/crates/resolver/src/knowledge.rs`, `kernel/src/log.rs`, `run.rs`, `protocol.rs`, `participants/src/behaviour/bank.ts`). The second pass at "why a run grew faster than its town" (above): the things on its list that leave every result as it was. Nothing a run does changes: the 1,000-person month ends at 7596cffa9d339a58 and the 3,000-person month at d1f018ee3c2cf458, as before, and both logs are the same entry for entry (466,506 and 1,456,576) but for the kernel's build in the header and the modules' source hashes in the entry after it.
  - **Starting.** The kernel held a large world's config five times at the start (the request's JSON value, the typed config, two copies of that, and its records again in the state) and made a JSON value of the whole state to hash it. A small JSON object costs about ten times its text as a `serde_json::Value` (a map node is made for eleven members whatever it holds), which is where "60 times the config" came from.
    - *The config is held once.* It is read out of the request's value, which is given up as it is read; the world made from it shares it (`Statics.config` is the same one), and the state shares its records and each participant's channels, controller and framing with it. All that is kept of how it was given is its canonical bytes (the ones its hash is of); the log's header is hashed and written from them part by part. A request's line is let go once read.
    - *Canonical JSON is written straight from a value's own shape* (`canon_ser.rs`): serde_json's own serializer with a formatter that writes as `canon` does and puts each object's members in order when the object ends, with no JSON value made on the way. What it can't write (a number that isn't a safe integer, a key that isn't a string, an integer or a boolean) it gives up on, and `canon::canonical` then goes the long way, which gives the right bytes or the right error. In a test build every call is checked against the long way; a test draws values and shapes of every kind serde has and compares. The state's hash reads the state's members from it.
    - *Nature's spin-up* was most of a small town's start (1.85 of 2.4 s at 1,000 people). A species' day now costs what the cells it is in do, not the whole ground (its numbers are kept sparsely through the spin-up; a home range is summed cell by cell where the species is in few cells). A field that comes back to a state it was in up to eight days before, under one scale on its sources, goes round those states without being worked out again (in the generated town heat settles in 4 days and water comes to go round a few states; pollution is still creeping after the ten years and is worked out every day). The same days, draw for draw: one body of code steps a species either way, and a test steps a ground with every such case a day at a time and compares.
    - A person's children are found once, not by looking through every entity for each; a valid instance is checked against its schema by the cheaper question first.
  - **The bank.** `bank.payment_system` went through every standing order at every plan: at 3,000 people about 3,000 orders at each of 2,800 plans a month, of which under 10,000 were due. An order is now kept under its next due time and gone through then; one with no due time yet (refused, or not its account holder's) at every plan, as before. Going through an order that isn't due does nothing unless something the bank's checks read has changed (the accounts it keeps and whose they are, the signatories it has been told of, the directory): when one has since the last plan (6 plans of 2,778 in the 3,000-person month), every order is gone through as before. The new `review_all` parameter goes through all at every plan; `participants/test/bank.test.ts` steps the module both ways over seeded streams (requests, withdrawals, deaths, signatory lists, accounts opened and closed, a position changing hands) and compares every wake. With the account or signatory check taken out, that test fails; with the directory check taken out it still passes (I could not make a case where the directory alone matters: an order that reached a due time has a known author already), so that check is belt and braces.
  - **Two smaller ones found by profiling the 3,000-person month again.**
    - *The resolver's map of a town's lots, one a seat.* Every citizen reads the register at its first look and the kernel kept a map of every lot's cells for each (127 KB a seat at 3,000: 0.4 GB; by the same count 4.4 GB at 10,000). A register shown whole to a seat that knows of no lot is taken in as one map, made once for the list and shared (a persistent map, so what a seat learns later is its own). `knowledge.rs` has the test.
    - *A move copied 64 buildings' occupants.* Who is in each building, and each space's departures, are behind an `Arc` in the state, as accounts already were: an act's copy of the state copies the one it changes.
  - **Measured** (30 days, one seed, the run server, `/usr/bin/time -l`; before is main at a5cd8fa, run the same night; other agents were using the machine, so times are given as the two runs taken):

    | | Wall | Kernel (commands) | Start | Modules | Most memory held |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | 1,000 people, before | 18.7 s | 6.2 s | 2.3 s | 3.5 s | 2.15 GB |
    | 1,000 people, after | 11.9, 14.7 s | 5.4, 5.5 s | 0.8 s | 2.3, 2.4 s | 1.93, 1.96 GB |
    | 3,000 people, before | 55.5 s | 23.9 s | 4.2 s | 13.9 s | 7.68 GB |
    | 3,000 people, after | 46.8, 48.1 s | 22.3, 23.2 s | 1.6 s | 10.7, 10.9 s | 5.90, 5.94 GB |

    The kernel binary alone, starting a town and hashing its state: 1,000 people 2.4 s and 645 MB to 1.2 s and 225 MB; 3,000 people 4.3 s and 2.3 GB to 1.8 s and 0.68 GB; 10,000 people 14.9 s and 14.8 GB to 7.4 s and 4.0 GB. The bank's module: 0.8 to 0.1 s and 3.8 to 0.6 s. The kernel's own tests (which in a test build check every canonical encoding against the long way), the type check and the whole suite pass.
  - **What the 3,000-person month's time is now.** The kernel is 57% of the host's time and reading its replies another 9%. Of the kernel's time, half is looking: building what is seen, writing it, hashing it. The citizens' first looks (the whole register of lots and titles each) are about 5 of the kernel's 23 seconds, a third of that SHA-256 over 1.9 MB a citizen. After the first day a look costs about 55 microseconds, spread thin: no one thing is a tenth of it. Comparing string keys in the state's and the seats' maps is about 13% of the kernel's time, and allocating and freeing more than a tenth.
  - **10,000 people: started, run under a guard of 40 GB, stopped on day 2.** The town starts in 7 seconds holding 3.9 GB in the kernel, which then stays flat (the kernel process, over the stdio transport, held 3.86 GB throughout). The host process is what grows: 10.7 GB half a minute in, 19.6 GB at 50 s, 37 GB when the guard stopped the run at day 2.0 (118 s). In two other attempts the machine was already compressing memory before the guard's sum was reached (the kernel's resident size fell from 3.9 to 0.6 GB while it worked): those reached day 4 and day 7 and are not a fair reading. Partway through one of them, after a large collection, the host still held 20 GB: so most of what it holds is in use, not waiting to be collected.
    - *Where it is:* in each citizen's own book of the two registers. A book holds, for each record it has seen, an entry by id and a place in two lists (measured alone: 159 bytes a record a book, 211 once it has been asked which lot covers a cell), and a citizen who looks at more than eight lots' titles then builds an index of every claim by what it is about (by estimate 1.5 MB at 10,000). 24,400 register records in each of 10,000 citizens' books, at 80 to 160 bytes each, is 20 to 40 GB; the indexes are the likeliest cause of the growth after the first looks, which I did not measure apart.
    - *An experiment, not committed* (it changes what every run does): with the `lots` and `titles` channels left out of citizens' channels in the generator, the 10,000-person month ran to its end in 292 s, the two processes holding 11.1 GB at most (host 7.0, kernel 4.1); kernel 99 s (over the pipe), modules 77 s (the citizens 62 s for 1.14 million steps: 54 microseconds a step where the 3,000-person town's take 23, so something a citizen does each step grows with the town and is the next thing to find). At 3,000 people the first day held 1.4 GB without them and 3.6 GB with.
  - **For Naz** (neither done):
    - *The registers in every citizen's channels* is the decision the 10,000-person town waits on, as the entry above said. Making `lots` and `titles` on request for citizens (searched when buying, selling or building; a citizen's own through `mine`) is the experiment's result, less whatever a search costs. I would do that.
    - *Keeping what runs do and sharing the registers between books instead* is possible and is a rewrite of `Book`: a list shown whole to many would be held once with its indexes, and each book would keep where it stands in its own order of seeing (a citizen sees its own titles first, through `mine`, so no two books' orders are quite the same, which is what makes this more than a cache). Several days' work on the class every module reads through, for what the first option gives by making the town more like a town.
    - *A bulk payment with an amount that isn't a number stops the bank's step* (`cents` throws inside the sum, before the check that would refuse the request): found writing the test. No code module writes one; a model seat's request could. It is a mechanism bug and a one-line fix (refuse it as the other malformed ones are), and it changes what such a run does, so it is yours to say.
