  - **Not verified:** a 10,000-person run to its end as the town is generated today; anything with a model seat (the kernel's and the bank's changes are on paths a model seat's run also takes, and the suite's model-seat tests pass, but no model run was made); a run resumed from a log (it starts through the same code); `canon_ser` against a serde_json built with other features (`arbitrary_precision`, `preserve_order`: the tree has neither; with either, the formatter would see numbers or members another way, and the test-build check against the long way is what would say so); the spin-up's shortcuts on a world with other species and fields than the profile's (the test's ground has five species and four fields, made to reach each case); the timings' spread beyond the two runs given.
  - **Revisit if** a hash is ever seen to differ between a test build and a release build (the direct writer and the long way disagree somewhere: `canonical_by_value` is the rule, and `canonical` can be pointed back at it in one line); a field's round is longer than eight states (it is then worked out every day, as before: raise `ROUND_KEPT`); the bank is given a check that reads something new (it must be added to what `changed` compares, or orders that are not due will not hear of it: the test with `review_all` is the guard, and needs the new thing in its stream); a seat is shown the register in two halves (each half is taken in a lot at a time, as before: nothing wrong, nothing shared); starting matters again at 100,000 people (what is left is the schema check, 2.5 s at 10,000, the state hashed twice at the start, 0.5 s, and SHA-256 over the config and the header); or the kernel's time per look does (the choices left are structural: one core, string keys in every map of the state, an observation hashed whole, a JSON value made of every observation for the resolver's knowledge).
- **The books share what they were shown in common: a 10,000-person month fits** (2026-10-08; `participants/src/behaviour/common.ts`, `citizen.ts`, `participants/test/book.test.ts`). The third pass at "why a run grew faster than its town" (the two entries above), and the second of the two ways the last one left for Naz: what runs do is kept, and the registers are held once. Nothing a run does changes: the 1,000-person month ends at 7596cffa9d339a58 and the 3,000-person month at d1f018ee3c2cf458, as before. No contract changes; no channel shows anyone anything else.
  - **Measured first** (the 3,000-person month, every book counted at the end; each structure's cost measured alone, 400 books of 7,470 shared records). A citizen's book held 7,640 records, 7,470 of them the two registers (3,735 lots and as many titles): 23.5 million entries in 3,216 books, 22.8 million of them the registers. An entry cost 104 bytes: 88 in the book's map of records by id, 11 in its list by record type, 5.5 in its list by statement kind. So about 780 KB a citizen and 2.4 GB in all, before what the collector had not yet taken back. The indexes were not it: claims by what they are about were kept by 425 books for 11,657 values together (three books for every value); lots by cell by one book; a kind's standing statements, 354,000 entries in all; a seller's latest list, 4,712 maps of about 120 entries. The last entry's guess that the claim index grew after the first looks was wrong: what grew was the first looks still arriving (a citizen's is at its first wake), and the collector behind them.
  - **What changed.**
    - *A long list shown in one look is held once.* A book's records are in parts, in the order seen: pages of its own, and shelves. A list of 256 or more public records shown in one look is read from a shelf: the one already made that begins with the list's first record and runs as the list runs, or a new one. A shelf has what a book's own pages have (records by id, by type, by kind; claims by holder, counterparty and object, and lots by cell, each made when first asked for), made once for every book that reads from it. A book keeps for a shelf only what differs for it: the records on it that it had seen before (a citizen sees its own titles first, through `mine`), which the shelf's part passes over. What the book has seen withdrawn is its own, as it was. Shelves are found by the records themselves (the same frozen record, handed over once: `record_refs`), never by what they say, so a list is on a shelf only if it is the same records in the same order; over the stdio transport without `record_refs`, or in a test that makes its records afresh, nothing is shared and nothing differs.
    - *The test of sameness is what the book would have seen.* A list runs as a shelf does, for a book, if the records of the list that are new to the book are, in order, the shelf's records that are new to the book. So a list with the reader's own records in among it, a list that shows a record twice, and a later, longer list (the shelf, then the rest one by one, or a second shelf) all read from the shelf, and a list in another order does not. Only public records go on a shelf.
    - *Every answer is put together from the parts in order,* less what each passes over: one part's own list where only one has any (no copy), otherwise a list joined when first asked for and again when the last part's has grown. `standingOf` still gives the same list until the standing statements change and another once they have (the bank and `Latest` go by that).
    - *A seller's latest list is worked out once for books with the same standing lists* (`Latest`: the last four worked out are kept, and a book whose list is the same records in the same order takes the answer).
    - *Claims by a key are kept for every value from the first asking* (the few-values-first rule was there to spare citizens' books, which no longer hold the registers).
    - *Two things in the citizen, found by profiling the 10,000-person month.* A shopper retrying a short order chose among the untried shops once for every shop it looked at (the draw is the order's own, so the same each time: 36 of 64 seconds of citizens' steps at 10,000); it is made once. A citizen finding the lot under a building copied the list of every lot and looked through it; it asks the book (`lotOver`), which answers the same.
  - **The test** (`participants/test/book.test.ts`): beside the earlier one, each book has a twin kept the plain way (every record in one list, taken in one by one, everything found by looking through it), and the two must agree on everything that can be asked (every list, lookup, withdrawal, replacement, signature, what was new at each look) after every look. Nine readers are shown one register differently: whole; after some of it one by one; with their own records in among it; later and longer; in two halves; twice in one look; never in one piece; after another list that begins the same; and with a record out of order. Thirteen faults put into the book one at a time (a passed-over record given out, a shelf taken for a list in another order, a replaced law looked for later on the shelf than the law replacing it, a joined list never joined again, and so on) each fail it.
  - **Measured** (30 days, one seed, the run server, `/usr/bin/time -l`; before is main at 8016e40, run the same night; other agents were using the machine, and the same code's wall time varied by a quarter between runs, so both runs are given):

    | | Wall | Kernel | Modules | Most memory held |
    |---|---|---|---|---|
    | 1,000 people, before | 16.4 s | 6.1 s | 2.9 s | 1.75 GB |
    | 1,000 people, after | 11.3, 15.0 s | 5.1, 6.7 s | 2.1, 3.0 s | 1.33, 1.32 GB |
    | 3,000 people, before | 48.1, 57.0 s | 28.0 s | 12.3 s | 5.89, 6.05 GB |
    | 3,000 people, after | 47.2, 60.3 s | 23.3, 30.3 s | 9.5, 11.7 s | 2.96, 2.84 GB |
    | 10,000 people, before | stopped on day 2 | | | past 37 GB |
    | 10,000 people, after | 407 s | 233 s | 72 s | 10.98 GB |

    At 3,000 a citizen's book now holds about 170 records of its own (some 18 KB) and reads the rest from shelves: 23.0 million of the 23.5 million entries are on 20 shelves. At 10,000: 4.7 million entries in 10,638 books' own pages (440 a book: 208 price lists, 89 enterprise agreements, 48 notices), 249 million read from 37 shelves; the register of lots is one shelf for 10,211 books and the register of titles one for 10,009. The 10,000-person month ended at d33f1f4c9dd5ac32 three times (with shelves of 64 or more and of 256 or more, and before and after the citizen's two changes); there is no earlier run of it to compare with. Its 11 GB is what the experiment that left the registers out held.
  - **Where the 10,000-person month's time goes** (profiled, 357 s): 56% in the kernel; 12% reading its replies (`JSON.parse`); 5% putting each record back where the kernel sent its id; 12% in the citizens (steps 30 s, taking in what they see 12 s). A citizen's step is 26 microseconds, as at 3,000: the growth the last entry asked about was the retry's choice.
  - **What still grows faster than the town.**
    - *A citizen's first look,* in time: the kernel builds, writes and hashes the whole of both registers for each citizen (6 MB each at 10,000), and the host reads 24,400 ids and finds each one's record. That is most of the kernel's time per wake (52 microseconds at 1,000 people, about 70 to 90 at 3,000, 200 at 10,000) and the 17% the host spends reading. Ending it is the kernel's: a list handed over once and named after that, as records are, and an observation hashed over its records' hashes (the second changes the log contract; the first entry above says so).
    - *Public records that arrive one by one* (price lists, enterprise agreements, notices): each citizen's book has its own entry for each, 440 a book in a month at 10,000 against 170 at 3,000. Half a GB at 10,000 in a month; it grows with the town and with time. The books see them at different looks, in the same order: a channel's records held once with each book's place in them would share these too, and is a larger change than this one (a book's order of seeing runs across channels).
    - *The citizen goes through everything new to it five times at each plan* (the whole register, at its first), and adds it to its list with one call (`fresh.push(...news)`: a list's length as a call's arguments, which has a limit somewhere above today's 24,400).
    - *Shopping compares every shop,* and the bank's payment system is 1.2 ms a step at 10,000 against 0.3 at 3,000.
    - *Every household holds shares in every firm* (the first entry above): untouched.
  - **Not verified:** anything with a model seat (a seat's own knowledge is the translator's, not a `Book`; code seats beside it use this one, and the suite's model-seat tests pass, but no model run was made); a run resumed from a log; runs longer than a month at 10,000; a town where the registers reach citizens in an order that differs between them (nothing is shared then, and the run is as it was before this entry: slower and larger, not different); the spread of the 10,000-person timings (one run each of three versions: 361, 391 and 407 s, the machine busier each time); that `SHELF.least` of 256 is the best figure (64 put small lists on shelves until a book had its six and the register of titles, which comes late in a look, went into 4,757 books' own pages: 18.3 GB; 256 and eight is what was run).
  - **Revisit if** a module is found to change a list a book gave it (they are typed read-only now; a list from a shelf is every reader's); a seat's channels show public records it must not hold in common for some reason (a shelf is found by the records being the same ones, so there is nothing to leak, but `visible_to` is what is checked); many seats are shown the same register at different lengths with records gone from the middle (each such list is its own shelf: the count of shelves in a run is the sign; `Book.held()` gives a book's); books are wanted with more than eight shelves; or `Latest`'s four kept answers hold a finished run's lists in a process that goes on to others (they are let go as new ones come).

- **How much rides on what `basis` holds: little in the earlier data, nearly everything in a Claude seat's** (2026-10-08; a count, no code). Of the 887 requests from real situations that two readers split on in the earlier batches (2,393 agreed), 9 are the same but for `basis`. Of pilot 10's 39 requests, 26 are. So the question is not behind the readers' disagreements in general (those are where a letter's words end, and one act or several); it is what a Claude finance or planning seat writes: every payment and every permit cites the records it rests on, several at a time. Settling it unblocks requests written in situ by that seat's model; it recovers almost nothing already written.

- **A Claude seat's answer in its own tool-use markup is read, not lost** (2026-10-08; `participants/src/llm/claude-code.ts` `invoked`). Found by the count of pilot 10's calls made for the seats' efficiency: in 48 of 450 seat answers the model wrote its tool uses as markup (`<invoke name="look"><parameter name="report">…`), not as the object the adapter asks for. The adapter read no tool use in them, asked twice more, then took the answer as words, and an answer with no tool use ends the sitting: 25 sittings in which a seat looked, wrote or confirmed and nothing happened. Each use in such an answer is now read as it stands (a parameter's value is a list, object, number or true/false where written as one, else its text); a use of a tool that isn't the seat's is left out. Of the 48, 45 are now read (the other three are empty uses).
  - **What this says of pilot 10:** about a tenth of what its seats tried to do in those sittings was dropped by the platform, not by them. Its log is kept as it is; anything read from it about what Claude seats do or fail to do must allow for that.
  - **Not verified:** on a live run (no model was called); that the markup always closes as in these 48.
  - **Revisit if** the share stays high on a live run (then asking for the object is the wrong way to ask this model, and the markup should be what is asked for).
- **An act may rest on several records: `basis` is one record or a list** (2026-10-08; a contract change, signed by Naz the same day: "ok, go with A"; the translator's prompt 0.7.5). Until now `basis` took a single record. A request that cites several could not be said (the gap `two_bases` in the real test), and in pilot 10 a finance seat's payment "citing dockets rec.11904, rec.16123, rec.21887, rec.25703, within the services budget rec.25150" was lost: the translator wrote the five as a list, and the resolver answered "basis should name something, not data" until the dockets were moved into the payment's purpose. Real paperwork cites the authority and the supporting documents together (a voucher names the appropriation and the dockets; a contract the permit and the resolution), and what an act said it rested on is what this platform is for reading afterwards. The entry above counts what rides on it: 26 of pilot 10's 39 requests.
  - **The contract change, spelled out.**
    - *Blocks* (`contracts/blocks.schema.json`): on the six that take `basis` (`record_claim`, `transfer_claim`, `release_claim`, `propose_agreement`, `publish`, `withdraw`) it is now `common#/$defs/Basis`: an id or a `$name`, as before, or a list of 1 to 64 of them, in the order cited, none twice. No other block has a basis (a `transfer` names what it `settles` and the request it carries out; neither is new).
    - *Records* (`contracts/records.schema.json`): a record's `basis` is `records#/$defs/Basis`: one id, as before, or a list of 2 to 64, none twice.
    - *One basis has one written form.* A single record is its id alone, never a list of one. A block may be given a list of one (it is that record) and the kernel stores the id; the command is logged as it was given. So every block, record, world and log written before is valid and reads as it did, and no state hash moves: nothing existing is rewritten, and nothing new can say an old thing a second way.
    - `contracts/common.schema.json` gains `Basis`; `contracts/calls.md` says how a call writes it (`basis=rec.1`, or `basis=["rec.1","rec.2"]`, a list written as the call language already writes one for `to`, `by` and `visible`); `contracts/README.md` has the change. The log, the participant API, the world config and the call's parsed form (`calls.schema.json`) are unchanged in text: they carry blocks and records by reference, and a call's argument was always any value. The contracts' version stays 0.8: nothing valid before is invalid now. Examples: three valid blocks (several; a list of one; a `$name` among them), a valid record, a valid call; three invalid blocks (an empty list, a repeat, something that is no id) and three invalid records (a list of one, a repeat, a `$name`).
    - *64* is a bound and no more than that: the largest real request so far cites five.
  - **The kernel** (the generic mechanism; it knows no law and none was added). `Basis` (a record's) and `BasisArg` (a block's, kept as written) in `kernel/crates/contracts`; making a record resolves each `$name`, keeps the order, keeps one that comes to the same record twice once, and checks that each exists, as it checked one (`unknown_record`, the act refused whole); it asks nothing else of them, so a law, a title, a docket and a letter can stand in one list. A world's starting records are checked the same way. What reads a basis: (1) a search's condition on the field `basis` (`=` holds for a record that cites the value alone or among several; `!=` for one that cites something and not it; a record citing nothing has no such field, as before) and a search's order by `basis` (the one cited first); (2) formal law, which sees a block as its data, so `block.basis` is a text or a list there: no law in `worlds/` reads it; (3) nothing else: the state's hash is over the record as written, and the story, audit and truth tools never read a basis.
  - **Everything else that reads one.** The two resolvers (this repository's and the engine's port, which runs use) pass a list through in the order given, write one record bare, drop a repeat, read a name bound on an earlier line as that line's record and anything not written as an id as a name the seat knows (or ask), and ask for an empty list, a list of anything but names, or more than 64. Code participants read a basis through `basesOf` and `cites` (`participants/src/contracts.ts`): the tribunal finds a respondent's answer that cites the application among other things; a citizen, a firm and the town find their own orders, leases and listings the same way (fourteen places; with one basis each does exactly what it did). A seat's papers and a draft's preview say "citing rec.4, rec.9 and rec.12". The measurement tables: `records.basis` is the record cited first (what it held), and `records.bases` all of them in order (new; empty where nothing is cited).
  - **The translator's rule** (in the prompt, where it says what `basis` is): "basis= is every record the request cites in support of the act (what it is done under, and the papers that back it: "under permit rec.30", "within budget rec.25", "citing dockets rec.4 and rec.9"), in the order the request gives them: one record bare (basis=rec.30), several as a list (basis=["rec.30","rec.25"]). None the request cites is left out, and none it doesn't cite is added. A record that is the thing acted on is not also a basis: the invoice a payment settles is settles=, the record withdrawn is withdraw=, the agreement signed is sign=, the form filled in is the form." One worked example (a payment on two delivery notes and a budget, settling an invoice).
  - **The tests' keys.** The three real cases marked `two_bases` now have the answer the language can say, the records in the order each request gives them (`c9-mayor-d24-1`: the budget, then the permit; `c9-mayor-d25-2`: the permit, then the budget; `9-mayor-d64-1`: each supplement its application and its answer, and `basis` is compared there now). The two parks each accepted either record alone before; neither is accepted now, since each leaves out a record the request cites. The gap is gone from `GAPS`. The real test reads a list of one basis as that record. Three seen cases with several (a payment, a building, a notice). No other case changed, and the held-out set was not opened.
  - **The trained model's encoding and the data pipeline needed no change to carry a list:** a list was already its two marks around a span for each item (`by`, `to`), and the pipeline already takes each item of a list as a value to find in the request. Tests now hold them to it for `basis` (the pilot's payment through `spansIn`, `strayIds`, `copies`, `encode`, `row` and back; two lines citing the same two records in different orders). The one change in `translator/train/data.ts`: a program whose basis is a list, asked for in the cited style, is told to cite every one in the order the call lists them. No program the generator writes has such a list yet, so no example changes and `DATA_VERSION` stays 0.9.1; no data was regenerated.
  - **Measured.** The month of 1,000 (`worlds/town-1000.yaml`, seed 1, to 2,592,000 s) ends at state hash 7596cffa9d339a58 before and after, and its log is the same entry for entry (466,506) but for the header's build id and the note of the modules' source hashes. The pilot's payment, as the translator first wrote it, resolves in both resolvers to one request to the bank citing all five records, and the kernel takes such a request (a test in each resolver, and one end to end: call, both resolvers, kernel, log, tables). The suite: the kernel's 159 tests pass (`cargo test --workspace`), `bun run typecheck` passes, and `bun run test:all` ends "563 pass, 0 fail across 90 files" (a first run ended 562 and 1: `participants/test/book.test.ts` over its five-second limit at 5.3 s, with the machine at a load of 32 from other work; it is 2.4 s in the suite's record, passes alone, and nothing here touches it).
  - **Not verified:** any model's translations under 0.7.5 (no model was run: whether a model follows the new rule, keeps the order, or over-cites records a request merely mentions is unknown, and the real test's three cases are the first to ask); a trained translator model on such requests (none has seen a list of bases: the generator writes none); a run with a model seat that cites several (no pilot was run); a run resumed from a log across this change; that 64 is enough; the two references added for the practice, which are from memory.
  - **Left as it is, on purpose.** The outside supplier's invoice still cites its first docket as its basis and lists them all in its data (`participants/src/behaviour/world.ts`): citing all of them would be truer to this change and would change every existing run's records. It is a one-line change for whoever decides that the runs may move.
  - **Revisit if** a model, shown the rule, cites records a request only mentions (the rule is "in support of the act"; the seats' "leave rec.9 as it is" sentences are the trap, and the generated data's riders are the place to teach it); requests cite records in different roles that the order alone doesn't keep apart (authority against evidence: then a basis wants roles, a larger change); the generator is to write programs with several bases (then `DATA_VERSION` moves, and an example whose records stand in the request in another order than its list should be dropped); a law comes to read `block.basis` (it must then take a text or a list); or more than 64 is ever cited.
- **A seat that takes fewer calls for the same office: looks kept ready for every sitting, no turn when nothing came of looking, a notebook added to** (2026-10-08; the participant loop, now 0.8.0: `participants/src/llm/participant.ts`, `render.ts`; tests in `participants/test/llm-participant.test.ts` and `orchestrator/test/llm.test.ts`). Naz: "we are trying to simulate as close as possible to the real world + be able to model gradual disempowerment, but at the same time we need the seat to be efficient, since more/longer/bigger simulations are important." So: fewer calls and fewer words written, with nothing taken from what a seat can know or do. No contract changes: a seat's tools are the participant loop's, and the participant API's actions underneath (`look`, `wait`) are as they were. Code-seat runs are untouched (the 1,000-person month ends at 7596cffa9d339a58).
  - **Measured first** (pilot 10, `runs/pilot-10/town-1000.seed2.jsonl`; the first 333 of its 450 seat calls, the part counted before; the whole log gives the same picture). 164 sittings, 2.0 calls each. A call costs about the same whatever it does (5 to 6 cents as recorded for one that only says it's done, as for one that opens a sitting), so calls are what to save.
    - *No wake that opened a sitting was wasted.* With office hours a seat is only ever called at a sitting (the day it gave, or the first sitting after a letter), and of 164 openings one ended in nothing by the seat's choice (day 1). At the rest it looked something up (80), acted (58), or gave an answer that was never read (25, below). There is no kind of wake to drop. A run counts its wakes by reason for all participants together (in the summary, not wake by wake in the log), so which wake led to which call is read from the loop and the calls: a seat with office hours asks for the time of its next sitting every time, so an opening is an "asked" wake (or "news", when a letter comes in working hours on a day with no sitting yet); a wake between sittings ("news", or "asked" on a day before the one it gave) makes no call; a later turn follows a "done" wake (its letter or act finished) or no wake at all (after looks, the same wake goes on). And a sitting on a day with nothing new in the reports it's shown still acted 3 times in 22 and looked 17 (a chaser after three days' silence is an act on nothing new).
    - *The calls that did nothing are later turns of a sitting:* 68 that only said done or wrote in the notebook. 22 came after looks that all found nothing, 24 after looks that found something, 21 after letters or other acts, 1 at an opening.
    - *41 answers (12%) were never read.* The seat wrote its tool uses in another shape than the one the Claude Code adapter asks for (after the adapter's own two more tries each), and an answer with no tool use ends the sitting as "done for now": 25 sittings where a seat tried to look or write and nothing happened, a tenth of the spending. It is the adapter's, not this change's: recorded here because it is the largest single waste in the log and it loses acts.
    - *The notebook:* 182 rewrites of 1,689 characters. About one output token in eight was notebook (three quarters of the output tokens are not in what the seat said at all: its thinking). Line by line, 2,238 of 3,929 lines or sentences were word for word what the notebook said before.
  - **1. Looks that don't cost a call each.** Several looks in one answer were already answered together; what cost a call was that looking is a turn of its own: 72 openings did nothing but look, mostly at the same things as the sitting before (the finance officer its bills, deaths and files; the planning officer its current applications; the mayor its filings).
    - *Chosen:* a look can be kept ready. `look` takes `each_sitting`: true, and from then on that same look is made as each sitting opens, before the seat is asked anything, and set out after the report it opens on, under "Looked up for you, as at every sitting"; false, no longer. At most six. It is the seat's own look, through the participant API as the seat would make it (the log has each as a `look` command of the participant's), so it shows nothing the seat couldn't look up and hides nothing: the opening report is whole, and of each look's report only what the opening one says in the same words is left out. A look it can no longer make (a post lost) is dropped, and said. A look made at two sittings running is said, with its result, to be one it could keep.
    - *Why this one.* A real office-holder has the morning's trays put on the desk because they asked for it; that is this. *Naming what to see at the next sitting* exists already (looks beside `done_for_now` are shown at the next): it needs the seat to ask again every time. *Showing by default what it was seen to look at* needs nothing from the seat, which is its merit, but then the platform is deciding what an office-holder attends to, from its habits; and what a seat chooses to look at, or stops looking at, is part of what a run is for. A look kept ready is the seat's choice and is in the log as one (`look_each_sitting` notes).
    - *On pilot 10* (had each seat kept ready the looks it made at the opening of at least half of its last four sittings): 21 of 333 calls if only the very same look counts (the seats varied how many they asked for), 35 if how many is let vary, 46 if any look into the same report counts. So 6% to 14% of calls, and only if seats take it up.
  - **2. Wakes that end in nothing.** Since no opening was wasted, the rule is about the turn after an answer. *After an answer that did nothing but look into whole reports (nothing to find, no one and nothing named) and write in the notebook, if there is nothing in any of them, and nothing else it hasn't been shown (no letter, no outcome, no note, nothing to confirm, no section of the report that differs), no turn is taken:* the sitting ends, it is back after the days it last gave, and it is told at the start of its next sitting ("When you were last here, there was nothing in what you looked into, so the sitting ended there.", with what the looks showed). A real office-holder who finds the tray empty isn't then asked whether there's anything else.
    - *On pilot 10:* 18 calls not made of 333 (5.4%), every one of which only said done (4 also rewrote the notebook); no call that acted or looked is lost. Of the 18, 13 gave the days they had given the time before, so the seat is back when it would have been; 5 chose other days, which is what is lost. With change 1 the two overlap (16 of the 18 are the planning officer's look at its applications): together 23 to 48 calls, 7% to 14%.
    - *What was counted and not done.* A look with something to find that finds nothing is an answer, and seats acted on it (2 of the 6 such: "no bank letters in my files today", then a letter chasing the bank): a turn follows, as before. After letters alone (12 such turns) 5 did nothing and 5 went on to act or look: a turn follows. Making `done_for_now` beside a look mean "unless something is found" would have added a call 14 times in 22. The office hours' wording was wrong and is put right: `done_for_now` with no days said "back at your next working day's sitting"; it is back after its usual days (seven in the town), which the description now gives.
  - **3. The notebook is added to or changed in part.** `notebook` takes `add` (words put at the end, on a new line), `change` (passages, each given as it stands and what it should say instead; empty strikes it out) and, as before, `text` (the whole, written afresh); in one use they are done in the order text, change, add, and what a use leaves alone stays. It still holds 4,000 characters. At the limit nothing is put in that doesn't fit and nothing is cut to make room: the addition or change is not made, and the seat is told how many characters are free (in the same sitting if a turn follows, otherwise at its next, as with anything not done). A passage that isn't there word for word, or is there twice, is not changed, and said. A whole notebook written too long keeps its start, as before. The seat is shown how full it is ("(1689 of the 4000 characters it holds are used.)").
    - *On pilot 10:* the 182 rewrites were 307,322 characters; said as additions and changes (each new line written, each line gone quoted by its first 40 characters) they are 217,477, 29% fewer, about 4% of all output tokens. Small beside the calls: the notebook was rewritten whole because it is reorganised each time, not only added to (5 of 182 rewrites took nothing out).
  - **What a seat now reads** is in the tools' descriptions (`tools` in participant.ts) and one sentence of its instructions ("Keep your notebook alongside: add to it or change a passage, rather than writing it all out again."). Nothing names a call, a token or the machinery (the tests check the words).
  - **Not verified:** no real model was run. Whether a seat keeps looks ready at all, or keeps the right ones; whether it keeps too many (each adds to what it is shown at every sitting, and a seat may then look less closely); whether it uses `add` and `change` or goes on rewriting; whether changes fail often on passages not quoted word for word (each failure is told, and a seat may then take a turn to put it right); whether a seat understands that a sitting ended on empty reports. A run resumed from a log with looks kept ready (the looks are remade from the answers served, which should give the same commands; not run). The counts are on one run of one model (Claude Sonnet through Claude Code), with a sixth of its sittings cut short by unread answers.
  - **A short pilot should check** (the notes are in the log: `look_each_sitting`, `no_turn`, `notebook_not_changed`): calls per sitting against pilot 10's 2.0; how many looks each seat keeps and how often it then looks again by hand; `no_turn` counts and whether the next sitting shows the seat confused by it; the share of notebook uses that are `add` or `change`, and how many fail; and whether anything is acted on later than in pilot 10 (applications waiting, invoices unpaid).
  - **Revisit if** seats don't take up `each_sitting` (then the default the other way: a look made at two sittings running is kept until the seat says no, ten lines in the loop; it was not chosen for the reason above); a seat is found to have meant to do more after empty looks (the rule could give it the turn when its notebook says so, or be dropped: it is worth 5% of calls); notebooks reach the limit more often now that adding is cheap (then the limit, or what is done at it, is the question); or the Claude Code adapter's unread answers are fixed and the picture of which calls do nothing changes.
- **Nobody has the land register by heart: what each seat is shown, and what it now looks up** (2026-10-08; Naz: "no entity should have a god like view of anything"; citizen 0.36.0, landlord 0.13.0, builder 0.11.0, generator 0.24.0; `worlds/generator/generate.ts`, `participants/src/behaviour/citizen.ts`, `firms.ts`, `institutions.ts`, `kernel/crates/perception/src/observe.rs`, `kernel/crates/world/src/kernel.rs`; docs/behaviour/ownership.md, "What an owner knows, and what is looked up"). Every citizen was shown, unasked, every lot and every claim in the town with every transfer and release of one (1,284 lots and as many titles at 1,000 people), and every workplace's enterprise agreement; every letting office and the builder, the plan of every lot. A seat is now shown what someone in its place would be sent, and searches for the rest where it is kept, when a decision needs it. **This changes what every run does (its hashes), by decision.**
  - **A contract change, for sign-off: a search may name a record's id, and the claim a transfer or a release names** (`QueryField` in `contracts/participant-api.schema.json`: `id` and `claim` beside `record`, `kind`, `at`, `author`, `speaks_for`, `basis`, `data.<path>`, `about.<path>`). Without them a register can't be asked for one named lot or for what became of one title: a lot's record carries its id nowhere but as its id, and a transfer names its title in a field of its own. Mechanical, as the others: a place in a record, compared. The kernel reads a search for one id straight from that record (the same answer as going through the register: every other condition is still asked of it; `kernel/tests/attention.rs` has the test). Nothing else in `contracts/` changed: `on_request` and searches were already there (D43). The model seat's own check of field names follows the schema; what a seat is told it can search by is as it was (so earlier pilots' prompts are unchanged).
  - **The audit** (every kind of seat in the generated town: what it is shown by default that is a whole register or a whole population, what its code does with it, and whether its counterpart would hold it).

    | Who (how many) | Shown whole, by default | What the code uses it for | Would a real one have it? | Now |
    |---|---|---|---|---|
    | Citizens (everyone) | `lots`, `titles` (every claim, transfer and release) | Their own home's lot and title; a title passed to them (bought, inherited) | No | **On request; searched by id** |
    | Citizens | `work`: every enterprise agreement | Their own employer's, since they started | No: their own workplace's | **On request; their employer's read at each plan** |
    | Citizens | `prices`: every price list, every property for sale | Every shop compared at each shopping trip; the cheapest home for sale; premises for a venture | Partly: shops one uses, adverts one reads when buying | Not changed (below) |
    | Citizens | `gatherings`, `teachings`, `elections` (with the electoral roll and the paper), `counts`, `laws` | Each read as published | Public notices and the paper, yes; the whole roll, no (one checks one's own enrolment) | Not changed (below) |
    | Letting offices (one a landlord) | `lots` | The cells of their own lots | No: their own deeds and plans | **On request; own lots by id** |
    | Letting offices | `own-sites`: their own cells, by name; every cell of the town if they had no land | Their own buildings and tenants | Own buildings yes; the town no | **No land: no channel** (no landlord in the generated towns was without land) |
    | Builder's foreman (one) | `lots` | The lots named in its contracts | No: the plan of its site | **On request; a job's lot by id** |
    | Builder's foreman | `ground`: every cell, weekly | Free cells on a job's lot; its own sites | No: it goes to its sites | Not changed (below) |
    | Planning officer, inspector, registrar, valuer, environment office | `lots`, the claims registry, a survey or patrol of every cell | The register, permits, new building, valuations, deaths' land | Yes: they keep or work from the register (the survey and the patrol are free and complete, which is the unreal part) | Not changed |
    | Water corporation | `lots`, the claims registry, every home's residents | Bills each property's owner, by how many live there | Partly: a customer file a property, and meters, not who slept where | Not changed (below) |
    | Every office of the town (mayor, finance, works, rates, environment) | `mine`: whatever names the town, which is every lot and every title the town issued | Only the mayor reads the land in it, for the town's own free lots | The town's own file, yes; its finance office needs none of it | Not changed (below) |
    | Employers, the bank, the energy retailer, the clinic, school and police | `deaths`: every death registered; the energy retailer every departure | Their own staff, account holders, customers, pupils | No: told by the family, the executor or the registry | Not changed (below) |
    | Bank (lending, payments), council clerk, migration | `who`: every entity and position | The applicant's kind; who wrote an instruction; the councillors; the people it brought | No: a bank knows its customers | Not changed (below) |
    | Funders of the clinic, school, police and town (outside) | A survey of every cell, monthly | One number: the residents | No: a published population | Not changed (below) |
    | Union organiser | Every shop's price list | The mean, as the year's rise in prices | A published index | Not changed |
    | Mayor (code or model), model seats | `town`: every cell, monthly; `files`: every record the town can see, on request; `who` | Roads and parks; the code reads neither `files` nor `who` | A survey of one's own town, yes; `files` is looked up, which is the rule | Not changed |

    Not through channels at all: the weather bureau and the world market are given the run's future weather and price shocks (they are the boundary: they publish each when its time comes); the union is given every employer at the start (shops founded later are never bargained with); landlords are given every shop's account (to spend their owners' draw at one).
  - **What changed, and how each look-up happens.**
    - *A citizen's own papers come unasked* (`mine`): a title it holds, a transfer of a title to it or by it, the valuation of its lot, rates recorded against it.
    - *The plan of its lot:* the first time it plans holding a title whose lot it has never seen the plan of, it searches `lots` for that lot's id (one search, in the same look as the boards).
    - *A title passed to it* (a home bought, land inherited): it searches `titles` for the title the transfer names and `dealings` (transfers and releases) for those naming that title, reads them in the order made, and counts the land as its own if each transfer was made by whoever held the title then, or by its issuer (as `ownerOf` reads a whole register). Then the plan, as above. Asked again only when something new passes it the title.
    - *The lot under its own home* is the one its title names (before: the register searched for the lot over the building's cell).
    - *Buying and selling are as they were:* listings are still shown (below); a buyer pays on the listing's word and knows the home is theirs when the transfer has reached them and the register bears it out.
    - *An enterprise agreement* is read at work: in work, each plan's look asks `work` for the agreements at its own employer made since it started. A pay at the new rate that came before the rise was read of counts, once it is, as wages paid as agreed.
    - *A letting office* looks up the plan of each lot it holds, a look each, before it first plans; *the builder* the plan of a lot a job names. The stepper every office is built on can now ask for looks before it plans (`stepper(absorb, plan, until, looks)`), keeping what followed from its last act over them.
    - Each seat is told its channels onto the register by a parameter (`register`, `work_board`); without one it looks nothing up and knows what it is shown, as in a world that shows it the register (the hand-built worlds of the tests).
  - **Four things found on the way, each a mechanism.**
    - *Addressing an enterprise agreement to the staff it covers woke them all.* That was the first way tried (an employer does tell its employees). A record that names a person wakes them, a wake for news is a plan, and the week's plan is counted from the last: a whole workplace then planned its week at the same moment from then on. The deaths test (deaths at home registered within a fortnight, which turns on when people are home) fell from 14 of 18 to 13. Taken out; the agreement is read at work instead.
    - *Reading the agreement some days late left a correct pay unrecognised.* A pay at the new rate that arrived before the employee's next plan matched no wage they knew of, and what they learn of an employer that pays as agreed was one lesson short: in one seed of three, a year parted from the old behaviour on day 65, when someone who had left a factory chose another to apply to. Now such a pay counts once the rise is read of.
    - *In the old behaviour, a rise agreed before an employee first woke was never learned, and they resigned as unpaid.* Found in the 10,000-person month, where the union's first agreements (day 0.4) come before many citizens' first wake (day 1). A citizen shown the agreement at its first look did not yet know its job (it learns that in its first plan), passed the agreement over, and was never shown it again; its pay at the new rate then matched no wage it knew, and after a pay period it resigned, "our wages haven't been paid". 38 people did in the old month of 10,000; none does now, because the look asks for every agreement since they started, whenever it was made. That is why that month differs in substance (1,157,258 wakes for 1,158,357). At 1,000 and 3,000 people no agreement came that early, and nothing differs. (A year of 1,000 people has 38 such resignations too, before and after alike, so from another cause: not looked into.)
    - *A look of its own costs a turn.* The register was first asked before the boards, in a look of its own. Acts in one moment are carried out round by round, and an owner's first acts then came a round later: the support office answered pension claims in another order, a pensioner's decision reached them a moment later, and their first shopping trip drew another shop. The 3,000-person month parted from the old behaviour on its first day for that alone. Asked in the same look as the boards, nothing moves.
  - **Measured** (the run server, `/usr/bin/time -l`, one seed, 30 days; before is main at c317017. Other agents' test suites had the machine at a load of 20 to 34 for the first baseline runs, so the baseline was run again in the same quiet hour as the changed code, and those are the figures given; a run's wall time still varied by a fifth between runs of one code).

    | | Wall | Kernel | Modules | Most memory held | Final state |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | 1,000 people, before | 17.1, 14.4 s | 7.7, 6.4 s | 3.3, 2.8 s | 1.36, 1.37 GB | 7596cffa9d339a58 |
    | 1,000 people, after | 11.2, 11.2 s | 4.7, 4.7 s | 2.1, 2.1 s | 1.38, 1.37 GB | 1fee363488718647 |
    | 3,000 people, before | 63.0, 48.6 s | 31.1, 24.4 s | 12.3, 9.1 s | 2.88, 3.05 GB | d1f018ee3c2cf458 |
    | 3,000 people, after | 40.0, 40.8 s | 17.6, 17.8 s | 7.9, 8.1 s | 2.84, 2.84 GB | 4f1d8ddcfe24cfed |
    | 10,000 people, before | 612 s (951 s under load) | 332 s | 119 s | 10.7 GB (9.4 to 9.8 GB sampled) | d33f1f4c9dd5ac32 |
    | 10,000 people, after | 243, 383 s | 101, 161 s | 54, 90 s | 9.9, 9.6 GB (9.9, 9.7 GB sampled) | 29ace6951a9053aa |

    Time falls by a quarter to a third at 1,000 and 3,000 people and by about half at 10,000 (the kernel no longer builds, writes and hashes both registers for every citizen's first look, nor the host reads them); memory does not (the books already held the registers once between them, since the entry above). At 1,000 and 3,000 people the month is the old month plus the looks: the same wakes (116,354 and 344,883) and refusals, 105 and 344 more commands (the letting offices' and the builder's looks; a citizen's are in a look it already made). **At 10,000 people it is not the old month,** and the reason is a fault in the old one (next).

    Each new hash twice. The 10,000-person month's memory is the run's and its children's together, sampled every five seconds under a guard of 40 GB.
  - **Outcomes, a year of the 1,000-person town, three seeds before and after** (`oikos diff`, 53 measures; `oikos health`; and from the logs: leases, rents, listings, homes for sale, titles passed, shop prices). **Nothing moved.** Seeds 2 and 3 are the same run but for the looks: the same wakes (1,393,360 and 1,450,898), and every measure equal to the last digit (leases 22 and 21, move-ins 41 and 47, median rent signed, listings standing at the end 0 and 1, homes for sale 0 and 2, none sold, shop prices, employment 62.5% and 61.9%, unemployment 2.7% and 2.1%). Seed 1 is the same too (1,414,365 wakes; 20 leases, 52 move-ins, one title passed to an heir, one home listed and unsold, unemployment 4.9%) but for one thing: the town was paid 11.10 more over the year. Two landlords each skipped a different small rates bill: whether a landlord pays a bill is a draw keyed by the bill's record number (`obeys("fine-" + id)`, with the generated town's landlords complying half the time), and the letting offices' looks at their first wake put their first acts some rounds later in that moment, which renumbers the records made in it. That is the order of acts in a moment, not anyone knowing less. Health: 5 of 15 measures off, the same five by the same amounts. Final states: 8bd31696f5c94119, e4e8e9a30bfc4e10, c58c5741029e0eb1 before; 3a109f2cfd3bcf3a, 47dd408945d3898e, 88e0a5d329d1f825 after.
    - *Is that what knowing less should do?* Yes, and it says how little the register was used: in a year of 1,000 people the register of lots was searched 285 times in all (150 owners and one heir, once each; the letting offices' 104 lots; some 30 of the builder's jobs), and `titles` and `dealings` once each; 150 owners hold one title each, one title was passed, no home was sold. People still know everything they acted on. What they no longer hold is what they never read. The same goes for the agreements: 51,000 to 58,000 looks at the board at work a year (in the look every plan already makes), for the 24 to 40 agreements made.
    - *What this does not show:* a town where homes change hands often (none sold here in a year; the home-market test's town is the only run that exercises a purchase end to end, and passes); or 3,000 and 10,000 people over a year.
  - **Not fixed, with what each would take** (each is a view the audit found; none is as contained as the above).
    - *Every shop's prices, shown to everyone* (and every home and shop premises for sale). Shopping compares every shop in town at every trip, which is also a cost that grows with the town. Lengnick's households know a handful of firms; ours were given the mind's choice among all of them (docs/design/ai-citizens.md 3b). Narrowing it is a change to how people shop (which shops one knows, how one learns of another), needing a rule with a source: not a look-up. Homes for sale alone would be contained (a search when the month's chance of buying comes up, and of one listing while a mortgage is waited on; premises for a venture read by every adult every month, as the petitions board is): three flows and a second board, left for a decision with the prices.
    - *Deaths, shown to every employer, the bank, the energy retailer and the support office.* Each wants the deaths of its own people. The registry (or the family, who already report the death) would have to tell those the dead person had dealings with, which it can't know: it needs the bank, employer and landlord to be named on the notification, or each of them to ask. A change to the registry's and the household's rules.
    - *The town's offices see every lot and title through `mine`* (the town issued them, and "involving" counts whom a record speaks for). Narrowing it needs either titles issued by a registry that is its own entity (a change to the generator and to `ownerOf`'s "or its issuer") or a channel filter for "addressed to" (a contract change).
    - *The builder's weekly walk of the whole ground; the water corporation's count of who lives in every home; the funders' survey of every cell for one number; the inspector's free and complete patrol.* A channel's area is fixed when the world is made, so "the lots of my jobs" can't be said: it needs areas that follow a seat's records (a contract change), or these offices given staff who go and look, at a cost in hours (the works crew and the environment office already do).
    - *The directory, for the bank and the clerk.* A bank needs the kind of one applicant and who may sign for one account: a search of the directory by entity (a directory channel can't be searched today: a contract change), or the customer saying so on the application.
    - *The union's list of employers* is the start's: a shop founded later is never bargained with. A mechanism gap, found in the audit, not a view.
    - *An heir sells an inherited house only if they can see it from where they live or work* (`street`): already so, and now written down (ownership.md).
    - *A landlord's compliance draw is keyed by a record's number,* so any change to the order of acts in a moment flips some coins (the 11.10 above). Keying it by what the bill is for (its lot and year) would make it steady; it changes what runs do, so it is Naz's.
  - **Not verified:** anything with a model seat (a seat's channels and prompts are unchanged, and the suite's model-seat tests pass, but no model run was made; a model citizen would have to make these searches itself); a run resumed from a log made before this (its seats' channels are in its own header, so it runs as it was configured); a purchase's title search beyond the home-market test; a year at 3,000 or 10,000 people; a world whose titles are dealt in more than 20 times (`search_limit`); the sources marked "(to confirm)" in ownership.md and references.md (the Transfer of Land Act sections, Land Use Victoria's search, the Fair Work Act section).
  - **Revisit if** a search fee is wanted (the registry would have to do the work: an office that answers a search request and is paid for it, in place of the kernel's mechanical search); buyers should search a title before paying (a conveyancer would: two more searches a purchase, and a listing by someone who isn't the owner then fails before money moves); a seat needs several records of one channel in one look (one search a channel a look today: a letting office with many lots takes a look each); model citizens arrive (the look-ups above are the code citizen's: a model one needs to be told the register can be searched and by what); or the remaining views above are taken up, most of which wait on the prices and deaths decisions.

- **Requests in a Claude seat's voice, and what closed the permit gap** (2026-10-08; prompt 0.7.6, `data-16`, `pod-small-d16-s1`, `-s2`).
  - **Written in situ by the seat's own model.** From pilot 10's log, 199 situations (the 17 seat-days on which a seat made one of its 39 real requests left out); writers Sonnet and Opus as the seat. Round 1 (59 situations, the writers' instructions as before): 448 requests, 358 agreed by two readers under 0.7.5: 206 not an act (asking to be shown), 91 letters, 31 forms, 7 payments, no claim. Round 2 (68 planning and finance situations, with `writer-round2.txt` asking for what waits on the seat's own decision: applications decided, bills paid, permits given, at most two requests to be shown): 266 requests, 189 agreed: 2 claims, 4 payments. A writer said why there are so few grants: the situations don't show who owns the lots, and a careful seat won't grant without that; the real seat granted on the days it had looked ownership up, which are the days held back.
  - **The rule for `basis` sharpened (0.7.6).** Under 0.7.5 the two readers gave the same program for 26 of pilot 10's 39 requests (10 under 0.7.4). On the grants they still split: is the application granted a basis or the thing acted on; is a law the request says the act meets a basis. 0.7.6 says: what the act answers or decides is cited where no other argument takes it; a law it is done under or meets is cited like a record; a record the request says is left alone is not. Where two readers' programs differ in `basis` alone, the example's basis is every record or law either gave, in the request's order (ids only, never a phrase): 12 in round 2, 13 of pilot 10's own. Not re-read under 0.7.6: the union stands in for it.
  - **Pilot 10's own requests, part released.** Of its 39, 27 are agreed (10 claims, 9 payments, 4 forms, 3 no such act, 1 letter). 19 go to training; every third of each kind, 8, is held back (`pilot-10-requests/held-back.jsonl`). They are the same seat on neighbouring days saying the same kind of thing, so the held-back eight show that the pattern is learned, not that a new town's seat will be read.
  - **Result.** `data-16` (data-13's examples plus those 566, four times): two seeds. On the eight held back: 8 of 8 right (the model before: 2 of 8, the claims refused as too much). On the nineteen trained on: 19 of 19. On the standing tests: real 145 and 143 of 180, held out 40 and 44 of 66, seen 51 and 51 of 67 (three seen cases and three real keys are new with several bases; data-13's three seeds had 147 to 148, 44 to 47, 50 to 52 of 64). So the Claude-seat pattern is in, at a cost of two to four on the real test, inside or at the edge of the spread.
  - **What this says:** nineteen real examples of an act closed what 611 written ones in my phrasing did not. What a seat model will write cannot be guessed; it has to be collected from that model in the seat, and a little of it goes a long way.
  - **Not verified:** a Claude seat's requests from a town or days the model has not been near (the next pilot); the two-to-four fall on the real test over more seeds; 0.7.6 with any model as the translator (no real model has been run with 0.7.5 or 0.7.6: the pilots' own translator used 0.7.4).
  - **Revisit if** the next pilot's seat is read no better than pilot 10's was before this (then the pattern learned is these nineteen, not the act); or the union of two readers' bases over-cites (records mentioned, not relied on) when checked against a seat's confirmations.

- **Asked back, the participant is told what was unclear, and nothing it didn't know** (2026-10-08; `translator/src/index.ts` `unsureText`, `translator/model/adapter.ts`). Naz: the translator "should provide reasonable feedback, but nothing that can be used to 'cheat the system' i.e. learn information that a caller otherwise wouldn't know"; and one model that works, no hand-off to a larger one.
  - **What was there:** questions the resolver asks once a call is understood are already particular and bounded by what the participant knows ("Which account should it come from: acc-1 or acc-2?"; "The council proposal needs: proposal"). The gap was the translator's own `none(unclear)`: one sentence whatever the trouble.
  - **Now** a translator may give, beside `none(unclear)`, what was unclear as one of a few kinds (`unsure` in its answer): the act, which thing is meant, which words are to be copied, a form's required field left out, a field the form doesn't have, a value a choice field can't take. The sentence the participant reads is made in `unsureText` from fixed words and the participant's own forms: a form it hasn't got, or a field its form doesn't have, is not said (the plain sentence stands and the translator is asked again as before), and a translator's own words are never passed on. Since a translator sees only the request and those forms, nothing said can be news about the world.
  - **The trained model** says which of the three it was least sure of (from the parts of its sureness: what it chose, where ids stand, where copied text begins and ends) when it is under the cut-off, and what about a form doesn't fit when the form check stops a draft. With what was unclear said, the translator isn't asked a second time: it would answer the same.
  - **Seen** (`pod-small-d16-s1`, cut-off 0.5, real test): 131 right, 7 wrong drafts, 42 not drafted, as before the change; of the requests it asked back for its own unsureness, 15 were which thing is meant, 9 the act, 4 the words.
  - **Not verified:** that a seat told "name it by its id" or "put the words in quotation marks" then gets through (no model was run: the first pilot with the trained translator in the loop is the test, counted as requests through within two tries); that "least sure part" is the part a person would call unclear.
  - **Revisit if** seats repeat the same request after a sentence (then the sentence doesn't say what to change, and the kinds want splitting or wording); or a kind is found to tell a seat something by its presence (asking back "which one" where two things share a name the seat hasn't seen: the model points only at the request's own words, so it shouldn't, but a pilot's log would show it).
- **Who owns firms: owners, the outside world, a retirement fund and a few direct holders, in place of every household in every firm. Proposed, on a branch, for Naz's review; not merged** (decided the same day: in, with the retirement fund off; two entries down) (2026-10-08; `worlds/generator/generate.ts` 0.24.0, `profile.ts`, `worlds/profiles/au-vic-2026.json`, `participants/src/behaviour/fund.ts` (`fund.retirement` 0.1.0), docs/behaviour/firm-ownership.md). Naz, of every household holding shares in every firm: "that seems... not right. this is not how things are in the real world". It was also the larger of the two things that grew with the square of the town (the entry of 2026-10-07).
  - **Contracts: none changed** (`contracts/` is untouched: a register is a statement, the fund an organisation with an account and an office, `retirement_fund` a kind of entity in the world's own vocabulary). **The profile's shape did change, and every profile must follow:** `wealth_quintile_shares_bps` and `outside_ownership_bps` are gone; `firm_ownership` replaces them. A new module, `fund.retirement`, with its manifest. A new statement kind the fund keeps, `fund_members`, and one it publishes, `fund_report`.
  - **What it was.** Each household's head held a part of every factory, shop and landlord, by the household's fifth of savings (the ABS shares of net worth), and a part of each firm was held from outside (80%, 20%, 30%: placeholders). In the 1,000-person town 430 households were on each of 60 registers; a year's dividends were 400 instructions of 430 payments each, 172,000 of the year's 237,000 payments of any kind. Nothing else reads a holding: no vote, no measure, no tax rule (a citizen counts whatever comes in as income). The design this came from asked for "a few households" (docs/design/phase-c-stakes.md); what was built gave every household a share.
  - **What is proposed** (docs/behaviour/firm-ownership.md has it whole, with each figure's source). A firm is held in one of three ways, the share of each kind of firm held each way being the profile's. *Closely:* by one household or a few, its owners, drawn mostly from the wealthiest fifth (80% of shops, 70% of landlords). *From outside:* a chain's branch, an absent investor (the rest of the shops and landlords; what they pay leaves town). *Widely:* the factories; 80% from outside as before, and the town's fifth by the retirement fund for its members (72% of it) and by the third of households that hold shares directly (8% of the poorest fifth to 72% of the wealthiest), one to four firms each, by their savings. The fund is an institution with a trustee's office: monthly it takes what the factories paid it, pays its costs (15% of what came in, to its administrator outside), credits the rest to members by their units, keeps a working member's part, pays a retired member's as a pension (with what was kept for them before, at the least yearly rate for their age), and on a death pays what was kept to the estate. It takes no contributions.
  - **What it is based on** (each opened 2026-10-08; docs/research/references.md): 97.4% of Australian businesses have under 20 employees (ABS, Counts of Australian Businesses, June 2026); 38% of households held shares directly in 2006 and 34% in 2010, 8% of the poorest fifth and 72% of the wealthiest, who held about four-fifths of the value, and 85% of business assets (RBA Bulletin, April 2009 and March 2012); 38% of adults hold listed investments (ASX, 2023); households' retirement savings are 4,433 billion against 1,750 billion of shares and other equity (ABS, Finance and Wealth, June 2026), and 84% of households have them (RBA Bulletin, June 2016); funds paid 143.5 billion in benefits in a year, as pensions and lump sums to the retired (APRA, March 2026), a pension being at least 4% to 14% of the balance a year by age (Moneysmart); 70% of housing investors own one property (RBA Bulletin, May 2026). The same shape in the United States: 21% of families hold stocks directly, the mean holding 27 times the median, 20% own a private business (Federal Reserve, 2023). Not confirmed at the source: family businesses as 67% of businesses (KPMG), fund fees of 1.1% of assets (Productivity Commission). Not found: who owns a regional town's shops and factories; the outside shares stay the placeholders they were.
  - **Measured: the starting data and the payments.**

    | People | Lines on all the registers | The registers | The whole config | Payments if every firm pays once |
    |---|---|---|---|---|
    | 1,000 (60 firms) | 25,800 to 448 | 1.62 MB to 42 KB | 9.64 to 8.11 MB | 25,800 to 448 |
    | 3,000 (180) | 224,460 to 1,338 | 13.96 MB to 126 KB | 38.3 to 24.7 MB | 224,460 to 1,338 |
    | 10,000 (600) | 2,472,000 to 4,756 | 151.9 MB to 0.44 MB | 244.0 to 93.0 MB | 2,472,000 to 4,756 |

    The fund's register of members is one record more (49 KB at 1,000 people; by the same count half a megabyte at 10,000), and its pensions one instruction a month (about 140 payments at 1,000). In the 1,000-person year (seed 1): the same 400 dividend instructions, 172,000 payments before and 1,864 after, with 1,554 pension payments in 11 instructions; all payments in the year, three seeds, 240,663 to 84,735.
  - **Measured: the 1,000-person month.** Final state 7596cffa9d339a58 before, c99f62688e44966b after (the same twice). The month holds no dividend: the first are paid at day 30, where it stops. So it shows only the smaller start: before, alone on a quiet machine, 12.3 s (kernel 5.6 s, modules 2.3 s, 1.35 GB at most); then, with other sessions' runs on the machine (load 15 to 25), back to back: 34.5 s, kernel 16.4 s, modules 7.2 s, 1.33 GB before; 36.0 s, 17.3 s, 6.7 s, 1.34 GB after. No difference that can be told from the machine's. Over 65 days (two rounds of dividends) the most memory held went from 1.67 to 1.27 GB, the log from 425 to 409 MB, payments from 37,501 to 17,141; the times (93 and 131 s, the load rising between them) say nothing.
  - **Measured: a year of the 1,000-person town, three seeds, main against this** (`oikos diff`, and a count from the logs of the households there at the start and still in town at the end, about 373 of 429). Beyond seed noise (three standard errors):

    | | Before | After |
    |---|---|---|
    | Households paid a dividend | 374 (all) | 147; and 104 a pension from the fund |
    | Household income: 10th percentile, median, 90th | 51,000, 104,700, 163,200 | 35,800, 80,600, 177,900 (the 90th: z 2.2) |
    | Its Gini; the top fifth's share | 0.264; 34% | 0.330; 39% |
    | The most dividends any household had | 61,600 | 242,100 |
    | Spent at the shops, the median household; its Gini | 77,600; 0.325 | 63,200; 0.373 |
    | Adults in town at the end | 740 | 731 |

    Within seed noise: mean household income (101,200 to 96,500), all households' spending at the shops (29.0 to 28.1 million, 3% less), jobs held (455 to 448), unemployment (3.2% to 2.6%), firms closed (one in three runs, both), households' savings and their spread (Gini 0.540 to 0.543; the top tenth's share, 33% to 34%, is beyond noise and small), rent stress, saving.
  - **Is each what the change should do?**
    - *Incomes more spread, the median down a quarter:* yes. Dividends were a fifth of household income and reached everyone, a universal payment in all but name, largest for the wealthiest fifth (mostly retirees with large savings: about 60,000 a year each). They now go to 40 owning households (189,000 a year on average, against 81,000 for a household holding nothing) and in small sums to direct holders. Not aimed at: Australia's Gini for equivalised disposable household income is 0.32 (ABS, 2019-20), which is not the same measure as this one (gross, per household).
    - *Total spending held:* three seeds can't show a 3% fall. Owners spent 78% of their income at the shops and households holding nothing 75%: citizens here spend much the same share of income whatever its size. Under a rule by which the rich save more, concentrating dividends would cut spending; here it only moves it.
    - *Ten fewer adults, more departures (93 to 101), fewer moves:* probably the households out of work that used to live on dividends and now leave sooner. Consistent, **not traced**: a finding, not a fix.
    - *Fewer leases and moves (21 to 16 leases, z -2.3; 47 to 35 move-ins, z -1.9):* within noise by the rule used here, and unexplained. A payment arriving doesn't wake anyone (only a message, a record naming them, or their own body does), so it is not that households plan less often for want of dividends; poorer households moving less is the likelier reading. Not traced.
    - *The fund keeps 1.1 to 2.0 million after a year,* the working members' part, with nothing to do with it. It is 2% of what households hold, and grows by about that each year: a slow drain on the town's spending that a real fund doesn't make (it invests what it holds). This is the mechanism's own weakness and the main thing to decide.
  - **What I'd ask Naz to decide.** (1) Whether the town's part of its factories should stand for households' shares and retirement savings at all, or whether those should be holdings in the outside world that pay in from outside (truer; new money entering the town, to be set as export demand is). (2) Whether the fund should exist before there is something for it to invest in: without it, the direct holders have the town's whole part of the factories (a profile with no `firm_ownership.fund` does that) and nothing lies idle. (3) Whether retirees' large starting savings, which stand for a retirement balance, should move into the fund and be paid as a pension. (4) The outside shares, still unsourced.
  - **One test of the whole suite fails on this branch, and it is not one to change: the deaths test** (`orchestrator/test/deaths.test.ts`: deaths at home registered within a fortnight, at least 80%). 13 of 19 here; 14 of 18 on main, which is its floor exactly. The same people die at the same moments in both. It is the weakness the entry of 2026-10-05 found and left for Naz ("that test has been passing on timing"): a death at home is seen only in a full plan made while it is still among the home's last five departures, and commuting overwrites those in a day or two. Followed here for one death (c0183, day 180.5, in both runs): on main the adult child at home was asked by a neighbour about room at work a day later, planned in full, and reported it; here nobody asked, the next wake only took them to work, and it was never registered. Nothing in this change touches how a death is seen. Not loosened, not fixed (it is the citizen's, and the earlier fix uncovered the next): **the decision on the sight of a death comes before this can merge.**
  - **Tests.** New: the generator's ownership (each firm held one of the three ways as the profile says; shares add up; no household on more than five registers, most on none; the fund's members adults with units); the fund's trustee stepped by hand (costs first; kept below the access age and paid from it; drawing down at the rate for the age; a death; a member who left). Changed: the factories' dividend test compared the first two holders to within 1% and now checks every holder exactly (each paid its share of one amount, rounded down), and that the fund's pensions go only to members of the retirement age. The crime test asked that some tribunal order to pay had been paid; those ordered are people in need, who had dividends before and now don't, and the one order of that run goes unpaid (its debtor never has the sum beside a month's essentials). It now checks every order: paid from the debtor to the account named, the sum ordered; or unpaid and the debtor never had the sum for a week.
  - **Not verified:** a run at 3,000 or 10,000 people on this (only generated and counted: the machine was shared, and one simulation at a time); the times, for the same reason; anything with a model seat (a model citizen would see the registers it is on, and nothing tells it of its fund membership until a pension comes); a run resumed from a log (the fund keeps what it holds for each member in memory, as other modules keep theirs); runs past a year (the fund's first yearly report comes at day 390; over decades its members die and nobody joins); the small town beyond its tests; households that arrived during the year (left out of the income counts); whether the wealthiest fifth holding 71% of direct shares, against about four-fifths in Australia, holds across seeds; holdings' skew (the mean direct holding is 1.1 times the median here, 27 times in the United States: savings here are far more even than real wealth, homes not being counted).
  - **Revisit if** shares come to be traded or valued (the fund could then invest what it keeps and pay from capital, and units could be balances); employers' contributions are wanted (a law, a cost on every wage bill, and the fund's reason to exist); a second profile is added (its `firm_ownership` is the first thing to source; the fund's rules were chosen with one country's in mind, as docs/profiles.md now lists); a town is generated with no household able to own a business (those firms go to outside, silently); owner-managers are to work in their shops; or the ten missing adults turn out not to be households leaving for want of dividends.
- **A death at home is made known for a reason, not by the timing of a plan** (2026-10-08; `citizen.default` 0.36.0 and 0.36.1, `firm.landlord` 0.12.3, `police.station` 0.2.0; docs/behaviour/deaths.md; no contract change). The entry of 2026-10-06 said the deaths test "has been passing on timing"; the firm-ownership branch then failed it (13 of 19 where main had 14 of 18, its floor) with the same people dying at the same moments. This is the fix of the mechanism, made on main before that branch comes in.
  - **What it was.** An adult looked for a death at home only inside the week's full plan. The kernel shows a home's last five departures, and a household's own going to work fills five within a day or two, so a death was seen only if somebody's weekly plan fell in that window; a message from a neighbour moving one plan by a day changed who was ever registered. Two more faults sat beside it. A landlord, who is shown who is in its buildings, reported a tenant's death the moment it saw one, often before the family (5 of the test town's 14 registrations): a landlord's report names no family, so the bank passed the estate to the town with a partner alive. And someone living alone in a home of their own was never found by anyone (2 of 18 in the test town; 7 of 22 deaths at home in the 1,000-person year went unregistered): the bank went on paying their orders for good.
  - **What it is now.** (1) *Those who share the home:* an adult looks for it at every wake at home. Of the surviving adults at home then, the first in the household's list reports it and writes the household's new record (it was the first in the list wherever they were); one who comes home later finds the record changed. Seeing a death makes that wake's plan the week's full plan. (2) *Anyone outside reports only a death in a home that has stood empty:* nobody in it, and nobody in or out for a day (`stoodEmpty`). Where people share a home one of them moves within a day and it is theirs to report. The landlord's report now waits for that. (3) *Someone with nobody at home is found by the police* (or first by their landlord, if they rent): each working day the police look at the town's homes, and a death in one that has stood empty is reported once the time it takes to be found has passed, drawn for each person from the profile's `life.found_alone_after` and costing an officer an hour of that day.
  - **On what basis.** Whoever lives with someone knows first, and a doctor must tell the registrar within 48 hours (Births, Deaths and Marriages Victoria: read). For someone alone, the one count found of how long it takes is Japan's (National Police Agency, 2025: 37% found the same or next day, 21% in 2 to 3 days, 14% in 4 to 7, 29% later, 9% after a month or more), read in a press summary, not the agency's tables: marked to confirm in the profile, and a placeholder for a local figure (none was found for Victoria or Australia).
  - **Why the police, and what is wrong with it.** In a real town somebody calls the police: a relative, a neighbour, the post. None of those is in the world, so the time until someone calls is the figure, and the police are given what lets them act on it: a channel showing who is in each of the town's homes and who last left, by name, seen only when asked for (`on_request`). That is more than a station knows, and the reason this is the part to look at again. The alternatives were worse or larger: neighbours seeing into next door (every citizen shown more, while another change is reworking what citizens are shown); a death shown by the kernel to someone by rule (a contract change); or leaving people who own their home and live alone unfound.
  - **The test** (`orchestrator/test/deaths.test.ts`) no longer asks for "80% within a fortnight". It asks that every death at home with another adult of the household alive and in town is registered within three days on a housemate's report (13 of 13; the longest 2.3 days, over a weekend), and that every death with nobody at home that is registered was reported by the landlord or the police and not within a day (5: landlords at 3.1 and 7.2 days, the police at 2.3, 4.3 and 25.4). No floor is set on how fast the police are, since that is the profile's distribution; `participants/test/police.test.ts` holds them to their times on a home shown by hand (not before the home has stood empty, not before the drawn time, never where someone lives, never twice). One refusal is now allowed there by name: an act whose doer died before it was done (`participant_inactive`, for that person only); it appeared because the run is a different one, and is what should happen.
  - **Measured** (`worlds/town-1000.yaml`). The month of seed 1 ends at b298aa3fba5d01af, twice (was 7596cffa9d339a58). A year, three seeds, before against after (`oikos diff`): of 53 measures one is past three standard errors, the share of people firm in the religious group's teaching at the end (10.1% to 11.6%), with no cause found and nothing in the change that touches it but the moment some people plan: taken as chance among 53 with three seeds, and recorded. Deaths, from the logs: 22 at home before, 15 registered (15 of the 21 with a fortnight left, within it); 23 after, 22 registered (21 of 22), one by the police after 229 days (the longest band) and one who died on the last day. Reported by a housemate 15 (10 before), a landlord 2 (5), the police 5 (0). Estates settled 22 (15); money to the town from estates and all else 1.75 to 2.16 million a seed on average (inside seed noise; more estates of people with no heir in the world are settled). Seed 1 posted 1,083 jobs and made 832 hires where it had 628 and 510; main's own seeds span 400 to 695, the tool counts it inside noise, and it was not chased.
  - **Added the same day, found when the firm-ownership change came in (citizen 0.36.1): a child tells an adult.** With that change the test town runs differently, and one death in a shared home went unreported: a family new to town took a home, the leaseholder moved in with their child of 15 and died the next day, before the step that tells the rest of the household where the home is. Her partner, never told, stayed at his workplace for the rest of the year; the child came and went from the home, so it never stood empty, and a child reported nothing while an adult of the household lived.
