  - **Limits.** `bunfig.toml` sets 120 s per test for the whole suite (no test in the repo asked for less than 30 s; the short timeouts in `adapters.test.ts` and `reliability.test.ts` are arguments to the code under test, not bun limits). The explicit 30 s and 60 s limits are now 180 s and the 900 s ones 2 400 s. A hang still fails, only later. Assertions are untouched.
  - **Runner.** Files run at once = cores less the current one-minute load average (counting its own running files), at most cores minus two, at least one; `--jobs N` fixes it. A file whose every failure is a bun "timed out after" (and no other error) is run again once, alone, at the end, and printed as `PASSED ON RERUN (timed out under load)`; a file that fails any other way is never rerun, and one that times out on its rerun fails. The final line keeps its form and gains `, R passed on rerun`.
  - **Preconditions.** Before anything, the runner installs the root and cel-check dependencies if absent and builds the kernel (`bun run kernel`) if the binary or the law wasm module is missing or older than the newest `.rs`/`Cargo` file under `kernel/`; failing that it stops with one `PRECONDITION:` line. `--no-build` makes a stale kernel a stop instead of a build.
  - **Killed runs.** On SIGTERM, SIGINT or SIGHUP the runner stops the files it started and prints `stopped by signal S after N of F files (not a failure of the tests, and no result)` as its last line (exit 143), so a killed run is not taken for a hang or a failure. (A signal during the kernel's cargo phase is handled when cargo returns.) This came from another agent's `pkill -f "scripts/test.ts"` killing every runner on the machine. **Agents must never kill by pattern** (`pkill -f`, `killall`): kill only a process id you started.
  - **Untimed files.** A file not in `scripts/test-times.json` counts as slow, so it is outside `--fast`; the runner now says so, naming them, and `--time-new` runs just those and records their times.
  - **Not verified:** that the load-based job count is right (loadavg lags about a minute; it is a heuristic, not a control loop); "only timeouts" is read from bun's output text, so a change in its wording would send timeouts back to being plain failures (visible, not hidden); a test that is slow because of a real regression is also rerun once, and passes if the machine was merely quiet then.
  - **Revisit if** a file keeps appearing as passed on rerun (it is too slow for its limit or shares state with its neighbours: fix it, don't lean on the rerun), or the suite is run on a machine where load average is not comparable to cores.
- **Pilot waves, second step: alerts, a mock dry run, worktree housekeeping, and the ask-back recorded** (2026-10-08; `orchestrator/src/pilot-alerts.ts`, `pilot-state.ts` `scanOptOuts`, `scripts/pilot-mock.ts`, `scripts/worktree-clean.ts`, `scripts/pilot-wave.ts status`; docs/real-models.md "Running many pilots").
  - **Alerts, kept boring.** One append-only `<wave>.alerts.log` of local-time lines, a macOS notification, and `OIKOS_ALERT_CMD` for anything else. A seat's opt-out is looked for in the log while the pilot runs (the `opt_out` command, read from the byte offset of the last look, once a minute), not only at the end, so an opt-out like pilot 11's is heard of on the day.
  - **A mock dry run of a wave** runs each entry as written with the mock everywhere, into its own folder, and fails any pilot whose log shows a non-mock seat or translator. It finds an entry that cannot run (a world that does not generate, a bad `--set`, a run that stops) before real usage is spent. The queue entry gets an `args` field for extra `oikos run` arguments: the `scenario:` presets another agent is adding have no mechanism on this branch yet, so I assumed none; once there is one, either `sets` or `args` carries it.
  - **Worktree housekeeping** removes only what is certainly safe: merged into `origin/main`, clean, unlocked, unused, not a wave copy, and holding no run in its own `runs/`. (The listing found a 13 GB `runs/` of test folders in one merged worktree, and the rule that nothing is ever deleted from a runs folder is why test-only is the bar.) `kernel/target` is most of the size and has its own flag.
  - **Ask-back versus refusal is now recorded.** `translation_failure` annotations carry `asked_back: true` when the translator asked back, including the plain "say it more plainly" that the sentence could not tell from a refusal; the report uses it and falls back to the sentence for older logs. This adds a field inside the annotation's `data`, which `contracts/log.schema.json` types as opaque: no contract changed.
  - **Not verified:** the alerts on a live queue (only the done and finished alerts, the hook and the opt-out scan were run, on fixtures); the macOS notification (switched off in tests); `--mock` on a 1,000-person town for 30 days (tried on the small town for one day); `worktree-clean` removing a real worktree here (tried on a throwaway repository; the real repository was only listed).
  - **Revisit if** an alert is missed or is noise; a mock run passes but the real pilot fails for an entry reason (then the mock lacks something); a worktree is removed that was wanted (then the bar is too low).

- **A shared machine that stays usable** (2026-10-08; `scripts/slots.ts`, `scripts/test.ts`, `scripts/worktree-warm.ts`, `kernel/build.rs`, `orchestrator/src/batch.ts`, `docs/dev-machine.md`, `CLAUDE.md`). Eight agents on the 14-core Mac each built the kernel, ran towns and ran the whole suite at once: load 40 to 100 for hours, tests failing by timeout, a suite of under ten minutes taking over an hour. Measured here with every other job paused (load 4 to 6 before each measurement unless said).
  - **The kernel was rebuilt on every cargo invocation in every worktree.** `kernel/build.rs` told cargo to watch `../.git/HEAD`; in a worktree `.git` is a file, the path is never there, and cargo treated the engine as changed each time. `cargo test` with nothing changed took 56 s and 391 core-seconds, `bun run kernel` 5.9 s; with the build script asking git for its directory they take 5.7 s and 0.14 s. This was the largest single waste found, and nobody had asked about it. The build identity (commit, `+dirty`, source fingerprint) is computed as before; the 30-day 1,000-person run ends at 8ae630800d86f847 before and after.
  - **Slots.** A pool of tokens for the whole user, as files outside any repo (`~/.oikos/slots`), each made by a hard link from a finished file (atomic, never seen half written), naming its holder's pid, start, purpose and directory. Cores less two by default. A dead holder's tokens are reclaimed under a short lock; waiters queue oldest first and only the head takes (so a job wanting seven is not starved by a stream of ones; it holds what it has got while it waits, which reserves them); a job that waits says so once a minute. A holder's children take nothing: marked in the environment, and recognised by ancestry where a child is given an environment of its own (bun's `spawnSync` does not pass on a changed `process.env`, which would otherwise have deadlocked a test process against its own child). The test runner takes one for each file running and half the cores for the kernel's tests; `bun test` by hand takes one for the process (a preload in `bunfig.toml`); `oikos run` one for each run under way; `bun run kernel` half the cores, and cargo is told to compile that many at a time. **A run that asks a model takes no slot** (a judgement beyond the brief: a pilot waits days for answers and would hold a token throughout). Under the slots the runner still follows the load average, for work that takes no slot. A 30-day run of 1,000 people ends at the same state hash with slots off, with slots on, and after waiting 25 s for a full pool.
  - **Two suites at once**, from two worktrees: 743 s and 813 s, both passing, no test timed out and none was rerun; the one-minute load averaged 19 and peaked at 36 while both were compiling the kernel's tests (before cargo was told how many to compile at a time). One suite alone on a quiet machine took 468 s before this work. Load sits above the 12 tokens because a test file is a bun process and a kernel process (about 1.3 cores).
  - **One full suite per merge**, written in `CLAUDE.md`, the handover and `docs/dev-machine.md`. `bun run test:changed` runs the fast tier and every other test file that is changed, imports a changed file at any depth (resolved as bun resolves it) or names a changed data file, since the branch left `origin/main`; it prints each choice with its reason and the changed files no test reaches. A kernel change adds the kernel's own tests only: every town test runs the kernel, and choosing them all would be the full suite again. A change to something most tests import (`orchestrator/src/run.ts`, a citizen's behaviour) still chooses most of the suite, and the runner says so.
  - **Tests that generate the same town: no cache built.** All 39 files that took over a minute under load were read. Every one but two tests starts from `worlds/town.yaml` (200 people, seed 1) and nearly all then edit the spec or the config, so their worlds differ. Unedited, the two-year town is run nine times, for 60, 90, 90, 100, 120, 120, 150, 300 and 395 days, and the one-year town (a different town: the weather is drawn for the spec's years) by three for 60, 120 and 365. Only two (retail and gatherings, 90 days, about 20 s each) run the same span. Sharing the rest means serving a prefix of a longer run: a cut log, and a summary (state hash, wake counts) for a span that was never the end of a run. That is new machinery every one of those tests would then depend on, for about 1,200 town-days of 200 people (at 9 s for 90 days, some two core-minutes of a suite that uses about sixty). Not built.
  - **Shortened instead: one test.** `nature.test.ts` ran two one-year towns of 200 people and asserts only on censuses, extinctions, the office's reports and registers; it now runs 80 people, 195 s to 27 s, every assertion as it was. Looked at and left: `perception.test.ts` (163 s; its length is how many looks the invariant is checked over), `elections.test.ts` (212 s; four towns in turn, polling day 200 a choice of the test's, but the editions, nominations and the neglected mayor's loss of support all need the time, and the comments say a smaller town's vote is thin), `migration.test.ts` (209 s; counts over a year in a town already cut to 120), `production.test.ts` (206 s; 300 days because the first dividend is that late), `households.test.ts` (201 s; a year of moves and births). What would shorten those is a decision about what each tests, not housekeeping.
  - **Warm worktrees.** A fresh worktree made ready (install, cel-check install, kernel build) took 31 s and 437 MB with the compile cache alone; cloned copy-on-write from the nearest checkout and then installed and built, 20 s and 77 MB. What is left is the kernel's own crates, which cargo builds from the worktree's sources whatever was cloned. Checked: a kernel source changed in the warmed worktree, with its date set back to 2020, gave a rebuilt binary carrying the change.
  - **cargo nextest: not adopted.** Same 160 tests; with nothing to compile, 1.5 s against `cargo test`'s 5.7 s; when the tests must be compiled, both take what the compiler takes (44 s against 56 s on the build-script bug's rebuilds). Four seconds of a suite is not worth a tool every machine must have. It is installed on this machine (`brew install cargo-nextest`) and can be used by hand.
  - **A check run** (`docs/dev-machine.md`): `--log lean --record-observations none`, and `worlds/town-300.yaml` (the small town one and a half times over). Ninety days: 13 s and 0.8 GB, against 44 s and 1.8 GB for 1,000 people; 116 of 158,280 commands refused, the same share as the other two towns, no health warning.
  - **The runner's precondition** judged the law module stale whenever any kernel file was newer than it, though cargo rightly leaves the module alone when the file is no part of it; after such an edit the runner stopped with a precondition failure for good. Now: both artefacts exist and something was built since the newest source changed.
  - **After:** the whole suite alone, 653 tests in 413 s (load 4 at the start), of which 65 s is the kernel's tests being compiled again because the commit had changed: the build identity carries the commit, so each commit costs one engine build per checkout that builds. Left as it is (log headers name the commit); running the kernel's tests beside the test files rather than before them would take that minute off the suite, and was not done.
  - **Not verified:** the slots under the load that prompted them (eight agents; two suites is what was run); pid reuse (a token whose dead holder's pid has been given to a new process is held until that one ends); a clone taken from a checkout that is in the middle of a build; `town-300` against the calibration targets (it runs and nothing warns; its measures were not compared); that agents will use `test:changed` rather than the whole suite; fairness beyond first come, first served; any machine but this one (APFS, macOS `ps`).
  - **Revisit if** load still sits well above the cores with everything going through slots (then a test file should weigh more than one token, or the pool be smaller); a job is seen waiting on a token whose holder is doing nothing (a hung test process holds its token until it is stopped: `slots.ts status` names it); pilots turn out to load the machine after all (then give model runs a slot); the prefix-of-a-run cache becomes worth it (many more tests on the unedited town); the suite's kernel tests grow past a few seconds (then nextest's gain is real).

- **A town from any place** (2026-10-08; `worlds/generator/generate.ts`, `worlds/generator/profile.ts`, `worlds/profiles/*`, `worlds/law/schedules.json`, `measure/`, `participants/src/behaviour/{firms,producer,retailer,institutions}.ts`, `orchestrator/test/places.test.ts`, `places-run.test.ts`, `retail.test.ts`, `docs/profiles.md`). Eight more profiles were written and their towns ran from 8% to 54% unemployed, by how each author had happened to scale prices and opening sums against wages. Measured on the 1,000-person town, 60 days, seed 1, each tree with a kernel built from it (the before runs: the tree before this change with this checkout's kernel, whose sources are the same; the first profile's ends at 2ba4ef479b56b016, as main's does).
  - **What set the scale, and what was wrong.** Nothing was wrong with the price of an item as such: an item's seconds of work, its inputs, the shop's price and its serving time were already derived from the profile's shares, so twice the price is half the items (now tested). Four other things decided whether a town worked:
    1. *A factory's opening money was a stated sum* (`opening.factory`; a shop's was stated too and overwritten by the generator). Each author scaled the first profile's 400,000.00 by a wage ratio or kept it. Against what a factory needs (a month's wages and a month's inputs for its opening staff) it was 36% in the first profile's town, 47% in the German one, 14% in the Indian one. A factory orders inputs when it can pay for the order and keeps nothing back for wages; whether it could just afford the order decided whether it could pay its staff a week later. The German town's factories went from 326 jobs to 87 in 60 days.
    2. *An hour's pay was the wage over the profile's weekly hours, while the working day was eight hours everywhere.* A firm's labour cost was its profile's labour share times (weekly hours / 40): 0.95 of it at 38 hours, 1.2 at 48, 1.3 at 52.
    3. *What the shops expected to sell* was reckoned from gross wages, with the pension counted as fortnightly whatever its period, and every home's rent as paid. Per shop: 599 items a month in the German town against 387 by the rule below (150 opening shop staff where 120 follow); 20 in the Kenyan one, where rents exceed wages and the sum went to nought (30 shops with one member of staff each, 65% of demand unmet in month 0).
    4. *Two faults in every town, the first profile's too.* A firm short of the next pay period's wages gave notice to its most recent hire at every look while the shortfall lasted, counting those already under notice as staff to be paid: one shortfall emptied a firm in days. And between factories at one price a shop chose by name: every shop in a town ordered from the same factory, which filled a few orders and refunded the rest, while the others' goods stood unsold. In the first profile's town, with the changes below in and this one not, shops got 27,098 of the 129,706 goods they ordered from factories in 60 days.
  - **What changed.**
    - A firm's opening money is derived: a factory's is a month's wages and a month's inputs for its opening staff (a shop's as before: a month's stock and three months' wages). A profile that states `opening.shop` or `opening.factory` is refused with the reason. Those two figures were removed from all nine profiles (a removal of what is now derived, not a change of a figure).
    - The working day is the profile's working week over the days people work, and an hour's pay is a pay period's pay over the hours worked in it. A week that cannot be worked is refused.
    - `prices.goods_world` may be left out (an item is then half an hour of factory work); stated, it is checked: an item's seconds of work and units of inputs must be within 5% of what the shares give as whole numbers, and no more than a working day. All nine profiles still state it and pass.
    - The shops' expected sales follow the citizens' own rule of spending: wages after withholding, pensions and the payment to the unemployed by their own periods, the rent of homes that are let, never less than the essentials, and the wealth share of savings beyond the months kept in hand.
    - Layoffs for want of wages count the staff who are staying (`Staffing.staying`; employer, producer, retailer, builder).
    - A shop has its own order among suppliers at one price (drawn once from its own randomness); cheapest first and short-last are as they were.
    - The bank makes a standing order's payment that fell due before the order was withdrawn. Found by the monthly-pay test: a firm that replaced its wage orders for a new rate in the hours after a pay day, before the bank's daily plan, lost those workers that pay (`Staffing.raise` reckons the next pay day as the one after now). It happened on fourteen-day pay too; a month's pay is a larger loss.
    - A factory that starts with no staff (the last one in a small town can) has one worker's working capital, not nothing.
    - **Money as the world writes it:** the generated laws and papers (mortgage, income tax, budget lines) use the currency's definition, not a division by 100. Measures: `runs` gains `currency` and `minor_units`; `economy/monthly` and `town/economy` divide by 10 to that power and their columns are `money_in`, `money_out`, `paid_to_town` (were `*_usd`). `town/rent-stress` reads each job's own pay period (it assumed fourteen days).
    - **Ages in seconds as 64-bit** in `economy/summary`, `economy/monthly`, `town/gatherings` (a retirement age of 69 or more overflowed 32 bits and the query failed).
    - **Voting** is `elections.compulsory_voting`; the election law says "must" or "may".
    - **Absent institutions:** `support.unemployment`, `services.landfill_levy_per_tonne`, `credit.serviceability_buffer_bps`, the rent-increase figures, the ballot figures and the notice of industrial action may each be left out, and then no payment, budget line or law exists (a place that requires notice but no ballot gets a law of its own, `schedule-industrial-action-notice`). `retirement_fund` was already optional.
    - **Pay period:** `pay_period_days` and `wages_per_pay_period` (and the doctor's, teacher's and officer's `*_wage_per_pay_period`) replace the fortnight names. The behaviour modules already took the period as a parameter; the generator gave 14 days everywhere.
    - **Added to every profile, each with a source note asking for its author's check:** `pay_period_days` (14, since every author converted wages to fourteen days) and `elections.compulsory_voting` (true for the first profile and the Brazilian one).
  - **Results**, 60 days, before then after (employment of adults %, unemployment %, demand unmet in month 0 %, households' saving %, median rent share %, refusals for flood):

    | profile | employment | unemployment | unmet, month 0 | saving | rent share | flood refusals |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | au-vic-2026 | 51.0 → 57.1 | 16.5 → 6.6 | 11.2 → 0.7 | 2.2 → -3.2 | 22.9 → 23.4 | 0 → 0 |
    | br-mg-2026 | 51.0 → 59.1 | 18.0 → 4.8 | 30.7 → 5.0 | 7.2 → 6.2 | 13.9 → 13.8 | 4,789 → 4,576 |
    | de-ni-2026 | 28.1 → 60.3 | 54.0 → 1.4 | 2.8 → 2.8 | -8.0 → -22.2 | 8.8 → 8.8 | 0 → 0 |
    | gb-eng-2026 | 47.7 → 53.6 | 14.5 → 4.0 | 19.0 → 0.0 | -2.6 → -14.3 | 13.5 → 14.5 | 0 → 0 |
    | in-tn-2026 | 38.5 → 46.9 | 26.1 → 9.1 | 12.1 → 0.1 | 2.4 → 5.3 | 39.0 → 39.8 | 0 → 0 |
    | jp-akita-2026 | 57.8 → 60.3 | 8.7 → 4.5 | 2.9 → 1.3 | -16.0 → -21.4 | 8.4 → 8.4 | 0 → 0 |
    | ke-nakuru-2026 | 55.5 → 65.9 | 19.9 → 4.0 | 64.7 → 0.2 | 10.2 → 20.5 | 85.2 → 85.2 | 0 → 0 |
    | mx-jal-2026 | 52.2 → 59.4 | 18.1 → 6.7 | 4.7 → 0.6 | 7.3 → 11.1 | 18.8 → 19.2 | 0 → 0 |
    | us-oh-2026 | 58.0 → 58.9 | 8.8 → 7.4 | 5.0 → 2.0 | -1.8 → -9.6 | 19.3 → 19.3 | 1,252 → 1,270 |

    Unmet demand in the last month of a run reads high in every town (7% to 21% after, 25% to 87% before) partly because an order is served over the fortnight after it is made; month 0 is the cleaner figure. The supplier fix alone took the first profile's from 9.0% to 0.7% and the German town's from 33.8% to 2.8%.
  - **Hashes.** Every town's changes. The first profile's 1,000-person town, 30 days: 8ae630800d86f847 before, 78940f9a00615cb2 after; 60 days: 2ba4ef479b56b016 before, 3177a4dbe1d9017c after. What moves it: a factory's opening money (400,000.00 to about 1,125,000.00), the working day (8 hours to 7.6, the profile's 38-hour week), the shops' expected sales (558 to 573 a shop, so 120 opening shop staff, not 90), the layoff count, the shops' choice of supplier and the bank's payments due before a withdrawal.
  - **Still wrong, and why, left as they are (outcomes, or figures that are their authors'):**
    - *Brazil:* the town floods from day 5 (4,576 refusals for flood in 60 days). Its `hazards` figures are placeholders, and the river's flow follows rainfall with no shape of its own (next tier).
    - *Kenya:* the median renting household's rent is 85% of its wages, and 1,579 acts were refused because a home was full: the profile's rents against minimum wages with informal work and larger households not represented (next tier), and a 52-hour week stated where the daily round is nine hours.
    - *India:* rent share 40%, employment of adults 47%: its labour-force share and joint households, not the scale.
    - *Saving is negative* in the German, Japanese, English, Ohio and first profile's towns (-3% to -22%) now that households get what they order (they were negative in four of them before). Not looked into: an outcome, and the citizens' spending rule is the place to look, with a real basis.
    - *Factories fill export orders before the shops'*: a shop's order in the first two weeks is mostly refunded (seen in the 300-person test town: 0 of 282 on day 0, orders filled from day 14). Not looked into.
  - **Tests of the first profile's town that turned on the old path**, each read and changed in what it assumed, not in what it holds: `insolvency` (the factory meant to have no customers was one the shops never ordered from only because of its name: its list now asks three times the imports' price); `production` (with slack demand the staff now go for want of wages, not by the month's-end rule: either counts); `unions` (someone hired after a stoppage was called worked through it: no making by anyone who is out); `households` (a newborn was at the clinic before it was brought home: its first move into a home is the one checked); and the two new run tests' own mistakes.
  - **No contract in `contracts/` changed.** What did change that others read: the profile's field names (above), the measures' column names and the `runs` table's two columns (`docs/measurement.md`, measure 0.5.0), and one more law schedule in `worlds/law/schedules.json`.
  - **Not done here, by size:** the helper names `usd` and `cents` across the behaviour modules (about 400 uses; several branches were in them); loan sums rounded to 100,000 and 10,000 of the smallest unit; module defaults in money; fortnightly shopping and fourteen days' notice whatever the pay period; the profiles' figures of nought standing for "none" and their fourteen-day wages (their authors'); the stated opening sums of the bank, town, builder and landlords. All listed in `docs/profiles.md`. Still required of every place: an age pension, a union, income tax, council rates, a clinic and school funded from outside.
  - **Next tier** (each a rule change, made generally): informal and own-account work (a kind of work with no employer, agreement or withholding: the citizen's work rule, the measures' employment); extended households (the generator's household shapes and a home's capacity rule); a pension age apart from the age people stop work (two profile figures where there is one; citizen and transfers); unemployment insurance linked to the last wage or limited in time (the transfers module's payment rule); tax deductions, rebates and ceilings (the tax law's data shape and the withholding function); storms by season (the weather draw); a river's flow with its own shape (the hazards model's input); digit grouping and where the sign goes in written money (the currency definition and `formatMoney`, with the resolver's twin and the translator's parser, which another branch is changing).
  - **Not verified:** the cause of the German factories' collapse beyond the figures above (taken from the notes of the session that began this work; the test holds that no factory is short of wages in its first six weeks after); a profile that actually leaves out `goods_world` or an institution, run for 60 days at 1,000 people (the tests generate and run such towns at 200); a 30-day pay period at 1,000 people; more than one seed; that the new supplier test fails on the old rule (it follows from the orders listed, not run).
  - **Revisit if** a new profile's town again turns on a stated sum (then that sum should be derived too: the bank's, the town's, the builder's and a landlord's are the candidates); a profile's item cannot pass the whole-number check at any sensible size (then goods need a finer unit than one item); authors want the working week and the round's hours stated as one thing; negative saving persists once profiles are checked (then the spending rule, with evidence).
- **The 1,000-person town's opening slump: what it was, what ended it, what is left** (2026-10-08; `worlds/town-1000.yaml`, code participants only; the first profile). Asked: on `origin/main` at ff0d1bc seed 1 at 60 days had employment 51.0% of adults, unemployment 16.5%, participation 61.1% and 38.1% of what households ordered unmet; is that a fault, a start-up transient or behaviour? Three faults and two faults of measurement, all since fixed (most of it by "A town from any place" above, which reached the same causes from other countries' towns while this was being traced in this one's logs), and one transient that is the scenario's own.
  - **The goods.** First bad day: day 0. 23 of the 30 shops sent their first order to `factory-1` (the name that sorts first), which had shipped its opening stock for export that morning: 6,417 ordered, none delivered; the next week to `factory-10`, a works with no staff; shops were out of stock from day 14. Each shop now has its own order among suppliers at one price (the entry above; this branch's by-hand test, `participants/test/retailer-suppliers.test.ts`, also covers suppliers found on the trade board in any order).
  - **The jobs.** On day 25 `factory-6` gave all 40 notice with 116,091.22 in hand (the pay of 38): the layoff count. With that fixed alone the same shortage came on day 31 and was worse (`factory-7` 26,618.42 on day 31, `factory-6` on day 32, `factory-2` and `factory-4` on days 49 and 50; factory jobs 317 on day 31, 176 on day 59; employment 38.5% and unemployment 34.1% at 60 days), because every works was short for one cause: it opened with a stated 400,000.00 and a month's inputs, which is its reorder point; a month's inputs for forty cost about 913,500, so it spent 487,000 on the next half month's on day 0, again on day 16, and the third order on day 31 took what was left four days before its next export receipts. A works' opening money is now derived (the entry above; this branch came to the same sum, a month's wages and a month's inputs for its opening staff, and its copy was dropped in the merge).
  - **Why no loan.** The bank lends against the least that came into an account in any of the last three months, and before day 90 that is nothing: nobody in a generated town can borrow in its first quarter, though every firm in it is meant to be years old. Left as it is: for Naz (below).
  - **How the fixes were measured before the merge** (180 days, seeds 1 to 3; employment, unemployment, unmet by the corrected measure; factory jobs at each month's end in seed 1): ff0d1bc 58.8%, 5.2%, 47.5%; 297, 291, 285, 313, 324, 324 (seed 2: 337, 58, 325). With the layoff and supplier fixes 55.1%, 8.1%, 0.5%; 317, 176, 251, 230, 255, 260. With inputs bought with wages kept, as well: 54.6%, 8.9%, 0.4%; 317, 270, 270, 269, 273, 275. With a works' money by rule, as well: 59.8%, 0.8%, 1.4%; 317, 319, 312, 304, 313, 313. (Final states of the last: 47d9b854aff960d9, 17c6cb14f03f9122, fbb692da19dbcfe1.)
  - **What this branch adds to main as it now is** (43323be, with "A town from any place"), and what it measures, three seeds:

    | | employment | unemployment | participation | unmet | saving, by seed | factory jobs at each month's end, seed 1 | final states |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | main (43323be), 60 days | 57.0% (56.8 to 57.1) | 6.5% | 61.0% | 0.2% | -0.3%, -2.9%, -2.1% | 307, 310 | 3177a4dbe1d9017c, 371266ea55a1f1f8, dd494dce2f27dae2 |
    | this branch (61323aa), 60 days | 56.9% (56.6 to 57.1) | 6.7% | 61.0% | 0.2% | -0.3%, -2.9%, -2.2% | 307, 310 | d93135e7bf18dbea, 6ce192fc77029a58, 8a7c2935eb980624 |
    | main, 180 days | 61.2% (60.4 to 61.7) | 1.1% (0.5 to 2.2) | 61.9% | 0.6% | 1.8%, 1.3%, 0.2% | 307, 310, 299, 288, 293, 293 | 49d55a4ff481c9a1, e34ea1e67efb9a8b, 6feda99c014e4256 |
    | this branch, 180 days | 60.9% (60.2 to 61.5) | 1.8% (1.4 to 2.7) | 62.0% | 1.5% | 1.5%, 1.4%, -1.3% | 307, 310, 297, 285, 295, 295 | 7df6844d61cef230, 1baa6b42ccf8d1c5, 0e2ba13c98c61c4f |

    Both measures are the corrected ones, read from each side's logs. **The 1,000-person month of seed 1 ends at 78940f9a00615cb2, twice: the same as main's**, since nothing this branch changes comes into play in a first month in which no works is short. From then on the runs differ, by the two things here that move a run (the layoff rule as this branch has it, and inputs bought with wages kept aside, next entry), and the outcomes do not: every difference in the table is within what seeds differ by. **So on main as it now is, this branch fixes nothing further in the town's first half year; what it adds is the second layoff fault (below), two measures, and the diagnosis.**
  - **Where both branches had a fix, which was kept.** *Layoffs:* this branch's. Both count only staff who are staying; this one also holds the whole test against them (main still asks for a loan when it is short only for those already going), brings the month's headcount down to those kept, and waits a day for a loan it has been granted or has signed for (on main a firm that signs for a loan still lets someone go two seconds before the money comes: 24 of 99 loans in one year). `participants/test/pay-period.test.ts` steps each by hand. The count is under main's name (`staying`). *Suppliers:* main's draw; the two were the same rule. *Opening money:* main's; the same sum.
  - **The transient that is left, and is the scenario's:** the spec houses 95% of households in a town with fewer homes than households, and a household without a home leaves after 45 to 90 days, its jobs with it: on main as it now is, adults 775, 771, 747, 720, 714, 715 at the months' ends and factory jobs 307, 310, 299, 288, 293, 293 in seed 1; newcomers from month 3. So the town has settled by about day 120 to 150. A steward who sits from day 0 meets this in the first quarter; it is what the spec asks for, and a housing matter a steward can act on.
  - **Not start-up:** over a year unmet demand climbed after the first quarter in the runs made before the merge (15.4% over the year, three seeds: what households order rose from 15,000 a month to 26,000 while shops' staff stayed at 80 to 90 with 1.4% to 3.2% unemployed). With main's larger opening shop staff (120) this needs measuring again; recorded, not chased.
  - **Is the town generated out of balance?** It was, in a works' money (fixed). Two things are left. Every works opens at its reorder point for inputs, so all order on day 0 (a works found on a random day would be anywhere between a month's and a month and a half's): harmless once it can pay. And the world's demand is shared among works by their room while staff fill works in order of name: eight works open full and two empty, the empty ones hold a fifth of the export demand, and the full ones make about a sixth more than they can sell, which costs one or two notices a month at months' ends (stock above demand) while the empty works hire one a month. Shops open with half a month's stock and were never short for that. Nobody has an order book or trade credit at the start, and with a month's inputs and wages in hand a works needs neither in these runs.
  - **Why households' saving was negative once they got what they ordered** (asked of this branch after "A town from any place": five of nine towns). Mostly a fault of the measure, and the rest is real.
    - *The measure:* it counted what a dead person's account pays out as spending. Someone who dies with no heir leaves their savings to the town; a retiree's are large. In the first profile at 60 days, seed 2 read -13.3% and two such estates (312,437 on day 14, 300,193 on day 21) were ten points of it. Fixed (two entries below): by seed, -3.2%, -13.3%, -2.1% become -0.3%, -2.9%, -2.1% at 60 days, and at 180 days 1.0%, -5.5%, -1.7% become 1.8%, 1.3%, 0.2%. The other profiles were not run again here.
    - *What is real* (first profile, 180 days, a run's people taken together, refunds taken off both sides): of what comes in, 79% goes to shops, 14% in rent, 4% to the town, 3% to the bank, 1% on energy. The rule spends on goods what is left of 93.5% of income after housing (rent or the home loan's repayment, and bills), never less than the essentials, and 3% a year of savings beyond the months kept in hand. Saving is under 6.5% because (i) adults with no income of their own, and households on their way out of town, live on savings (about 2 points: 178 such adults among those who stayed, three seeds); (ii) the share of savings spent each year is spending out of no income; (iii) rates and other sums owed to the town are paid on top of the rule, which counts only rent, the home loan and bills as housing. And before any of this showed, refunds for goods that could not be had were saving nobody chose.
    - *Still in the measure:* repaying a loan's principal is counted as spending (it is saving); not changed, since a repayment's parts are not on the transfer.
    - *For Naz:* whether rates belong with housing in the spending rule (an owner's housing costs are counted with rates in the housing survey the profile cites for renters); I would say yes, and it is one line. Not done: it is a change to what households do.
  - **For Naz (judgement, not done):**
    1. *The bank and a town's first quarter.* Recommend: an account that exists at the start is an old customer's, and the months before the run began are unknown to the bank, not empty; it should reckon regular income over the whole months it has seen, at least one (an account opened in the run stays as now). With a works' money derived no works needed a loan in these runs, so it is no longer urgent.
    2. *Opening staff spread over the works* (each opening at about four fifths of its room, job slots dealt round the works in turn), so that demand shared by room matches the staff. One line in the generator; it moves every worker's employer, and I did not measure it. Recommend it.
    3. *The employment and participation targets cannot both be met by this town.* The profile puts 80% of adults under 67 in the labour force and nobody of 67 or over. On main about 79.6% of adults are under 67 (twenty towns), so participation is at most 63.7% of adults against a target of 67.1%; with adults' ages as the census has them (75.2% under 67: the ages fixes, below) at most 60.2%. Recommend participation by age band in the profile, from the same labour force survey, in place of the one placeholder; not a number to tune.
    4. *A works that is short still lets go everyone it cannot pay next fortnight*, with a month's inputs and a week's goods in hand. Whether real firms of this size cut hours or pay late first is the earlier entry's question, and stands.
    5. *Inputs bought with wages kept aside* (next entry): to accept or drop.
    6. *The ages fixes* (below): on the branch `slump-ages`, with seven town tests to re-point before they can merge.
  - **Starting pilots from a town that has run.** Sound, if the days before are the same town under code seats and are kept as part of the pilot's record: every steward takes over a going concern. But the first quarter is where the unhoused leave, which a steward might have acted on, so a late start hides the one thing the scenario was built to show; and after these fixes nothing in the first quarter is an artefact. Recommend day 0. If a late start is wanted: the platform has no saved state to start from (the kernel takes a config; `oikos resume` runs the log's own config again from the start, serving recorded model answers, and a mock seat's answers are not served to another model, so a mock warm-up cannot be handed to a model seat). What would do it within today's design is an office that its code module holds until a given day and a model from then on (one run from day 0; the first 120 days of 1,000 people took 161 seconds on a busy machine); checkpoints remain deferred (docs/design/rebuild.md).
  - **Not verified:** the whole suite (run on the merge); towns of 3,000 and 10,000; other profiles, for anything here; a year on main as it now is; this branch's layoff rule against main's in a like-for-like run (the table's difference is that and the inputs rule together).
  - **Revisit if** a works is again short of a pay period in its first quarter (then its opening money or the bank's rule is wrong for that profile), or pilots are started late (then the handover day needs building).

- **A works buys inputs with the next pay period's wages kept aside** (2026-10-08; `firm.producer`, `participants/src/behaviour/producer.ts`; docs/behaviour/producer.md; no contract change; for Naz to accept or drop, it is one commit, 9a51a0b). A shop restocks with "only as much as it can pay for while keeping the next pay period's wages aside"; a works spent its whole balance on an order of inputs if the balance covered it. The same works reckons itself short when its balance is under the next pay period's wages, and then borrows or lets people go: so it spent on stock for the month after next the money it would count itself short of the same morning. Now it orders what it can pay for beyond those wages (for the staff it has or is hiring to), and the rest as money comes in.
  - **Basis.** The firm's own rule for when it is short, and the shop's rule for buying; inputs are bought a month ahead of use and wages fall due within the fortnight. That firms hold money against the wage bill is Lengnick's buffer; that working capital is what carries a firm between paying for inputs and wages and being paid is the cash conversion cycle (docs/research/references.md; the second to confirm).
  - **Measured, honestly: no gain shown.** Before the opening money was derived, alone it did not help over three seeds (employment 54.6% from 55.1%; seed 1 kept 270 factory jobs where it had 176, seeds 2 and 3 fewer): a works with too little money sits at the threshold, and the next wage paid takes it under. With the opening money derived no works is short in 180 days with or without it, and against main the branch's outcomes are the same within the seeds' spread (the entry above). My own advice is to drop it unless the consistency with shops is wanted for its own sake: one `git revert 9a51a0b`. A works with almost nothing makes an order a day as money trickles in (17 in one test run). It is here because the fault is plain in the log and the rule is the one the docs give shops; it moves every run.
  - **Tests.** `participants/test/producer-inputs.test.ts`, by hand: with money to spare, an order up to a month and a half's; short, what the balance beyond the wages pays for; with no more than the wages, none.
  - **Not verified:** a works with several kinds of inputs (the money is shared in order of the kinds' names); the module's version and manifest (not raised: for the merge).
  - **Revisit if** works come to buy on credit from their supplier, or many small orders a day become a cost.

- **Adults' ages and siblings' ages: two faults in how a town's people are drawn, fixed on the branch `slump-ages`, not here** (2026-10-08; `adultAgeBands`, `drawHousehold` in `participants/src/behaviour/household.ts`; docs/behaviour/households.md there). Asked: why the town has fewer adults of working age than the profile's age bands give. It has more, among too few adults.
  - **Grown children counted twice.** The ages of adults no family places are the census's adults less those families place; only parents were taken off, and children at home run to 24. So the bands under 25 were filled twice and every older band was short: over twenty towns 79.6% of adults are under 67 where the census has 75.2% (fixed: 75.7%), and adults of working age are 601 in 1,000 where the census has 594 (fixed: 577).
  - **Siblings born in one year.** The eldest child's age was drawn first and each younger one's was that less one to four years, stopping at nought: an eldest of one had two siblings of nought. 8.2% of the town is under five where the census has 5.4% (fixed, the years between drawn first: 5.1%; adults 766 in 1,000 from 755, of working age 580).
  - **What still differs after both:** people of 5 to 19 are 21.1% of the town against 17.8%, so adults are 766 where the census has 791 and adults of working age 580 against 594. The household-type shares are the whole country's and the age bands the region's; children's ages at home are uniform to 24. The profile's data, not a rule.
  - **Why not here.** Each fix draws another town from every seed, and seven tests that simulate the 200-person town then fail, each on an outcome in the one town it was written against: `elections` (the neglected mayor's share 18.4% where under 16.7% was asked), `production` (one notice or quit where two), `clinic-school` (schooling gained 7,750 where over 8,500), `river` (no landlord builds in the time), `ownership` (18 titles where over 19), `home-market` (the seller chosen never lists), `building` (two where three). Without the siblings fix only `home-market` fails. None is a fault the fixes introduce; each fixture needs pointing at a town where its case arises, and that is seven pieces of work. Also seen with the fixes: employment is lower by about four points (56.1% against 61.2% at 180 days) because a quarter of adults are then 67 or over, as the census has it (measured with the rest of this branch, before the fixes were set aside); and in seed 2 the school's staff run out of hours from day 119 (112,598 acts refused in the half year).
  - **Tests** (on that branch): `participants/test/household.test.ts`: over 20,000 households the shares of adults under 25 and at or past 67 are within 2.5 points of the census; no two children of a family are the same age; the under-fives are within a point of the census.
  - **Revisit when** the seven fixtures are re-pointed (then merge `slump-ages`), or a profile gives household types for its own region.

- **Unmet demand does not count orders still being served when the log ends; saving does not count a dead person's estate** (2026-10-08; `measure/queries/economy/monthly.sql`, `summary.sql`, `measure/src/health.ts`; no contract change; neither changes a run).
  - **Pending orders.** A household's order is served over the days it is for, up to a fortnight, so a log cut on any day has about a week of orders with no delivery note; all were counted as gone without (7.8% unmet at 60 days in a town that read 0.5% at 180). The monthly query has them as `pending`, unmet is what was wanted less pending and got, and health's share is of settled orders.
  - **Estates.** What a dead person's account pays out is wealth moving, as what a leaver takes is (which was already left out); to an heir it was never counted (person to person), to the town or the tax office it was counted as spending. Left out from the death on.
  - **Tests.** `orchestrator/test/diff.test.ts`: in a town cut at 20 days, pending is more than nothing and is what first orders without a note add up to, and each month's unmet is wanted less pending less got. `orchestrator/test/deaths.test.ts`: in the year of deaths, saving by the query is higher than by the query with the clause taken out, where an estate went to other than a person.
  - **Not verified:** an order refunded in part and tried again elsewhere whose second try is pending still counts as unmet (small); what comes into a dead person's account after the death is still counted as income (small).
  - **Revisit if** orders come to be cancelled without a note, or loans' repayments come to say what is principal (then principal repaid is saving).

- **The pipeline's three unfinished things: the pod step run for real, scoring on its own test sets, and which two readers** (2026-10-08; `translator/model/pod/pod.sh`, `translator/eval/set-run.ts`, `oikos translator-eval --set`, `orchestrator/src/pipeline.ts`, `orchestrator/src/reader-compare.ts`, `participants/src/llm/claude-code.ts` and `codex.ts`, `docs/translator-pipeline.md`; no contract change). The pipeline entry above left three things unverified or unbuilt. The work was done in two sittings (the first was stopped mid-step; what it left was read, found sound and finished).
  - **The pod step.** `pod.sh` was rewritten around one rule: a rented pod is ended on every way out, and ended means the provider no longer lists it. `run` ends it on its way out (done, a failure at any step, INT, TERM, HUP); a watchdog of its own process ends it when the run that rented it is gone or at the time limit; ending is asked again until the pod is off the account's list; every pod carrying the batch's name is ended too; a pod dearer than a limit is not kept; the key goes to curl on standard input, never a command line. Scoring happens after the pod is ended. Tried on real pods (`runs/translator-model/podsh-state/pod.log`): a TERM while renting (ended and seen gone in 8 s), the run killed outright (the watchdog had ended it within 20 s), a time limit of nothing (the watchdog ended it and the run left with 4). The first whole run sat idle for eleven minutes: the script asked the pod whether a queue was going by its command line, and the asking matched itself; the queue is now started and checked by its process id. The second whole run trained one seed of `microsoft/deberta-v3-small` on data-13, came home, the pod was ended (0 pods listed) and the run scored here: 28 minutes at 0.39 an hour. In all 0.25 of RunPod balance (8.89 to 8.63), tests included.
  - **Why that run scored 141 of 180 where the hand runs scored 147 and 148: the scoring changed, the training did not.** `podsh-small-s1` against `pod-small-s1` (same data, reader, seed): `config.json` identical; 592 batches an epoch in both; losses 0.9307, 0.1707, 0.0758, 0.036 against 0.9347, 0.1697, 0.0747, 0.0355; validation 2,932 against 2,945 of 3,255; the real-situation validation 178 against 177 of 228: as close as two seeds of the same setup were on 2026-10-08, and on different cards. Then the hand-run model was scored again with today's code (`pod-small-s1/eval-rescored-2026-10-08.json`; its first score is kept): real **141** of 180 (was 147), held out 44 (was 44), seen 52 of 67 (was 52 of 64). Its six lost real cases: two give the same answer as before against a key that has since been given several bases (`c9-mayor-d24-1`, `-d25-2`), and four were right and are now asked back, their sureness under the cut-off of 0.1 that became the adapter's default on 2026-10-08 (the hand runs were scored with none). The seen set grew by three cases the same day. So 141 is what data-13 models score today; nothing in `pod.sh` trains differently, and no second pod was rented. A run scored by `pod.sh` that is home and not scored is scored with no pod (checked: the same 141, and the scoring ends by itself now that the command lets the model's process go; the script no longer ends anything by its command line).
  - **Scoring on the pipeline's own test sets.** `translator-eval --set FILE` (several allowed; `--only-sets` for those alone) scores any model, trained or prompt-driven, on a file of labelled requests as the pipeline writes them: each request through the translator as a run would send it (the same prompt, tool and parser, one attempt, the forms its seat knew shown exactly as its line has them), the answer compared with the label by meaning (`sameProgram`, as the two readers were compared), a call the resolver answers with a refusal's own words counted as that refusal. Reported as the other groups are (right, no draft, wrong draft), in all and by pilot, seat model, profile, currency, origin and function where the lines say them; the pipeline writes pilot, seat model and profile onto each test line (the profile from the pilot's `batch.json`). **Each case is read in its own world's money**: the line's currency is what the translator's knowledge and the comparison are given, so an amount as that world writes it is right and another world's sign is not; a line that names none is read in the test town's and counted. After training the pipeline scores each run on each test set, keeps every answer in the run's `eval-sets.json` (skipped when the sets and the prompt are unchanged) and puts the counts in the manifest (`test_scores`) and the report. Along the way: a standing example of a test-only pilot named without `runs/` before it (as the pilots' own requests are) was not dropped from training; it is now.
  - **What it gives, on what exists** (`runs/translator-model/podsh-small-s1/eval-sets-smoke-1.json`; the first smoke's files, pilot 11, a town and seat model data-13 never saw): on its 34 labelled requests 25 right, 9 not drafted, none wrong; by origin, written in the seats' situations 24 of 25, the seats' own 1 of 9; by function, message 17 of 17, requests to be shown something 7 of 7, forms 1 of 10 (the budget proposals, asked back). Its held-back file is three of those nine: 0 of 3. That is pilot 11's finding from the other side, and it is the kind of thing the breakdown is for. No pipeline has yet run with a test-only pilot and training together on real models, so `test_scores` in a manifest is tested with the mock only.
  - **The readers.** Four candidates read the same 40 requests of pilot 11 (34 with a label two earlier readers had settled) exactly as a pipeline reader does (`runs/reader-comparison-2026-10-08/`; prompt 0.7.6, one line about amounts short of 0.7.7; 8 calls, none repeated since):

    | Reader | Read whole | With the settled labels | Seconds a request | Cost a request, as reported | Output tokens |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | claude-code/claude-sonnet-5-5 | 40/40 | 34/34 | 1.4 | 0.0115 USD | 8,823 |
    | claude-code/claude-haiku-5-5@low | 40/40 | 24/34 | 1.5 | 0.0162 USD | 14,896 |
    | codex/gpt-6-luna | 39/40 | 29/34 | 0.8 | none per call | 3,763 |
    | codex/gpt-6.1-sol | 40/40 | 34/34 | 2.0 | none per call | 3,636 |

    Agreeing, of 40: Sonnet with sol 36; Sonnet with luna 31; sol with luna 31; Haiku with luna 25; Sonnet with Haiku 24; Haiku with sol 24. **Chosen: Sonnet 5.5 and gpt-6.1-sol**, two families, the pair that agrees most and the only two that matched every settled label (the labels were settled by Haiku and Sonnet, so sol's 34 of 34 is the independent one). **Fallback:** gpt-6-luna in place of sol (still two families; 31 of 40 with Sonnet, so about an eighth fewer examples); if Claude Code cannot be had, sol with luna, one family, to be said in the run's notes. Haiku 5.5 at low effort is not a reader: it agrees least with everyone and is not cheaper as reported (1.7 times Sonnet's output tokens for the same answers). The cost column is Claude Code's own figure for calls on the plan; in these calls it is the tokens at one rate for both models, so it counts tokens and is no model's price. The nine-to-one of smoke 2 did not recur: here it is 1.4 to one, with most of both in writing the long system message to the cache.
  - **The hand-in form no longer needs the repair.** Writers and readers were handing their file in through a tool whose input the two command-line adapters carry as a string of JSON, which one reader escaped by hand (25 of 34 programs). The file is now asked for as the model's answer in a closed shape (`answer` on a request: `--json-schema` for Claude Code, `--output-schema` for the Codex CLI; any other adapter gets the same shape as a tool's input), every field a plain string. Live, in the comparison: 160 answers from four models, none escaped by hand, Haiku at low effort included. `undoubled` stays as a guard and its use is counted in the manifest (`escaped_by_hand_and_repaired`); it has still only ever been exercised on stored answers.
  - **Not verified:** a whole pipeline run on real models with training (the pod step was run by hand through `pod.sh`, not from `oikos translator-pipeline`); more than one run on a pod, a run that fails there, a pod that stops answering; the readers on more than 40 requests, on requests of a Codex seat, or under prompt 0.7.7; writers through the new hand-in form (only readers were called); a prompt-driven model through `--set` (the code path is the trained model's; no call was made); a test set in a currency other than the test town's on a real model (tested with an invented currency and the mock).
  - **Revisit if** two scorings of one model on one day differ (then the scoring is not deterministic, and the comparison above is void); a later trained run's `test_scores` by pilot or seat model differ by more than the seed spread (then that is where data is thin); Sonnet and sol agree on much less than nine in ten on a larger run (then look at what they split on before blaming either); either model is withdrawn or its limit makes it unusable (the fallback, and measure again with `reader-compare.ts`); a model escapes by hand again through the new form (then the count in the manifest will say so).
- **The kernel's part of a large town: looking things up, an observation written from its own shape, a cohort's looks on several cores** (2026-10-08; `kernel/src/kernel.rs`, `protocol.rs`, `run.rs`, `schedule.rs`, `log.rs`, `kernel/crates/perception/src/observe.rs`, `kernel/crates/world/src/state.rs`, `kernel/crates/resolver/src/knowledge.rs`, `kernel/tests/replay.rs`). A pass on the kernel's time for towns of 10,000 people and more, begun by two sessions that were stopped without a write-up and finished by a third; this entry is the write-up of all of it. **Nothing a run does changes: every step ends at the same state hash as the main it was merged with, and the logs replay byte for byte.** No contract changed.
  - **What changed, in the order it was done.**
    - *Looked up, not looked for.* The state keeps each organisation's positions and each participant's held positions beside the positions themselves (derived, never serialised, checked against the scan in debug builds); the scheduler keeps each participant's units. An observation, a transfer and a record's news no longer go through every position and every unit.
    - *No JSON value is made of an observation, an event, or a request to observe that was checked before.* An observation's canonical bytes are written straight from it, a seat's knowledge reads it as it is (`Seen`), an event is logged from its own shape and the reply reuses what was logged. A building is encoded once for a look, not once for each cell it stands on. A channel of many accounts (a bank's) and a directory read what changed since the last look from a list kept by comparing the state's persistent maps, not by going through every account, entity and position.
    - *A cohort's looks are made ready together* (`Kernel::plan`). When the scheduler hands over everyone waking at a moment, the kernel is told which observations will be asked for next and builds, encodes and hashes them a window of 256 at a time on up to eight threads (`OIKOS_KERNEL_THREADS`; 1: as before), each from the same state, read and never changed, and delivers each when it is asked for, in the order asked. Whatever is asked that is not the next planned look, or changes the world, lets go of what was made ready. What a host has been handed is counted at delivery, one at a time. Reproducible because nothing a thread does is ordered against another: each result is put where its request stands. `kernel/tests/replay.rs`: a log with observations planned ahead at random is the log without, byte for byte.
    - *What is near a place is worked out once for the place* (this session, found by profiling the 10,000-person month). The cells within reach of a body were kept by entity, worked out again at each move as a set of cell ids written out one by one, behind one lock; the threads above spent 36% of their samples waiting for that lock. They are now kept by the footprints of the buildings a body is in and the reach (the same for everyone there, for the whole run), behind a lock readers share. And the records placed in a scope are found by going through whichever is fewer, the scope's cells or the cells anything is placed on, and not at all when nothing has been placed since the last look (12% of the kernel's time at 3,000 people was going through every cell of every look's scope for nothing; checked against the plain scan in debug builds).
  - **Measured** (2026-10-08, evening; the run server, 14 cores, shared with other agents the whole time: **the one-minute load was 14 to 27 during every measurement and never came down**, so wall times here are noise and are given only with that warning. What load does not move is the count of instructions the run's process retired, all its threads together (`/usr/bin/time -l`), and that is the measure used. Thirty days, seed 1, the worker run directly inside one slot; main is `97bb93a`, the branch merged with it).

    | whole run, thousand million instructions | 1,000 people | 3,000 | 10,000 |
    |---|---|---|---|
    | main | 170 | 570 | 2,918 |
    | as found (the first three changes), eight threads | | 543 | 2,605 |
    | as found, one thread | | 524 | |
    | now, eight threads | 154 | 490 | 2,428 |
    | now, one thread | 150 | 466 | 2,365 |

    | the kernel alone (a replay of the 3,000-person log, one thread) | instructions |
    |---|---|
    | main | 329 |
    | the first two changes | 316 |
    | and placed records found the short way | 285 |
    | and near scopes by place | 261 |

    So: 9% to 12% fewer instructions at 1,000 people, 14% to 18% at 3,000, 17% to 19% at 10,000; in the kernel alone, 21% at 3,000. Against the second main (`21396a1`, two runs a side): 164 against 148 and 149 at 1,000 people, 543 and 544 against 468 and 468 at 3,000. The first two changes give little on today's main (4% of the kernel alone): they were written when every citizen was shown both registers, and "Nobody has the land register by heart" has since taken that cost away. Peak memory (resident, the process and its threads): 1.1 to 1.2 GB, 2.0 to 2.2 GB and 5.3 to 6.1 GB on both sides; the threads cost about 0.6 GB at 10,000 (5.98 against 5.31 on one thread, 5.35 on main).
    Wall times under that load, for what they are worth: the 10,000-person month took 1,033 s on main (the kernel 518 s of it by the host's clock), 624 s on the branch with eight threads (254 s) and 635 s on one thread (264 s), each run once, the load 14 to 27. The 3,000-person month took between 58 and 168 s across five runs of the three versions, in no order that follows the code. The one fair pair: against the second main, the two sides were run side by side, at the same moments under the same load (13 to 24), twice: 115 and 117 s on main, 91 and 90 s on the branch at 3,000 people (the kernel 60 against 38 to 40 s by the host's clock); 42 and 49 s against 33 and 41 s at 1,000. No kernel share is given: the host's clock counts waiting for a core as the kernel's time.
  - **What the threads give is not shown.** On a machine with no idle core the eight threads cost 3% to 5% more instructions (starting threads for each window, memory freed on another thread than made it) and gave no wall time back (624 s against 635 s at 10,000, inside the noise). At 10,000 about four fifths of the building of looks is done on the threads (by samples), so on idle cores the gain could be the larger part of the kernel's observing; that has to be measured on a quiet machine before anyone relies on it. A run still takes one slot while it works on up to nine cores in bursts: with a batch of several runs that is more than the slots count. Left as it is, said here.
  - **Where the 10,000-person month's time goes now** (sampled, the branch before the last change, load 18): the host's own JavaScript (modules, books, reading replies) 52% of the run's thread; the kernel 46%, of which 7 points were waiting for its threads. Of the kernel's own time: building what is seen about a third (in a replay at 3,000: site cells 8%, encoding 13%), applying acts 15% to 16%, the log 7%, annotations 6%, checking annotations and commands against the contract 6% (part of it inside the last), dropping observations made on another thread 6%, the start 6%.
  - **The next biggest costs on the way to 100,000 people**, largest first as measured:
    1. *The host, not the kernel*: over half the run at 10,000. One JavaScript thread runs every citizen. Running citizens of one cohort on several workers is the same idea as the kernel's planned looks (they read, then act in key order) and is the largest step left; it is participant code, not done here.
    2. *A scope is a set of cell ids as strings*, and a site channel builds every cell of it at each look (string keys looked up one by one: `site_cell`, 8% of the kernel at 3,000). Scopes as rectangles and cells by number would end both. This is view code that the knowing rebuild (`worktree-agent-a0755e27f5b209db9`) is rewriting; left to it.
    3. *String ids everywhere* (the 13% found earlier is still there: `memcmp` is 14% of the kernel's own samples at 3,000): ids as numbers inside the kernel, strings at its edge. A change to every crate; it should follow the knowing rebuild, not cross it.
    4. *A state map's leaf is copied whole when one value in it changes* (sixteen structures or entities with their lists, for one stamp): 30% of applying an act. Values behind shared pointers, as accounts already are (`Map<Id, Arc<..>>`), about 37 places in `state.rs` and `exec.rs`.
    5. *Annotations and commands are checked against the whole contract each time* (a decision's annotation against four alternatives): about 6% together. A check that picks the alternative by its name first gives the same answer.
    6. *The start* (19 s at 10,000 under load: the config read, checked and hashed, nature spun up) and *the log* (0.6 GB a month at 3,000 people, about 2 GB at 10,000).
    7. *Memory*: 6 GB a month at 10,000 is 60 GB at 100,000 if nothing else grows; the replay holds the whole log (5.9 GB for the 3,000-person month).
  - **Where this touches the knowing rebuild's code** (to be merged by hand): `kernel/crates/perception/src/observe.rs` (the record index: `Changes`, `prepare`, `observe_prepared`, `placed`, `scope` and the `Scopes` it keeps; buildings as `Arc<SiteStructure>`), `kernel/crates/world/src/state.rs` (`positions_of`, `held_by`), `exec.rs` and `kernel.rs` (18 and 51 lines), `kernel/src/kernel.rs` (`Encoded`, `seen`, `plan`). Nothing here changes what any seat is shown.
  - **Checks.** The kernel's tests (163). For each main merged with (`97bb93a`, then `21396a1`), a clean worktree of it built fresh, and the 30-day runs of 1,000 and 3,000 people twice on each side: `8ae630800d86f847` and `891c8efe8f07efa5` on the first, `78940f9a00615cb2` and `71969a3cca1abfee` on the second, the same on both sides every time; 10,000 people once on each side and each version, `8c5562da7ea97d8d`. Each kernel change replayed the 1,000 and 3,000-person logs written before it byte for byte.
  - **Not verified:** any wall time on a quiet machine, and so the gain from the threads; the gain of each of the first three changes alone in a whole run (the first two were measured together, in the kernel alone); a 100,000-person town (none was generated); a run longer than a month at 10,000; 10,000 people against the second main; a lean log (no observation is encoded for it, and nothing is planned ahead); anything with a model seat (the same protocol; no model run was made); memory of the near scopes in a town with very many buildings (one set of cell ids for each building and reach, kept for the run: some tens of MB at 10,000 people by an estimate, not measured); a machine with fewer cores than eight.
  - **Revisit if** a quiet-machine measurement shows the threads give little (then one thread should be the default and the planning kept for when the host is parallel too); a batch of runs is seen to load the machine past its slots (then the orchestrator should give each run its share of cores through `OIKOS_KERNEL_THREADS`); a state hash ever differs between one thread and several (the test above is the guard: it would be a real fault, to be found, not tolerated); the near scopes' memory shows in a large town (then scopes as rectangles, item 2); bodies come to stand outside buildings (a scope is then by where they stand, and the count of scopes kept grows with the ground).

- **Scenarios: named starting conditions, as data** (2026-10-08; docs/scenarios.md; `worlds/generator/scenario.ts`, `chooseWeather` in `weather.ts`, `worlds/scenarios/`; generator 0.31.0; `oikos run --scenario`, `oikos scenarios`). Approved by Naz so that pilots stop starting from the same calm town and seats ask for a wider range of acts.
  - **What one is.** A file of shifts to what a generated town starts from and to the figures its mechanisms run on, applied to the town's description and to a copy of its profile before anything is generated. Never an event: nothing is made to happen on a day and no outcome is set. Thirteen and a `baseline`: `wet-year`, `dry-year`, `hot-summer`, `election-due`, `lean-council`, `export-slump`, `jobs-slump`, `ageing-town`, `housing-shortage`, `dirty-industry`, `doctor-shortage`, `police-understrength`, `energy-price-shock`. Named together they apply in order (`wet-year+lean-council`); two that set the same thing differently, or each choose the weather, are refused.
  - **Why applied to the profile's figures, not to the finished world.** A shift then reaches everything the generator derives from the figure by its own formulas (fewer in work: shops staffed to what a poorer town spends; an older census: other households, pensions, deaths), and a scenario is written once for every profile. Patching the generated config would have meant knowing its ids and shapes, and would have left derived figures inconsistent with the one moved.
  - **Why the weather is chosen, not re-parameterised.** A wet year could have been made by raising the climate's chance of heavy rain. That changes the climate, with no source for the size, and "how rare is this year" stops meaning anything. Instead 199 years are drawn from the profile's climate, each from a seeded stream of its own, and the run is given the one at a stated percentile (the wettest five days at the 99th; the river's inflow at the 1st; heatwave days at the 95th), with the run started in the season that matters. The basis for the size is then the convention for how rare an event is planned against, which does have sources.
  - **The rules a scenario is held to** (checked when one is read, and tested): money and quantities only by multiples of the profile's own; only shares and counts of days, months or years set outright; a profile's currency never; the world market's conditions only from day 0 (a later day is an event made to happen); not the town's seed, size, name, profile, wages or a day-by-day weather; every change with its basis. So no scenario can carry one place's figures, and the rule "no place in the code" holds for them as data.
  - **Unseen by seats.** A scenario's name is in `meta.description` only ("Starting conditions: wet-year."), which no participant is shown (the dataset manifest carries it, for whoever reads the run); a test checks nothing else of the config holds a scenario's name or words. `batch.json` has the description with its `scenario`.
  - **No contract changed.** `scenario` is a field of the generator's own town description (not a contract); the world config's shape is as it was.
  - **Baseline unchanged:** `worlds/town-1000.yaml`, 30 days, seed 1, ends at `8ae630800d86f84767d5e63468626fdc270b8aa38a5afdd84d9a0ea957653410` before this work, after it, and after it with `--scenario baseline` (the three hashes identical in full). With no scenario the description and profile aren't copied and the weather is the one draw from the stream it always was.
  - **What the 1,000-person town showed under each, at first** (superseded by the next entry, which measures each against the plain town on the town as it is now; seed 1, code offices, one run each, on a machine under heavy load; counts are of the log, not compared against a plain run of the same length except where said):
    - `wet-year` (130 days): the bureau warned on day 111, fifteen floodings from day 114, two receded by day 126, complaints to the town from day 127. The mechanism answers.
    - `dry-year` (180 days): the bureau warned of the drought on day 108. **No water restrictions in the 72 days of drought that followed**: at 40% of a low season's inflow the river still gives the town what it draws. The scenario is honest and, for the water office, weak; that is the river's and the town's sizes (placeholders), not the scenario's.
    - `hot-summer` (90 days): three heatwave warnings from day 12; 31 conditions begun in 90 days (the plain town had 7 in its first 30: no like-for-like comparison was run).
    - `election-due` (75 days): notice on day 0, 13 nominations, the result within the run.
    - `lean-council`, `export-slump` (120 days each): ran clean. Nothing in the coarse counts shows a town or a factory in trouble by day 120 (no insolvency, no closure); whether the town's account ran short was not looked at.
    - `jobs-slump`, `ageing-town`, `housing-shortage` (60 days): benefit claims 298, 513 and 271 in 60 days; `housing-shortage` starts with 348 homes for 429 households.
    - `doctor-shortage`, `police-understrength` (60 days), `dirty-industry`, `energy-price-shock` (100 days): ran clean; nothing in the coarse counts differs visibly from the others'. No taking was reported in either, as in the plain town.
  - **Naz's examples that have no honest mechanism.** A crime wave: offending follows need and opportunity at a chance the profile keeps deliberately rare and calls a placeholder; a slump brings almost no more of it, and raising the chance would be setting the outcome with no source. What can honestly be moved is the police's strength and how many are in need. A flood as such: only the wet year; the flood is the river's (it came). A drought as an effect on the town: the dry year is given, the restrictions didn't follow. A council "in deficit" on day 0: an account can't open below nothing and the town has no opening debts; `lean-council` is a town without reserves.
  - **Not verified:**
    - (Since done, in the next entry: the citations, the like-for-like comparisons and the other profiles.) **Every citation for a size was from memory** (the session's web searches were used up): all were marked "(to confirm)" in docs/research/references.md. Two sizes have no source and say so (the town's and the factories' opening balances, whose profile figures are placeholders themselves).
    - That each scenario changes what seats ask for: no model run was made (mock only, as instructed). The sixty-day runs show the towns run, not that the pressure reaches an office in a way a model would act on.
    - Like-for-like effects: each scenario against a plain run of the same length and several seeds (`oikos diff`). Only the coarse counts above were read.
    - Any profile but the first. The rules are general and a test generates every scenario from every profile there is, but there was only one. `season` and `weather` read `rain_by_month_bps` and `summer_months` as the first profile gives them.
    - The weather choice ranks over the first 180 (or 90) days; for a run much longer or shorter, another window may be the one wanted.
    - The slow test (`orchestrator/test/scenarios.test.ts`: every scenario for sixty days at 1,000 people with the mock in three offices, four at a time) uses four cores where the suite's runner counts one a file.
  - **Revisit if** a second profile can't be put under a scenario for a reason other than not describing the mechanism (then the operation is too narrow, or the scenario carries a place); a scenario is wanted whose conditions begin partway through a run (then it needs a mechanism in the world that brings them, e.g. a business cycle at the boundary, not a later `from_day`); pilots show a scenario's pressure never reaches its office (then the mechanism is what to look at: the water office under drought first; calibrate mechanisms, not outcomes); the profile's crime figures get sources (then a scenario that moves offending may have a basis); or researchers start wanting the same scenario at several sizes (then scenarios take parameters, which they don't now).

- **Scenarios, measured against the plain town: which reach an office, which were resized, which are inert** (2026-10-08; `worlds/scenarios/`, `worlds/generator/scenario.ts` (`status: inert`, `offeredScenarioIds`), `oikos scenarios [--all]`, docs/scenarios.md, docs/research/references.md; generator's `generate`/`generateFrom`; runs in `runs/scen-diff-2` of this work's checkout, with the scripts that read them). The first entry's scenarios had only been run, not compared; three visibly bit. Here each is set against the plain town like for like, and the test is what an office is shown or sent.
  - **After today's merge** ("A town from any place"): a town is made by `generateFrom(spec, profile, under)`, and `generate` applies the scenarios first and hands on the weather choice and the names. `export-slump` had shifted `opening.factory`, which no profile states now (a factory's opening money is derived): it failed, clearly, for all nine profiles, and is the world market's demand alone. Every scenario then applies to every one of the nine profiles (1,000 people, generated; a quick test applies each to each and generates each profile's town under them together; the slow test generates each from each, one by one). **Baseline unchanged:** `worlds/town-1000.yaml`, 30 days, seed 1, ends at `78940f9a00615cb289c5eeaae6fffae1a06ed8b1eca10f64a9306d6f08494cdb` with no scenario and with `--scenario baseline` (main's `78940f9a00615cb2…`).
  - **How measured.** 1,000 people, 120 days, seeds 1 and 2, the mock in the mayor's, planning and finance offices, lean logs, eighteen offices' observations recorded; `oikos diff` (52 measures, 3 standard errors); the log's records by kind, sender and addressee and the main accounts' balances, a difference counted when both seeds lie outside the plain town's two; and, from the recorded observations, each office's distinct records by kind, letters by sender and shape, and its accounts' lowest and last balance. Two seeds is thin: a third was run where it mattered (`lean-council`), 240 days where a result might only be slow (`export-slump`).
  - **Reaches an office:**
    - `lean-council`, **resized**: a hundredth of the profile's opening balance (a tenth was never drawn below 84% of itself in 180 days). In two seeds of three the bank writes to finance and the mayor from day 16 that a wage payment failed for want of funds (seven letters), the crew resign from day 23 and record their wages as debts, the tribunal sends notices of hearing, and 17 to 24 applications come for the posts; in the third the first rates arrive in time (lowest balance about a quarter of the opening one). The size has no source (the profile's opening balance is a placeholder); it is about nine days' spending, 2% to 3% of a year's. Halving the grants matters little: they are under a tenth of the town's income.
    - `jobs-slump`: 6.9% out of work at day 120 against 2.5%, an eighth more support claims, a third more people leaving. An office of the town is shown only more applicants for a post it advertises (the police 22 to 24 against 8 to 9; the town 4 to 6 against 2 to 3) and more notices of departure. The support office publishes its count of recipients monthly and no office is shown it without looking.
    - `housing-shortage`: 12.5 households without a home against 6.5; 87 leave against 59. The mayor, finance and rates are shown the departures ("... has left town.", with no reason) and a tenth fewer ratepayers. Planning is shown no more applications (38 to 40 either way): landlords already build as fast as their rule lets them.
    - `energy-price-shock`: the retailer's own books only (a third of the plain town's balance at day 120). Its tariff is fixed and nobody sets it, so nothing reaches a household or the town.
    - `wet-year`, `hot-summer`, `election-due`, run again on the town as it is now: the wet year's warning on day 111, 14 floodings, 31 to 33 leaving flooded homes, 6 to 7 complaints to the town of waste uncollected there (130 days); the hot summer's three heatwave warnings, which the mayor is shown, and illness within seed noise (41 to 47 conditions against 35 to 44); the election's notice, 16 to 17 nominations, about 650 votes, a result and an appointment.
    - `dear-rents`, **new**: every rent a quarter higher. 44% of renting households pay over 30% of income against 24%; the rent petition the mayor is shown has 42 to 65 signatures by day 120 against 27 to 28; a sixth more loan applications.
