  - **Inert** (`status: inert`, with `inert_because` in the file; kept and can be named; `oikos scenarios` names them last with what is missing; `offeredScenarioIds()` leaves them out):
    - `dry-year`: one public drought warning (day 108). Confirmed, and worse than first written: not "the river still gives the town what it draws at 40%" by a margin, but by hundreds of times. Every riverbank cell (140) is sent `utilities.water.river_inflow_kl_per_cell_per_day` (1,000), the town draws about 150 kL a day, and the supplier restricts only when a draw is refused (`participants/src/behaviour/utilities.ts`), where real suppliers restrict at a level of storage or flow, about once in ten to twenty years.
    - `export-slump`, **resized** from 12% to a quarter (Japan's export volume in 2009, where the world's fell 12.2%), and still nothing in 120 days; at 240 days nine more redundancies across ten factories, unemployment unmoved, no office shown anything. The producer's rule is slow by design (Lengnick): over-stocked, a factory stops making, keeps paying, and lets one worker go a month only once stock passes a month's demand (it opens with a quarter of one). Not a fault; a slump the size of a real one doesn't reach anyone within a pilot.
    - `ageing-town`: a smaller labour force and more pensions (outside); no office of the town shown anything in 120 days.
    - `dirty-industry`: identical to the plain town, to every species' count. `nature.pollution.per_factory_per_day` (40) against a tolerance of 400, both placeholders; nobody measures the field; no illness from it.
    - `doctor-shortage`: 29 to 37 requests for care in 120 days; a doctor gives about twenty consultations a day (`health.consult_minutes` 30, hospital-level conditions only). All seen the day they ask, with one doctor as with two.
    - `police-understrength`: no taking reported in 120 days in either seed, with or without. Confirmed: `crime.take_bps_per_month_in_need` (200) is unsourced and applies only to someone in need who works where goods are kept; `investigation_hours` (4) is a placeholder too.
  - **Sizes not raised, and why.** `energy-price-shock` was tried at three times the grid's price (one market's quarter in 2022): the retailer couldn't pay for a month's supply, placed two orders where the plain town's places six, and ended richer than in the plain town, having billed for energy it never supplied. Kept at twice. No chance was raised anywhere.
  - **Platform problems found (listed, not patched):**
    1. Drought never restricts water: above (`utilities.water.river_inflow_kl_per_cell_per_day`; restriction on refusal only).
    2. Offending is a placeholder and yields no reports: above (`crime.take_bps_per_month_in_need`, `crime.investigation_hours`). A recorded rate to set it against: about 8,700 offences a year per 100,000 people (to confirm; references).
    3. The energy retailer that can't afford a month's order stops buying without telling anyone, asks nobody for credit, and goes on billing each customer's use (`participants/src/behaviour/utilities.ts`, the order made only `if (acc.balance >= q * unit)`); a home without supply is no different from one with; the tariff (`utilities.energy.price_per_kwh`, `supply_per_day`) is fixed with nobody who sets it.
    4. The clinic is about one part in a hundred used: only hospital-level conditions exist (docs/behaviour/health.md, "Leaves out"), so no staffing figure can bind.
    5. The works crew is far larger than its rounds: the town's waste is about half an hour's collecting a day at `services.collect_seconds_per_10kg` (12, a placeholder), for six staff (`services.council_staff_per_1000`). A crew cut by 30% changed nothing (tried as `works-understrength`, dropped).
    6. Pollution's unit has no source, no effect at any plausible multiple, and no measurer (`nature.pollution`).
    7. The town's opening balance is a stated sum (`opening.town`), from under two years' rates in one profile to over a hundred in another, so `lean-council`'s hundredth is a different thing in each. It should be derived from what the town spends, as a firm's now is (the entry "A town from any place" names it as a candidate).
    8. Nobody tells the town why: a departure notice gives no reason; unemployment is published by the support office and shown to no office; a factory's short time is reported to no one. Much of what a slump does is there and unreported.
    9. The bank lends without limit (it draws funding from outside below `credit.reserve`), so a credit squeeze has no mechanism.
    10. Seen in what offices were shown, for the work on who knows what: the mayor, finance, works, rates and the environment office were each shown about 650 electors' votes under `election-due`, 777 enrolments, and 1,284 lots with their titles; bank lending is shown every account's balance. The bank's letter of a failed payment gives the sum in the smallest unit by name ("Payment of 239334 cents to ...": `participants/src/behaviour/common.ts`, the note written with the amount it is handed).
    11. The clinic and the school are generated only together (a profile without `health` has no school either).
    12. The slow scenario test removed `runs/test-scenarios-*` before each run; it now writes to a temporary folder and removes that.
  - **Sources.** Opened and confirmed: the 1% flood (the United States' base flood); English councils' funding down 49.1%, and that councils run out of reserves; world trade down 12.2% and Japan's exports 24.9% in 2009; unemployment 5.0% to 10.0%; people 65 and over, 29.4% and 17.9%; the backlog of 4.3 million homes; manufacturing's emissions down 60%; 13.1 and 31.2 physicians per 10,000. Still "(to confirm)", each with what was tried in docs/research/references.md: Australian Rainfall and Runoff, the rainfall deciles bulletin, the drought planning guideline and the 2012 report on extremes (each site refused the request or the file couldn't be read); a council's usual reserve; the housing stock the backlog is a share of; police numbers; wholesale electricity prices; the rent index.
  - **Acts no seat has asked for** (goods, account, occupancy, entity, vote, presence, position, take): mostly a matter of which offices are given to models. A councillor votes on every ballot; the electoral office appoints; the bank's payments office opens accounts. Five scenarios that would give the mayor's or finance's office reason are proposed in docs/scenarios.md, each needing a mechanism that isn't there (a post unfilled; homes of the town's own; an institution the town lacks; a grant tied to a purpose; emergency stores). Built: `dear-rents` only. Tried and dropped: a short-handed works crew (5 above); a thin bank (9); a boom drawing newcomers (its figures are the migration module's defaults, not a profile's); founding a business (nobody can: docs/behaviour/entry.md).
  - **Not verified:** any scenario with a model in a seat (mock only); any profile but the first beyond generating (no run); whether two seeds' agreement holds over more (the comparison is crude: outside the plain town's two); `hot-summer` at more seeds (illness may rise by less than the noise here); `wet-year` was run to 130 days and set against the plain town's 120, so only what the plain town has none of was read from it; the plain town here ends 120 days at 2.5% out of work, which was not set against the 6.6% at 60 days of the entry above (recorded, not chased).
  - **Revisit if** the river, offending, routine care or pollution get sourced sizes (then the inert scenario for each is measured again, and its status removed if an office is shown something); the town's opening balance is derived (then `lean-council` becomes a multiple of spending and means the same in every profile); the retailer's fault is fixed (then the grid's price can be the size a real crisis had); pilots with models show an offered scenario's pressure is not acted on (then what the office is shown is what to look at, not the size).
- **The ages fixes landed, and the town tests that held one town's accidents were reworked** (2026-10-08; `participants/src/behaviour/household.ts`, `participants/test/household.test.ts`, `orchestrator/test/{elections,production,clinic-school,river,ownership,home-market,building}.test.ts`, `worlds/test/scenario.test.ts`, docs/behaviour/households.md; no contract change, no kernel change). The two fixes set aside on `slump-ages` are back: grown children at home are counted among those a family places when the other adults' ages are drawn from the census, and brothers and sisters are a year or more apart, the eldest at least as old as the years between them add up to. (They were never off main's history: 7a441f7 and c0d9a32 made them and 8b3ab04 took them out again. This reverts 8b3ab04; there was nothing to cherry-pick.)
  - **Ages, generated towns against each profile's census** (people per 1,000, ten towns of 1,000 each, before then after; the census in brackets; the last column is the largest gap in any one five-year band):

    | profile | under 5 | 5 to 17 | working age | retirement age and over | worst band |

    | us-oh-2026 | 64 → 40 (55) | 140 → 146 (165) | 632 → 616 (599) | 165 → 198 (181) | 30 → 15 |

    Seven of nine come much closer. Two do not, and neither is this rule's doing. *Akita* has two and a half times its census's children with or without the fixes: its profile's children-at-home figures are placeholders, and its extended households were folded into couples with children (next entry). *Nakuru* loses half its under-fives (113 to 63, the census 127): the old rule's surplus of infants happened to stand in for a fertility the household shares cannot say (a family has at most three children at home, of any age to 24 evenly). The first profile's town now has too few people of working age (582 against 594) where it had too many, because people of 5 to 17 are over by 26: its household shares are the whole country's and its ages one region's. All three are their profiles' figures.
  - **Every town moves.** The 1,000-person month of seed 1 ends at c9006abcdcd8ec5d, twice (was 78940f9a00615cb2, checked twice from a copy of main). Sixty days, seeds 1 to 3: 47e25534a852b555, cee47118a5e4093e, f1656a8550e29d7c (were d93135e7bf18dbea, 6ce192fc77029a58, 8a7c2935eb980624); 180 days: 0620eb8f2293a355, 77532ad25f5d8f13, 9c8401cfb66f9c38 (were 7df6844d61cef230, 1baa6b42ccf8d1c5, 0e2ba13c98c61c4f).
  - **Measures, the first profile's 1,000 people** (mock seats, lean logs; mean and range over `--seeds 3`):

    | after, 60 days | 51.5% (50.7 to 52.3) | 9.0% (7.4 to 10.2) | 56.6% | 0.3% | -2.0%, -3.9%, -2.5% | 27.6% | 46, 158, 564, 232 |

So all three columns are the town of seed 1, and that town as now drawn is an old one: 233 of its 1,000 are 67 or over where forty towns average 186 and the census has 197. Five points of employment is that draw as much as the fix. Two other towns (the spec's seed set to 2 and to 3, one run each):

    | | employment | unemployment | participation | 18 to 66, 67 and over at the end |
    |---|---|---|---|---|

    So: nothing to three and a half points of employment less, with participation down by as much, as the slump entry foresaw (the profile puts nobody of 67 or over in the labour force, and the town now has the census's old people). Unemployment, unmet demand and saving do not move beyond what towns differ by. Unemployment at 60 days is higher in the town of seed 1 only.
  - **The tests, each: what it held, and what it holds now.** Seven were known; an eighth (`scenario`) had been written since.
    1. *building* (a demolition order). Held: each tenant put out is refused entry once. That was true of tenants who were all at work. The town now drawn has a retired couple in that house: the one who does not hold the lease is told by the leaseholder to come home, is refused, tries once more on their own daily round, and stops, which is the documented rule (households.md: "a move the world refuses is tried again by the daily round"; then "a place pulled down, or coming down: not again"). Now: only that house's household is refused, and nobody tries the same way twice (a way is known by the reason on the act).
    2. *clinic-school*. Held: every class has over 170 lessons in the year and every pupil gains over 170 lessons' schooling. That was true where both teachers lasted the year. In the town now drawn a teacher dies about day 150, the school posts the job, and that class is untaught for some forty school days until someone is hired: the school's own rule. Now: the school teaches on over 170 days; one lesson a class a day and one class a teacher; each teacher teaches every school day from their first lesson to their last except while unwell; a school that loses a teacher posts the job; a pupil's gain is no more than the lessons their own class was given and no more than thirty short of them (the old allowance, now against what was taught). One more refusal is allowed, as the households and deaths tests already allow it: a partner gone to work while a conception was under way.
    3. *elections*. Held: a mayor who funds no works crew gets under three quarters of the first preferences it gets in the town it runs well. In the town now drawn it gets 18.4% against 22.3%, with fourteen standing in one and eighteen in the other; the rule is at work as strongly as before (mean approval 1,488 with a crew, -49 without; fifteen electors think the town badly run, then sixty-nine). Now, the rule itself: approval is lower on average, and for most of those who voted in both; within the neglected town those who complained approve less than those who did not; the incumbent stands lower in voters' rankings. Not held: its share of first preferences, which turns on how many stand.
    4. *production* (too little demand). Held: at least two of a factory's staff go in five months (it had been four). In the town now drawn one goes, of thirty-one with work for a fifth of them. The count is dropped; kept and added: someone goes, every redundancy a factory gives is for one of the rule's two reasons, and making stops at the band's top. Why so few: making stops at the band's top, so stock never passes last month's demand while demand is steady, the month's-end rule never fires, and staff go only as money runs down. That is the slow shedding docs/scenarios.md already records under the export slump; this test had been keeping pace with it by lowering its count. Seen on the way, not asserted: in the same test's town with "plenty" of demand, the one staffed factory lets people go by the month's-end rule while the empty one hires and cannot pay, because the world's demand is shared by the works' room and the staff are not (the slump entry's question 2).
    5. *ownership*. Held: between 50% and 95% of houses are owner-occupied. The town now drawn has 18 of 38 (47%) at the profile's 72%. Now: each owner lives on the lot they hold and heads the household there; the town's count is within four standard deviations of the profile's share; over twenty towns the share is within five points of the profile's; and what the test's own header promised and never checked, that every mortgage's repayment is within the bank's share of the household's pay and no more than the profile's usual repayment. "More than five loans" is "at least one". Recorded, not chased: over forty towns 69.7% are owned where the profile has 72%, and towns spread more than a plain draw would (18 to 33 of 38).
    6. *river* and 7. *home-market*: **each held a real behaviour, and the town now drawn shows a fault in it. Neither is changed; each is marked `test.failing` with the reason, so the suite is green and turns red the day the fault is fixed.** See below.
    8. *scenario* (generation only). Held: the jobs-slump town has fewer jobs than the plain one, and the ageing town over 1.15 times its share of people 65 and over, one town against one. In the town of this spec's seed the slump's further five in a hundred fall on nobody (82 jobs either way), and the plain town is 29% old where the census has 22%. Now, over three towns: fewer jobs in total; the plain towns within four points of the census's share and the ageing ones within four of what half as many again comes to; the ageing older than the plain.
  - **Two faults found, for Naz. Neither is fixed here: each changes what a participant does, outside household assembly, and would have put a third and fourth change into these tables.**
    1. *A landlord whose offer the builder declines never offers again, and never builds anything else.* (`firm.landlord`, `participants/src/behaviour/firms.ts`, the development steps.) Once it commissions a building its plan is "contracted", and only a building appearing on the lot clears that. The builder declines in writing when the offer is under its price (the fix from pilot 9); the landlord never reads the reply. In the river test the builder republished its prices in the very moment the landlords commissioned (its costs had changed), two of four landlords offered at the list just before, both were declined, and neither built in the 150 days left; the other two, twenty minutes later, built two houses each. Any landlord can meet this whenever a price list changes. The fix is small: on an `offer_declined` for its contract, go back to "granted" (or "refused") and offer the price the reply names. I would make it. It moves any run in which a decline happens.
    2. *The bank refuses a mortgage at exactly its own limit, by a fraction of the smallest unit.* The buyer asks for the price less a deposit rounded down (`citizen.ts`: `price - floor(price × deposit)`), so the loan is 80% rounded up; the bank refuses anything over 80% exactly (`credit.ts`: `amount × 10,000 > price × max_lvr_bps`). Wherever a fifth of the price is not a whole number of the smallest unit, the answer is "it's more than 80% of the home's price". A first asking price divides; a price after a 5% cut usually does not, and a buyer needs three months of income before the bank will lend at all, by which time an unsold house has been cut. In the test: refused on day 91 at 568,959.11 and on day 183 at 513,485.61. The fix is one line on either side (the buyer rounds the loan down; or the bank compares to the limit rounded up). I would round the loan down. It moves any run in which a house is bought after a cut, which may be most purchases: worth knowing how many past runs had a sale at all.
  - **Not verified:** the whole suite (run on the merge). Here: `bun run test:changed`, which reaches 43 of the 48 slow files, 695 pass and 1 fail across 110 files, the one being the scenario file, since reworked (10 of 10); then `bun run test:fast`, 574 pass and 0 fail across 67 files; towns of 3,000 and 10,000; more than three generated towns in a run; a year. That `test.failing` is honoured by the merge's runner was checked only as a passing file. The other eight profiles were generated, not run, for this entry.
  - **Revisit if** a profile gives participation by age (then the participation the ages now cost is the survey's, not a placeholder's: the slump entry's question 3); the two faults are fixed (then `river` and `home-market` go back to `test`, and their runs move); a town test fails again on a new town with its mechanism intact (then it is a pin this pass missed: the same treatment); or batches are wanted over several towns (then `--seeds` needs a twin that sets the spec's seed, and the tables before this one should be read as one town each).
- **Extended households: a family plus relatives, and the occupancy standard and a home's room as the profile's** (2026-10-08; track B of docs/design/profile-depth.md, item 2; `participants/src/behaviour/household.ts`, `citizen.ts` (one parameter), `worlds/generator/generate.ts` (people and homes) and `profile.ts`, `measure/queries/town/households.sql`, `worlds/profiles/{jp-akita,mx-jal}-2026.json`, docs/behaviour/households.md, docs/profiles.md; generator 0.32.0, `citizen.default` 0.40.0, measure 0.5.1; **no contract change, no kernel change**: a household is a record, a home's capacity is world config, and both were already free to say this).
  - **The form.** A profile states extended households as a share of *all* households, beside the five kinds (`households.extended_bps`), which is how a census table has them; this is not the design's "share of family households", because Akita's table gives the row that way and the shares then need no restating. An extended household is a family (a couple, a couple with children, one parent: `extended_nucleus`, or in proportion to the profile's own three) plus one kind of relative (`extensions`: `parents`, `child_family`, `sibling`, `relative`, each with a share and a count). `parents` and `child_family` are the same people, and differ in who holds the lease. The parent of a family's head is older by the mother's age at a first birth, the figure that already ages a parent from an eldest child; nothing new was needed for ages. docs/behaviour/households.md has the table, the rules and the assumptions.
  - **The occupancy standard** is a rule with a place's figures (`households.occupancy`: the most to a bedroom, the age for a room of one's own, the age under which a boy and a girl share, whether the living room is slept in), read by the citizen (how many bedrooms to look for), the generator (dealing the first homes) and the landlord (its ceiling). **A home's physical room** is `homes: { bodies_per_bedroom, bodies_besides }`. Stated by no profile, each is what it was: two to a bedroom, 18, 5; two bodies a bedroom and two besides. `town/households` now counts crowding two ways: `overcrowded` by the town's own standard, and `over_three_a_room` by one yardstick for every place (more than three people to a room, a home's rooms its bedrooms and one more; after UN-Habitat's sufficient living area, to confirm). The query's other columns are the same on old runs (checked on three).
  - **Which profiles got figures, and from what.** Only the two whose own notes give the share; nothing was opened again, and nothing was added that a note does not give.
    - *jp-akita-2026:* 412 of 1,515 households (Census 2020, table 12-3, Ikawa: "other relatives-only"), `extended_bps` 2,719. The note says exactly what was folded (`couple_children_bps` = 2,257 + 2,719), so the fold is undone: 4,977 to 2,257. The author called them three-generation households and counted each as a couple with children; both readings are kept (the family is a couple with children; the relatives are parents) and marked as the author's, not the census's, whose row is wider. How many parents is not in the note: not stated, one drawn.
    - *mx-jal-2026:* 25.0% of the 85.9% of households that are family households (IIEG from INEGI, Censo 2020), `extended_bps` 2,148. The note says they were spread over the three kinds of family by a split of 14, 70 and 16; they are taken out in those proportions (1,196, 5,978, 1,366 to 895, 4,474, 1,023). The family in the middle and who the relatives are are not in the note: not stated, so the family is drawn by the same three shares (which is what the spreading assumed) and the relative is one other adult.
    - *in-tn-2026* and *ke-nakuru-2026* say extended households are common and give no share (the first's `group_bps` is a placeholder standing for them; the second has a national share of nuclear households only). Left as they were, with the gap written into docs/profiles.md for their authors. No profile's note gives an occupancy standard or a home's room, so none has one: every town still judges crowding by the first profile's standard, which for the Kenyan and Indian towns is not their place's.
  - **The standing first test: a town whose profile uses none of the new fields is the same town.** The first profile's 1,000 people from this code: c9006abcdcd8ec5d at 30 days (three times) and 47e25534a852b555 at 60, the figures of the entry above. And the world generated for each of the seven profiles that state none of it is the same to the byte before and after, at 200 and at 1,000 people (the generator's version in the description aside); Akita's and Jalisco's differ, as they should. In `drawHousehold` nothing is drawn for a kind the spec does not have, and the ages estimate is made a second time only where there are extended households (both tested).
  - **Akita and Jalisco, 1,000 people, 60 days, before (the entry above) then after** (mock seats, lean logs, one run each; states 1befe1fbabb3d3d3 to 61b9802cf33c1ec0 and 4fefc8305adf886e to 3a1f8011da21bc20, the after ones twice):

    | | households | employment | unemployment | unmet | rent share | overcrowded, own standard | over three a room | unhoused | 65 and over per 1,000 (census) |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | Akita | 366 → 344 | 60.8% → 53.2% | 3.5% → 2.0% | 1.4% → 0.4% | 8.1% → 8.8% | 0.3% → 0.0% | 0 → 0 | 0.3% → 0.0% | 165 → 240 (435) |
    | Jalisco | 320 → 305 | 56.5% → 54.9% | 8.0% → 8.8% | 0.4% → 0.5% | 19.2% → 15.2% | 0.0% → 1.3% | 0 → 0 | 1.3% → 0.7% | 58 → 64 (83) |

Akita's employment falls because its town now has more of its old people, who are not in the labour force; Jalisco's rent share falls because a household has more earners. Nobody is over three to a room in either: both towns still have the first profile's homes, which are large for these households.
  - **What it does not fix, said plainly.** Akita's town is still far from its census by age: 249 people under 18 in 1,000 against 97, and 240 of 65 and over against 435. Extended households were not the cause. Its children-at-home figures are placeholders (any age to 24, evenly, one to three of them), and most children living with parents there are grown; and the family an extended household is built around is, by its author's reading, a couple with children. For its author: the census's split of that row, and children at home by age.
  - **What today's citizen does in such a household** (docs/behaviour/households.md, "How an extended household lives"): one home found by the leaseholder for everyone by the standard; the rent split evenly among those with any income; every adult's money otherwise their own; the leaseholder deciding when the household leaves. Nothing in the citizen was changed but the standard it reads. A 45-day run of the Akita town at 200 people holds it (`places-run.test.ts`): no refusals but the usual races, shares of the rent paid by standing order.
  - **Left to the citizen redesign** (listed in docs/behaviour/households.md): pooling, and who pays for the children and the old; care (grandparents minding children, the old cared for at home); who works at what; households forming and splitting (so over a long run those that began extended stay so); who holds the lease other than by the rule and after a death; the relatives' own kin.
  - **One difference kept on purpose.** Where no standard is stated, the generator's dealing of first homes still leaves out the youngest of different sexes sharing a room, as it always has, so that existing towns begin as they did; a town with a stated standard is dealt by the whole rule. To fold in at the next change that moves every town.
  - **Tests.** `participants/test/household.test.ts`: no extended households, the same draws and the same derived ages; each kind at its share and what it says; the two profiles at their shares, as their notes describe; the standard with other figures. `orchestrator/test/places.test.ts`: extended households only where stated, at the share, everyone with room and each household in one home; the standard and the room the profile's where stated and nobody's where not. `orchestrator/test/places-run.test.ts`: the 45-day run; and the measure against the rule, on a town of studios, for a stated standard and for the common one (the query's count is the count `bedroomsNeeded` gives for the same households and homes).
