The UN-Habitat yardstick and the UN classification of households are cited from memory (docs/research/references.md, to confirm).
  - **Revisit if** a profile's census gives several kinds of relative in one household (then a household needs more than one kind, and `extensions` becomes independent chances); a place's own standard cannot be said with the four figures (then the rule's form is wrong, not the figures); the leaseholder rule matters to an outcome (then who heads an extended household needs a source); or the citizen redesign lands (then the section "How an extended household lives" is replaced by it, not patched).

- **What seats asked for that the call language could not say: a proposal awaiting sign-off** (2026-10-08; `docs/design/call-language-gaps.md`; no code and no contract changed). From 442 requests in 22 runs: the gaps, counted by office and model, with what seats did next; a design for each (one new argument, `statement(hold=…)`; one word, `visible=own`; one refusal label, `look_first`; offices that take written directions and have forms for their own papers; and two things not to add: a program that looks and then acts, and an act that sets what an office does by itself); a build order around the knowing work; eight questions for Naz. **Revisit if** a later pilot shows a gap not on its list, or a model other than gpt-6.1-sol asks to change another office's practice (all 45 such requests are one pilot's).

- **A hold, an entry on one's own file, and look_first: the one call-language change before the knowing work** (2026-10-08; built on Naz's approval of `docs/design/call-language-gaps.md`, its steps 1 and 2; resolvers 0.5.0, `bank.payment_system` 0.12.0, translator prompt 0.8.0, data pipeline 0.11.0). Seats asked 21 times to pause a standing payment and could only cancel it for good or write a letter the bank does not act on; they named records by describing them and nothing told them to look the id up; they wanted lines in their own books and got public reports.
  - **What happens to payments under a hold: what the sources say.** Naz's question was whether a payment missed under a hold is skipped or made up ("skipped doesn't sound right… I want this to reflect the real world"). Read (citations in `docs/research/references.md`, "A hold on a standing payment"): the United Kingdom's Payment Services Regulations 2017, reg. 83; the United States' Regulation E, 12 CFR 1005.10(c); the Direct Debit scheme's own page on cancelling; two Australian banks' pages on pausing a PayTo agreement; Commerzbank's page on suspending a standing order; LHV's standing order conditions; the Employment Rights Act 1996, s. 13. They say three things. (1) A standing order is the payer's instruction to its own bank, which the payer may stop up to a cut-off before the due date (the end of the business day before; three business days). (2) A bank that suspends one skips the executions under the suspension and resumes with the next: none read describes a bank paying afterwards what fell due meanwhile, and bank terms for the nearest case (no funds on the day) say it is not paid later beyond a few days' retry. (3) Stopping the payment does nothing to what is owed: "you'll need to organise another way of paying" (Direct Debit), "isn't the same as cancelling or pausing your contract" (PayTo), and wages not paid on pay day are a deduction the worker can recover. So "skipped" was half the answer: the *instruction* skips, the *obligation* does not. And it does depend on the instrument: a payment the payee collects can be presented again by the payee; this world has only payers' own instructions, so nothing is presented again.
  - **The rule built** (`docs/behaviour/bank-payment-system.md`, "A hold"). A hold is the customer's instruction to the bank, read at the bank's next plan. From whoever may instruct on the account the order pays from (the check any instruction gets), it stops every payment under the order that falls due from a day after it was given (`hold_notice`, a parameter) until it is withdrawn; what fell due before that is still paid. Nothing passed over is made up. The order, its history, and the agreement it pays under are untouched, so what a passed-over payment was for is still owed, and settling it is the payer's own act, a payment of its own. The bank answers once, saying which payments stop from which due date and that the rest is the payer's to settle; a hold on anything that is not the writer's to hold gets one sentence that does not say whether such an instruction exists. The payee's remedies are the ones the world had: an unpaid worker resigns after a pay period and five days and records the debt; a landlord counts arrears; a lender defaults a loan. Shown in a run (`orchestrator/test/holds-run.test.ts`): the code finance officer holds the town's wage instruction, the bank passes over one pay day and pays the next two; with the missed wage paid by the town itself the worker stays, and left unpaid the worker resigns and records "Unpaid wages (1 pay period)" against the town.
  - **Where nothing records the obligation** (so a held payment does vanish): a household member's share of the rent paid to the leaseholder has no agreement behind it; union dues have one, but the union follows no arrears. Both were as true of a withdrawn order. Listed in the behaviour doc, not fixed: a debt inside a household would be an invented mechanism.
  - **No kernel change.** A hold is a published statement and its withdrawal. The kernel, the block schema and the generator are as they were. The 1,000-person town (`worlds/town-1000.yaml`, seed 1, 30 days) ends at 78940f9a00615cb2, as on the `origin/main` this branch started from (43323be): no code participant holds anything or writes to the bank in that month.
  - **Contract changes** (`contracts/README.md` has them): `statement` takes `hold` (a record or a list); `visible` may be `own`; an eighth refusal label, `look_first`. And two sentences seats are told: `look_first` ("That needs something found out first. Look it up (in your own papers, or by asking whoever keeps it), then say what is to be done, naming each paper by its id."), which is fixed and says nothing of the world, and `no_such_act`, which now ends "If it is for someone else to see to, direct them in writing, on their form for it if they have one."
  - **Where the build departs from the design, and why.**
    - *Who a hold is addressed to.* The design: whoever the record is addressed to, and "if it is addressed to nobody, the hold is published as the record is". Built: the resolvers keep, of what a seat has seen, who each *addressed* statement is addressed to, and a hold on anything else is a question back ("You don't know of a standing paper called X that someone acts on…"), unless the call says who is to hold it (`to=`), which is taken as said. Reason: knowing whether a seat has seen an unaddressed record means keeping every record every seat has seen, in every seat's knowledge, for a case nothing reads yet (below).
    - *Other readers of standing papers.* The design had `Book.open` leave out a paper its author holds, so that an advertisement on hold is not applied to. Not built: `Book` is the participants' shared reading code, which the knowing work is rewriting, and the design itself marks the claim that this changes no run as argued and not measured. Only the bank honours a hold today.
    - *When a hold counts.* The design's test had a payment "still paid if it fell due before the bank's plan". Built by the records' own times: a payment is stopped if it falls due at least `hold_notice` after the hold was given, whenever the bank happens to plan. That is the real rule's shape (a cut-off before the due date) and it does not depend on the bank's cadence.
    - *The refusal sentence for a hold that is not the writer's.* The design had the unauthorised "answered in writing, as an instruction from them is now"; an instruction from someone unauthorised is in fact ignored silently unless signatories are named. A hold is always answered, in one sentence for "no such order", "not yours" and "you may not sign": it leaks nothing.
    - *A payment and its entry.* The one real request for a ledger entry asks for a payment and its entry together. The key accepts the two lines, the payment alone (as before) or `too_much`; the prompt does not say which, to keep its change small. A rule is owed if seats ask for this again.
  - **The translator's prompt, 0.7.7 to 0.8.0**, exactly: the line on cancelling now says that pausing, suspending or holding is `statement(hold=<it>)`, one statement for several, with nothing else written, and that resuming is withdrawing the hold (it used to say "see no_such_act"); a new line for an entry on one's own file; the basis line's list of what another argument takes gains "the record held is hold="; `not_an_act`'s bracket says a look followed by an act that depends on it is `look_first`; `no_such_act` loses holding and the record named by description; a new label line, `look_first`; one example changed (the hold: from `none(no_such_act)` to `statement(hold=rec.44)`) and three added (lifting a hold, a hold on "every standing payment", an entry); the gloss of `statement` names the hold and the entry. From the contract, which the prompt quotes: the label line in the syntax, `hold` in the function list, three examples. No world's word was added (the test that the prompt says nothing of banks or instructions still holds).
  - **The trained model.** Its closed set of choices is its own (`config.json`'s vocabulary, made from its training data), so the model on disk loads and answers exactly as before and can never answer with `hold=`, `visible=own` or `look_first`: asked to pause something it gives what it was taught, `none(no_such_act)`, whose sentence now tells the seat to direct someone in writing. `DecideAdapter` reads the model's set and says once what it lacks (`cannot`, `ADDED`). One safe fallback was added for the other direction: steps that decode to a program outside the language as it stands (a label or an argument it does not have) are asked back, not passed on. **A retrain needs:** the data made again with pipeline 0.11.0 (holds and entries as gold programs, in every currency drawn; `look_first` briefs of three kinds; the old "held" brief gone from `no_such_act`), written and blind-checked as before, exported, and trained. Nothing was retrained and no model was called.
  - **The data pipeline, 0.10.0 to 0.11.0.** `synthetic.ts`: of statements, about one in seven is a hold (one record, or a list of two to four) and one in ten an entry (its words mostly carrying a sum in the world's money). `data.ts`: how each is said to a writer (`SAYINGS`, `PARTICULAR`, which can now ask for an argument's value: `visible=own`); a withdrawal is also said as lifting a hold; an entry's kind and `own` are implied by the wording, like `public`; `look_first` briefs (a standing payment named only by its accounts and amount; a record only described; a look and an act that depends on it); and refusal briefs are now always dealt in a seeded order, because six labels worded by hand against styles that alternate would give three labels every seat's voice and three none.
  - **Eval keys changed, each with a note in `translator/eval/pilot-cases.ts`.** Seen: `hold-payment` (now the hold), and twelve cases added. Real, 23 of 180: the eleven with gap `pause` (nine to `statement(hold=…)` of the ids the request names; two to `look_first`, because one names only accounts and the other names six jobs and "their corresponding signing jobs"); the six with gap `unnamed_record` (`no_such_act` replaced by `look_first`); the five with gap `look_then_act` (gain `look_first` beside what they had); the one with gap `ledger_entry` (gains the payment with its entry; `no_such_act` replaced by `too_much`). No other real case's answer changes: every remaining `no_such_act` is going to see or an office's own practice, and requests that direct someone to pause something stay letters. **The held-out cases were written before this change and were not read**; any that ask for a pause or name a record by description are keyed to the old rules and will count against a model that is right now. Whoever keeps them should re-key by case id.
  - **Not done.** The seat's instructions do not say that a paper is named by its id (the design's one clause on `do`): they are participant code the knowing work is in. The design's mock-seat test (refused by description, searches its files, asks again by id) waits for the same. A dated hold, a hold on part of a payment, and telling the payee: left out on purpose (the behaviour doc's "Leaves out").
  - **Not verified.** No model, trained or otherwise, has seen the new prompt: that the three rules are learnable, and that `look_first` is told apart from `no_such_act`, `not_an_act` and `unclear`, is untested. No writer has been given the new briefs. Memory: every seat's knowledge in the engine now keeps who each addressed statement it has seen is addressed to; not measured (the 1,000-person month ran in the same time). The scheme rules themselves (Pay.UK's, the European Payments Council's, NPP Australia's, BECS) were not opened; what is cited from Australia is banks' descriptions to customers. That no bank makes up payments on resumption is the absence of any source saying one does, plus the terms for a failed payment: not a rule found stated.
  - **Revisit if** a source turns up in which a resumed standing order pays what it missed (then it is a parameter of the bank: `make_up`); the world gains a payment the payee collects (its hold needs that instrument's rules: presenting again, the payee told); seats hold things other than payments (then the readers of those papers must honour a hold, and the resolver's rule for unaddressed papers is owed); seats ask for a payment and its entry together more than once (the prompt should say whether that is one request); a trained model confuses `look_first` with `no_such_act` (one label too many is the design's stated cost); or holds are used to starve a creditor (that is a finding, not a fault: the remedies are the world's).

- **Profile depth: a proposal awaiting Naz's sign-off** (2026-10-08; `docs/design/profile-depth.md`). What the nine profiles' authors could not say in the structure (informal and own-account work, extended households, a pension age apart from stopping work, unemployment support shapes, tax shapes, seasonal hazards, a river's own flow, how money is written, and ten smaller ones): for each, the smallest general change, what it touches, its basis, its size and its tests; then an order, what can be built side by side, and eleven decisions for Naz. Nothing was built or run. **Revisit if** the citizen redesign lands first (several behaviour rules then belong in it, as the document says).

- **Wave 2 of pilots is planned and awaits Naz's go-ahead** (2026-10-08; `docs/waves/wave-2/plan.md`, its queue files and `make.ts`): thirty pilots in three stages (a six-pilot shake-down first), seven profiles, five seat models, offices no model has held, eight test-only pilots; nothing was run but a ten-day mock of the shake-down. **Revisit if** the shake-down's reports contradict its estimates of requests or usage, or the knowing architecture changes the seat ids it names.

- **A place states how it writes money** (2026-10-08; docs/design/profile-depth.md item 8, track C; `contracts/world-config.schema.json`, `kernel/crates/contracts/src/config.rs`, `kernel/crates/resolver/src/calls.rs`, `participants/src/money.ts` and `calls.ts`, `contracts/test/amounts.json`, `translator/train/data.ts`, the nine profiles; on Naz's approval of the design and the contract change). Why: every world's money was shown one way and the parsers refused "1.234" and read "1,234" as a thousand whatever the place did; Brazil, Germany and India write otherwise, and a seat in such a town read amounts that looked wrong to it.
  - **The contract change, exactly.** Six optional fields on each entry of `world.currencies` (`additionalProperties` stays false; contracts version stays 0.8): `decimal_mark` (`"."` or `","`), `group_mark` (one of `,` `.` space, U+00A0, U+202F, `'`, U+2019), `grouping` (`[3]` or `[3, 2]`), `symbol_after` (boolean), `symbol_spaced` (boolean), `number_words` (object: word without spaces, digits, `.` or `,` to an integer from 10 to 10^12). The schema refuses the same mark as both decimal and group. The kernel's config check (`validate`, config.rs) refuses the same things and counts none of them: presentation only. Absent, each means what a block without it always meant; `state_hash` of `worlds/town-1000.yaml` is unchanged (below). **One built-in word list changed:** lakh and crore are no longer in either parser; a currency that wants them lists them in `number_words`.
  - **Writing** (`formatMoney`, `format_money`; one table, `writes` in amounts.json, holds both): the whole units grouped in threes or, with `grouping` `[3, 2]`, the last three and then twos, with the group mark; the decimal mark before the decimals; the sign (or code) before the digits, or after them with `symbol_after`; a space between them with `symbol_spaced` (a code is always set off by a space, as before). `formatMoney` takes `{ decimals: false }` for a round figure in a law's words (the generator sliced the text before; slicing breaks once the sign can follow). Marks: decimal mark absent is a point unless the group mark is a point; group mark absent is a comma unless the decimal mark is a comma.
  - **Reading** (`parseAmount`, `parse_amount`, step for step): a mark written once is what the currency says, where it says: its decimal mark is the decimals (so "1,234" in a comma place has three decimals, refused where the currency has two, and "1.234" in a point place is a decimal with a digit too many, no longer "two ways"); its group mark groups if exactly three digits follow and one to three digits not starting with 0 come before it ("1.234" in a comma place is 1,234; "1.500k" is 1,500 thousand; "0.500" stays a decimal). A space or an apostrophe between groups is read only where `group_mark` is one (a space: only before exactly three digits that no digit follows). Twos are read where `grouping` is `[3, 2]` and the group is the currency's own, or, with no `grouping`, where the group is a comma (as before); a `grouping` of `[3]` refuses them. `number_words` are tried before k, m, b, t, thousand..trillion, longest first, under the same rule (a marker or the end must follow). Unchanged where nothing is stated: a comma once with three digits groups, with one or two it is a decimal comma; a point once is decimals; both marks present, the last is the decimal mark. **Still refused as ambiguous:** a point once with three digits after it, one to three digits (not 0-led) before it, where the currency has fewer than three decimals and the block says neither its decimal mark nor that a point groups digits ("1.234" in a block with no marks; with only a space or apostrophe as group mark). Also refused, as before: badly grouped digits, more decimals than the currency has, another currency's sign or words, numbers in words, "5 lakh" in a currency that does not list lakh.
  - **Cases that changed in `amounts.json`** (the other 214 of the 217 pass unchanged with the fields absent): "5 lakh" and "2 crore dollars" in `aud` and "9 lakh yen" in `jpy` are now refused (they were read because lakh was built in; that is the move the design asked for); `inr` in the table gained `grouping: [3, 2]` and the number words, so its lakh cases read; "conto" left `brl`'s unit words. Added: 90 cases, 17 refusals with their wording, 29 `writes`, for `de`, `br` (the profiles' fields) and nine made-up blocks, one or two fields each (comma and point decimals, apostrophe, plain and narrow no-break space groups, a point group mark alone, three decimals, twos with a point, `[3]` alone, own number words). The test also holds the round trip (what a block writes, its parser reads, for every currency in the table and twelve sizes).
  - **The generator and the contract are one notion.** `Writing` is gone: the invented currencies carry the six fields themselves (data.ts `WrittenCurrency` is the config's `Currency` plus `country_sign`, the one habit the config has no field for, a sign also written with two letters of the code); `amountWritings` starts from `formatMoney` and adds the conventional marks and the other places for the sign, and its words for large numbers come from the currency's `number_words`, not a built-in lakh. A profile's real currency now brings its own fields into the training data (`worldsCurrencies`). Old sets that carry a `written` block still work: `withWritten` lifts it. The invented lakh-and-crore currency keeps the words in its `number_words`. What this changes in the data: forms for the nine profiles' currencies are now their place's own first (a seed's examples for `de`, `br`, `in` differ from before; `DATA_VERSION` not bumped here since no set is made by this change, bump with the next).
  - **Profiles, with sources** (each in its `sources.currency`): CLDR (cldr-json `cldr-numbers-full/main/<locale>/numbers.json`, opened) for all nine: `de` decimal comma, group point, pattern `#,##0.00 ¤` so `symbol_after` and `symbol_spaced`; `pt` (Brazil) decimal comma, group point, `¤ #,##0.00` so `symbol_spaced`; `en-IN` `grouping` `[3, 2]` with Wikipedia "Indian numbering system" (opened) for lakh 100,000 and crore 10,000,000, the only `number_words`; `en-AU`, `en`, `en-GB`, `es-MX`, `ja` and `en-KE` the English marks, the sign before, no space. All nine state `decimal_mark` and `group_mark` (so "$1.234" is read, not refused as two numbers, in the English-mark places too); GOV.UK's style guide (opened) backs the British. Kenya sets `symbol_spaced` because the Wikipedia "Kenyan shilling" article writes "KSh 10 million" while CLDR's `en-KE` has no space: the two differ; it affects only what participants are shown, since either is read. **Not opened:** the Australian Government Style Manual (the request timed out), the Reserve Bank of India's style, DIN 5008 and the Duden, the Brazilian presidency's drafting manual, the Central Bank of Kenya, Banxico, the GPO manual: no profile cites them. CLDR's German and Portuguese patterns use a no-break space between digits and sign; a plain space is written and either is read.
  - **"Conto" removed from the Brazilian unit words.** Priberam ("mil escudos") and Dictionary.com ("1000 escudos", "1000 milreis", "an unofficial Brazilian monetary unit worth 1000 cruzeiros") give it as a thousand of the unit and none gives one real. A search summary said it is colloquially R$ 1,000 in Brazil; not confirmed by a page opened, and consistent with the dictionaries. Either way it is not one real, so "5 conto" is now refused (in the table, with the words "not an amount: 5 conto") instead of read as five. A word for a thousand reais could be a `number_words` entry; none is added.
  - **Hash.** `worlds/town-1000.yaml`, seed 1, 30 days (`--until 2592000`): `78940f9a00615cb289c5eeaae6fffae1a06ed8b1eca10f64a9306d6f08494cdb` on `origin/main` (6bcea0d, built in a separate directory), on this branch with the currency fields absent, and on this branch with the Australian profile stating `decimal_mark` and `group_mark`. Stating them changes nothing code participants read in that month; the law texts the generator writes (`asWritten`) are the same characters.
  - **Not verified.** No model was called and none retrained: the translator prompt text is unchanged (no version bump). The trained model has never seen a sign after the digits, a decimal comma or a space between groups in a seat's own voice; towns made from `de` and `br` now show such amounts to seats, so the first pilots in them should be read for it before trusting the numbers. No town was generated and run for a month from each of the nine profiles at 200 people (the design's first test): the generality, generation and contract tests passed; the other eight profiles' money text changed in what seats read and was not compared run against run. Not checked against real usage: whether a Kenyan, Indian or German seat writes the forms refused here ("1,234 €" with three decimals is refused in `de`; "1,234" in `in` still reads as a thousand). The apostrophe, curly apostrophe and narrow no-break space group marks are in the schema and tested with made-up blocks only; no profile uses them. A space group mark reads a space before exactly three digits, so "5 000 pesos" and "5 000k" read but "5 0000" does not.
  - **Revisit if** a profile's place turns out to write otherwise than its CLDR locale (a regional style guide, a bank's usage); a seat writes a form this refuses and the refusal costs real acts (then "1,234 €" in a comma place might be read as the decimals with a rounding refusal, or asked back); a place needs its own language's words for large numbers (`number_words` holds them; a source for how they appear in English text is needed first); a place groups by four (myriads: Japan, China) or needs another grouping than `[3]` and `[3, 2]`; `symbol_spaced` for Kenya is settled by the Central Bank of Kenya's usage; the nine states are not enough and a currency changes marks within one world (the kernel counts money per currency, so a second currency can carry its own).

- **A landlord whose offer is declined reads the builder's answer, and offers again a week on** (2026-10-09; `firm.landlord` 0.13.2, `participants/src/behaviour/firms.ts`; docs/behaviour/firms.md, building.md; no contract change; the first of the two faults "The ages fixes landed" left for Naz).
  - **The fault.** Once a landlord asked the builder, its plan was "contracted", and only a building appearing on the lot ended that. The builder declines in writing; nothing read the letter. One decline, and that landlord never built again.
  - **What happened in the river test's town, exactly.** The builder published a list on day 60.4. On day 90.5 two landlords offered at it in the moment the builder republished (its costs had changed). The builder honours a price "published in the last 30 days" (`quote_valid_days`), counted from when the list was published: 30.1 days. So an offer at the list that stood until that very moment was under the builder's price by its own rule, and declined.
  - **The rule built.** The landlord remembers the agreement it offered and when. At its first plan a week or more after offering (its own cadence; a wake is not always a week apart, since news wakes it too, so the wait is by time and not by which wake), it looks for the builder's answer:
    - *declined for the price:* the offer is dead and the permit stands, as any permit in hand does. It decides again by the rule it already had (can it pay?), at what the builder charges now: its standing list, or the figure in the letter where the landlord holds no list as late as the letter. If it cannot pay, it waits with the permit until it can, as before.
    - *declined for the site* (the builder says the lot is built on): the plan is over, and it waits 180 days before applying to build again, the wait it already keeps after a refusal it accepts.
    - *an offer that could not be made at all* (the act refused in the moment): the permit stands, and it tries again a week on. Before, this too left it "contracted" for good.
  - **Real-world basis.** A merchant's price list is an invitation to order; an order that crosses a change of list is answered with the new price, and the client orders again or does not. Nobody re-orders in the minute the letter arrives, and nobody stops building for life. No source was sought for "a week": it is the landlord's own planning cadence, used so that no new figure enters.
  - **Why not as first proposed** ("on a decline, go back and offer the price the reply names"). That is this, without the wait and without the two other cases. Built that way first, the landlord offered again in the same minute (it plans again whenever an act of its own is done), and a decline for the site would have been offered again without end.
  - **Tests.** `participants/test/landlord.test.ts`, by hand, whatever town is drawn: three declines in a row (the letter's price where no later list is held; a later list's price over an earlier letter's; then the site), each offer a week after the last and never on the day the letter woke it; the 180-day wait and the fresh application after it; a landlord short of the new price offers once and keeps waiting; one not declined offers once. `orchestrator/test/river.test.ts` is a plain test again: the two landlords declined on day 90.5 offer again on day 98.3 at the price named, both houses begin that day, and the inspector finds the river one. It asserts the re-offer only where the town drawn has a decline, so it pins the rule and not this draw.
  - **Others of the same shape, looked for in the landlord, the builder and the firms.**
    1. *The town's office* (`city.mayor_code`, `government.ts`): a park or road it commissions and the builder declines is not offered again (the permit's decision is marked done before the offer). Same fault. **Not fixed here:** that file is outside what this pass was to touch and is being rewritten by the knowing work. It is not "never again" (the next year's park is applied for afresh), and for roads it is the opposite fault: see the next point.
    2. *The town's office, roads: declined, and asked again every week.* In all three 1,000-person towns measured below the office commissions a road on a lot the builder has already built on, is told so (`site_taken`), and commissions it again a week or so later: four times running in town 2. Eleven declines in three towns (ten for the site, one for the price), every one to the town, none to a landlord. Reported, not fixed (same file).
    3. *A landlord's invoice* is marked answered before it checks it can pay: one that arrives when the balance is short is never paid. Not a decline, so left in this change; a builder unpaid for a house would be the result. (It was the result, 42 times in three towns: fixed three entries below, "Why landlords' later houses fell".)
    4. *A landlord's order to demolish:* the builder never declines a demolition for its price (it checks no price where nothing is built), so there is no decline to miss.
    5. *Not faults:* the builder answers each agreement once by design (the client offers a new one); an order of goods declined unpaid is ordered again by need (works, shops, the builder); a loan refused is asked again after 60 days; a home buyer refused looks again the month after that.
  - **Found on the way, for Naz: the builder's quote rule.** A list is honoured for 30 days from the day it was published, even while it still stands. So a list published 31 days ago and never replaced is not honoured in the moment it is replaced, though a client could have read nothing newer. A list that stands is the builder's price; the 30 days would more naturally run from when it stopped standing. Not changed: it is the builder's stated rule, and with this fix the cost of it is a week's delay.
  - **What it moves.** Any run in which a landlord is declined. In the three 1,000-person towns of the first profile over 180 days: none was (table under "Towns and runs", below), so those runs are the same to the last hash. The 1,000-person month of seed 1: c9006abcdcd8ec5d, as before (nobody builds in a town's first 90 days).
  - **Overlap, known when this was written.** The knowing work (`worktree-agent-a0755e27f5b209db9`) fixed the same fault independently: on a decline the landlord goes back to its permit and offers at the published price at its next plan, which is the same minute, and for a decline of any kind. This entry's rule is the one kept (the wait, the site, the letter's price), merged on top of that branch's.
  - **Not verified:** a year; a landlord declined in a 1,000-person town (none met); a decline for the site that reaches a landlord in a run (by hand only); a model seat as the builder, whose letter may carry no `price` field (then the landlord reads it as a decline for the site and waits 180 days: a letter it cannot read ends the plan, which is conservative, and wrong if the letter named a price in words only).
  - **Revisit if** a landlord's planning gets a cadence of its own apart from its wakes (then "a week" becomes "its next plan"); the builder's quote rule changes (then fewer offers cross a list); the town's office is given the same reading of the builder's letters (then one shared helper, not two copies); or a seat writes declines in words alone.

- **A home loan asked a fraction over the limit: the buyer rounds its deposit up** (2026-10-09; `citizen.default` 0.40.1 (one line), `bank.lending` 0.3.1, `participants/src/behaviour/credit.ts`; docs/behaviour/credit.md, ownership.md; no contract change; the second of the two faults "The ages fixes landed" left for Naz).
  - **The fault.** A buyer asked for the price less a deposit rounded *down* to a whole unit of the smallest kind, so the loan was four fifths of the price rounded *up*. The bank lends at most four fifths, exactly. Wherever a fifth of the price is not whole, the loan asked was a fraction of one unit over the limit and was refused ("it's more than 80% of the home's price"). A first asking price divides; a price after a 5% cut usually does not.
  - **Fixed where it was wrong: the borrower.** The deposit is rounded up, so the loan is the rest of the price rounded down (`loanFor`). Where the deposit and the bank's share make up the whole price (the generator sets the bank's share as the remainder of the profile's deposit), that is exactly the most the bank lends, at every price.
  - **The bank's comparison, checked.** It compared `amount × 10,000 > price × share`, whole numbers on both sides: no rounding edge of its own, and it refused the old request correctly. It is now written as what it means: the most it lends on a price is its share in whole units, rounded down (`mostAgainst`), and a loan of exactly that is within it. What it admits is what it always admitted. The two helpers that take a share (`shareDown`, `shareUp`) use the remainder to round and make no fraction on the way; beyond exact whole-number arithmetic they stop with an error instead of rounding.
  - **Real-world basis.** A lender's loan-to-value limit is a ceiling on the loan, and a borrower who puts down "20%" puts down at least 20%: on a price that does not divide, the odd fraction of a unit falls on the deposit. Nobody is refused a home loan over a hundredth of the smallest coin; the fault was arithmetic, not behaviour.
  - **The same edge elsewhere, looked for.**
    - *Other loans* (a household's to tide it over; a firm's working capital and debts): the borrower asks for what it is short of, never for the lender's limit, so no request sits on an edge.
    - *Serviceability* (the repayment at the assessment rate against a share of income): the bank computes both sides itself; no borrower aims at the limit. The repayment formula is worked in floating point and rounded up, as documented ("ordinary arithmetic, so every machine agrees"): where the exact repayment is a whole number it can come out one unit higher. That is the amount then agreed and paid, so nothing is refused by it. Left as it is, and said here.
    - *Rent against income* (a household's third, and its bearable half): cross-multiplied whole numbers, the household's own rule against a stated rent. No edge.
    - *The seller's price cuts and floor, the landlord's asking rents and cuts:* each is one party's own figure, rounded down, compared with nothing of another's.
    - **No other instance found.**
  - **Tests.** `participants/test/lending.test.ts` (new, fast): the helpers; at 213 prices and six splits the loan asked equals the most the lender admits, a unit more is over, and the deposit is never short of its share; the lending office, stepped by hand, approves exactly its share of 56,895,911 (the price refused on day 91) and refuses a unit more. `orchestrator/test/home-market.test.ts` is a plain test again: on day 91.6 the buyer asks 45,516,728 of 56,895,911, is approved, and buys on day 92.9. One line of it changed, because it asserted the fault (`price - floor(price × 0.2)`): it now holds the deposit rounded up and the loan within four fifths; and it holds that no decision on the buyer's applications is a refusal for the size of the loan against the price.
  - **What it moves.** Any run in which a home is bought on a mortgage at a price a fifth of which is not whole. In the three 1,000-person towns over 180 days nobody applied for a home loan at all (1% a month of renters who hold a deposit), so those runs are the same to the last hash (table under "Towns and runs"). The 1,000-person month of seed 1: c9006abcdcd8ec5d, twice, as before. **How many past runs had a sale at all was not counted.**
  - **Not verified:** a purchase in a 1,000-person town (none in 180 days in three towns); a profile whose deposit is not the remainder of its bank's share (none: the generator derives one from the other); a model seat as the buyer, which states its own amount (the bank's rule is unchanged for it, and its refusal still names the share and the price, not the most it would lend).
  - **Revisit if** a world sets a lender's share apart from buyers' deposits (then a buyer should ask the lender's most, read from its published terms, not derive it from its own deposit); the bank's refusal should say the most it lends on that price (a real lender does; one more figure in the letter); or the repayment formula is moved to whole-number arithmetic.

- **Towns and runs: "three seeds" has meant one town run three times; `--towns` runs different towns** (2026-10-09; `orchestrator/src/batch.ts`, `cli.ts`, `measure/src/diff.ts`, `health.ts`; docs/measurement.md, handover.md, calibration.md, dev-machine.md, reliability.md; no contract change; nothing an existing command line does is changed).
  - **Two seeds, named.** The *town's seed* is the spec's `seed`: what the generator draws the town from. The *run's seed* is the config's `seed`, the kernel's randomness: what happens in a town once drawn. `oikos run <spec> --seeds N` generates the town once, from the spec's seed, and runs it N times with run seeds from that seed up. Who lives there, how old, in which homes and which firms exist never varied in any batch. The agent who found it worked around it with `--set seed=2`.
  - **`--towns T`.** T towns, generated from the spec's seed and the T - 1 after it (after any `--set`), each run `--seeds` times (once if not given), a town's runs seeded from its own seed up. So `--towns 3` is three towns once each, `--towns 3 --seeds 2` six runs, and town 2 of a `--towns` batch is exactly the run `--set seed=2` makes (checked: the same final state). Runs are named `<world>.town<S>.seed<N>`, since two towns can share a run seed. Only for a world generated from a spec; a world given whole is one town, and the batch says so before it runs anything.
  - **Why the run seeds follow the town** and do not all start at one figure: so that the new flag reproduces what was already being run by hand, and no second convention for "town 2" exists.
  - **What the commands print now.**
    - `oikos run`, a second line under the first: `1 town (generated from seed 1), 3 runs of it (run seeds 1 to 3)` or `3 towns (generated from seeds 1 to 3), 1 run of each (run seeds from each town's own seed)`; with towns, each run's line reads `town 2, seed 2: ...`.
    - `batch.json`: `towns` (the seeds the towns were generated from), `runs_per_town`, `shape` (that line), and each run's `town` where towns were named. `seeds` stays the runs' seeds.
    - `oikos diff`, a first line: `Baseline: 3 runs of 1 town. Change: 3 runs: 3 towns, 1 run of each.` and, where a side is one town run several times, a line saying its noise is that town's alone. Its count now reads "3 runs against 3 ... beyond the noise between runs" (it said "seeds" and "seed noise").
    - `oikos health`: `Health: 6 breaches of 15 measures, over 3 runs: 3 towns, 1 run of each.`, with the same caution for one town.
    - How they know: from the measurement tables, not from a manifest. Two runs are of one town when everyone the world began with, and when each was born, is the same (`drawn`, in `measure/src/diff.ts`). So it is true of old batches and of folders of logs, and of whatever was put side by side by hand.
  - **Unchanged, checked.** `worlds/town-1000.yaml` run as before (`--seeds`, no `--towns`), 30 days: c9006abcdcd8ec5d, twice, the figure of the commit this began from (cf92e62). Run ids, file names, `batch.json`'s existing fields and every existing flag are as they were. `scripts/pilot-queue.ts`, `pilot-mock.ts` and the wave queues pass `--seeds 1` with the town's seed as a `--set seed=N`: one run of one named town, which is what they mean, so they are left alone.
  - **Which of the log's comparisons rest on one town.** Every batch before this entry. Those dated 2026-10-08 that report "three seeds" (or two) as a before and after of the first profile's 1,000-person town, all of them town 1 run two or three times:
    1. "Nobody has the land register by heart" (a year, three, before and after).
    2. "Who owns firms: owners, the outside world, a retirement fund and a few direct holders" (a year, three, main against the branch).
    3. "A death at home is made known for a reason, not by the timing of a plan" (a year, three).
    4. "Who owns firms is in, with the retirement fund off" (a year, three).
    5. "Nobody has a god's view: the whole views removed, and the ones left" (a year, three).
    6. "Among suppliers at the same price a shop has its own usual order" (a year, three).
    7. "The 1,000-person town's opening slump" (180 days, "seeds 1 to 3", and its table of main as it then was).
    8. "A works buys inputs with the next pay period's wages kept aside" (three).
    9. "Scenarios, measured against the plain town" (120 days, "seeds 1 and 2", a third for `lean-council`: each scenario's town and the plain town are both drawn from seed 1).
    10. "The ages fixes landed" (its `--seeds 3` table; it says so itself and adds towns 2 and 3, one run each).
    - Also one town: the knowing work's year "of three seeds against main" (in its own entry, on its branch when this was written), and every earlier table in this log and in docs/calibration.md ("five seeds", "10 seeds", "3 seeds × 2 years").
    - Not towns at all: the translator entries' "seeds" are training runs.
    - What this means for them: "within seed noise" in those entries is "within one town's run-to-run noise". A difference they flag is real for that town. A difference they do not flag may still be there in other towns, and a level they report (employment 61%) is town 1's: the ages entry found town 1 an old town, five points of employment under towns 2 and 3.
  - **Three different towns, 180 days, before and after the two fixes above** (`worlds/town-1000.yaml`, `--towns 3`, mock seats, lean logs; before: the commit this began from, cf92e62, the three towns run one by one with `--set seed=`; after: this branch with all three changes):

    | | employment | unemployment | homes bought | buildings begun (houses) | declines by the builder (to a landlord) | home loans asked | final state |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | town 1 | 56.2% → 56.2% | 1.9% → 1.9% | 0 → 0 | 36 (34) → 36 (34) | 2 (0) → 2 (0) | 0 → 0 | 0620eb8f2293a355, both |
    | town 2 | 62.5% → 62.5% | 0.9% → 0.9% | 0 → 0 | 33 (30) → 33 (30) | 6 (0) → 6 (0) | 0 → 0 | a6b3c2dadec0b385, both |
    | town 3 | 61.3% → 61.3% | 1.3% → 1.3% | 0 → 0 | 38 (36) → 38 (36) | 3 (0) → 3 (0) | 0 → 0 | 5f7087f07d59455a, both |

    **Nothing moved, and that is the finding:** in half a year of these three towns no landlord was declined and nobody applied to buy a home, so neither fix had anything to act on. Every decline was to the town's office over a road (the entry on the landlord, point 2). The fixes are shown by their tests, in the 200-person towns where the faults were met. The identical hashes also show the new flag's towns are the towns `--set seed=` made.
  - **Tests.** `orchestrator/test/towns.test.ts` (new): the plan of a batch with and without towns, its names and its refusals; two towns against two runs of one town (the run ids, the first runs the same and the second different, town 2 the same as `--set seed=2`, the manifest, the towns read back from the tables as 2 and 1, and the first lines of the diff and the health report).
  - **Not done.** `oikos diff` still pools a side's runs: with several towns its noise is town-to-town and run-to-run together, which is the honest noise for a claim about towns but a weak test. Comparing town by town (town 2 before against town 2 after) would be far sharper where the generator is unchanged between the sides; not built. No old entry's table was rerun over several towns.
  - **Not verified:** `--towns` with a real model, with `--keep measures` (the recipe carries `--town`; not run), with a stop and resume (a resumed run takes its id and config from its own log, so it should be unaffected; not run); towns told apart in measurement tables older than the `born_at` column (it then says the number of towns is not known, by design; no such folder was tried).
  - **Revisit if** before-and-after tables are wanted town by town (then `diff` pairs runs by town); a spec's towns should be named some other way than consecutive seeds (a list: `--towns 3,7,11`); or the standing practice becomes several towns for every table (then `--towns 3` should be what the docs' example commands show, and the check run in docs/dev-machine.md with it).

- **Why landlords' later houses fell under the knowing work: they were houses nobody paid for. A landlord now keeps books of what it owes** (2026-10-09; `firm.landlord` 0.13.3, `participants/src/behaviour/firms.ts`; docs/behaviour/firms.md; no contract change; asked for by the coordinator after the knowing work's year against main showed contracts to build falling from 38.3 to 29.0 a year, "not explained").
  - **The question.** On main the first wave of houses comes on the day landlords' homes have been let for ninety days, and five or six more are commissioned in the weeks after. On the knowing branch the first wave is the same and the later ones are one or none. The fear: a landlord can no longer find something out.
  - **What was followed.** Town 1 of the first profile, 1,000 people, 160 days, on the main the knowing work was compared with (c115aa4) and on the knowing branch (82f3c31). Landlord 11, who asks for a second house on main and not on the branch:
    - *On main:* day 91.25 it asks for a house on its town lot. Day 91.29 the house is begun and invoiced; in that one moment the landlord tells the bank to pay the invoice, applies for a permit for its second lot, is granted it, and asks the builder for the second house. Its balance had not yet moved (the bank paid 228 seconds later): it asked for the second house on the money it had just told the bank to pay for the first. The second house is built and invoiced on day 98.5. That invoice is never paid.
    - *On the branch:* the builder's foreman goes to the lot before taking the job, so the first house is begun and invoiced four hours later in the day. That time the bank's payment leaves the account two seconds before the permit for the second lot comes back, and the landlord, seeing what it really has, does not ask. It holds the permit and waits.
  - **So nothing was lost that a landlord could know.** It is shown the same things on both: its own account, its own lots and buildings, the builder's list, the planning office's answers. The difference is the order of two events seconds apart, and on main that order let a fault through. **No channel is missing; none is added.**
  - **The fault, which is on both.** Two things in the landlord:
    1. *It decided on its bank balance alone.* It began a building at one and a half times the price in the account and asked the builder at the price in the account, counting nothing it already owed: not the house it had asked for and not yet been invoiced for, not a payment it had told the bank to make a moment before.
    2. *An invoice was marked answered before the balance was looked at.* One that came when the landlord was short was never paid (point 3 of "A landlord whose offer is declined", where it was listed and left).
    - Together: in three 1,000-person towns over 180 days (the start of this branch, cf92e62), landlords were invoiced for 100 houses and paid for 58. **42 were built and never paid for.** On c115aa4, town 1, 160 days: 33 invoiced, 14 never paid; on the knowing branch: 26 and 8. The builder carried it: nobody chases an unpaid invoice.
  - **The rule built.** A landlord keeps its own books beside the bank's balance. Owed: the price of each building it has asked for, from the offer until that building's invoice is paid or the offer is declined; each payment it has told the bank to make, until it sees it leave the account (a week at most, then it stops counting it and does not pay again); each invoice in hand. It begins a building when what is left is one and a half times the price, and asks the builder when what is left covers the price: the two tests it always had, on what it can actually spend. An invoice is paid when the account covers it beside payments on their way, oldest first; one it cannot pay yet stays open and is paid when it can.
  - **Real-world basis.** A firm that commissions work has a payable from the day it signs, and decides on its money less its payables; an invoice it cannot pay this week is still owed next week. Ordinary bookkeeping. No source was sought, and no figure is new: the one and a half and the price are the landlord's own.
  - **Three different towns, 180 days, before and after** (`worlds/town-1000.yaml --towns 3`, mock seats, lean logs; before: cf92e62, which the three entries above leave unchanged to the last hash; after: this):

    | | employment | unemployment | homes bought | buildings begun (houses) | houses invoiced to landlords: paid, never paid | permit applications | declines by the builder | final state |
    |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
    | town 1 | 56.2% → 57.0% | 1.9% → 0.5% | 0 → 0 | 36 (34) → 21 (20) | 20, 14 → 20, 0 | 47 → 21 | 2 → 0 | 0620eb8f2293a355 → 0bc8cb9afb92988c |
    | town 2 | 62.5% → 62.8% | 0.9% → 0.9% | 0 → 0 | 33 (30) → 20 (19) | 19, 11 → 19, 0 | 48 → 21 | 6 → 0 | a6b3c2dadec0b385 → f70acd3205fe6989 |
    | town 3 | 61.3% → 61.4% | 1.3% → 1.6% | 0 → 0 | 38 (36) → 19 (18) | 19, 17 → 18, 0 | 45 → 20 | 3 → 0 | 5f7087f07d59455a → 95a6c4987b665ab7 |

    The houses that go are the ones that were not paid for: paid-for houses are 58 before and 57 after. Employment and unemployment move by less than the towns differ from each other; with one run of each town there is no run-to-run noise to judge them against, so they are recorded and not read. Permit applications halve (a landlord applied again each time it thought itself flush). The eleven declines to the town's office over roads are gone in these runs; not looked into (the builder's crew and yard are less busy; the road's lot is likely free when asked).
  - **On the knowing branch's code** (its tree with this landlord module in place of its own, town 1, 160 days): 18 houses asked for, 18 invoiced, 18 paid, against its 26, 26 and 18 paid. So after this change main and that branch should agree on building: about one house a landlord in the first half year, each paid for.
  - **The 1,000-person month of seed 1:** c9006abcdcd8ec5d, twice, as before (nothing is built in a town's first 90 days, and no landlord is short of a water bill).
  - **Tests.** `participants/test/landlord.test.ts`, two more, by hand, in a test world where an act takes a minute and the bank pays later (the race itself): with twice the price and a fifth a landlord asks for one house, not two, and pays for it; with two and three fifths it asks for both and pays for both; an invoice that comes when it is short is paid at the first plan after the money is back, once. Both fail on the code before this change.
  - **Not done, and worth a decision.**
    - *The builder does nothing about an invoice unpaid.* With this change a landlord pays when it can; a client who never can is still never chased (no reminder, no claim, no tribunal). The mayor's office and any model seat as a client are in the same position as landlords were.
    - *The town's office decides on its budget lines, not on books like these;* not looked at.
    - *A demolition a landlord orders* is not counted as owed between the order and its invoice (small sums; the invoice is, once it comes).
  - **Not verified:** a year; the whole suite (the merge's); the merge with the knowing work itself (its `firms.ts` conflicts with this one in the building steps: this entry's and the first entry's rule is the one to keep there, with that branch's line on certified copies of a death's registration; checked only by putting this file into that tree, as above); a payment the bank refuses (the landlord stops counting it after a week and does not pay again: the invoice is then lost as before, a narrower case than the one fixed); whether rents or vacancies move over a year with a third fewer houses begun.
  - **Revisit if** the builder chases what it is owed (then "never paid" becomes a debt with consequences, and a landlord's books should show it as one); a landlord may borrow to build (then what it can spend includes credit); or the bank tells a customer when a payment fails in a form the landlord reads (then an invoice whose payment failed is opened again instead of dropped).
- **A payment and a tax are published laws their offices read; people stop work at ages of their own** (2026-10-09; tracks D and E of `docs/design/profile-depth.md`, sections 3, 4 and 5, approved by Naz; the curve for every town on his "yes move the towns to a retirement curve"; `world.transfers` 0.3.0, `citizen.default` 0.42.0, `world.migration` 0.6.1, generator 0.35.0 (as merged); docs: `docs/behaviour/public-finance.md`, `boundary.md`, `citizen.md`, `docs/profiles.md`).
  - **The one idea.** A support payment and a tax on pay were settings of the module that applied them, in one fixed shape each (a flat sum by age; marginal rates and one levy into one account). Now each is a law the outside world publishes as data, general enough for all nine places, and the office that acts reads it: the payments office the support law, every employer the tax law, a person both.
  - **The shapes.** *A tax law is a list of charges*, each with what it is charged on (a year's pay; less deductions: an allowance that may taper, a share of pay within a least and a most, what other charges take; up to a ceiling; from a threshold; or another charge's amount), a schedule (bands of marginal rates; a rate may rise in a straight line through a band), reliefs (a sum off; none due up to an income) and a payee (the general government, a named fund, the town), and whether its report names each worker. *A support law is a list of payments*, each a rule in three parts: who qualifies (ages, out of work, contributions on the payer's record, the household's income as declared, no other pension, children), how much (flat; a share of previous pay, gross or net, with a least and a most by age and another share with a child; a sum for a household, a member and a child) and for how long (so many days, until an age, and what may be claimed after), with how often and from which account. *Stopping work*: the share in the labour force by age band; each person's age of stopping is drawn so the shares come out as the survey's.
  - **Contracts and kernel: nothing changed.** The design had inferred that a law's `data` could hold these and had not checked. Checked: a record's `data` is an opaque object in `contracts/records.schema.json`, so a law holds a list of charges or payments as it held brackets. What is new is all inside records' data and statement kinds, which the contracts leave open: `data.charges` and `data.payments` on a law; `declares` on a `benefit_claim`; `until` and `reason` on a `benefit_decision`; a `benefit_end` statement; `charges` and `staff` on a `withholding_report`. A named fund is an account whose holder is the outside world, which the kernel already lets go below nought as it does the outside world's first account (`exec.rs`: any account an edge holds).
  - **Institutions do the work, and nobody is shown what they do not keep.** The employer withholds charge by charge and pays each into its own account, with one report to the collector. The payments office decides on the claim's own statements (taken as stated, as a date of birth always was) and on its own book: the employers' reports addressed to it, where the law has a charge reported worker by worker. That book is how it knows a claimant's contributions and previous pay; it is given the channel only where a payment needs it. It reads the published tax law to reckon net pay. It is shown nobody's income, household or records. A false declaration is possible and uncaught (an audit is a later module). No participant view code was touched; the generator's edits are the two laws, the funds' accounts, the office's two channels and the people's ages of stopping.
  - **Where the build departs from the design.** (1) A fund is an account of the outside world's, not an organisation with an office: one payments office pays every payment, from the account the law names. Smaller, and enough until a world brings the higher government inside. (2) Not built, each listed in the docs' "leaves out": a charge the employer bears; take-up below everyone who fits; rules by sex or year of birth; an early or late pension; a waiting time; a payment that tapers with income. (3) The measures' working ages are still 18 to the profile's `retirement_age` (the design wanted one stated band for every place); the labour force is now those who have not reached their own age of stopping. (4) A person reads the law to know what exists and claims by circumstance; it applies to itself only the conditions it knows of itself (its household, its income, another pension, whether it has worked here). It still claims a payment for those out of work whatever its age and is refused by the office, as before: changing that would have changed the first profile's town for no real-world reason.
  - **The standing first test: the same town when the rules are the same rules in the new shape.** The state hash covers every record, so a law in a new shape, and a law that did not exist, move it by themselves. So the test was done in two steps. *First, the new arithmetic and the new claims logic on the old records* (commits 9d40024 and 69e8e54): all nine towns end at main's state (1,000 people, seed 1, 30 days; the first profile's 78940f9a..., and its 60 days d93135e7...). *Then, the laws in the new shape and published* (1b41132, a2fec10): the first profile's run is main's run entry for entry, 311,152 log entries compared after the config, of which three differ: the modules' versions, and the paper's edition that quotes the tax law's words (a command and its record). The configs differ in the tax law's record, the support law's record, one channel of the payments office and the people's and offices' parameters. Its hash is de54db6c... from the two records alone. The final code with the curve taken out of the profile gives de54db6c... again.

    Read with care: one seed, 60 days. What is the mechanism's doing and not noise: in Ohio the 36 people paid before are 12 now, because the 5% out of work on the first day have no wages on record here, and a claim needs one (true of a new entrant; untrue of someone laid off the week before a run begins, whom a run cannot know). In Kenya 8 of 12 pensions went unpaid on main: see "found on the way". Kenya's workers take home 6% less (the NSSF). Germany's town paid over the same sum to five accounts in place of one.
  - **The first profile's town with the curve** (Naz: "yes move the towns to a retirement curve"). Pension claims did not move: the pension's age is its own. Saving did not move beyond the seeds' spread. The new 30-day hash, twice: eadf8406... and eadf8406....
  - **Merged with main's ages fixes, extended households and currency writing (2026-10-09, at 1594cb4), and what that changes here.** Every table above was measured on this branch's first base (6bcea0d), before those three landed; the towns are different towns now (main's first profile, 30 days: c9006abc...), so the tables stand as the before-and-after of this change on the towns of that day, and were not made again. After the merge, 1,000 people, seed 1, 30 days: au-vic-2026 4b115e02... (run twice; with its curve taken out, def154fb...; main's own run, c9006abc...), br-mg-2026 b3508250..., de-ni-2026 6de31577..., gb-eng-2026 af3e9284..., in-tn-2026 0e2747e0..., jp-akita-2026 f871128b..., ke-nakuru-2026 120724d0..., mx-jal-2026 237d2f7c..., us-oh-2026 0c4337b2.... The standing test holds on the merged tree too: without its curve the first profile's run is main's run entry for entry (307,659 log entries after the config; three differ: the modules' versions, and the paper's edition quoting the tax law's words). *The ages and the stopping ages compose:* the stopping age is drawn after the people are made and changes nobody's date of birth: over ten towns of the first profile every person's date of birth is the same as on main, and per 1,000 there are 48 under 5, 181 of 5 to 17, 582 of 18 to 66 and 188 of 67 and over (the census: 54, 155, 594, 197), as main's own entry has them. In those ten towns 79% of adults of 18 to 54 are in the labour force, 70% at 55 to 59, 58% at 60 to 64 and 15% from 65 (the curve on the profile's 80% gives 80, 71, 56 and 15). *The laws' words* quote sums through `formatMoney`, so they are written as the place writes money. *Versions:* both sides had taken `citizen.default` to 0.40.0; it is 0.41.0 (0.41.1 with main's later 0.40.1), and the generator 0.33.0. *A test:* the scenario test's jobs slump compared three plain towns with three under the slump and found five more jobs under it; it now holds over twelve (1,055 jobs plain, 1,011 under the slump). The cause is older than this work and is not fixed: a town under a scenario that changes who has a job is another draw of the town, not the same people, because a person's first pay day is drawn from the same stream as everyone's work and savings, and only for those with a job. A paired comparison would need that draw on a stream of its own, which redraws every town: for whoever next redraws them.
  - **Merged again with main at 09b24ed** (landlords' books, the home buyer's deposit, towns and runs): no hash above moved. Two more things the merged towns showed in the tests that run a town for months. (5) *A union member who left town while in a job did not tell the union*, so it took them to work there until their membership's notice ran out (a fortnight; the truth audit allows a week): on leaving, a member in work now says they no longer are. (6) The rent test asked every tenant given an increase to answer it; one had died alone eighteen days before the landlord wrote and had not been found: the test leaves out a tenant who was dead by then. Neither moved a 30-day hash.
  - **Merged with the knowing architecture (main at f77487b), and the tables made again on three different towns.** The hashes of the two merges above are superseded. Now, 1,000 people, seed 1, 30 days: au-vic-2026 f596db72... (run twice), br-mg-2026 4d0de5fd..., de-ni-2026 152dc585..., gb-eng-2026 5b7bc82f..., in-tn-2026 b66d4a1b..., jp-akita-2026 b366dfb9..., ke-nakuru-2026 4cdb37dc..., mx-jal-2026 38668069..., us-oh-2026 23962caa.... The standing test on this tree: the first profile without its curve ends at cc4e9a03..., and its run is main's (2e393e6d...) entry for entry: 308,669 log entries after the config, three differ (the modules' versions; the paper's edition quoting the tax law's words, a command and its record). `citizen.default` 0.42.0, `world.migration` 0.6.1, generator 0.35.0. *What the payments office is shown:* its own papers as `mine` (claims and changes addressed to it; deaths and departures it is told of; and, only where a payment needs a record, the employers' reports addressed to it), and the published laws. Nothing else: no directory, no register, no household's record, no account but its own. The means test and "no other pension" rest on what the claim declares and on nothing the office looks up; the one check beyond the declaration is against the office's own payments (it refuses someone it pays another pension itself). Asking the keeper of one record about one claimant (an audit) is not built. The guard on whole views passes with nothing owed.

    What holds across all three towns of a place: Ohio pays far fewer of those out of work (the first day's have no wages on record); Kenya's pensions are paid (on main a payment of nought to the unemployed has the world refuse the batch the pensions are in: 5 of 71 granted were paid); Kenya withholds about twice as much (the NSSF); every pension claim is as it was. Employment and saving move within what the three towns differ by among themselves, except as said for the first profile.
  - **A wrong figure in this entry's first version: households' saving.** The saving rows first read about -40% at 180 days for the first profile, where the start-up-slump work measured about +1.5% on the same town. The measure was not the repository's: it was this work's own script, which summed everything into and out of people's accounts over the whole run and so counted a dead person's estate, a leaver's money and a newcomer's as spending and income, the fault `17d311c` had already taken out of `economy/summary`. Nothing was wrong in the base (it has that commit) or in the measure. By `economy/summary` on the same runs the first profile's town saves 1.5%, 1.4% and -1.3% at 180 days before the curve and 3.6%, 1.3% and 2.2% with it; the rows above are now by that definition.
  - **Found on the way.** (1) *A payment of nought stopped real payments.* In the three towns whose profile had "no unemployment benefit" as a payment of 0, the office granted it, tried to pay nothing, and the world refused the whole batch of up to thirty payments it was in, pensions included: in Kenya's town on main 4 of 12 pensions granted were paid in 60 days; now 11. (2) *A striker who learned of a stoppage in the middle of another act went to work the next morning* (the round asked whether they had walked out, not whether they were on strike): fixed in `citizen.ts`; it showed when a 79-year-old who now works was one of forty strikers. (3) *A worker who died while under notice was paid to the end of the notice*: fixed in `Staffing`. (4) Two tests' reading of a run was refined for the towns the curve makes: who had "been in" a home counts those there from the first day (someone who has stopped work and stays in), and a couple's act refused because one had just gone out is a race like the others the test allows. Fixes 2 and 3 moved no hash: the nine 30-day and eight 60-day runs were made again after each, and every one ended where it had.
  - **Left to the citizen redesign** (written into `docs/design/ai-citizens.md`, Open 4): a payment changing how choosily someone looks for work, and exits bunching when it runs out; a pension's terms moving the age people stop; take-up; what a person chooses to declare. **Left to a yearly assessment, which does not exist:** credits paid out as money (a refundable credit, a credit for children), refunds and offsets, withholding on the year so far. **Left to track F or later:** a charge the employer bears; a tax on a small trader's turnover.
  - **Stand-ins that remain in the profiles** (each note says so, and what form the true rule would take): Brazil's family transfer paid flat to those out of work (the note has the average, not the rule: the form is there, `who.income_under` per head and `amount.household`); Tamil Nadu's pension paid without its means test (no income line in the note); Germany's tariff, Japan's two taxes and Brazil's reduction as the authors' steps; no time limit on Japan's allowance (the note has a range, not a table); no figure for how long someone must have been insured, anywhere (any contribution on the record qualifies); Ohio's municipal income tax (a range, not this town's rate: it would be a charge with `paid_to: "town"`, which is built and tested). The other eight pensions are still flat sums.
  - **Not verified.** The curves are national, 2025 (Kenya 2021), read from ILOSTAT's data file, not from each national office's own table and not for the profile's region; the United States' shares above 65 were read from the BLS page by a research agent and not re-read. Every figure moved into the new shapes is its author's, and the rules said for the first time are from the authors' notes, which say themselves which were opened: no law or rate page was opened for this work. That real offices decide as built (declarations taken as stated; previous pay as the mean of reported monthly pay) rests on no source opened. Nothing was run with a model; nothing longer than 180 days; the four-town table is one seed. The whole suite was not run on this branch (the fast tier and the tests of what changed were: the merge runs it).
  - **Revisit if** a profile's author finds a payment or a tax these short lists cannot say (add to the list generally; three times, and a condition language is the answer, as the design says); early stoppers with no income until the pension's age leave town in numbers in a long run (then a fund's or an early pension's income is the missing piece, not the curve); those who work on past the open band's start skew old enough to show (split the band from the office's own table); the shares at 60 to 64 keep coming out under the survey's in other seeds (drawn 48% against 56% expected of this town's participation, on 58 people); a world brings the higher government inside (funds become organisations, each with its office and its own book); the citizen redesign lands (the age of stopping and the search rule are written once, there).

- **Knowing, stage a: a channel says which way it is, and a whole view cannot be written** (2026-10-08; Naz: the guard "is a bandaid"; "lets do the 'hard work' now and fix this properly"; "I approve all changes"; design: docs/design/knowing.md; sources: docs/behaviour/knowing.md; generator 0.31.0; `kernel/crates/contracts/src/config.rs` `check_channels`, `kernel/crates/perception`, `kernel/crates/world/src/sites.rs`, `worlds/generator/knowing.ts`, `kernel/tests/knowing.rs`). The guard of the entries above found whole views by reading a generated world and listed the ones still there. This makes them impossible to write: information lives with whoever keeps it, and a channel is one of the ways it reaches a seat, or it is not a channel.
  - **The four ways, as the contract now has them.** A `records`, `sites` or `directory` channel carries `way`. *It is theirs* (`mine`, with accounts and holdings): records naming the seat's own entity. *They are there* (`here`): the ground, or notices put up, near where the seat is or at an organisation's own premises; anywhere else they *go to look* (`visit`): a look that names places, takes the looker's time and returns what was there. *It was published* (`published`): kinds the world declares published, each with its reason; a board (jobs, homes, what sellers advertise) is published but never shown, only searched, a few at a time; a named supplier's own lists (`from`). *They asked the keeper for one specific thing* (`ask`): a register someone else keeps, answered on request to a search that names its subject by a field the keeper answers by. *They are its keeper* (`kept`): only at a seat of the organisation the world declares as that register's keeper, with the reason.
  - **Contract changes** (`contracts/world-config.schema.json`, `participant-api.schema.json`, `log.schema.json`; the version stays 0.8 for now, see "Not verified").
    - `Channel`: new required `way` on records (`mine`, `here`, `published`, `ask`, `kept`), sites (`here`, `visit`) and directory (`ask`). `match.involving` is **removed** (`mine` is always the seat's own entity). `area` is `{near}` or `{premises: [cells]}` for `here`, `"all"` only for a board's placed notices; a list of cells, a place and (for a map) `"all"` are **removed**; a map's `near` is at most 2. New `from` (published: up to 8 named publishers), `of` and `by` (ask: the keeper, and the fields asked by). `match` must name what it shows for `published`, `ask` and `kept` (never "any record").
    - New top-level `knowing`: `published` (`{ what, why, board?, most? }`), `keepers` (`{ entity, what, why, answers?: [{ by, to?, most? }] }`), `visit_seconds`.
    - `LookRequest`: new `who` (a directory look-up: one entity, optionally with a role; one participant; one position; or a name) and `visit` (`{ places }`, 1 to 16). `QueryField`: new `holder`. `ChannelData` (sites): new `visited`.
    - New log events `visit_started` and `visited`.
    - During the migration only: `way: "whole"` (the old unscoped forms), each listed in the guard's `OWED`. It leaves the schema when `OWED` is empty.
  - **What the kernel refuses when a world is loaded, and when a position is made during a run:** a published channel over a kind the world has not declared published; a board shown unasked; a `kept` channel at a seat that is not the keeper's; an ask of an organisation that is not the keeper, by a field it does not answer by, or by a field it answers only to others (the land register by holder); premises claimed by two organisations, or by a person (for an office made during a run: more than a building or two, 4 cells). Twelve worlds the schema rules out and nine it cannot are in `contracts/examples/invalid/world-config` and `contracts/examples/refused/world-config`, each loaded by `kernel/tests/knowing.rs`; the hamlet in `examples/valid/world-config/knowing-four-ways.json` has every way.
  - **What the kernel refuses of a look:** a register asked of its keeper without `field = value` on one of the channel's `by` fields ("everything", "everything but", an order over the register); a board read without a search; a directory look without `who`, or naming two things; a visit to nowhere, to more than 16 places, or through a channel that is not one of places gone to. An answer is cut to what the keeper gives at once (`most`), never refused for asking for more.
  - **Going to look, in the kernel** (used by nothing in the town yet: stage c). A visit is placed on the looker's own timeline as an act is (a person's; one of an office's lanes) and takes the way from where they are (a person: the building they are in; an office: its premises; an office with none in the world: the nearest edge of the ground) to each place in order and back, at the world's travel times, plus `knowing.visit_seconds` a place. When it ends the kernel builds what is at those cells then (who is inside by name only if the channel's seat goes in) and keeps it for the looker's next observation; the scheduler wakes them for it as for an act done. Same seed, same visit: `a_visit_is_the_same_every_time`.
  - **The generator** writes every channel through a helper that says its way (`worlds/generator/knowing.ts`: `mine`, `published`, `board`, `from`, `posted`, `kept`, `ask`, `premises`, `around`, `visits`, `lookUp`), and the world's `knowing` from what is published (29 kinds, 6 of them boards, each with its reason) and from each seat's module's `keeps`. The 81 views still owed are written `way: "whole"`. The electoral office's one channel became two (its roll, kept; the notices it publishes); a firm's "market" channel is its named suppliers' own lists (`from`), not every record that involves them.
  - **Measured.** The 1,000-person month (`worlds/town-1000.yaml`, seed 1, to 2,592,000 s): 169,725 commands, 109 refused, 114,813 wakes, before and after: no seat is shown anything different. The final state is 37b06a5ebbbde160 (8ae630800d86f847 before): the state holds each seat's channel definitions, which now say their way. Wall time on the shared machine is not comparable between the two (51 s and 122 s, with other work running); the process's own time 35 s and 39 s; most memory held 1.21 GB and 0.76 GB.
  - **Tests that pinned the old forms** (each changed with a comment saying why, none loosened): the kernel's test world gives its seats `mine`, the street, notices put up there, a look-up and places gone to in place of every statement's map of the town and directory; `physics` "the river scenario" checked a fortnightly patrol channel and now checks that nothing of the bank is seen until someone goes; `places` checked a channel over a whole place and now checks that such a world does not load and that a detailed place's cells can be gone to; `position` gave an office a map of every home with names and now sends it round; `attention` read every lot but one through the register and now checks that this is refused.
  - **Not verified:** the contracts' version (the schemas' ids still say 0.8: renumbering to 0.9 touches every world, log and dataset while other work is in flight on them; the README has a dated note, and the renumbering is Naz's to schedule); a hand-written world other than the examples; how far `near` should reach for notices (a map's is at most 2 cells; for notices put up at places the contract sets no bound, since in a town of six shops a household that deals with seven knows them all: a world decides how far its people get about); how a model seat reads a refusal of a look (stage b).
  - **Revisit if** a real reader is refused for want of a way (then the way is named and reasoned, not waved through); `published` turns into the new place whole views hide (every kind there has a reason a reviewer can argue with; the guard's test lists them); or a keeper's `answers` are found to give away the register piece by piece (the log has every look).

- **Knowing, stage b: who's who is a look-up** (2026-10-08; docs/design/knowing.md section 7; bank.payment_system 0.12.0, bank.lending 0.4.0, city.council_clerk 0.7.0, world.migration 0.5.0, the model seat 0.10.0, generator 0.31.0; `lookUp`, `whoWrote` in `participants/src/behaviour/common.ts`; docs/behaviour/bank-payment-system.md "Who wrote it", credit.md, government.md, boundary.md). The directory (every entity, which participant acts as each person, every post and who holds it) was shown whole to the bank's two offices, the council clerk, migration, the mayor and every office handed to a model: 36 of the 81 lines owed. A directory channel's only way is now `ask`.
  - **What a look-up is.** A look through a directory says whom (`who`): one entity (its kind, its name, who acts as a person); one participant (the person they act as); one position (whose, its title and role, who holds it); a name (the entities of exactly that name or id, the positions of exactly that title, role or id, the person a participant of that id acts as; capitals aside; at most 20); or an organisation's holders of one role (the councillors of the town; at most 20). Several look-ups may go in one look (up to 16) and are answered together. Nothing comes unasked, and no look returns everyone. The seat's view keeps what it has looked up: its address book.
