    - in the schedules, eight example figures in the descriptions of amendment-form fields ("for example: 12 months", "60 days", "3 days", "14 days", "50"), which are four places' laws.
  - **Fixed.** A sum a law names stands in the example code as `{name}` and is written in by the generator from the profile, through the world's currency (`formatMoney`), and the law carries the sum (`data.fine`); a name the profile does not give is an error. The fine is `town_finance.unpermitted_building_fine`, and the inspector is given the same figure. The law on ending a tenancy is written from its schedule with `tenancy.end_notice_days` and `tenancy.tenant_notice_days`. The tax on rent is gone from the file (the modules for one stay, for a world that writes its own law). Succession says "adult children". The field descriptions say what the figure is and give no example.
  - **The figures are placeholders and say so**, in every profile: the fine is the first profile's 50,000 units, scaled by each profile's own ratio for its other placeholders (so it changes in five towns: Minas Gerais 27,500, Tamil Nadu 151,000, Akita 3,000,000, Nakuru 180,000, Jalisco 125,200, where every town's inspector had fined 5,000,000 of the smallest unit); the tenancy notices are 60 and 30 days carried over, so that law reads as it did everywhere. Each note says to source it from the place's own law. Real-world basis for the change in the fine: none beyond not carrying one place's sum into another's money; fines are rare in runs (none in the 180-day runs measured here).
  - **Every string the generator writes** was scanned in a town from each profile (records' words, agreements' words, forms' descriptions, what offices are told, seats' framings, channel titles): no other place's sign or word and no bare count of the smallest unit was left after the above. Two things of the same family are in fix 3's entry (a law saying "0 seconds" and "0%"; a tax law saying "0% above" nought).
  - **The guard.** Two tests in `generality.test.ts`: the example code's words (texts, breakdowns, schedules' words, labels and descriptions; not a rule's arithmetic) hold no sign, no word for any currency and no digit at all; and in a town from every profile no word it is given holds another place's sign or currency word or five digits in a row, the planning law's fine is the profile's and the inspector's, and every law made from figures reads exactly as its schedule writes it.
  - **Contracts:** none. Profiles gained three figures (`town_finance.unpermitted_building_fine`, required; `tenancy.end_notice_days`, `tenancy.tenant_notice_days`).
  - **Not verified:** any of the three figures against a place's law (all placeholders); that no pilot's training data quoted the old form descriptions (the data pipeline writes from the generator, so the next data is the new words).
  - **Revisit if** a law needs a second kind of figure in free words (a share, a count): then `{name}` takes a way to write those too, or the law gets a schedule; or the inspector's module default (still a sum, overridden by every generated town) is met by a world that states none.

- **A rule a place does not have is absent from its law** (2026-10-09; generator 0.38.0, city.council_clerk 0.9.1; `worlds/law/schedules.json`, `participants/src/behaviour/common.ts`; the live check's finding 6).
  - **The fault.** The German town's law on industrial action read "with at least 0% of them voting ... written notice of at least 0 seconds"; the Indian town's "at least 0% ... more than 0% of the votes yes"; the Ohio town's "notice of at least 0 seconds". Each profile's own note says the place has no such rule, and each had a nought standing for it. Yesterday's change let a profile leave an institution out and gave a notice-only schedule; the profiles were left for their authors.
  - **Stand-ins that became absences**, each because the profile's own note says the place has none, and no other figure changed: the landfill charge (de-ni-2026, in-tn-2026, ke-nakuru-2026); the serviceability buffer (us-oh-2026, de-ni-2026, in-tn-2026, mx-jal-2026); a ballot's turnout (de-ni-2026) and both ballot figures (in-tn-2026); the notice of industrial action (us-oh-2026, de-ni-2026); and one the sweep found that the list did not have, interest on overdue rates (gb-eng-2026: its rating law said "interest at 0% a year"). Each note is kept as its author wrote it, with a line in front saying what was left out. In behaviour nothing changes for the buffer and the charge (the generator already read nought and absent alike: the levy of nought made a budget line of nought, which is gone, so those three towns' budgets have one line fewer).
  - **A law of whichever requirements a place has.** Every combination is real somewhere (a ballot and notice; notice alone; a ballot alone; a majority with no turnout), and a schedule for each would be seven schedules for one law. A schedule's figure may now be `optional`, and the clauses that turn on optional figures are a list (`<<separator;;clause;;clause>>`) of which each is kept where its figures are given. One schedule for industrial action writes all of them; the notice-only schedule of yesterday is gone. The laws on rent increases and on ending a tenancy are written the same way (a rule with one figure of its two was "taken with the other at nought", which would have printed "once every 0 months"). An optional figure given as nought is not given, so a profile that still states nought gets no clause, a council removes a requirement by amending it to nought and adds one by giving it, and no generated or amended law can state a nought threshold. With no figure at all there is no law, as before; the schedule and its form are in every town, so a council can make the law its town lacks.
  - **Checked: the reverse and the neither.** A ballot and no notice (the Ohio town): "Industrial action is protected only where each of these holds. At least 50% of the members concerned have voted in a ballot on it. More than 50% of the votes in a ballot of the members concerned are for it." Notice and no ballot (Tamil Nadu): "... The employer has had written notice of it of at least 14 days." A majority alone (Lower Saxony): "... More than 75% of the votes in a ballot of the members concerned are for it." Neither: no law. Each rule (the formal condition) has the same clauses and no others.
  - **Durations in words.** A law's duration is worded in its schedule's unit where whole, else in days, hours or minutes where whole, else in the schedule's unit as a decimal: never in seconds (nought fell through to "0 seconds" because nothing divides it). What a law does not have is "none" in the clerk's words.
  - **The tax law's words** no longer say "0% above $0": the first band is said by where it ends ("nothing up to $18,200, 16% above $18,200"; "20% up to £37,700").
  - **Every town with these laws reads differently now** (sentences in place of one long one), so every town's hash moves again. The union reads the law by its rule, which says what it said where all three figures stand.
  - **Hashes.** `worlds/town-1000.yaml`, seed 1, 30 days: 5be63866badc311a, twice, with fix 2 beneath it (after fix 1: fc0a262f12eb296b; the same 166,784 commands and 130 refusals: words changed, and no act).
  - **Contracts:** none (a schedule is a record's data, read by `LawSchedule` in the participants' code; `LawFigure` gained `optional`, and a schedule's templates the list form). A profile's `public_finance.rates_penalty_interest_bps` is optional.
  - **Tests.** `participants/test/drafting.test.ts`: a law of two optional figures in every combination, nought as not given, none as no law, the world's three schedules each written from any one figure alone, and the clerk adding a requirement, removing one by nought, refusing to empty a law and making one where there was none; `orchestrator/test/places.test.ts`: notice alone, a ballot alone, a majority alone, nothing but noughts, a rating law without interest, no profile with a nought for a rule, and no town's law stating a nought, seconds or basis points.
  - **Not verified:** that the German 75% and the Ohio ballot figures should be in a law at all (each note says they are union practice: left for the authors, and said in docs/profiles.md); a pilot seat amending a law on the changed forms; the translator's training data, which quoted the old sentences.
  - **Revisit if** a law needs a clause that turns on one figure or another (either given), which the list form cannot say; or the sentences read worse to seats than the one long sentence did.

- **Raw figures in the words of papers on file: what generation wrote, and what offices write as they go** (2026-10-09; `orchestrator/test/generality.test.ts`; the live check's finding 6, its content part).
  - **The question.** Papers reach seats with `"prices":{"apartments":320553600`, `"rate_bps":1600`, `"value":5184000`. Those are a record's data, and how a seat is shown a record is another's work. This entry is about a record's own words: which were written with a bare count of the smallest unit, seconds or basis points.
  - **At generation** (every word a town is given, from each of the nine profiles: records' texts and breakdowns, agreements' words, forms' and schedules' descriptions and labels, what offices are told in words, seats' framings, channel titles). Found and fixed, each in the entry named:
    - a law saying "0 seconds" and "0%" ("A rule a place does not have is absent from its law");
    - the tax law's "0% above" nought (the same entry);
    - one currency's sign and sum in the planning law, in every town ("No money and no place's figure in the example legal code");
    - the opening budget's papers were new and are written in the town's money from the start; the form-shaped proposal among them carries its amounts as the form does (the smallest unit, in `data`), and its words give each as money ("A town opens with its first year's budget adopted").
    Nothing else at generation: sums were already written through the town's currency (`asWritten`), and no generated word counts in seconds or basis points. The guard now holds that too (no word a town is given says "seconds", "basis points" or "bps", beside no five digits in a row).
  - **Written by code offices as a run goes** (not generation; found by the same scan run over a log). One was fixed here because the budget work rewrote it: the code mayor's budget proposal said "parks 15000000 cents, roads 30000000 cents"; it now gives each line and the sum as the town writes its money (city.mayor_code 0.12.0). The rest are **not changed**, and are for a decision:
    - fourteen places word a sum as a count of the smallest unit by its name (`smallestNow`: "Hiring: 300000 cents every 14 days."): job advertisements and the employment agreements made from them, leases and their renewals, rental listings, a landlord's tax report (`firms.ts`); the union's membership offer, twice, and the membership agreement (`union.ts`); the bank's two letters that a payment could not be made (`bank.ts`); the builder's invoice (`institutions.ts`); the tax clerk's overdue letter (`government.ts`);
    - a form that writes a paper words a length of time in whole days, "seconds otherwise" (`forms.ts`, `durationWords`).
    They were left because they are one deliberate convention (a paper counted out in the smallest unit, as a ledger gives a figure: `inSmallest`), and since the office-papers work a paper filled in on an office's form must be the paper its module makes, to the letter: the form's words are written by `forms.ts` and by the engine's resolver (`kernel/crates/resolver/src/forms.rs`), both held to `contracts/test/forms.json`. Changing the modules' words alone would break that; changing all of it is a contract test's change across two resolvers and the mock seat, in files three other pieces of work were in today.
  - **Recommended:** make it in one piece once those have landed: `smallestNow` in a paper's words becomes the town's money, in the modules, in both resolvers' form texts and in the forms test's cases, and `inSmallest` stays for ledgers nobody reads as prose. Until then a seat reads "300000 cents" in advertisements, leases and invoices.
  - **Contracts:** none. **Hash:** unchanged by this entry (a test and this note): 5be63866badc311a.
  - **Not verified:** texts made by code offices in situations the 180-day runs did not reach (the scan of a log sees only what was written).
  - **Revisit if** the seat-facing work words a record's sums for the seat whatever its text says (then the paper's own words matter less), or a pilot shows a seat copying a count of the smallest unit into an act.

- **What seats needed and could not find out** (2026-10-09; the model seat 0.13.0; live check, finding 4; `participants/src/llm/render.ts`, `participant.ts`; tests `participants/test/seat-finding-out.test.ts`, `seat-papers.test.ts`). Each case decided within the knowing design (docs/design/knowing.md), and why.
  - **Who holds a post.** Who's who answered "post: Finance officer (finance), for city: Finance officer": the holder was a system of the town's whose title is the post's, so the answer named nobody, and a mayor concluded the post "has no named holder". A town publishes who its officers are, and the look-up already returns it. It now reads: a person, "held by Ada Okafor"; an office that runs itself, "filled: the office answers for itself, with no person named to it (write to the post, by its id: finance)"; nobody, "vacant: nobody holds it". No contract change: the answer is read from what the look-up gives (`held_by` equal to the post's title is an office held by its own system).
  - **A name that finds nothing.** Six names asked, two shown, not a word of the other four. Who's who is open to anyone, so that it has no entry under a name tells the asker nothing that was not there to be looked up: "Nothing is entered under: council clerk, roads office, parks office. (A name is matched whole: ...)". A register asked of its keeper already says "Nothing found." to a question that names its subject, which is all it should say.
  - **People as ids only** ("c0297 (c0297), a person"). The generated town gives its people no names: a person's name is their id. That is the generator's (another's work, and a question for the citizens' redesign); here the id is said once ("c0297, a person"). A seat learns a name where one exists by looking the person up, as before.
  - **A paper by its id; a kind by its plain word.** Done with the look into papers (the entry three above): `paper`, the one id in `named`, or `find` by id; "employment agreement" finds the six, and "employment" is answered with them as kinds like it.
  - **Going to see.** No seat sent anyone anywhere in four pilots. The offer was a clause two thirds of the way through the `look` tool's description, and the `do` tool told a seat that `look` is for looking things up in files. Where a seat has places to send someone to, `look` now begins "Look into one of your reports yourself, or send someone to go and see a place, when you choose.", and `do` ends "To find out how a place stands, send someone to go and see it: that is a look too." (the design's words for it, docs/design/knowing.md section 9). A seat with nowhere to send anyone reads neither. Of the three seats piloted only the mayor has such a report; whether finance and planning should is the generator's.
  - **Left as designed:** who owns what a dead resident held (the land register answers by lot and by title, and by holder only to the bodies a world lists: a seat writes to the registry, as the sol planning seat did).
  - **Contracts:** none. **Text a seat reads that changed:** who's who's lines for a post and for names not found; the first sentence of `look` and the last of `do`, for a seat with places to send someone to (a seat without reads what it read; the writers' instructions are unchanged).
  - **Not verified:** that seats now send someone (no model was called).
  - **Revisit if** a wave still shows no visits (then the tool is the wrong place for it, and going to see wants a tool of its own), or when people have names.

- **No id of the platform's own reaches a seat** (2026-10-09; the model seat 0.13.0; live check, finding 6; `participants/src/llm/render.ts` (`WhoIs`), `participant.ts`, `orchestrator/src/run.ts`, `translator/src/preview.ts`; tests `participants/test/seat-names.test.ts`, `orchestrator/test/seat-sight.test.ts`).
  - **The fault.** A participant's id is the platform's handle for whoever acts (`mayor-system`, `bank-payments-system`, `c0297-self`). Letters arrived "From mayor-system", papers were "from finance-system", what waited to be confirmed was `draft.mayor-system.1`, and seats then wrote to `mayor-system`.
  - **Who someone is in the world.** The run answers, for any id, who that is in the world's terms (`whoIs`): an office's system is the post it holds, with the post's title and whose post it is; a person's seat is the person; a post, an organisation and a person are themselves, with what they are called. It is read from the run's own cast as it stands (posts change hands).
  - **Where it is used.** A letter is signed: "From Council clerk (council-clerk), Town of Millbrook (city) (year 1, day 3)", "From Payments (bank-payments), Millbrook Savings Bank (bank)", "From c0297". A paper's writer, where nobody is spoken for, is the post or the person. Then, over everything a seat is shown (each report, what is on file in its instructions, a draft's preview and its reference), any participant's id still standing in someone's words is put as the id the world knows that party by: `draft.mayor-system.1` is shown as `draft.mayor.1`. On the way in, a reference to something waiting to be confirmed is read back as the seat's own draft of that number; a letter to a post or an organisation by its id needed no mapping (the engine has always taken them), and one to a participant's id still goes.
  - **Names in a preview.** Where the seat's own knowledge has no name for someone it names by id, the preview takes what they are called from the same source: "addressed to Town of Millbrook (city), which you act for", "You will write to Council clerk (council-clerk)", "ask Millbrook Savings Bank (bank)". A name is what a letter's signature or the town's own list of offices would give; nothing else about anyone is told.
  - **Why this reveals nothing.** Who wrote a letter or a paper is on it. Putting a party's world id where its platform id stood names the same party, by the id a seat could already write to.
  - **Limits.** The sweep of free text knows a participant's id by the form generated worlds give it (ending `-system` or `-self`); a world written by hand with other ids has its letters' signatures and papers' writers put right (those go by `whoIs`, not by form) but not an id quoted inside someone's words. The log keeps the draft's summary as it was made; what the seat read is in its transcript, with the ids as it saw them.
  - **Contracts:** none. A draft's id in the log, the sign-off and the pilot report is unchanged (`draft.<participant>.<n>`). **Text a seat reads that changed:** the line a letter is headed with; the writer of a paper; the reference of a draft.
  - **Not verified:** a run in which a post changes hands mid-run; any model's use of the new references.
  - **Revisit if** a hand-written world is piloted (then the sweep wants the cast's ids, not their form), or when people have names (then a person's letter is signed with one).

- **Three smaller things from the live check** (2026-10-09; the model seat 0.13.0; finding 11; tests in `participants/test/seat-letters.test.ts`, `orchestrator/test/pilot-report.test.ts`).
  - **"From day 1 to day 1"** in a debrief whose notebook ran to day 8: the span was taken from the days the seat acted in the world, so a sitting spent on its notebook did not count. It is the days it sat (fixed with the letters' count, two entries above).
  - **"stopped: aborted (stopped at 1036800), reached its until"** of a run that finished. A run cut at `until`, short of the world's whole length, ends "aborted" in its log by the log's contract, which has two endings; that is left alone. The pilot report now says "finished: it ran to its until" for such a run (and for one that completed), and carries `finished: true`.
  - **The notebook's room.** 4,000 characters were full by day 5 for the seats that write most, and seven changes were refused for room; a seat was told only the count used. It holds 8,000 now: a seat that keeps a line a day for a half-year has about 44 characters a line at 4,000 and 88 at 8,000, and the cost is at most 4,000 more characters in an opening prompt of 10,000 to 40,000. From 6,000 used it is told, with its notebook: "it is nearly full, with N left. To make room before something won't fit, strike out what is settled (change the passage to nothing), or write the notebook afresh, shorter." The tool's description says how to make room too.
  - **Text a seat reads that changed:** the notebook tool's description (8,000; how to make room) and the line under its notebook; the writers' instructions follow.
  - **Not verified:** that 8,000 is enough for a half-year (no model was called; pilot 7's year-long mayors kept within 4,000, sol's seats did not).
  - **Revisit if** a wave's reports still show `notebook_not_changed` for room.

- **A draft that fails its own checks is not put to the seat as its mistake** (2026-10-09; the model seat 0.13.0; live check, finding 7; `translator/src/index.ts`, `orchestrator/src/run.ts`, `participants/src/llm/participant.ts`; tests `translator/test/ask-again.test.ts`, `participants/test/seat-letters.test.ts`). The translator's model is untouched and nothing was retrained.
  - **The fault.** For a budget proposal with a staff line, the trained translator answered `form(... kind=budget, parks="c0007", ...)` at sureness 0.109 against a cut-off of 0.1. The resolver's answer to that call, "not an amount: c0007. Please say it another way.", was passed to the seat as "A note on your request:", two days later, at the head of its next sitting among everything else. The seat had named no such amount, and in that sitting said it had filed the proposal.
  - **Whose words the figure was.** When a draft cannot be read at a figure (an amount, a length of time, a count), the translator's wrapper now asks whether the seat wrote it. If the seat's request has those very words, the seat is asked about its own words: ""about three hundred or so", as you wrote it, could not be read as an amount. Please say it again with that written as a plain figure." If it does not, the call is at fault: the fault goes on record against the call (`invalid`: "the call gave something the request did not: ..."), the translator is asked once more with that as its feedback, and if it fails again the seat is asked for its request again in terms of the form it can see, with nothing of the failed draft: "It could not be set out on the council proposal form from what you said: the form takes these amounts: environment, parks, roads, services, staff. Please say it again, giving each one you mean as a figure and leaving out any you don't." Both are recorded as an asking back.
  - **Marked as about that request, and that nothing was filed.** Every note on a request that came to nothing now begins with the request in the seat's own words and what became of it: "About what you asked for ("File the year 1 budget proposal: ..."): nothing was filed or done." and then what is asked. The heading "A note on your request:" is gone: nothing a seat is told depends on a heading to say what it is about.
  - **At once.** A seat that said it was done for now in the same answer used to leave, and read the note days later. Now the sitting goes on for one more turn (once a sitting): the request's result carries the note, and "done for now" is answered "Not yet: something you asked for in the same answer was not filed or done, so the sitting goes on. See what came back from it, and say it again or leave it; then say when you're done." If it happens again in that sitting the seat goes as it asked, and the note is the first thing under its notebook at its next: "When you were last here, after you had finished: about what you asked for (...): nothing was filed or done. ...".
  - **Text a seat reads that changed:** those sentences. **Contracts:** none.
  - **Not verified:** the trained model's second answer to the feedback (it may give the same call; then the seat is asked by the form, which is the point); a real model's use of the extra turn.
  - **Revisit if** the count of such ask-backs in a wave is high (then the cut-off for sureness, which is the translator's, is the thing to look at), or if seats answer the extra turn by saying done again without reading it.

- **Two faults of the mock seat** (2026-10-09; mock 0.6.0; `participants/src/llm/mock.ts`, `orchestrator/src/run.ts`; tests `participants/test/mock.test.ts`, `orchestrator/test/seat-sight.test.ts`).
  - **A look asked for while reading what was missed was dropped.** The mock's code brain reads every observation the seat was shown between sittings; a look it asked for while doing so was thrown away, and a brain asks for each thing once and waits, so it was never made (a mock mayor never sent anyone to see a lot, and built no park). Such looks are now made at the sitting, with the brain's look of that moment if it has one; more than one answer takes (eight) are made at the next turn.
  - **The clerk that "counts no votes".** Not reproduced as a miscount: in three runs here (the small town and the 1,000-person one; mock clerk with code councillors, and with mock councillors and mayor) the mock clerk published "Passed: 5 for, 0 against." with all five votes. What a twenty-day run showed was no result at all, and the cause is the mock's rhythm: it said "done for now" with no days, so every mock office sat weekly whatever it was. A proposal filed on day 1 was seen on day 8 and put to the vote then; votes came on day 14; the count came on day 21. The mock now says it is back when its brain would next look in (the time the brain gave, or its module's own rhythm): the same town has the ballot on day 1, the votes on day 3 and the result on day 15, as with a clerk run by code.
  - **What a real model in the clerk's seat meets.** It is shown the proposal ("Council clerk: Proposals (1 new)"), the votes tallied by ballot ("5 votes on rec.527: yes 5"), and the office's own forms for a ballot, a result and an adopted budget (the offices' forms work), so it can put a proposal to the vote, count and declare. What it also meets is the same rhythm: only a letter brings a seat back before the days it gave, and a filed proposal, a vote and a closing ballot are not letters. The `done_for_now` tool now says so: "sooner, at the next sitting, if someone writes to you (a letter: a paper filed with your office, a vote or a notice does not bring you back sooner, so give fewer days when you are waiting on one)". Whether a paper filed with an office should bring its seat back like a letter is not decided here: it would be truer to an office that opens its in-tray each working day, and it would add sittings (and model calls) to every seat whose office is filed with often, the mayor's most of all. For Naz.
  - **The hash of a mock-seat run moves** with the mock's rhythm (its offices act on other days). A run with no mock seat is untouched.
  - **Also:** a figure whose unit is written "basis points" reads as a percentage (a law's "majority": "50%").
  - **Not verified:** the coordinator's first report of "0 for, 0 against" (not seen here; if it recurs, the run's log has the clerk's prompts, which show the votes it was given).
  - **Revisit if** a model clerk in a wave leaves a ballot uncounted for more than a few days after it closes.

- **A seat whose model call fails sits again the next working day, and is told it missed a sitting** (2026-10-09; live check 2, finding 1; `participants/src/llm/participant.ts`, `participants/src/llm/call.ts`, `orchestrator/src/run.ts`, `participants/src/llm/codex.ts`, `claude-code.ts`, `orchestrator/src/pilot-state.ts`, `pilot-report.ts`, `scripts/pilot-queue.ts`; tests `participants/test/llm-participant.test.ts`, `adapters.test.ts`, `orchestrator/test/run-machinery.test.ts`, `pilot-report.test.ts`, `pilot-queue.test.ts`).
  - **The fault.** In pilot 3 three seat calls got no answer in 3 x 180 s, and none of those seats observed again for the five or six days left. The cause: a step that failed never told the engine when its unit was due, so the unit fell back to the seat's own rhythm (`cadence_days`, a week) counted from the failed wake: day 25 plus seven is past the run's end. Any failure in the last week of any run emptied the seat for the rest of it; earlier ones emptied it for up to a week.
  - **Now.** The participant treats the failure as a sitting it was not at. Everything it was to be shown is kept (the report, what was waiting, what its last answer's acts showed), the sitting is closed, and the failure carries when it sits next: the next working day's sitting, or a day on for a seat with no office hours, whatever days it had last given. The run finishes the unit's wake with that time. At that sitting the first thing under its notebook is "You missed your sitting on year 1, day 25: you were not at your desk, and nothing was read or done that day. What had come in for it is set out below, with what has come in since." (or, where the call that failed came after an answer: "Your sitting on year 1, day 25 was cut short: you were away from your desk after your last answer there, and nothing more was done that day. How what you had asked for went is set out below, with what has come in since."). Nothing of models or the platform is said.
  - **Why a day is missed, and not asked again within the hour.** The adapter has already tried three times over nine minutes; a person who does not come in is in the next day. Trying again at the next tick would also stop a run for "model out of reach" after one bad quarter of an hour, where three working days running is the better measure.
  - **Noted, counted, alerted.** The log has `sitting_missed` (day, whether midway, when it is back, why) beside the run's `participant_error` (now with `back_at`). The queue alerts each one as it happens ("SITTING MISSED ..."). The pilot report counts them by seat and, if there is any, begins "NOT A FINISHED PILOT: a seat sat for less than its run. ..." (read from old logs too: on pilot 3's log it names all three seats and the day each last sat).
  - **A limit that looks like a time-out.** Why pilot 3's three were retried as time-outs cannot be told from its log: the Codex adapter read what the CLI said for a limit only when the call had not run out of time, and kept none of it when it had. Now what had come by the time limit is read the same way (so a limit reported and then waited under stops the run as a limit, at the first attempt), and a call that ran out of time records the last thing the CLI said. The Claude Code adapter does the same. A provider that limits by saying nothing at all cannot be told from a slow model call by call; three calls in a row unanswered stops the run as before ("the model can't be reached"), and the queue now waits and resumes such a pilot as it does at a limit ("MODEL NOT ANSWERING ...") where it used to count a failure and leave the pilot after two.
  - **Contracts:** none. **Text a seat reads:** the two sentences above.
  - **Not verified:** what the Codex CLI prints while limited and hanging (no model was called); whether those three calls were a limit or a slow model (planning answered between them, which points to slow).
  - **Revisit if** missed sittings cluster on long prompts (then the time limit per call is the thing to raise), or a wave shows runs stopping as unreachable and resuming into the same wall (then wait longer between tries).

## Open (to decide at the Stage 2 checkpoint or before)

- **Calibration, measured again after the first-year risk fix** (2026-10-05, at 0251d3a, `runs/calibration-2026-10-05` on the run server; the small town, 3 seeds × 2 years, code only, as below). Health: 4 breaches of 15 (8 measured). Recorded, not chased: Naz decides what to do with the numbers.
  - **Still off:** unemployment 2.0% (1.0 to 2.9; target 4.6 ± 1.5), 1.1 points below the band, as before. Group participation 34.5% (32.5 to 36.3; target 50 ± 5), 10.5 points below the band (was 39%). Religious affiliation falling 6.1 points a year (5.3 to 7.0; target 1.6 ± 1), 3.5 points past the band (was 3.1). Demand unmet 16.7% (at most 5; was 7.0%).
  - **Now in range:** employment to population 63.6% (was 68.7; target 63.9 ± 3) and participation 64.8% (was 70.1; target 67.1 ± 3). Saving 4.0% and renters' housing costs 22.4% of income stay in range.
  - **Deaths and illness, two years, about 200 people:** 2, 6 and 3 deaths; 36, 40 and 46 illnesses; 12, 12 and 8 injuries. No health measure has a target for these yet.
  - **Not comparable draw for draw** with the 2026-10-02 measurement: the rename changed every random draw, and the heat and first-year fixes went in between. Which of those moved demand unmet, groups and religion isn't known; nothing was looked into.

- **Calibration after the world expansion (measured 2026-10-02, not chased: for the checkpoint).** The small town, 3 seeds × 2 years, code only, at 2fd20b4. Health: 6 breaches of 15.
  - **Labour market too tight:** unemployment 1.9% (target 4.6 ± 1.5); employment to population 68.7% (63.9 ± 3); participation 70.1% (67.1 ± 3). A likely cause, unconfirmed: the expansion's public jobs (clinic, school, police, environment office) in a small town, all filled from the start.
  - **Social groups and religion:** group participation 39% (50 ± 5); religious affiliation falling 3.1 points a year (1.6 ± 1).
  - **Demand unmet** 7.0% (at most 5).
  - **Saving now in range:** 7% (6.5 ± 5), against 17–21% before. Likely the savings drawn by age, and the energy loop's drain fixed.
  - **Suggestion:** look at each against its mechanism first (who's hired, and why; what keeps people in groups), not by tuning rates. Several moved with today's changes, so a mechanism, not a rate, is likely behind each.

- **Periodic tenancies (a contract change, proposed 2026-09-30).** A lease can say a fixed `term` or none. Real tenancy law (Victoria's included) continues a fixed-term lease as a periodic one when neither side gives notice. Proposal: an optional `then: "periodic"` on an agreement's terms, read by `terms` as "in force after the term until notice". Until signed off, landlords renew instead (above).

- ~~**Construction payments must be fleshed out.**~~ **Resolved by phase B** (noted 2026-10-01): builders buy materials from a supplier (the world market, `world.trade`) as stock that construction consumes (`materials_at`); `materials_cost` and the money paid to nowhere are retired (contracts/README.md, v0.5). A local quarry, if wanted, is world-expansion §6.1. The original note: **Accepted only to unblock us** *(Naz, 2026-09-25: "not happy with this, but will accept it for now")*. In v0.2, `construct` and `demolish` pay materials and demolition costs straight from the builder's own account to the boundary. That skips the builder's bank (contrary to D26: money moves only when a custodian's seat moves it) and doesn't model suppliers at all. To return to it: have builders buy materials from supplier entities (possibly outside-world suppliers via the boundary) through ordinary bank-executed transfers, with materials as a physical stock that construction consumes, or through the work/process model (D24), where building is a task with inputs. Until then it's a documented liberty; see contracts/blocks.schema.json (`construct`, `demolish`). Known quirk: since materials are paid to the outside world, the outside world's own seat builds for free (it pays itself). Only a researcher-configured boundary module can do this; it disappears once construction payments are fleshed out.

- **Fatality by age on a condition's band (a contract addition): signed** (Naz, 2026-10-01: "yes to both, signed off"). A band has one `fatal_bps` for all ages, but real case fatality rises steeply with age, so no single figure can carry a share of the life table's deaths without killing far more children than die. Proposal: an optional `fatal_by_age: [{from, bps}]` on a severity band, read like `other_causes`. The generator would then move each age's "treatable" deaths from `other_causes` into conditions, set so that with care as usual the life table holds, and without care more die. Until signed, care shortens illness but saves no one.

- **Contract additions proposed by M2** (docs/design/m2-llm-participants.md, "Open"): `notices` on the Observation (translation failures and similar orchestrator-to-seat notes), and `framing.debrief_text`.
- ~~Directory refresh interval (D33)~~: superseded by D42 (perception is changes).
- **Calibrating word of mouth** (D45): hires via contacts are far below the 30–60% target. To address with citizen calibration (M3 batch runs give the measurement).
- ~~Citizen behaviour in the Rust kernel vs TypeScript code seats.~~ **Resolved by D30.**
- ~~Which D15 laws become parameters.~~ **Obsolete** under D18/D19: there are no laws in the kernel; laws are records in a world's config.
